# Ten teachings of bhakti. A Vaishnava сatechism

## Preface

This book has been compiled as a catechism, a manual for teaching the philosophy and practice of Krishna bhakti. It contains the main points of the teachings of Chaitanya Mahaprabhu, as relayed by the great masters over hundreds of years. The entire teaching is presented in ten essential points, each with commentary. Some Sanskrit texts, analogies and word-illustrations have been added for clarity and simplicity. The original was written by the grandfather of the Hare Krishna movement, Srila Bhaktivinode Thakur; he called them ‘Ten Essential Maxims’ or Dasha Moola Niryas, and he referred to the same points throughout his many writings.

I have admired Srila Bhaktivinode Thakur from the day I first heard of him, and I have always deeply respected what he accomplished in Victorian India. He struggled to reclaim the legacy of Chaitanya-bhakti from the hands of imposters, wrote about it in the English language for the first time, and started more than five hundred kirtan groups in his local towns and villages. He wrote songs in simple Bengali language, then published and distributed them through the countryside so they would become popular. He wrote for the thinkers and philosophers, for British scholars, and for the genuine Vaishnavas too. He spoke in the drawing rooms of the Calcutta elite and restored dignity and gravity to the Chaitanya sampradaya.

This book is a humble attempt to share his logical, systematic presentation. I hope readers will find it useful for conversations, introductory talks, formal public lectures, and personal study. If it serves to refresh the reader and is of use in teaching others, then I will be very happy.

Kripamoya Das

Akshaya Tritiya 2021

## Introduction

The founder and spiritual master of ISKCON, His Divine Grace Srila A.C. Bhaktivedanta Swami Prabhupada, embodied the spirit of Srila Bhaktivinode and was a compassionate, systematic, and exacting teacher. He used all the styles and techniques of the classical guru, coupled with a warm and friendly delivery to a young, liberal audience in a modern world.

In his public lectures, private conversations, or morning walk discussions with his disciples, and in his many written works, he supported his teaching of philosophy with analogies and metaphors drawn from nature. He told captivating and humorous stories, shared his personal life experiences, and was a non-stop stream of Indian wit and wisdom. He made himself relevant by making references to topical issues. He corroborated everything he said by quoting Sanskrit or Bengali verses from classical texts. Listening to how he taught is still the best way to learn how to teach.

Yet not all of us might be as qualified a speaker as Srila Prabhupada, and our listeners today may not be as keen as his young followers were. In fact, we may at times feel hard pressed to deliver the teachings with as much conviction as he did, or to find listeners as enthusiastic. The times have also changed from the counter-cultural spring of the 1960s. While the idea of personal spirituality has a legitimate currency, there is much more resistance to any set of ideas that are packaged or too structured. Postmodern people prefer to sort out their own ideas and come to their own conclusions. And while it is considered an advantage to join a small group of genuine spiritual seekers, you will be hard pressed to find anyone who thinks it beneficial to join an organisation.

For a few years during the 1960s and 1970s, spiritual ideas from the east were in vogue. They were fresh and their messages promised a new perspective. But as the groups teaching these ideas became popular and swelled in size, scandalous behaviour also began to appear, and the spiritually seeking public grew wary. They saw that western religions did not have a monopoly on problem priests, and that Buddhist lamas and Hindu gurus could also be embroiled in unsavoury antics.

Despite that, yoga has gone mainstream, and Sanskrit words have begun to pepper our vocabulary. The wisdom of the east – and the groups espousing it – are perceived as part of a new, established order. But because the growth has also been accompanied by periodic disappointment, a healthy caution is often overshadowed by unhealthy cynicism. This can prevent a seeker from becoming involved in spiritual discipline sufficiently to experience tangible inspiration.

As many people that want to be regarded as ‘spiritual,’ the very same number will shrink from the title of ‘religious.’ No-one, it seems, wants anything to do with religion. Religious faith is regarded as a dubious, if not harmful, idea. So much brutality is daily committed in the name of God, that the very notion of an invisible yet benevolent deity is regarded as a dangerous and outdated idea. A god that can inspire acts of love, but who can equally lead believers to perform acts of cruelty or violence, is a god we do not need in a civilized world, people say.

While there’s certainly validity in this move away from the sectarian and tribal gods of history, and while it is advisable to avoid cheaters in the search for a teacher, the present climate does demand a new approach. God needs better packaging and a new marketing strategy. A fresh effort must be now made to allay general suspicions, remove concerns, and create a rational approach to life’s ultimate questions.

Vedanta offers such a rational approach. Avoiding the vast leaps of faith normally required from a spiritual seeker, Vedanta can take the reader from no belief to a firmly held conviction through a sequence of logical philosophical statements. It was designed so that the enlightenment gained from it would be cumulative, and that the reader could proceed step-by-step, then stop when they had reached what they thought was a conclusion. Because of this idea the ancient culture in which Vedanta thrived did not insist that everyone submit themselves to a one-size-fits-all religious faith. Individual spiritual discovery was encouraged, and freedom of belief and expression was considered the natural birth right of every soul on its progressive journey. The result was a truly ‘multi-faith’ culture. Even today, the land of India remains a unique country of vastly different personal choices of religious belief.

Yet if freedom means to be allowed to believe in an ultimate truth, it also means the freedom to believe in nothing at all. So how do we balance freedom with proselytising, the act of attempting to convince someone to take to one path? Is that not interfering in their free choice? Should we not simply allow everyone’s curiosity to guide them towards spiritual ideas – and if so, what is the need for any kind of proselytising in religion?

Consider a fruit market. Sellers assemble in the town square with their stall of colourful produce, and potential customers come to inspect the goods. But the vendors also call out descriptions of their fruit, arresting the attention of the public and inviting them over to examine their wares. Schools of philosophy are just like that. Although their ideas can be purchased by anyone who is in search of an idea to improve their life, the schools do not sit in silence; they also call out to attract the public to their wares. Philosophical conviction does not prevent someone from engaging in the sophisticated art of persuasion.

We all eat fruit. We like the taste and it is healthy. And in perhaps a more subtle way we all digest philosophy. It is all around us, and even if we don’t discuss it that often, we buy into it by allowing it to form our interpretation of the world, of society, politics, morals, and the very values we stand for. Politicians, ad agencies, internet influencers, popular culture, and good friends, they all sell ideas which have their roots in philosophy.

Those who follow the path of the Bhagavad-gita also do this – they have done it through history for thousands of years. Not in an aggressive or martial manner, but by humility and a peaceful demeanour, cutting wit, wisdom, and a humorous analysis of the human condition, they extend themselves to everyone with an offer of one specific teaching: that Krishna is the origin of all, and that the soul’s joy is to be reunited with him. The path is bhakti, pure love of Krishna. Even though they wish everyone the freedom to choose, the distinct offer remains, and whether the listener buys their wares or not, the devotees of Krishna will make the same offer to any potential customer.

In this book I have provided the ten essential instructions of Chaitanya, the great eastern teacher and avatar, but tried to do so with a logical sequence of explanatory notes, an order that would make it suitable for presentation. I have also taken into consideration that most of the classical Vaishnava texts are the recorded conversations of highly enlightened beings. The discussions took place between great sages, celestial gods, or an incarnation of God and some very select individuals. As readers of the scriptures, we listen in on conversations between people who already knew a lot before the talking began.

For example, although Arjuna was in despair at the beginning of the Bhagavad-gita, that does not mean that he was not already experienced in yogic discipline and the science of Godhead. Although we often explain that Bhagavad-gita is an ABC of spiritual life, it is a conversation that reminds Arjuna of teachings he has heard many times before. He was not an absolute beginner, by any means. If the Gita is one hour long, it is only a few minutes into the conversation when Krishna declares His divine identity. If we have a one-hour discussion with a newly interested person, a few minutes into the conversation may not be the most tactful point at which to reveal the identity of Krishna.

Similar are the discussions between Sri Chaitanya Mahaprabhu and his early followers, Sanatana Goswami and Rupa Goswami. They were already learned in shastra, or the sacred texts, before meeting their Master and were acquainted with the messages and narratives of the Shrimad Bhagavatam. When he told them about Krishna it wasn’t in a vacuum of knowledge of Krishna; Chaitanya added to their knowledge but did so in a way that took them from where they were to where he needed them to be.

Since the Dasha Moola Niryas, also rendered much later as the Dasa Mula Tattva, are an expression of siddhanta, a series of conclusive philosophical statements, they properly begin where more introductory teachings end. That Krishna is the Supreme Truth is second of the ten points, whereas in actual delivery, in conversation or public talks, it may be more appropriate as a much later point for beginners. Newcomers already struggling with some of the primary concepts of eastern philosophy such as the soul and reincarnation may take time to graduate to such ideas as the existence of God.

So, if you are a Vaishnava teacher, especially in a large western city, do not forget that many of the basic ideas will need explanations, from ‘different angles of vision,’ before your students can understand them. You will need to give time for the ideas to be digested. People must be allowed time to think before moving on to the next idea, and they do not have to be given everything at once. It may help you, in your teaching, to learn the body language that indicates that someone needs a short rest in between ideas. You can always speak to them again. If you are using this book to prepare a public talk, you can choose your main themes, then judge whether those ideas are being ‘digested’ by your audience as you deliver. The book may also be helpful if you are teaching individual students as part of their curriculum. When speaking one-to-one you can cover a certain idea, then begin with the following idea at the next meeting.

I have provided several word-illustrations for the ten points, as well as Sanskrit citations. There are many more, of course. Most of them will be common knowledge to experienced teachers. In the Appendixes there are helpful shastra references. The unique feature of this book is not the provision of new evidence but that all the pieces are assembled in one place. Because many natural examples are drawn from Indian history, and the Indian sub-continent, the listener may be unfamiliar with them. Teachers should feel no hesitation in creating new illustrations which are more culturally or geographically relevant.

One of the key elements in the transmission between a teacher of Vedanta and a sincere enquirer is that by systematic presentation doubts are removed and conviction established. In contrast to this, I have found that it is not uncommon for someone to hang on to doubts for many years, either burying them or not taking the risk of appearing foolish. This tends to diminish when we address the doubt – expressed or concealed – by speaking about the area of concern. With gurus today sometimes not directly teaching their disciples, this compendium of all the essential points will also be helpful as a device or program for ensuring that each student is taken through a gradual and systematic education in Vaishnava philosophy and practise.

Naturally, given the right person and the right level of interest, it is possible to say anything about Krishna and in any order at all. Enthusiasm and conviction on the part of the teacher, and a keen interest on the part of the student can create the highest level of spiritual understanding. If this book helps then kindly regard it as my service to you, but if you prefer to be spontaneous or intuitive then please always allow your inspiration to be your guide.

## Definitions

**Before examining the important subject matter of this book, it may help the reader, in the spirit of the philosophers of old, to first try to agree on working definitions of common words throughout the text.**

**Absolute Truth** - The statement “It is raining,” is true for as long as it is raining. When it stops raining, it is no longer a true statement. Because the statement is dependent on a set of conditions – rainfall – it is known as a conditional statement. Because it is only true while it is raining, and the statement is only true relative to the condition of rainfall, it is known as a ‘relative truth.’ Since conditions are always fluctuating, the only truth which is always true will be one which is not conditioned by any such fluctuations. This unchanging truth, or set of truths, is known as the ‘absolute truth.’ Philosophers search for that.

**Ultimate Reality** - That which is real is something that can be perceived by the senses; it can be seen, heard, or touched. Yet the senses can be wrong, as in the case of a mirage in the desert. Therefore, reality cannot be defined by sense perception. Reality must be real quite independent of sense perception. When we discover that which does not depend on the flawed instruments of perception, which is real whether it is perceived or not, we have discovered an ‘ultimate reality.’

**Cause of all Causes** - Every phenomenon has a cause. When chemical elements combine to form a compound, the elements are the cause and the compound the ‘effect.’ Compounds can then combine as part of an organic system. The compounds then become the cause of the organic system. In turn the organic system itself becomes the cause of actions which generate further effects. In this way, all phenomena are composed of multiple chains of causes and effects. Vedic philosophy speaks of ‘instrumental’ causes, those close to the witnessed phenomenon, and ‘remote’ causes, those closer to the beginning of the chain of causes and effects. When the original beginning of all chains of causes and effects is found, we have discovered the ‘first cause’ or ‘the cause of all causes.’

**Philosophy** – the love of knowledge or wisdom. The study of the fundamental nature of knowledge, reality, and existence. In the classical West, philosophia had three main branches: Epistemology (the ways of gathering knowledge), Ethics (personal and social morality and values), Metaphysics (laws of higher nature). To these were later added aesthetics, politics, the philosophy of religion, the mind and language. Each of these became quite separate academic branches. The Vedic concept of philosophy is the search for wisdom that leads to personal transformation and liberation from the world. The ‘search for wisdom’ is not separate from the exploration of the sacred texts such as the Upanishads, and the teaching of qualified gurus who represent them.

**Religion** – from the Latin word for ‘scrupulous’ in the sense of an attention to the details of behaviour, especially regarding the divine. Awareness of, prayer to, service, glorification, and love are all included in religious expression. Postmodern study of religion has often been from the point of view of psychology, anthropology, and the notion of man-made patterns of behaviour. The Vedic concept of religion is that the divine is an objective reality, not a subjective phenomenon, and is the origin of all dharma, or moral and spiritual disciplines leading to ultimate freedom.

**Theology** - the systematic study of the nature of the divine and, more broadly, of religious belief. Theology analyses the transcendent, that which is above nature, but also deals with religious epistemology, or how religion claims to have special knowledge and how that is revealed. A theologian may study religion from the viewpoint of critical study of the texts, from a social and historical perspective, or using the insight of the anthropologist or ethnographer. Theology may be used to propagate, reform, or justify a religious tradition or a theologian might use it to interpret the world or to address a current need.

**Spirituality** - The term spirit generally means "animating or vital principle." It is derived from the Latin word spiritus (soul, courage, vigour, breath) and is related to spirare (to breathe). The same idea as the Greek pneuma and the Hebrew ruach. The meaning of the word ‘spirituality’ has developed and expanded over time, and various connotations can be found alongside each other. In traditional Christianity, spirituality referred to a process of re-formation which "aims to recover the original shape of man", oriented at "the image of God” as exemplified by the founders and sacred texts of the religions of the world. The term was used to refer to a life oriented toward the Holy Spirit and broadened during the late Middle Ages to include mental aspects of life.

In modern times, the term has spread to other religious traditions and broadened to refer to a wider range of experience. Modern usages tend to refer to a subjective experience of a sacred dimension and the "deepest values and meanings by which people live", often in a context separate from organized religious institutions, such as a belief in a supernatural (beyond the known and observable) realm, personal growth, a quest for an ultimate or sacred meaning, religious experience or an encounter with one's own "inner dimension.” The Vedic concept is that the inner self is always existing, and the outer self is always changing. Since the latter is temporary, our real self is the spirit, and anything done to enhance our sense of identity with this eternal self is ‘spirituality.’

**Mysticism** - the experience of alternate states of consciousness and religious ecstasies together with whatever ideologies, ethics, and practices may be related to them. In modern times, "mysticism" has come to mean the aim at the "union with the Absolute, the Infinite, or God". The Vedic concept is one of ecstasy (ananda) derived from the soul’s union with God (yoga).

**God** – In Western thought, God is traditionally described as the supreme being or ultimate reality that possesses at least three necessary properties: omniscience (all-knowing), omnipotence (allpowerful), and omnibenevolence (supremely good). In other words, God knows everything, has the power to do anything, and is perfectly good. God is the creator and ruler of the universe and source of all moral authority. The word ‘God’ is derived from the Old Germanic Gudan, from the Proto-Indo-European Ghutom which, in turn, may have come from the Sanskrit Huta, ‘One who is called, invoked and worshipped by sacrifice.’

**Theism** – the belief in one God. In the words of the man who coined the word: “That a perfectly conscious understanding being, or mind, existing of itself from eternity, was the cause of all other things.” There are many forms of belief which are grouped under the broad term of ‘Theism.’

- Monotheism- a belief that there is but one God, as opposed to there being many gods.
- Polytheism- a belief that there are many gods.
- Pantheism - one God existing in the creation, that “God is the creation and the creation is God.”
- Panentheism – “The physical universe is part of God, but God is more than the universe. The world is God, but God is more than the world.”
- Agnosticism - no clear or definitive knowledge of whether there is a God or not.
- Kathenotheism – “Many gods, worshipped one at a time. Each is Supreme at that time.”
- Monism – the belief that all is of one essential essence, substance, or energy.
- Deism – “God exists but has nothing to do with this world.”
- Monistic theism - a variant of both monism and monotheism, viewing God as both immanent (within the world and accessible) and transcendent (independent of the world and inaccessible).
- Atheism – a belief that there is no God. It is worth noting that while modern atheists make efforts to reduce God to a psychological projection, the God of the Upanishads, conceived of as the ultimate consciousness of which the limited human consciousness is a part, remains of some intellectual interest to them.

**Theodicy** – the problem of the existence of a benevolent God while there is suffering in the world. “If God is all perfect, why is His creation so imperfect?”

## The art of argument

**In philosophical circles, the gentle art of establishing truth is often done in the form of discussion and debate. Evidence from authorities is presented, word definitions examined, and moral and ethical principles weighed and accorded their relative importance. Through this discipline the intellect is kept sharp, the mind clear, and the heart pure.**

In ancient India, in a green mountain valley fed by rushing ice-cold streams, two groups of philosophers sit down to debate in public. Eyeing one another with keenness and memorizing last-minute lines of handwritten text, the pandits wait patiently while hundreds of people gather to watch. Coloured flags flap in the fresh breeze, and the fragrance of freshly lit incense perfumes the air. The atmosphere is tense with expectation. Between the two contenders is an adjudicator, an expert in the Veda. Sanskrit verses will be recited in quick succession, first the champion from one school and then his opponent.

From quoting verses, the debaters will move on to syllables, arguing over the minute differences between positions of vowels. The adjudicator, always from a third school in a distant village, must be quick to spot any weakness in grammar, logic, rhetoric, and philosophical consistency. Listening for accurate pronunciation, he knows he must be strict but fair. Each school has a lot to win, and the losers may forfeit more than their reputation. He knows that sometimes these matches are over quite quickly, sometimes they take hours with breaks for lunch, and occasionally the event lasts for days.

In some cases, the losing school would have to submit to the other, all the disciples surrendering to the opposing school’s champion as their guru. Along with the intellectual surrender of the entire school came the assets of the school, which in some cases totalled several villages with land, buildings, temples, and revenue. Opening your mouth and speaking philosophy was a serious business. You had to know exactly what you were going to say, and no vague ideas were acceptable. No half-formed, abstract, or poorly articulated notions would be tolerated – you had to be clear, logical, and systematic in your thought and presentation. One declaration had to lead to the next, and the chain of statements to a firm conclusion. Everything had to be backed up by logic, supportive verses from the Vedas and grammatical clarity. If you could not prove what you stood for by citing Vedic evidence, the discussion would end there, and the other person would assume that he had gained a victory.

This method, continued for thousands of years, led to philosophical clarity, razor-sharp presentation skills and a widespread memorisation of large portions of the Veda. It also led to each school placing great emphasis on codifying their conclusive philosophical precepts down into a short list of statements. Each statement would have to be supported with verses from the Veda, and therefore irrefutable. Choice of Veda portions would vary from school to school. The statements could then be memorised by each generation of students, and expanded and commented upon by successive head gurus, or acharyas. The list of terse, philosophical points was known as the school’s siddhanta, or ‘conclusive doctrine.’

The word siddhanta is used in two ways. It can refer either to the entire gamut of teachings and outlook of an individual school, or it can be used to indicate the singular, ultimate, distinguishing feature of such a school. Either way, and well before stepping into the arena of debate, young students would be taught the siddhanta as part of their training. It was essential in their developing a sharpened, intellectual mastery of the theology and grasping the underlying reasons for the specific daily practices of the school. It was also needed to help distinguish one school from another, particularly when schools were established with only shades of difference in their conception or practice. The siddhanta was an expression of the philosophical conclusion and yoga methods of the original guru, sometimes written down hundreds of years before. The students would recite daily a litany of such statements, along with any relevant supporting quotes, particularly verses from the Upanishads and Vedanta Sutra. When they had learned them verbatim, they would debate with each other and then, like newly qualified lawyers, step into the debating arena for the first time.

## Saying it succinctly: The Sutra

When a conclusive philosophical statement, a siddhanta, is expressed in a series of short statements or aphorisms it is known as a sutra. A sutra is a ‘thread’ or ‘necklace’ of ideas, often connected by a logical sequence of reasoning. Each bead of an idea might be formed from several statements. When condensed into a compact sutra form, the siddhanta, or any aspect of the philosophical arguments that led to the formation of the siddhanta, could be easily referred to, memorised for personal learning, and also used in a debate. For the student to grasp the detailed meaning, the sutra could also be expanded using insightful commentaries written according to rules of Sanskrit grammar and philosophy. Teachers would also write commentaries on existing commentaries providing a cascade of knowledge over the centuries. When commentaries became too unwieldy, the knowledge would again become condensed into a sutra. So, expansion and contraction of the verbal and written forms of scriptural knowledge was an important facet of the Vedic tradition.The father of the Veda is known as Srila Vyasadeva, and his father is known as Parashara Muni. In the ancient Purana which bears his name, the Parashara Purana, the explanation of the sutra form and the necessity for commentaries is given. This extract is from the 18th chapter, verses 11-23:

**“O sage, the Veda-śāstra unexpectedly appears, for the people, to be having many meanings. But when described with logic (nyaya), it again shines brightly as having one meaning. It is by the order of Lord Shiva that the great philosophers thus present conclusions in the form of sutras.”**

**“Knowers of sutras know sutra as a statement having minimal syllables, to be conclusive in meaning, possessing in itself the essence of the discussed matter, unique in comparison to other sutra-statements, universally comprehensive, and having no faults in its linguistic presentation.”**

**“It is due to the satisfaction of Lord Shiva, holder of the trident, that both sages and humans, make the meaning of a sutra known as it is by the form of the bhashya (extended commentary).”**

**“Knowers of bhashya know bhashya as that kind of commentary where the meaning of the sutra is described by statements explaining the words of the sutra. This explanation causes the reader to comprehend the true meaning of the sutra.”**

**“In turn, due to satisfaction of Lord Rudra and Bhavani, some persons, by the strength of their austerity, make commentary on bhashya known as vyakhyana (literally ‘the process of explanation’).”**

**“Division of words, speaking the meaning of words, analysis of a compound word into its constituent parts, connection of all the words in a sentences in a row of statements, substantiation of them and providing answers to real or foreseeable objections, – these five characteristics constitute vyakhyana.”**

**“And some speak the meaning of bhashya by making commentary in form of a varttika – an explanation derived from its meaning, known as the vritti.”**

The text continues by explaining that all commentaries, short or long, serve to illuminate the true meaning of the text. They are variously known, according to their length and formation, and with reference to their position as primary, secondary, or tertiary commentaries as bhashya, varttika, vyakhyana, prakarana, tika and tipani. In whatever form the shastra is heard, concludes the speaker, it must be heard from the guru for the disciple to become illuminated as to the nature of the soul.

Sometimes a sutra would take the form a miniature argument. The argument would begin with a proposition followed by another statement. A conclusion would complete the sequence. Today this form of argument is known as a syllogism or ‘deductive reasoning drawn from two given propositions.’ Although a sequence with three logical premises would be common, the standard seems to have been five, and some commentators, such as the ancient sage Vatsayana, mentions that some logicians used 10-membered sequences.

One way of demonstrating that the speaker fully understood the arguments of his opponent was that he would articulate fully the opposing view. This was known as a purva-paksha. Having shown that his grasp of the other’s view was correct, and that he was arguing for the purpose of understanding, not selfish indulgence, he would then introduce a counter argument, ending with conclusive statements. Where logical arguments involved 5-membered sequences, they would run as follows:

1. Vishaya – Thesis or statement.
2. Samshaya – Expression of doubt.
3. Purva-paksha – Antithesis or presentation of opposing ideas.
4. Siddhanta – Synthesis or ‘the conclusive truth.’
5. Samgati – Agreement or demonstration of consistency.

The fifth element, samgati, involved the speaker or author ensuring that each section of the argument, particularly when written, was logically related to a further six elements:

1. Objection (Akshepa)
2. Illustrative examples (Drishtanta)
3. Counter illustration (Prati Drishtanta)
4. Harmony with context (Prasanga Samgati)
5. Citations from other texts (Utpatti Samgati)
6. Denial (Apavada Samgati)

As in many traditions within the Vedic culture, the precedent for how to debate – either by spoken word or by writing a book - had been set many centuries before. The Upanishads are the philosophical portion of the Veda and are known as the ‘end of the Veda,’ or Vedanta. The grand compiler of the Veda and their corollaries, Srila Vyasadeva, also authored a summary study of the content of the Vedanta, known as the Vedanta Sutra or Brahma Sutra. His object was to explain and reconcile different statements from the Upanishads and to provide a key to understanding them as the ultimate teaching of the Vedas. In 189 terse syllogisms, comprised of 555 verses, he set out a document which, to this day, has served as the foundation of Indian philosophy. In connection with the Vedanta sutra, Srila Prabhupada writes:

“Anyone familiar with such sūtras must be aware of the Vedānta-sūtra, which is well known among scholars by the following additional names: (1) Brahma-sūtra, (2) Śārīraka, (3) Vyāsa-sūtra, (4) Bādarāyaṇa-sūtra, (5) Uttara-mīmāṁsā and (6) Vedānta-darśana.

There are four chapters (adhyāyas) in the Vedānta-sūtra, and there are four divisions (pādas) in each chapter. Therefore, the Vedānta-sūtra may be referred to as ṣoḍaśa-pāda, or sixteen divisions of aphorisms. The theme of each and every division is fully described in terms of five different subject matters (adhikaraṇas), which are technically called pratijñā, hetu, udāharaṇa, upanaya and nigamana. Every theme must necessarily be explained with reference to pratijñā, or a solemn declaration of the purpose of the treatise. The solemn declaration given in the beginning of the Vedānta-sūtra is athāto brahma jijñāsā , which indicates that this book was written with the solemn declaration to inquire about the Absolute Truth. Similarly, reasons must be expressed (hetu), examples must be given in terms of various facts (udāharaṇa), the theme must gradually be brought nearer for understanding (upanaya), and finally it must be supported by authoritative quotations from the Vedic śāstras (nigamana).”

So important was Vedanta Sutra that it was one of the three texts on which mediaeval schools had to base their arguments. The great debater and teacher, Adi Shankaracarya (788-820 AD), during his period as an intellectually dominant philosopher in India, declared that three texts should henceforward be the ‘starting places’ for all scholarly debate. Evidence cited from any one of them, or all of them, was to be held as authoritative by all schools. The first was Vedanta Sutra, and the others the Bhagavad-gita and the Upanishads. Collectively, they were known as the prasthana-trayi or ‘three starting places.’

For any group to be recognised as an authentic sampradaya – a school acknowledged all over India, the group had to present a unique written commentary upon the Vedanta Sutra. Through this commentary, the group had to demonstrate that their philosophical conclusions were thoroughly grounded upon solid scriptural evidence. Failure to produce a commentary, debated in the time-honoured form of syllogistic argument, would mean they could not be recognised as a distinct school.

The Vaishnava school has often had to defend itself from counter argument and has produced very tight and systematic argumentation in response. Even as recently as 1718 the followers of Sri Chaitanya Mahaprabhu had to produce their own commentary on the Vedanta Sutra in response to accusations that they were not a distinct school. There were good reasons for this. Coming in the line of the renowned Madhvacarya (1238-1317) the Gaudiyas, as they were called, had always accepted the Bhagavata Purana or Shrimad Bhagavatam as the natural commentary on Vedanta Sutra because it was composed later by the same author, Srila Vyasa, in the height of his spiritual maturity. Sri Chaitanya MahР”Рѓprabhu had spoken:

*ataeva bhāgavata-sūtrera ‘artha’-rūpa*  
*nija-kṛta sūtrera nija-‘bhāṣya’-svarūpa*

**Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam gives the actual meaning of the Vedānta-sūtra. The author of the Vedānta-sūtra is Vyāsadeva, and he himself has explained those aphorisms in the form of Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam.**  
**Madhya 25.142**

To demonstrate just how the entire siddhanta is present within Shrimad Bhagavatam in minute detail, Śrīla Jīva Gosvāmī (1513-1598) composed six sandarbhas , or ‘collections,’ where he arranged verses from the Shrimad Bhagavatam under thematic categories. Thus, the Ṣaṭ-sandarbha became the most complete exposition of the siddhanta for the followers of Chaitanya, known as the Gaudiyas. In his magnum opus , the Chaitanya Charitamrita , (1616) the author Krishnadas Kaviraj Goswami writes:

*bhāgavata-sandarbha’-nāma kaila grantha-sāra*  
*bhāgavata-siddhāntera tāhāṅ pāiye pāra*

**In particular, Śrīla Jīva Gosvāmī compiled the book named Bhāgavata-sandarbha, or Ṣaṭ-sandarbha, which is the essence of all scriptures. From this book one can obtain a conclusive understanding of devotional service and the Supreme Personality of Godhead.**

**Chaitanya Charitamrita Antya 4.229.**

Other works of the ācāryas explain the same truths, sometimes deeply focusing on one constituent of the vast body of siddhānta. Rūpa Gosvāmī’s Bhakti-rasāmṛta-sindhu provides the teachings on rasa in all details, his Laghu-bhāgavatāmṛta establishes Kṛishna as the highest form of the Lord, the source of other forms, and Sanātana Gosvāmī’s Bṛhad-bhāgavatāmṛta shows the super-excellent position of Goloka and the gopīs in the hierarchy of the worlds and devotees respectively.

In terms of their affiliation to a lineage of great teachers, many Bengali followers of Chaitanya (1486-1534) claimed only that their line began with him, whereas others, wishing for more formal recognition, traced their ancestry back to Madhva Acharya (1238-1317). But by the beginning of the eighteenth century, and with the geographical growth of the community, the nuances between the Gaudiyas and the Madhvas had become all too evident. It was clear that the philosophy of Chaitanya and that of Madhva were of sufficient difference as to constitute two distinct schools.

In addition, many intellectuals did not accept their proposal that the Bhagavatam was a commentary on Vedanta Sutra, and without their own commentary, they reasoned, how could the Gaudiyas be recognised as a distinct community? A scholarly debate to decide the issue was required. The champion of the hour was the greatly learned and devoted Baladeva Vidybhusana who not only composed his own commentary on the Vedanta Sutra, the Govinda Bhashya, within an astonishingly short time, but successfully argued its conclusions before a large gathering of royal scholars at Galta, near Jaipur, northern India.

The tradition of writing commentaries on the Vedanta Sutra continues today. The Swami Narayana group began in Gujarat, India, when the saint and reformer Sahajananda Swami (17811830) attracted many thousands of followers to his message and, in 1826, wrote his instructions as the Sikshapatri. Although the group had this book and others, and though it had a claimed connection with Sri Ramanuja (1017-1137) and his sampradaya through one Ramananda Swami (1380-1475), and although it has, in the last few decades, spread successfully throughout the world, the group had no commentary on the Vedanta Sutra. So, in 2017, a Sanskrit bhashya was completed and the group became formally established as a unique sampradaya.

Another form of siddhanta is the karika – ‘a concise doctrinal statement in verse.’ Great historical teachers such as Rūpa Goswami, Kavi Karṇapūra and Baladeva Vidyābhūṣaṇa wrote karikas to encapsulate the essential meaning in a concise, poetry form, then explained them either in other poetic verses or in a prose commentary.

## The Truth, the Means, and the Goal

The general content demanded of any form of siddhanta, whether a short poem or longer exposition, is that it should offer nothing less than a cogent, wide-ranging and well-evidenced explanation of Brahman, the ultimate reality and the infinite origin of all things. The explanation must describe how the observer, the individual, is related to both Brahman and the observed world of matter, the phenomenal world of perception, and must explain why the consciousness of the individual cannot already experience the infinite. The siddhanta must explain the practical methods of becoming free from illusion and fixed in that higher reality, and the ultimate end of the soul’s journey into that reality. In this, the siddhanta goes well beyond a credo, a simple expression of faith or belief. It includes both a description of the perfection of God realisation, and the practical means to achieve it. In Christian terms, an expression of siddhanta is credo, regula, and visionem mysticam, or belief structure, spiritual discipline, and mystical vision, all in one.

And so, the siddhanta begins with a statement of the position of the conscious self, the observer of the external universe. According to Vedanta, reality or tattva is divided into two categories: para or what is ultimate, and apara or what is dependent on the one ultimate reality. The expressions used are para-tattva and apara-tattva. The apara-tattva is also of two kinds: the sentient beings or souls that have consciousness and matter which does not. So that makes three categories of reality: the one reality upon which everything else is dependent, the souls, and matter. Sometimes these three are referred to as the tattva-traya. All schools of Vedanta accept these three tattvas.

Differences arise between scholars of Vedanta regarding the relationship between the ultimate reality and the dependent realities, the para-tattva and the apara-tattva. Regarding the nature of the goal, the doctrines advanced by different schools also vary. Some conclude that the Brahman is formless and devoid of attributes, whereas the Vaishnavas hold that Brahman cannot be devoid of anything that manifests from it. Since attributes can be experienced in the universe, they must be present in its origin also. What is common to every school of Vedanta, and on which they all agree, is the need for cessation of bondage leading to realisation of the supreme end, the absolute reality.

Since the Vedanta Sutra is written in such a way as to allow variant readings, each group must provide a distinct understanding by answering such questions as: Brahman is related to matter, and the souls are related to Brahman; and the souls are somehow related to matter. What connects them? Are the differences between them real or an illusion? Is the world a perception only or is it substantially real? Is individual identity real or an illusion? If Brahman is the source of everything, both material and spiritual, why do souls suffer in contact with matter, and is pain and pleasure an illusion or real? How do the souls transcend matter and attain eternal peace? Is there anything beyond this life? When all these initial questions have been answered, the conclusion is known as tattva or ‘the truth.’

The next set of questions that need to be answered are those relating to practical actions taken in the light of the foregoing evidence. Vedic philosophy is pragmatic rather than theoretical, so the commentary must offer methods to become free from illusion thereby attaining a blissful state, and to describe the end of the soul’s journey:

What does someone need to do to free themselves from the bondage of illusion? Out of all the ways offered in the Vedas, what is the best? Does it involve action or non-action? Integration with the world or extrication from it? What are the specific techniques, and what can go wrong on this path? What are the major obstacles along the way? What must someone avoid to secure success on the path? What are the restrictions and special observances? When all these questions have been answered and evidence provided, the conclusion is known as hita or ‘the means of attainment.’

Finally, when the soul becomes progressively free from illusion, and becomes situated in eternity, what does that feel like? What does it look like? Where is the individual observer located then, exactly? Is this nirvana an empty void, a type of oneness or a personal paradise? And how would you know that it was real and not merely another aspect of illusion? Once there, is there any coming back to matter? What or who is God?

This end goal, the destination of the soul’s journey is known as purusartha. Over the last thousand years these three classic terms, tattva, hita and purusartha also became known in some parts of India by alternative Sanskrit terms: sambandha (the relationships), abhidheya (that which is being indicated) and prayojana (the end).

Reducing an entire body of metaphysics into an easy-to-remember summary list is a challenging yet rewarding exercise for a philosophical school. But so too is the composition of a sutra which successfully argues the viewpoint of that school. And again, writing exhaustive commentaries on both the list and the sutra, particularly so that the common reader can understand the ideas, is a noble and compassionate act. All have their hallowed place and purpose in the world of the Vaishnavas.

## Vaishnava sutras

In the last chapter we learned of the three elements of reality: (1) Brahman, the one source of everything, (2) the conscious souls and (3) unconscious matter. The Mundaka Upanishad takes these three elements and gently expands them into five essential subjects, explaining that these five should be thoroughly understood by all.

In the ancient text known as the Harita Samhita, the following verse is found:

*prapyasya brahmano rupam*  
*praptusca pratyagatmanah*  
*praptyupayam phalam caiva*  
*tatha prapti-virodhi ca\|*  
*vadanti sakala vedah setihasa puranakah*  
*munayasca mahatmanah vedavedantinah\|\|*

prapyasya - of the sought after; brahmano rupam - the characteristics of Brahman; praptuh -of the seeker (the jiva); pratyagatmanah ca - the jiva; praptyupayam - the means of attaining the sought after Brahman; prapteh phalam - the benefit of the attaining the sought after fruit; tatha – likewise; prapti virodhisca - the obstructions while following the means to achieve the sought after; sakala vedah - all the Vedas; itihasa puranakah - the persons who are thoroughly conversant with Ramayana, Mahabharata, Vishnu Purana etc; veda-vedantinah - the persons who have studied the Vedas and Upanishads etc; mahatmanah - the persons of impeccable personality; munayasca - the persons who have Him as the single subject of their thoughts; vadanti - say that these are the topics a person must know.

**“Those great souls who have studied all the Vedas and Upanishads, who are conversant with the histories and Puranas, and who are of impeccable personality, only having the supreme as their object of meditation, say that these are the five topics that all must know.”**

1\. Prapya – the nature of Brahman to be attained.

2\. Prapta – the nature of the soul who seeks to realise Brahman.

3\. Prapti-upaya – the method of attaining the goal.

4\. Phala – the nature of the goal.

5\. Prapti-virodhi – the obstacles in the way of realisation of the goal.

This five-fold scheme of categorisation was employed by early Vaishnavism, notably the Sri Vaishnavas, who termed it the artha-panchaka. It was spoken of by Nammalvar in his book Tiruvaymoli. Later, in the 13th century, the work Artha Panchakam was written by Pillai Lokacarya, who explained each of the five elements with five further comments. Vedanta Deshika, a junior contemporary of Lokacharya, also wrote a short work of the same name, explaining each of the five points of doctrine with two lines of commentary. None other than

Srila Bhaktivinode Thakur liked the Artha Panchakam, and he wrote a Bengali commentary to it. In this way the work made its first appearance in Bengal. His son, Bhaktisiddhanta Thakur, later hosted two speakers from the south of India who visited the ashram and spoke on the work for two days.

After Ramanuja Acarya (1017-1137) came another great Vaishnava known as Ananda Tirtha, better known to the world as Madhva Acharya (1238-1317). He was also a great commentator on Vedanta and one of his spiritual descendants was Vyasa Tirtha (1460-1539), who summarised his teachings in nine points. Sri Chaitanya Mahaprabhu (1486-1534) taught his greatest followers Rupa Goswami and Sanatana Goswami the essence of Vaishnava Vedanta. Sri Rupa received the divine teachings over ten days at Prayag (Allahabad), and Sri Sanatana over the course of one month in Varanasi (Benares). Both Rupa and Sanatana prepared lists and sutras of philosophical points for the generation of followers that came after them.

Some two hundred years later, in perhaps 1730 or 1740, Baladeva Vidyabhusana, a follower of Chaitanya in the lineage of Madhvacarya, wrote a short book, the Prameya Ratnavali, in which he included the same nine points of philosophy of Madhvacarya. He added that these were taught by Sri Chaitanya:

1\. Vishnu is supreme.

2\. He is the subject of all sacred texts and is known by them.

3\. The world is real.

4\. Living beings are different from Vishnu.

5\. Living beings are servants at the feet of Hari.

6\. In both conditioned and liberated states there is a hierarchy among the living beings.

7\. Liberation is not impersonal, but the attainment of the feet of Vishnu.

8\. Untainted worship of him is (its) cause.

9\. The means of correct knowledge are the three sources headed by direct perception (direct perception, logical inference, and authoritative testimony of the Vedas).

The unique contribution of Sri Chaitanya Mahaprabhu was to resolve a seemingly intractable dispute: was the world one with God or different from it? If it was one with God, then why the apparent difference? If the world was different from God, then how was God connected? Both viewpoints involved slight, but difficult logical weaknesses. The resolution of Chaitanya was the philosophy of ‘inconceivable one-ness and difference.’

In Sanskrit achintya means 'inconceivable', bheda is 'difference', and abheda translates as 'nondifference'. So achintya-bheda-abheda means ‘the philosophy of inconceivable one-ness and difference.’ Chaitanya’s teachings differentiate the tradition from the other Vaishnava lineages. It can be understood as an integration of the strict dualist (dvaita) theology of Madhva Acharya and the qualified monism (vishishtadvaita) of Ramanuja.

Sri Chaitanya Mahaprabhu also taught that knowledge of God was incomplete if occluded by any tinge of desire for worldly benefit, mystical power, or impersonal liberation. Even love of God mixed with a sense of awe and reverence was not the ultimate end of the soul’s journey. He taught that God is ultimately Krishna, the charming flute-player, and that each soul is related to Him eternally in one of five primary loving moods, known as rasa. Only experience of this rasa would be perfection. Thus, to the teachings of his predecessors Sri Chaitanya added small, yet profound additions.

## Ten truths for today

**Though poetic and beautiful in their brevity, powerful sutras invite great teachers to use them in the enlightenment of ordinary men. Thus commentaries, or bhashyas, are born, and elaborations, known as varttikas, tikas and tipanis, are brought to the public. By such compassionate writing, people become inspired and new religious movements are born.**

Each generation presents a Vaishnava teacher with a new cultural climate, a new set of ethical considerations, new ideas on social politics – even a new philosophical language. In each generation the spiritual reformer must take the original sutras and expound on them, giving voice to ancient wisdom but in a contemporary voice. In the latter part of the 19th century, the great Vaishnava reformer, Srila Bhaktivinode Thakur, wrote a series of books, each a message for a specific audience. And yet each book was an elaboration on the same essential teachings of Sri Chaitanya Mahaprabhu.

In his earlier writings he used the nine points of Baladeva Vidyabhusana as a framework, and later used his own version of these which, although very similar, came to ten points. He wrote at a time when many were presenting what they passed off as the teachings of the Sri Chaitanya Mahaprabhu, but which were compromised in various ways. The Thakur took it upon himself to restore the original teachings, just as they had been given. He wanted to correct the drifting that had taken place and make the Vaishnava school fit for a new generation. Even the siddhanta rendered by other historical teachers of the sampradaya were subject to amendment if they were too unspecific in either their details or directions.

Bhaktivinode Thakur wanted to include a reference to basic Vedanta philosophy in his declaration of the ultimate truth. In this way the members of the sampradaya would remain firmly underpinned by a good understanding of tattva-traya, the three ontological truths of classical times. In addition, he wanted any declaration of siddhanta to include a description of abhideya, a modus operandi of how the practitioner would actually attain the ultimate truth described in the statement. His view of the need to make necessary corrections is evident in his treatment of a 400-year-old \`statement composed by Srinath Chakravarti, disciple of Advaita Acharya and guru to Kavi Karnapura. He evidently felt that his predecessor’s statement of siddhanta, while true, was insufficient for clarity and direction. There were additional points which Bhaktivinode Thakur considered essential. His reasoning was later written down and published in his own Sajjana Toshani magazine. It began with the text in question:

*ārādhyo bhagavān vrajeśa-tanayas tad-dhāma vṛndāvanaṁ*  
*ramyā kācid upāsanā vraja-vadhū-vargeṇā yā kalpitā*  
*śrīmad-bhāgavataṁ pramāṇam amalaṁ premā pum-artho mahān*  
*śrī-caitanya mahāprabhor matam idaṁ tatrādaro naḥ paraḥ*

**“The Supreme Personality of Godhead, the son of Nanda Mahārāja, is to be worshiped along with His transcendental abode, Vṛndāvana. The most pleasing form of worship for the Lord is that which was performed by the gopīs of Vṛndāvaṇa. Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam is the spotless authority on everything, and pure love of God is the ultimate goal of life for all men. These statements, for which we have the highest regard, are the opinion of Śrī Caitanya Mahāprabhu.”**

“Seeing the published article entitled Introduction to Śrī Caitanya’s Doctrine we felt a particular type of happiness from disappointment. We hoped that respected author will explain vaiṣhṇava teachings on the basis of philosophical statements of the learned Goswamis. However, when we saw the verse ārādhyo bhagavān vraje-tanayaḥ in the very beginning we understood that respected author does not know that there is a subtle difference between Vedāntic statements and statements regarding bhajana. In his verse Śrīnātha Cakravartī Ṭhākura described Śrīman Mahāprabhu’s teachings pertaining to bhajana. But he did not list all of His philosophical teachings. In this verse jīva-tattva, jaḍa-tattva, śakti-tattva, sādhana-bhakti-tattva and many other topics are not mentioned. From the point of view of tattva this verse is not complete. In order to give a complete list of philosophical teachings it is necessary to make an exposition of the teachings explained in the Ṣaṭ-sandarbhas. It is written in the Bhakti-sandarbha:

**“In the four previous Sandarbhas relationship (sambandha) was discussed. In those Sandarbhas, that supreme principle endowed with the complete, eternal, supreme bliss was described by the designations Brahman, Paramātmā and Bhagavān as a factor in those relationships, and the highest manifestation was said to be Bhagavān. And the highest form of Bhagavān was concluded to be Kṛṣṇa. In enumerating the powers of Paramātmā, the jīvas, forms of the taṭastha śakti, whose unchanging nature is consciousness, were described. The jīva’s essential knowledge is covered by māyā, by the misfortune of being opposed to the Lord, which means that the jīva has, without beginning, no knowledge of the beginningless supreme entity. The jīva consequently believes he is made of dull matter composed of sattva, rajas and tamas and undergoes sufferings in the material world.”**

And it is also said in the PrР”«ti-sandarbha:

**“Here the supreme truth enunciated in scripture was previously established in four sandarbhas. Worship of the supreme entity was described in the fifth Sandarbha. Now the goal (prayojana) is described in appropriate order.”**

**If we read these essential statements from the Sandarbhas we will clearly understand that Kṛṣṇa, kṛṣṇa-śakti and bhagavān-tattva manifesting different pastimes of Kṛṣṇa, as well as jīva-tattva, who is part and parcel of the Lord manifested in two categories of nitya-baddha and nitya-mukta, as well as māyā-tattva who covers the jīva, as well as sādhana-tattva and sādhya-tattva – all these tattvas taken separately make up nine tattvas. These nine tattvas are prameya and the Vedas, which are a self-effulgent śāstra, as well as the smṛti-śāstras headed by Bhāgavata are pramāṇa. Vaiṣṇavas will never accept as Vedāntic any exposition which is devoid of these ten siddhāntas explained separately.” (Sajjana-toṣaṇī Vol.4, #3, p.53)**

Bhaktivinode Thakur wrote Sri Caitanya Sikshamrita in 1886. It was a philosophical work in Bengali prose based on Sri Chaitanya’s teachings to Rupa Goswami and Sanatana Goswami as found in the Caitanya Caritamrita. The teachings were compared to a succession of ‘rainfalls of nectar,’ each of which is divided into several ‘downpours.’

In 1888 he wrote the Vaishnava Siddhanta Mala, which was meant to be a catechism, a manual for religious education. Based on Baladeva’s nine points, it was a question-and-answer introduction to a five-chapter book on how to spread the teachings of Chaitanya Mahaprabhu through the formation of small groups. He then wrote the Amnaya Sutram in 1890. The book contains 130 Sanskrit aphorisms describing the Absolute Truth in terms of sambandha (the connection between metaphysical entities), abhideya (the path of action indicated in sacred text) and prayojana (the final goal for the living being). Two years later, in 1892, he had compiled his own ten-point list as a feature in a new book, the Sriman Mahaprabhur Siksha. Again, it was the same nine-point teaching but slightly adjusted to incorporate the nuances taught by Chaitanya. He was concerned that liberties were being taken with the teachings of Chaitanya Mahaprabhu, and that he had to write clear and strong philosophical points to refresh the tradition. Disconcerted at yet another misleading book publication, he wrote in Sriman Mahaprabhur Siksha:

“Some time ago I happened to see one new publication called Sri Caitanya Bodhini. The authors had vowed to give the pure teachings of Sri Caitanya to the world. The vow is not bad, but the system they proposed to gather Sri Caitanya's teachings is very dangerous. The authors thought that they could extract the Lord's teachings from the Sanskrit works of the Goswamis. They forgot that the essence of the Goswamis works is contained in Sri Caitanya Caritamrta. It is sufficient to take this work in order to present the teachings of Sri Caitanya to the world. There is no one as learned as Kaviraja Goswami today. Anyone who thinks that he can extract the essence from the Sanskrit works of the Goswamis better than Kaviraja Goswami is certainly worthless and foolish. I have firm conviction that by presenting to the world clearly the teachings of Sri Caitanya Caritamrta, nothing else need be known.”

“However, many topics in Kaviraja’s work are described in a cryptic way. In those places it is best to consult the Sandarbhas, Bhakti Rasamrta Sindu and other works of the Goswamis in order to clarify matters. The aim of the Caitanya Bodhini however was to bypass the superior authority. In the present work we will reveal the teachings of Sri Caitanya clearly, based on the statements of Sri Caitanya Caritamrta. I have one request to the readers. The teachings of Mahaprabhu are a very confidential science. Without applying proper concentration, one cannot understand them. It is a common practice nowadays to go to sleep after eating while reading a novel. One cannot read this book in that manner. These teachings are the esoteric truths of the Vedas and Vedanta sutras. If one slowly reads these teachings with faith and concentration while discussing the topics with devotees, the truths will be properly understood. Therefore, give up the bad habit and attentively study the ten topics presented in this book. All of Sri Caitanya s teachings - whatever they it may be - are discussed in terms of three divisions: sambandha, abhidheya and prayojana.”To clarify the teachings of Chaitanya he summarised them into ten essential points. One unique contribution of Chaitanya, additional to anything spoken by any Vaishnava acharya that had gone before, was the delineation of the philosophy of ‘inconceivable, simultaneous difference and non-difference’ or achintya bheda-abheda tattva. Another unique contribution was the teaching of rasa-tattva, or the science of multiple levels of love of God, culminating in the pure devotion to Sri Krishna, the only possible object of the highest devotional love. The Thakur included both essential points in his list, adding them in logical sequence to the points of Baladeva Vidybhusana written one hundred years before him. He prefaced his ten essential points as follows:

**“In teaching Sanatana Gosvami, Sri Caitanya said:**

**‘In Vedic literature, Krishna is the central point of attraction, and His service is our activity. To attain the platform of love of Krishna is life’s ultimate goal. Therefore Krishna, Krishna's service and love of Krishna are the three great riches of life. When one accepts Vedic literature by interpretation or even by dictionary meaning, directly or indirectly, the ultimate declaration of Vedic knowledge points to Lord Krishna. C.C. Madhya 20. 193, 196. The meaning is this: Vedas are the scriptural authority. What the Veda states is true. Any religious person must act according to the instructions of the Vedas.**

**The ultimate aim of the Vedic literature is to reveal Krishna as the only Lord, either by secondary, or indirect interpretation of the statements, or by primary or direct meaning of the statements. In other words, if one examines the sambandha or principle of relationships described in the Vedas, one will find no one except Krishna. On considering the abhidheya or process recommended in the Vedas, only devotion to Krishna will be found. In considering the prayojana or goal of the scriptures, only Krishna prema will be found. In order to discuss in detail, the sambandha, abhidheya and prayojana, the ten conclusions taught by Sri Chaitanya will first be presented in the form of one verse, and later, each topic will be discussed separately in detail.”**

Sriman Mahaprabhur Siksha is in eleven chapters. In the first chapter the Thakur summarizes Sri Chaitanya’s philosophy in ten points. The following ten chapters fully explain each point individually and all philosophical conclusions are supported with profuse scriptural quotations in Sanskrit. These are accompanied by Bengali prose translations and explanations.

In 1893 came Jaiva Dharma, a novel in which the teachings of Sri Chaitanya are given in the form of dialogues between various characters. In chapters 13-22 the teachings follow the same logical progression of the ten points, and there are also chapters that address the popular philosophies and religious conceptions of Hinduism in Victorian India. Bhaktivinode Thakur writes his own Sanskrit sutra to list the Ten Essential Truths:

*amnayah praha tattvam harim iha paramam sarva-saktim rasabdhim*  
*tad-bhinnamsams ca jivan prakrti-kavalitan tad-vimuktams ca bhavad*  
*bhedabheda-prakasam sakalam api hareh sadhanam suddha-bhaktim*  
*sadhyam tat-pritim evety upadisati janan gauracandrah svayam sah*

1\. Pramana: The teachings of the Vedas received through guru-parampara are known as amnaya. The infallible evidence of the Vedas, of the smrti-sastras headed by the Srimad-Bhagavatam, as well as evidence such as direct sense perception (pratyaksa), that concur with the guidance of the Vedas, are all accepted as pramana (evidence). This pramana establishes the following prameyas (fundamental truths):

2\. Parama-tattva: Sri Hari alone is the Supreme Absolute Truth.

3\. Sarva-shaktiman: Sri Krsna is the possessor of all potency.

4\. Akhila-rasamrta-sindhu: He is the ocean of nectarean mellows.

5\. Vibhinnamsa-tattva: Both the mukta (liberated) and baddha (conditioned) jivas are His eternally separated parts and parcels.

6\. Baddha-jivas: Conditioned souls are subject to the control and covering of maya.

7\. Mukta-jivas: Liberated souls are free from maya.

8\. Acintya-bhedabheda-tattva: The entire universe, consisting of the conscious (cit) and unconscious (acit), is Sri Hari’s acintya-bhedabheda-prakasa, that is to say, it is His manifestation which is inconceivably both different and non-different from Him.

9\. Suddha-bhakti: Pure devotional service is the only practice (sadhana) to attain perfection.

10\. Krishna-priti: Transcendental love and affection for Krishna is the one and only final object of attainment (sadhya-vastu).

The first topic concerns the nature of proof (pramana). The next seven topics concern the sambandha which is ascertained in the Vedas. The ninth topic concerns the abhidheya or means. The tenth topic concerns the goal or prayojana.”

Having introduced his reader to the ten topics and how they deal with the three main questions of Vedanta philosophy, Srila Bhaktivinode introduces two important words. The first is pramana, which means ‘evidence’ or more pertinently ‘proof.’ Since nothing in a debate or a court of law can be proven without trustworthy evidence, and since the spiritual path of life, being the most important, demands the most crucial evidence, it is his primary duty to establish the source of evidence. The second word is prameya, which means ‘that which is to be proven.’

If the topics are divided into two groups — source of knowledge (pramana) and what is proven by the source of knowledge (prameya), the first topic is pramana and the rest are prameya. Among the sambandha topics the first three define Krishna and the next three define the jiva. The seventh sambandha topic deals with the relation between the jiva and Krishna, inconceivable difference and nondifference. Please concentrate on each of these topics as they are discussed.

The author does not publish the Sri Vaishnava philosophical framework, the Artha Panchakam, until three years later in 1899, but it makes an appearance in the Jaiva Dharma. In the story he describes a group of Sri Vaishnava sadhus arriving on pilgrimage to Navadwipa. They sit and discuss the Artha Panchakam.

Lord Chaitanya’s teachings were again summarised in the Sri Gauranga Lila Mangala Stotram of 1896. In this book of 104 Sanskrit verses the Thakur gives a condensed description of the teachings and biography of Sri Chaitanya. The ten teachings appear in verses 75-89. But his introduction of forty-seven pages was in English prose and summarised the contents of the book. The introduction was titled Sri Chaitanya Mahaprabhu: His Life and Precepts and, as a separate publication, was famously sent to the British Library in London, McGill University in Canada, and to several other important scholars. Again, the teachings section of the introduction – about two thirds - was based around the ten essential truths, which by this time were known as the Dasa-Mula-Niryas. Under this title the same teachings appear in the Thakur’s magazine the Sajjana Tosani in 1896. Bhaktivinode Thakur must have been satisfied with his ten essential truths because he uses them for several years. The same list also makes an appearance, in simple Bengali language, in chapter seven of the Harinam Cintamani published in 1900.

Around the same time, Bhaktivinode Thakur’s diksha-guru Vipin Bihari Goswami completed his magnum opus on the same subject, naming it Dasa-Mula-Rasam (Vaishnava Jivana) — “The Ten Essential Truths (The Life of the Vaishnavas).” The book is over a thousand pages long and presents a detailed exposition of the principal tenets of Gaudiya philosophy, with many quotations from standard Vaishnava scriptures. Because of the timing of this work’s publication, there is some contention about who was the first to describe the concept of dasa-mula, Bhaktivinode or his guru. However, it appears from the concluding words of Dasa-Mula-Rasam that Vipin Bihari Goswami started working on the book in the beginning of 1898 upon the desire of a certain dear disciple of his (whose name he does not mention) and finished the work in April of 1899, more than ten years after Bhaktivinode Thakur published Vaishnava Siddhanta Mala, his first work on the subject.

Also, in his own book, Vipin Bihari Goswami directly quotes the amnaya prahah tattva verse written by his disciple. From this it would appear they were in discussions about the subject for some time, and that the guru’s magnum opus was directly inspired by the work of Bhaktivinode Thakur. In his turn, the illustrious son of Bhaktivinode Thakur, Srila Bhaktisiddhanta Saraswati Thakur (1874-1937), made the daśamūla developed by his father a compulsory study for even less philosophically inclined disciples. In the Gaudīya Maṭha, his missionary organisation, each newcomer would be taught the knowledge of daśamūla usually prior to or shortly after harināma-dīkṣā. All members of the Gaudīya Maṭha were supposed to know the verse by Śrīla Bhaktivinode Ṭhākura encapsulating the ten basic principles of siddhanta specific to Gaudīya understanding. In his important book, Prākṛta-rasa-śata-dūṣiṇī , it is clearly seen how Srila Bhaktisiddhanta Saraswati views the comprehension of siddhanta. In verses 26-28, he writes:

*siddhānta vihīna hoile kṛṣṇe citta lāge nā ।*  
*sambandha-hīnera kabhu abhideya haya nā ॥ 26॥*

**When the heart or consciousness is devoid of understanding of siddhanta, it will not be fixed on Krishna. Abhidheya (the practical means of attaining bhakti) does not take place in one who is devoid of sambandha (philosophical understanding).**

*sambandha-vihīna jana prayojana pāya nā ।*  
*ku-siddhānte vyasta jana kṛṣṇa-sevā kore nā ॥ 27॥*

**One who is devoid of sambandha, does not obtain prayojana (the ultimate goal). One who is deluded by far-fetched conclusions (ku-siddhānte), does not perform service to Krishna.**

*siddhānta-alasa jana anartha to' chāḍe nā ।*  
*jaḍe kṛṣṇa bhrama kori' kṛṣṇa-sevā kore nā ॥ 28॥*

**One who is lazy to understand siddhanta, cannot get rid of anartha (obstacles on the path of bhakti). One who confuses Krishna with matter does not perform service to Krishna.**

Srila A.C. Bhaktivedanta Swami Prabhupada (1896-1977) created an entire movement for the study and application of the siddhanta of Chaitanya, and he wrote extensively on all the ten points, also explaining that a student should not be lazy or neglectful in the matter of studying, discussing and fully understanding siddhanta. Indeed, his eighty books on the subject can all be regarded as lengthy expositions on the ten points. He warned that his followers should all ‘scrutinizingly’ study his books and that, if they did not, they would simply ‘eat, sleep, and fall down’ from the path of bhakti.

## Using the ten teachings

**If you could sum up the gentle art of persuasion you might not do better than this: ‘Speak to the right person, at the right moment, with the right words, in the right manner.’ My apologies to the person who spoke those words, whose name I do not know. Choosing the right person to speak to, selecting the right time to speak to them, choosing your words carefully, with the right emotional delivery – this fine art marks all great leaders, orators, and inspirational teachers.**

In life, we are all on the receiving end of energetic persuasion, be it from our own children, a political activist, a sports coach, military officer, doctor, or salesman. Everyone who sees our life in terms of its potential – to us or to them – will try to convince us to take steps towards a greater commitment to a higher pursuit. They may exhort us to abandon smoking and fatty foods and take more exercise, or if we’re already fit, they’ll inspire us to do more abdominal crunches. Other persuaders use their powers to ask for our vote in an upcoming election. Or they might push us over the edge when we’re standing in a used car showroom. In turn, we also persuade others: family members, friends, work colleagues. We want them to believe in us, to endorse us, or to go along with our plans. Wherever we go, we are never far from being persuaded by someone, or attempting to persuade someone. Like it or resist it, we all live in a world of persuasion.

No-one uses persuasion more than those with genuine spiritual conviction. Born of a tangible experience of the mystical, and being unshakeably fixed on tasting it again, they pass on the message of Vedanta using all the skills at their disposal. Their need to persuade springs not from a desire for selfish personal advantage or the interests of a sectarian religion; it comes from compassion and a desire to help others. Not for nothing have the greatest mystics of history also been some of the greatest public speakers, writers, and poets. Their powers of persuasion come from realisation and an affinity for their audience. The ancient Greeks had their public speaking down to a fine art. They listed the skills of a persuasive speaker to be four: ethos, pathos, logos and kairos.

Ethos is an appeal to ethics, and it is a means of convincing someone of the character or credibility of the persuader. Pathos is an appeal to emotion and is a way of convincing an audience of an argument by creating an emotional response. Logos is a method of persuading an audience by logic and reason. Kairos means timing and refers to knowing the opportune moment to use the other three skills. Using all four of these skills, a speaker formed a strong connection with his audience, convinced them of his good character and hence trustworthiness, satisfied their need for reasoned argument and intellectual satisfaction, and aroused their emotions in a manner favourable to his call to action. Whether the great spiritual and religious preachers of history knew these skills consciously, or had somehow imbibed them naturally, their speaking was not bereft of these essential components.

Another talent related to pathos is the art of making your audience laugh. If the audience laughs with you – and not at you – they are already on your side. And if you can manage to get them to find the human condition amusing, they will be prepared to listen to an alternative viewpoint. Millions of people pay good money to sit and listen to a comedian, and many comedians employ an observational form of comedy narrative that basically pokes fun at the different kinds of illusion we are all in.

One great art of the preacher is to make his or her messages relevant for the times. While the truth is eternal, and the scriptures ancient, any preacher must make the messages immediate and as relevant as today’s news headlines. That is why although the truth is one, it is presented differently according to (here comes some Sanskrit instead of Greek) the desha, kala, and patra, or the ‘time, place and circumstances.’ For this reason, the great souls of the past preached either for one or another set of principles. In India, they sometimes endorsed the prevalent social systems or railed against them; they sometimes taught in the classical Sanskrit or used the vernacular of the village. Sometimes they supported the Brahmana community, the priests of society, and sometimes not. Sometimes they created temples and at other times neglected temples in favour of creating popular movements without altars or rituals. The need of the hour is always what will help people most, and the preacher tries to uplift as many as possible, by the most favourable means.

For this reason, one great Vaishnava saint in the 18th century, Vishvanatha Chakravarti Thakur, preferred to use Bengali language to write his original poetry, immediately making it the preserve of the common man rather than the intellectual elite, who wrote in Sanskrit.

Bhaktivinode removed the external trappings of Brahmanism from his followers, while his son Bhaktisiddhanta brought it all back, including the garb and vows of the sannyasi. His disciple, the well-known founder of the international movement for Krishna consciousness, A.C. Bhaktivedanta Swami Prabhupada, employed the English language and youthful American and European men and women to spread his message. Every one of them stirred up controversy by their adaptation of the norms for the times they found themselves in, because relevancy often appears to be breaking the tradition. Srila Prabhupada’s rule was quite simple: anything could be used to spread the message – but the message itself should not become obscured or lost.

It is interesting that when using the Ten Teachings of Sri Chaitanya to create illuminating books for his countrymen, Srila Bhaktivinode Thakur chose to dwell on topical issues of philosophy that he felt needed an extra explanation. Because of the influx of Christianity into Victorian India and the ascendancy of British culture, there were feelings of embarrassment amongst Anglicised Hindu intellectuals. Although there were many theological points in common between the Church and Hinduism, there was disquiet about the worship of the divine images within the temple. For 700 years the Muslims had been telling the Hindus that it was an insult to God to have the Deity depicted in stone, metal, or wood, and now the British Christians were telling them the same thing. Since all the best jobs in India were government jobs, there was an increased slide towards the British point of view in all matters of religion. As a result, Hindu organisations that disagreed with Deity worship sprang up. The Thakur took a chapter or two, sometimes more, to deal with this very topical subject.

He was also an active campaigner in other literary ways. In 1899 the Calcutta Christian Tract and Book Society, established in 1823 to supply books to missionary-aided vernacular schools, published an article in their periodical entitled Professor Max Muller on Durga, in which Kali, Durga and Shiva were slandered from the Christian viewpoint. In an activist mood, Bhaktivinode

Thakur wrote The Hindu Idols, a thirty-two-page English letter in response. This exhaustive response quoted from the Vedas and Puranas on the factual identity of the semi-divine persons in question. He concluded by assuming the proper Christian attitude of universal love, which is devoid of sectarian dogmatism, and humbly begged the gentlemen of the Society to give up their philosophical inconsistencies.

By the middle of the 1800s the line of Chaitanya Mahaprabhu had become somewhat stagnant, the rushing river of mercy choked up by weeds that had grown from the river-bed of spiritual imitation, religious misconception, territorial squabbles and the self-serving Brahmana class. Bhaktivinode wrote several chapters, in at least four books, to address these problems. Although the Jaiva Dharma was perhaps the culmination of his teaching books, Bhaktivinode does not begin his elucidation of the Dasa Mula until the thirteenth chapter, using the first twelve chapters to challenge misconceptions held by thinkers of his day.

In the same way, in the 1960s and 70s, Srila Prabhupada wrote extensively about the issues in the western world that he found needed addressing. The scientists were a favourite subject.
