Brahman must therefore be Purushottama, the Supreme Person, identical with Narayana and Vishnu, the Supreme Being with a divinely beautiful body. The qualities of Brahman are many, but major points are found throughout the Upanishads:

• Satyam – The Truth, free from any relativities of time or space.

• Chit – Omniscience – The Knower of the universe and all living beings.

• Anantam – Infinitude – That which is infinite and yet continues to expand.

• Anandam – Beatitude – enjoying unlimited bliss.

• Amala – Purity – free from all flaws, deterioration, and purifying.

Although there are general qualities, there are also specifics which, if taken into consideration, lead the seeker towards a Brahman with will, choice, reciprocity, and affection.

*anor aniyan mahato mahiyan*

**Smaller than the smallest, bigger than the biggest.**

**Katha Upanishad 3.15**

*niskalam niskriyam shantam*

**Without any movement, unchangeable.**

**Svetashvatara Upanishad 6.19**

*na tatra caksur gacchati na vag gacchati na manah.*

**It transcends all worldly concepts, though it is not, in itself, a negation.**

**Kena Upanishad 1.3**

*Sarva loka ishata*

**The foundation of the universe.**

**Svetashvatara Upanishad 3.1**

*Ishavasyam idam sarvam*

**All this is pervaded by Isha (God)**

**Isha Upanishad 1**

*iccha matram prabho sristi*

**Creation happens by the mere will of the Master.**

**Mandukhya Upanishad**

*na tasya karyam karanam ca vidyate na tat-samas cabhyadhikas ca drsyate parasya saktir vividhaiva sruyate sva-bhaviki jnana-bala-kriya ca.*

**He does not possess bodily form like an ordinary being. There is no difference between His body and His soul. He is absolute. His senses are transcendental. No one is greater than Him or equal to Him. His potencies are multifarious, and His deeds are automatically performed as a natural sequence.**

**Svetasvatara Upanisad 6.8**

*hiraṇmayena pātreṇa satyasyāpihitaṁ mukham*  
*tat tvaṁ pūṣann apāvṛṇu satya-dharmāya dṛṣṭaye*

**O my Lord, sustainer of all that lives, your real face is covered by your dazzling effulgence. Kindly remove that covering and exhibit yourself to your pure devotee.**

**Isha Upanishad 15**

*satya-sankalpa akasatma sarva-karma sarva-kamah sarva-gandhah sarva-rasah sarvam idam abhyato avakyan adarah.*

**He is effulgent and glorious. His every desire is automatically fulfilled. He is all-pervading. He is the original creator of everything. He fulfils all desire. He possesses all pleasant fragrances. He is all sweetness. He is present everywhere. He cannot be described in words. He cannot be known.**

**Chandogya Upanishad 3.14.1**

*nāyanātmā pravacanena labhyo na medhayā na bahunā śrutena \|*  
*yamevaiṣa vṛṇute tena labhyastasyaiṣa ātmā vivṛṇute tanūm̐ svām*

**The Self is not to be attained by a study of the Vedas, nor by intelligence, nor by much hearing, but the Self can be attained, only by him who seeks to know it. To him, the Self reveals its true nature.**

**Katha Upanishad 1.2.23 and Mundaka Upanishad 3.2.3**

*mahatah param avyaktam avyaktat purusah parah*  
*purusan na param kincit sa kastha sa para gatih*

**Above the mahat tattva is avyakta, above avyakta is Purusha, and beyond Him there is nothing else. He is the limit, the ultimate destination.**

**Katha Upanisad 1.3.11**

*sodhvanah parama pruti tad visnoh paramam padam*

**Vishnu is in the supreme abode.**

**Katha Upanishad 3.9**

*taddhaitadghor āṅgirasaḥ kṛṣṇāya*  
*devakīputrāya uktvovācāpipāsa eva sa babhūva*

**The rishi Ghora Angirasa practiced this Purusha Yajna with the dedication as: "This is subservient to Krishna, the son of Devaki.”**

**Chandogya Upanishad 3.17.6**

*om tad vishnoh paramam padam sada / pashyanti surayo diviva chakshur-atatam tad vipraso vipanyavo jagrivamsaha / samindhate vishnor yat paramam padam*

**Just as the sun's rays in the sky are extended to the mundane vision, so in the same way the wise and learned devotees always see the supreme abode of Lord Vishnu. Because those highly praiseworthy and spiritually awake brahmanas can see the spiritual world, they are also able to reveal that supreme abode of Lord Vishnu.**

**Rig Veda 1.22.20**

**Summary**

Brahman is described in the Upanishads:

\- as the creator, foundation, and dissolver of the universe

\- as the inner controller, supervisor, and witness of all beings

\- as the soul of the universe, with the universe as its body

\- as the super consciousness of which our individual consciousness is a part

\- as the highest being with no material elements yet possessing attributes

\- as the God of Religion

## Brahman with Attributes

An attribute is ‘a quality or feature regarded as a characteristic or inherent part of someone or something.’ So as a lotus flower might have the attributes of pink colour and fragrance, and a candle flame both heat and light, a substance has inseparable attributes which are both one and different from it. The fragrance is not the same thing as the flower, and the light of the candle is not the flame. One is the substance and the others the attributes.

Similarly, although Brahman is infinite and indivisible, within its one-ness there is the power to extend itself. Therefore, Brahman is the substance and ‘extensions’ its inherent attribute:

**“Just as the illumination of fire, situated in one place, is spread all over, the energies of the supreme Brahman are spread throughout the universe.” -Vishnu Purana 1.22.53**

## Brahman as superior to the Devas

In the four Vedas, there are many devas or demigods referred to by name. Although the concept of celestial controllers is foreign to western philosophers, being thought of as a product of a more anthropomorphic and less scientific culture, still the concept persists. The Upanishads are the philosophical portion of the shastra, coming at the end of each of the four Vedas.

Brahman is described as the very soul of the universe. In one conversation, a sage is asked how many gods there are. “3,339,” comes the reply. Again, the question is asked, and the answer is 333. Asked a third time, the sage responds that there are only 33 gods. Finally, the sage explains that there are only 3 gods, and in truth only One.

Although Vishnu is mentioned only five times in the Rig Veda, it is very much in superlative terms. When Vishnu is mentioned in the Upanishads he is again described as ‘superior to other gods.’ Although these statements do not always explicitly confirm Vishnu’s identity with Brahman, or the Ultimate Reality, they do point in that direction.

## Brahman as Purusha, Narayana and Vishnu

Brahman has many names in the Vedas, including some names that indicate a personal God, more like the God of traditional religion. Vishnu, for instance, is mentioned in the oldest part of the Vedas, in the Rig Veda. The Purusha Sukta, a text from the 10th mandala of the Rig Veda, is dedicated to ‘Purusha.’ The Vedic etymologist Yaska has said in the Nirukta dictionary that Purusha is made of two root words. Pru (All, Every, Infinite) and Usha (Sustain, Pervade, Present). Hence Purusha means "The All Pervading One" which is an epithet of Ishwar or God in the Vedas.

The Ahirbudhnya Samhita, part of the Pancharatra texts, says that the Purusha Sukta is extolled as the most important passage because it expounds the doctrine of the ultimate reality as enunciated in the Upanishads. The Purusha Sukta is found in all four Vedas and is therefore a text of greater authority.The Narayana Upanishad states that the Purusha of the Purusha Sukta is Narayana and the Taittiriya Narayana Upanishad states that the Purusha of the Rig Veda is Narayana. Another hymn appears in the Yajur Veda:

**Narayana is the supreme Brahman,**  
**Narayana is the supreme reality,**  
**Narayana is the supreme light,**  
**Narayana is the supreme self,**  
**Narayana is the most excellent meditation.**

**Narayana Suktam, Yajur Veda**

Narayana is another name of Vishnu and it is clear from Vishnu Gayatri of the Narayan Suktam which occurs in the 10th prapataka of the Taittiriya Aranyaka in the Krishna Yajurveda which equates Narayana and Vasudeva with Vishnu:

*ōm nārāyaṇāya vidmahe vāsudevāya dhīmahi, tanno viṣṇuḥ pracodayāt*

**‘We meditate upon Narayana. For that we devote ourselves to Vasudeva. May that Vishnu invigorate us.’**

Regarding the name Vishnu, the Shatapatha Brahmana states ‘Yagna is Vishnu.’ Vishnu also means 'the Sustainer' according to Yaska. And Yaska has also said Vishnu is made of Vishwa (Entire, Whole) and Nuha (Pervade) means Vishnu has the same meaning as that of Purusha meaning 'The All-Pervading One". Hence, Vishnu and Purusha are the same in one context.

Taittiriya Upanishad says that: ‘Maha is Brahman, all other are devas. Narayana is that Brahman. All are created by Him and are angas.’

No other deity is described in the same way in the Rig Veda. The Aitareya Brahmana states:

*agnirvai devana mavamo vishnuh paramah tadantara sarva devatah*

**Agni is the lowest and Vishnu is the highest among devas. All other gods occupy positions that are in between.**

**Aitareya Brahmana 1.1.1**

## The Agamas

The Agamas are a body of religious treatises devoted to modes of worship. The word ‘gam’ with the proposition ‘a’ means to move toward an object to be gained.

The body of religious treatises is also known as Tantra. The word ‘tan’ means to spread, and ‘tra’ means to save the aspirant from bondage, from ‘trayate,’ to free.

Pancharatra is a section of the Agamas or Tantra.

Mahabharata says that Narayana is the promulgator of the Pancharatra system.

The Vedanta Sutra also refers to the Pancharatra. In the Vaishnava Agamas, worship of Vishnu is the exclusive means to salvation.

There are two types of Vaishnava Agama, the Vaikhanasa and the Pancharatra. The Vaikhanasa says that Vishnu is Narayana. This establishes that they are the same and that Brahman is identified with Vishnu.

From Pancharatra we get the six principal attributes of God (sadguna), the chatur-vyuha, deity worship, the five-fold daily religious observances (panchakala-prakriya) the theory of nitya-vibhuti, the concept of parama-pada (Vaikuntha) and the concept of Bhagavata, or the beloved of Vishnu.

So, from all these pramanas, Brahman, Purusha, Narayana and Vishnu are epithets for the same ultimate being. The great philosopher Vedanta Deshika reasons, synthesizing his opinion from all the statements in the Veda, that the supreme being is:

Sarva vyapi – all pervasive

Antaratma – imminent in all beings as an inner controller

Adhara – the foundation of all existence (cause of all causes)

Sarveshvara – the controller of the universe

**Mahabharata**

In the Narayaniya section of the Shanti Parva section of the Mahabharata, the identity of Vishnu with Vasudeva, Narayana, Bhagavan and Krishna is established. The devoted worship of Vishnu as the sole means of moksha, or liberation, is brought out emphatically in the Bhagavad-gita, 700 verses in chapters 23-40 of the Bhishma Parva.

**Puranas**

These assert that Vishnu is the primary cause of creation, sustenance, and dissolution. They speak of Vishnu being Bhagavan and Vasudeva. He is free from defects and has the ‘six attributes.’ An undifferentiated being has no bearing on the universe.

## Investigating the Ultimate Reality

Shrimad Bhagavatam is the ‘ripened fruit of the tree of Vedic knowledge’ and focuses the mind of the reader on the multi-level nature of the ultimate reality. Close to the beginning of the 18,000 verses we find the following:

*vadanti tat tattva-vidas tattvaṁ yaj jñānam advayam*  
*brahmeti paramātmeti bhagavān iti śabdyate*

**Learned transcendentalists who know the Absolute Truth call this nondual substance Brahman, Paramātmā or Bhagavān.**

**Shrimad Bhagavatam 1.2 11**

This is a definition of the Ultimate Reality as ‘un-seconded consciousness’ or ‘non-dual substance.’ Tattva-vidas means ‘those who know reality.’ The Sanskrit word tat or ‘That’ is a demonstrative pronoun used to point out something that can be seen. Something that exists. This verse says that those who know reality, they ‘talk about it.’

The word advaya in this verse means not that the Ultimate Reality is a ‘oneness’ (which would be the word ekatvam) but a ‘not-two-ness’ (a+dvaya) which is distinctly different. It is a form of oneness defined as being not two of something but stopping short of being a unity. The subtle difference is important. The meaning is: ‘there is more than one, but it’s not two.’

The word jnana, meaning ‘knowledge is a special kind of knowledge, or ‘uninterrupted knowing.’ When the consciousness is not limited by misperception, illusion, or forgetfulness.

Three words are used in the above verse, in order – and the order is important:

Brahman – the knowing stuff.

Paramatma – the over-self.

Bhagavan – one who has everything.

The Ultimate Reality is conscious, and consciousness wants to be conscious, to experience consciousness, unlimited consciousness. The individual self is like the supreme self or Brahman because it also wants to know without limit. The sense of this ‘non-dual substance’ is that what we call reality comes from consciousness. The supreme consciousness includes, and generates, all other realities.

Just as we might see a box containing a toy, and it reads ‘batteries included.’ There are two things the toy and the batteries, but the batteries are included in the toy, not the other way around. So, the individual brahman is included in the supreme Brahman. Brahman is summum bonum, or the ‘highest good,’ and the ultimate goal.

Advaya means ‘there is nothing but this.’ It is sarva karana karanam and anadir adir – the ‘cause of all other causes’ or the ‘causeless cause’ or ‘the beginningless beginning.’

Jiva Goswami comments that everything and everyone is fully a manifestation of advaya jnana yet is fully dependent. Just as a flower is dependent on the roots. It comes from the root, seems to have a separate existence, but is fully dependent on the roots. The roots do not depend on the flower, even though they are one unit.

Vedic hermeneutics states that if a statement is made at the beginning of the book and at the end, then the contents of the book are normally all about the reaching the conclusion. This verse appears at the beginning of the Shrimad Bhagavatam and therefore it is understood to be the essence of the book. In the first verse of the Shrimad Bhagavatam we find janmadyasya yatah and other clues as to the definition of what will be discussed in the book.

There are three stages of observing the non-dual reality – three ways of perceiving it. Our perception, being limited, attempts to apprehend the non-dual reality. According to the abilities of our perception, we can perceive it. If we fail to perceive Paramatma or Bhagavan then that is due to the limitations of our perception, not the fault of the Ultimate Reality.

The sages of old, dwelling as many did on mountains, have offered the analogy of a mountain to help us understand the three stages of perception:

“Viewed from a distance, a mountain has no detail, and may be shrouded in cloud. Closer, and the trees may be seen. Move closer still, and the creatures may be seen. In this way, understand the three levels of perception.”

Brahman is the substrata, consciousness itself, a potential state.

Paramatma is specificity. Param means ‘paramount’ meaning uppermost or ‘meta.’ Bhagavan is the one who has everything.

These are not three different things; they are three different perspectives of the one thing. Three degrees or extents of perception. Brahman is nothing distinct, existence itself. But that does not mean it is nothing. If I say ‘I have zero oranges’ it doesn’t mean that there is no such thing as oranges. Infinite means ‘boundless or limitless,’ it does not mean ‘nothingness.’

## Paramatma

Just as colours within white light are revealed by a prism, so the distinction within Brahman is revealed by the instrument of sadhana, or ‘the means of attaining something.’

The word atma means ‘special’ as in the ‘special thing’ that makes the essence of something – ‘atmika.’ But the word is normally used as ‘soul.’ It is an experience of individual subjects experiencing individual objects all of which manifest within the supreme.

There is a hierarchy of experiencers but the anu (small) is contained within the vibhu (great).

There is a continuum of consciousness to which we are subject. Although our consciousness is subjective, still if it rains for me it also rains for him, therefore we are subject to an objective reality beyond ourselves. There are ultimate states like death. Everything in the sub-set reflects the super-set it comes from. We are ksetra-jna the ‘knower of the body.’ Paramatma is knower of the universe.

## Bhagavan

‘Opulence-owner’ or ‘Enjoyment-Possessor.’ Unlike Paramatma, Bhagavan has partiality and emotion for these objects and is awake and interactive with them as a distinct individual. We are not the owner of the manifested objects, he is. Imagine if a hand were to envy the stomach and not feed the mouth, or a leaf envied the plant and caught the rainwater before it reached the root. Separate consciousness always debilitates the individual. We are a part of the supreme and not separate enjoyers. Our separation of consciousness from the sum total consciousness debilitates us.

## Is consciousness reality?

**Objection**

Consciousness is dependent on instruments (our senses) and their objects.

Consciousness changes according to the nature of the object it perceives, so it cannot be eternal. It is a product of objects. Logic: our consciousness of an object is produced by the object since if there is no object there is no consciousness of that object.

**Reply**

This is a materialistic argument, in the sense that the phenomenon of consciousness – and therefore perception – is attributed to matter. It sounds like a modern argument but the followers of the ancient, materialist sage Charvaka Muni also spoke like this.

The observed changes. The observer does not. Emotions produced by observations also change, but the one who experiences the emotions does not. Objects do not create observers. Observers create objects of observation.

Time is the way that ‘cause’ gradually morphs into ‘effect.’ There is no past and future only perception. We only experience ‘now’ because that is all there is. The feeling of past and future is a superimposition onto that. There is no time in Brahman because there is no time in that state – there is no change, therefore no past and future. Yet there is movement and change in Brahman, but it is not controlled by time.The conscious entity is not created or destroyed, nor does it grow or diminish. It simply experiences such movements within the flow of time. The power of perception is always constant. It only seems to diminish or change by experiencing things through the senses, via the life-air.

**“Brahman, the eternal soul, was never born and will never die, nor does it grow or decay. That spiritual soul is the knower of the youth, middle age, and death of the material body. Thus, the soul can be understood to be pure consciousness, existing everywhere at all times and never being destroyed. Just as the life air within the body, although one, becomes manifest as many in contact with the various material senses, the one soul appears to assume various material designations in contact with the material body.”**

**Shrimad Bhagavatam 11.3.38**

**Objection**

Consciousness changes according to the nature of the instruments \[senses, brain\] it uses. Therefore, it is not eternal, it is a product of certain instruments. For example, if we see white light through a blue or yellow glass, we see blue or yellow. Therefore, our consciousness has been modulated by the coloured glass. Therefore, consciousness is not something fixed, superior, and independent, it is dependent.

**Reply**

You have stated that: When one perceives, one becomes that which one perceives. No, consciousness merely observes. Similarly, the consciousness observes ‘sadness’ and ‘happiness.’ Consciousness is contracted or expanded according to the quality of the instruments. The instruments change. The user does not. The instruments used by consciousness affect how consciousness can operate, but do not affect consciousness itself. Instruments do not create musicians. Tools do not create workers.If a blue filter is placed over a white light, the light itself has not changed, just the perception of it. The life-air connects consciousness to many different tools of perception, in bodies born from eggs, embryos, seeds, and in other ways. When the body goes into deep sleep, the senses and even the ego-carrying mind are shut off. Yet the unchanging core of consciousness is distinct from all these – for when we wake up, we remember.

**“The spirit soul is born in many different species of life within the material world. Some species are born from eggs, others from embryos, others from the seeds of plants and trees, and others from perspiration. But in all species of life the prana, or vital air, remains unchanging and follows the spirit soul from one body to another. Similarly, the spirit soul is eternally the same despite its material condition of life. We have practical experience of this. When we are absorbed in deep sleep without dreaming, the material senses become inactive, and even the mind and false ego are merged into a dormant condition. But although the senses, mind and false ego are inactive, one remembers upon waking that he, the soul, was peacefully sleeping.”**

**Shrimad Bhagavatam 11.3.39**

**Objection**

Consciousness is merely the sum of all the observations we have.

**Reply**

No, the observations create the mind’s state, according to how the mind responds.

Man creates a computer, and then we become dependent on them, but that does not mean that the computer creates the man.

Mind ‘unites’ with the object, such as when we watch a film, we unite with the hero. Consciousness identifies with the mind, and mind connects with the body via prana.

Consciousness manifests three states:

• Awake - Jagara - Consciousness linked to instruments (mind / senses).

• Dreaming – Svapna - Partial link to instruments (mainly to mind).

• Unconscious – Sushupta - No link to instruments.

Yet, waking up from deep unconscious sleep, we remember it. “I slept so deeply. I was completely out.” This is evidence that consciousness exists beyond the mind. It perceives through the mind and senses, but also has the inherent ability to perceive itself even without utilizing a mind and senses.

*yad vai tan na paśyati paśyan vai*  
*draṣṭavyān na paśyati*  
*na hi draṣṭur dṛṣṭer viparilopo vidyate*

**Though he does not see, he sees!**  
**Even if he does not see objects,**  
**the seer’s sight cannot be removed.**

**Bṛhadāraṇyaka Upanishad 4.3.23**

**Conclusion**

Objects and instruments affect the ability of consciousness, much like lenses over a light affect the light that comes through.

Some objects delight the perception, others disgust it. The delight and disgust happen in the layers over the consciousness, not in the consciousness itself. Consciousness simply witnesses the changes in the layers.

Consciousness is the eternal, unchanging substrate of reality.

As consciousness is freed from the limitations of matter, the ultimate reality, the supreme consciousness, is revealed.

This is progressively manifest as Brahman, Paramatma and Bhagavan.

## Vyasa’s mystical vision

**Fifty centuries ago, high up in the snowy Himalayan mountains, there was an ashram known as Samyaprash. It was there that the sage Vyasa and his disciples lived, learned, and prayed for the salvation of mankind. Knowing that an age would soon come when the minds of men would be weak in memory, and their lives short, Vyasa and his disciples committed the eternal Veda to script for the first time, writing down the ancient oral tradition in beautiful Sanskrit handwriting.**

Vyasa divided the one Veda into four books and supplementary sections, entrusting each part to his numerous disciples. He then oversaw the compilation of the Upanishads and wrote the five hundred verses of the Vedanta Sutra. He had completed an enormous literary output, yet still felt strangely dissatisfied. This puzzled him, as he had taken great care to compile a wide range of literature for the welfare of the world. What could be the source of his confusion? At that moment, as if in answer to his heart’s prayer, his guru, Narada Muni, arrived at the ashram consoled him with kind words. But then he instructed him to complete one more literary effort.

To prepare himself, Vyasa went into a deep meditation during which he entered samadhi, a deep trance wherein the experiencer perceives no difference between himself and the phenomenon being experienced. It was a grand spiritual revelation, and an early sequence of verses in the book he subsequently wrote tells of this mystical vision and how he saw before him a complete vision of reality and its constituent elements. This book, the final fruit of Vyasa’s realisation, was the Shrimad Bhagavatam.

Understanding his experience is crucial for understanding the Shrimad Bhagavatam and its subject. In the following four verses there is a sequence of revelation of what Vyasa saw, from 1.7.4 until 1.7.7 inclusive.

*bhakti-yogena manasi, samyak praṇihite ’male*  
*apaśyat puruṣaṁ pūrṇaṁ, māyāṁ ca tad-apāśrayām*

**When his mind became absolutely pure by full absorption in bhakti-yoga he could clearly see the Complete Supreme Person. He also saw Maya originating from and sheltered by him, at a distance.**

**Shrimad Bhagavatam 1.7.4**

Some key words in this verse are as follows:

apaśyat - “Unseen” He perceived reality to a previously unseen extent.

puruṣa - “Person” He saw a person (conscious entity) as the root of reality.

pūrṇa - “Complete” He saw the Supreme Person (the supreme conscious entity) as the root of reality. He experienced advaya-jnana, or ‘non-dual consciousness’ fully, as Bhagavan who contains Paramatma and Brahman.

*yayā sammohito jīva, ātmānaṁ tri-guṇātmakam*  
*paro ’pi manute ’narthaṁ, tat-kṛtaṁ cābhipadyate*

**He saw Maya mesmerizing individual living beings (jiva), who unfortunately forfeit their superior nature to make themselves a part of Maya due to their desire for her three mesmerizing qualities.**

**Shrimad Bhagavatam 1.7.5**

These first two verses in the series explain the tattva-traya, or three elements of reality. Firstly, there is not simply purusha that he sees in meditation, but purna-purusha, the ‘complete purusha.’ He sees Maya, or the bewildering external energy, in a subservient role, and he sees the jivas becoming ‘mesmerised.’

The jiva, Vyasa explained, wants to be mesmerised. Mohita means illusioned or ‘drunk’ or ‘intoxicated.’ Sammohita means ‘deeply illusioned,’ or as we might say in modern idiom, ‘plastered’ or ‘wasted.’ Along with this intoxication, identity has been lost and along with it, dignity. Anartha means they have forgotten their true worth. The fallen jiva thinks that is something that Maya has generated. If samadhi is the ultimate state of consciousness, when the individual is united with the primal consciousness, then the fallen jivas have made a grand mistake. They want to have samadhi with Maya’s gunas or qualities, completely absorbing themselves in matter.

Maya means extraordinary power, magic, bewildering power, illusion. Vyasa saw advaya-jnana with shakti, with incredible power or magic. The first mentioned bears the name Maya (Bewilderer). Most people think ‘maya’ means illusion, but it means ‘unusual power.’

Maya is associated with what it does, therefore because it, along with the desire of the jiva, bewilders the jiva, it is described as ‘illusion.’ But it is not illusion, rather it creates illusion.

Magic means to effect sleight of hand by directing attention away – misdirection, so Maya misdirects away from the Purusha. Because the jiva wants that. Maya is not a competitor to the Purusha, He is her ashraya. She co-operates with Him.

Consider an example from ordinary life. When we are watching a film, we do not like to be told what happens next by someone who has seen it before. And worst of all, we do not wish to be reminded that it is merely a film. The enjoyment lies in suspending our disbelief, in believing that what is happening is somehow real. To be reminded that it is only a film completely ruins the magic. The illusion that it is not a film is essential for the enjoyment. In a similar way, the jivas elect to become influenced by Maya. They choose to suspend their disbelief and enter a world of illusion. Maya, who creates that illusion, is a shakti, or energy, of the Purusha.

Sometimes philosophers like to say that ‘this world is an illusion’ and that ‘nothing is real, it is all illusory.’ According to Vyasa, however, the world is real, but it produces an illusion. The material energy, in a constant state of flux and therefore impermanence, creates for the complicit jiva, who wishes to exploit it for enjoyment, the illusion that the world is somehow permanent, and that enjoyment is possible.

## Jiva – the individual consciousness

(yayā sammohito jīvāḥ) Māyā bewilders the jiva but can never bewilder the Purna Purusha. The jiva is ‘conscious consciousness.’ ‘Bewilder’ means ‘to lead astray.’ The jiva is an individual unit of consciousness that becomes bewildered. Because it wants to.

A contemporary example might be the case of becoming fascinated by something you cannot produce. The substance, or person, is fascinating because you do not possess what they have, and neither can you produce it. In this condition of fascination, it is easy to become bewildered. You become dependent on that item or person, and such dependency reduces subsequent freedom of choice. When choice is severely limited then bewilderment has taken hold. Similarly, we can become infinitely and eternally fascinated by Maya’s magic. The jiva thinks: “I myself am Bhagavan, give me a world to enjoy.” So, it is provided. The entire world then becomes endlessly fascinating for the jiva. Other words in these first two key verses are:

ātmānaṁ triguṇātmakaṁ - Maya can create beautiful things with her three qualities (tri-guṇa). These fascinate the jīva.

paro’pi manute’narthaṁ - It is sentience (paraḥ). It can think (manute). It has a ﬂaw anartha – which is that it has a fascination with something external to itself.

tat kṛtaṁ cābhipadyate - It thinks itself a product dependent on Maya.

## Maya-vada Ideas

However, there is another viewpoint which has become popular. The Advaita philosophy says that Purusha itself becomes illusioned by Maya and ends up mistakenly thinking that ‘I am jiva.’ And that nothing the illusory jiva experiences is therefore real. This idea poses several logical fallacies. For this to be true we must explain how Purusha can be illusioned by something other than Purusha. Or why it would desire such a state of existence. This is the philosophy of the Advaitins or Maya-vadis – those whose conclusion, or ‘vada,’ is that ‘Maya’ or illusion is responsible for all phenomena. Since all phenomena are part of a singular illusion that Purusha is undergoing, everything is no more substantial than a dream. Everything is an illusion.

This idea is completely different from the actual situation, that the jiva is illusioned. In the Mayavadi philosophy the Purusha is in illusion, and part of the illusion is the individual jiva. The jiva does not exist. It is only Purusha imagining that ‘I am a jiva.’ In Vyasa’s philosophy, the jiva is real, but illusioned by a power beyond itself. The Mayavadi philosophy teaches that the entire world, along with each jiva, is nothing more than the illusion of Purusha, and that nothing is real. Vyasa’s writing, in stark contrast, explains that the Purusha has energies that become transformed into all phenomena. Purusha is the origin and possessor of energy or Shakti and is therefore known Shaktiman. Shaktiman and shakti are not ‘all one.’

It is surprising that the Mayavada philosophy has survived. It is, as Srila Prabhupada taught, a form of ‘word jugglery.’ It can only be perpetuated as a proposal, by bewildered jivas, that even though the world failed to deliver on the hoped-for enjoyment, there is still a chance for them to become Bhagavan. Therefore, Mayavada philosophy is part of the illusion rather than a path out of it. Vyasa continues:

*anarthopasamam saksad, bhakti-yogam adhoksaje*  
*lokasyajanato vidvams, cakre satvata-samhitam*

**He saw bhakti to Adhoksaja as the direct cure for this misfortune. People are unaware of this, so wise Vyasa composed this spiritual Veda.**

**Shrimad Bhagavatam 1.7.6**

Sakshad means ‘direct.’ Loka is ‘plane’ or ‘community.’ Another shakti is bhakti. This connects us with Purusha.

## Bhakti – Connection and Consciousness

Yoga means ‘connection.’ It also means shakti (connecting cause to effect). Bhakti means ‘connection’ or ‘sharing.’ It also means love and happiness because it reveals our true happy nature.

The word Bhakti comes from ‘bhaj’ which is linked to bhoj, ‘to enjoy’ and bhoga ‘enjoyment,’ and bhagavan means ‘one who has all the enjoyment.’ It has roots in the sense of becoming unified with the object, for instance by eating, we become unified with the food.

The construction of the word Adhoksaja is interesting, in that it indicates a supreme reality in comparison to all other realities which can be seen.

Akshaja = ‘eye-born,’ ‘something the eye produces,’ ‘a see-able thing.’

Adha = ‘something that pushes down, that belittles.’

Adhokshaje = Adha+Akshaja - something better than anything you can see.

That which is ‘adhoksaja’ is that which is better than any other thing which can be experienced.

adhokṣaje - Bhakti is the shakti that enables jiva to connect with Adhoksaja - The one superior to (adha) any external experience (aksa-ja).

anarthopasamam saksat - bhakti nullifies the jiva’s fascination with Maya by revealing the more fascinating Complete Person. And who is that Purna Purusha or ‘Complete Person?’ The next verse clarifies that ‘Krishna is the Parama Purusha.’

*yasyāṁ vai śrūyamāṇāyāṁ kṛṣṇe parama-pūruṣe*  
*bhaktir utpadyate puṁsaḥ śoka-moha-bhayāpahā*

**Simply by giving aural reception to this Vedic literature, the feeling for loving devotional service to Lord Krishna, the Parama Purusha, sprouts up at once to extinguish the fire of lamentation, illusion, and fearfulness.**

**Shrimad Bhagavatam 1.7.7**

Four chapters before the account of his samadhi, Vyasa records a discussion wherein a sage named Suta is giving a list of incarnations of Vishnu. In the 28th verse, after describing the names and actions of twenty-two avatars, the sage states that ‘all of them are full portions, or portions of the portions’ of Krishna who is the svayam bhagavan or ‘complete Bhagavan.’

*ete cāṁśa-kalāḥ puṁsaḥ kṛṣṇas tu bhagavān svayam*  
*indrāri-vyākulaṁ lokaṁ mṛḍayanti yuge yuge*

**All the above-mentioned incarnations are either plenary portions or portions of the plenary portions of the Lord, but Lord Sri Krishna is the original Personality of Godhead. All of them appear whenever there is a disturbance created by the atheists. The Lord incarnates to protect the theists.**

**Shrimad Bhagavatam 1.3.28**

**Summary of this section**

Shaktiman Purusha has three types of potencies:

1\. Maya — the shakti which produces delusions.

2\. Jiva — the shakti that experiences delusions.

3\. Bhakti — the shakti that frees the jiva from Maya and gives it a relationship to Krishna.

## The infinitely powerful

**This chapter continues the important discussion of the origin of the phenomenal world, of reality and illusion, and of energies and their energetic source. The energetic source and its energies can be counted as two – spirit and matter – or as three – the Purusha, the souls, and matter. Matter is then analysed according to its numerous constituent elements. This analysis has resulted in a wide range of terminology, according to the respective classical text which describes it.**

When we talk of the sun, we also indicate the sunshine. The sun’s rays are the energy of the sun, energetic transformations that originate in the sun itself, and they cannot be separated from it. So, the very concept of ‘sun’ also includes ‘the sun’s energies of heat and light.’ Just as the illumination of the sun, which is situated in one place, is spread all over, so the energies of Para Brahman, or ‘God,’ are spread all over the universe.

The simplest description of God’s energies is that there are only two – spirit and matter. These are described by Krishna as superior and inferior. Spirit is eternal, infinite, and never degraded by change or time. Matter is always changing in composition as the elements that make it up combine and dissolve over time. It is in a permanent state of flux. Krishna describes matter as His bhinna-prakriti, his ‘separated energy,’ meaning that it is Him – but a separated part of Him.

Two natural examples are given in scripture. The first is the milk-giving cow. When she stands in the field, she has the milk inside her, it is part of her body, but it is invisible. Therefore, we speak of ‘this cow’ and not ‘this cow and milk.’ When she feeds her calf, the milk comes out. The milk is non-different from the cow, since it is her very body, but now it is her ‘separated energy.’ It becomes categorised differently. Similarly, when God creates matter, it comes out from spirit but as a ‘separated energy.’ Just as the cow’s milk depends on the cow for its existence, so the material energy, of which everything we see is made, depends on the spiritual energy of God. Because it is a dependent energy it is classed as an ‘inferior’ energy.

The second example given in the Upanishads is of the spider and her web. The web is inside the spider, invisible yet existing as potential. The spider exudes the silken strand and creates a web. Now there are two entities, the spider, and the web, one the origin of the other. At a certain time, the spider, if she wishes, can recoil the strands within her, winding up and re-absorbing the entire web. The web cannot exist without the spider, but she never becomes entangled in it. In a similar manner, Para Brahman exudes the entire material cosmos, allows it to remain for some time, never becomes influenced by it, then winds the entire manifestation up again, absorbing it within Him.

This second example serves to illustrate the reality of the relation of matter to spirit. At one point there was only spirit, then there was matter and spirit, then only spirit again.

There are only two principles, spirit and matter, and the numberless spiritual souls all emanate from the Supreme Spirit. The numberless souls are known as dependent, and the one Supreme

Spirit is known as Independent. In Sanskrit this relationship is known as Svatantra - Paratantra, or Independent-Dependent. There is a famous verse from both the Katha Upanishad and the Svetashvatara Upanishad:

*nityo nityanam cetanas cetananam / eko bahunam yo vidadhati kaman*  
*tam atma-stham ye ’nupasyanti dhiras / tesham shantih sasvati netaresam*

**There is one eternal amongst many eternals: and one conscious entity among many conscious entities. The difference is that the One is the maintainer of all the others.**

So now there appears to be three categories of energies: independent spirit, dependent spirit, and matter. These are known as internal, external, and marginal. The internal energy consists of the supreme and all His expansions and incarnations, as well as the entirety of the infinite spiritual world. The external energy is the manifestation of the material elements, combined to make up the perceivable world. The marginal energy is the living being. It is said that of the total radiation of energies from the Para Brahman, three-quarters is the internal and only one-quarter the external. Srila Prabhupada writes:

“The seventy-five percent expansion of His radiation is called His internal energy, whereas the twenty-five percent expansion is called the external energy of the Lord. The living entities, who are residents of the spiritual as well as the material expansions, are His marginal energy and they are at liberty to live in either of the energies, external or internal.

Those who live within the spiritual expansion of the Lord are called liberated souls, whereas the residents of the external expansion are called the conditioned souls. We can just make an estimate of the number of the residents of the internal expansions in comparison with the number of residents in the external energy and may easily conclude that the liberated souls are far more numerous than the conditioned souls.”

The individual soul is known as the tatastha-shakti of God. Tatastha translates as ‘one on the shoreline,’ because it is situated on the boundary between the two energies. Like a creature on a beach, sometimes the soul is covered by the waves and sometimes not. Once the living being chooses to identify with the material energy, it is helplessly placed in the burning sun of suffering, life after life.

All energies in existence have their origin in God, and this is the very definition of God: the source of everything. God is an inexhaustible energetic source that never becomes depleted. In ordinary dealings, if a man has ten bananas and gives three to a friend, he will have seven remaining. Through his generosity he has increased the availability of bananas, gained the gratitude of his friend, but depleted his own stock.

Similarly, if a woman gives birth to three children, she has the rewarding experience of a lifetime’s loving exchange with them. But as time passes by, and as she watches them grow into adults, she herself diminishes in strength and eventually dwindles and dies. This is material nature.

This phenomenon of depletion or reduction is so common to us that we even project it onto God. Even great philosophers mistakenly assume that if God manifests various energies for the creation of the universe His stock of energy must be accordingly reduced; or if God manifests souls, He must eventually be proportionately depleted. Some thinkers have put forward the idea that God – the original spiritual substance – fragments Himself into countless billions of divine sparks, or souls. The process of self-realisation for the individual soul, they claim, then becomes one of God trying to rediscover His forgotten identity – God trying to realise Himself.

But the Upanishads are very clear: In the case of Para Brahman, 10 – 3 = 10. God never becomes depleted, no matter how much energy comes from Him. He is infinite and inexhaustible. And though numberless souls come from God, still He remains eternally at full strength and power. Finally, though each individual soul is also eternal, blissful, and cognisant, God’s consciousness of Himself remains as it is. He never becomes forgetful of who He is and has no need to engage in ‘looking for Himself.’ God is always full in Himself, supremely independent, and is never overcome by forgetfulness. Therefore, the invocation to the Isha Upanishad begins:

*oṁ pūrṇam adaḥ pūrṇam idaṁ/ pūrṇāt pūrṇam udacyate*  
*pūrṇasya pūrṇam ādāya/ pūrṇam evāvaśiṣyate*

**The Personality of Godhead is perfect and complete, and because He is completely perfect, all emanations from Him, such as this phenomenal world, are perfectly equipped as complete wholes. Whatever is produced of the Complete Whole is also complete. Because He is the Complete Whole, even though so many complete units emanate from Him, He remains the complete balance.**

Without understanding the three energies of Para Brahman and how they relate, it is easy to fall into thinking that, since everything is spiritual, ‘everything is God,’ and that the soul, when liberated from identification with the body, will become God. The other trap is to think that God is so completely different from the soul that the soul and God can never be truly related at all. The one idea leads to nihilism, the other to an existential loss of hope.

The soul is simultaneously one with, and different from, God. It is known as bheda-abheda or ‘different-non-different.’ Because to have two states of existence at the same time is mentally inconceivable it is known as an ‘inconceivable truth.’ Thus, the philosophy taught by Chaitanya Mahaprabhu is known as achintya-bhedabheda-tattva.

## A Message to the West

In his carefully worded message to Western intelligentsia in 1896, Bhaktivinode Thakur writes in The Gauranga Lila Smarana Mangala Stotram:

‘By infinite powers must be meant powers which know no bounds either in space or in time, as His powers alone created space and time.

‘His powers are identified with His person. In material objects, there is a difference between the person and its powers, between the thing and its attributes, its name, its form and action; but it is a spiritual truth that in spirit the thing is identical with its name, form, attributes and action. This truth cannot be subjected to dry reason which deals with gross matter alone.

‘Krishna is Supreme Will in Himself and He exercises His supreme power at His pleasure, which submits to no law, because all law has proceeded from His Will and Power. Power is known from its exercise.

‘In this world we have experience of only three of the attributes of His power. We see the material phenomena and we understand that His power has the attribute to create matter. This attribute is styled in the Vedas as Maya-shakti. We see man and we understand that the Supreme power has the attribute to produce limited and imperfect souls. The sastras call that attribute as Jiva-shakti. We conceive of One who is spiritual and supreme in his realm of eternal spirits. We understand that His power has an attribute to exhibit perfectly spiritual existences. The Vedas call that attribute by the name of Atma-shakti or Chit-shakti.

‘All these attributes together form one supreme power which the Vedas call as Para-shakti. In fact, power (shakti) is not distinguishable from the person of that Being. Still the powers are separately exhibited in their separate actions. This is styled Achintya-bhedabheda prakash or inconceivable simultaneous existence of distinction and non-distinction. Hari being Will above Law, exercises His infinite powers, while He Himself remains unaffected.’

## Energies of Brahman are all-pervading

*Eka desa sthitasyagner / Jyotsna vistarini yatha*  
*Parasya Brahmana saktis / tathedam akhilam jagat*

**Just as the illumination of fire, situated in one place, is spread all over, the energies of the supreme Brahman are spread throughout the universe.**

**Vishnu Purana 1.22.53**

Just as fire has both heat and light, so the supreme has all energies existing simultaneously.

Vedanta says: sakti-saktimatayor abhedah‘- The shaktiman is non-different from the shakti.’

## Two Energies: Purusha and Prakriti

The shaktis of the Purna Purusha are categorised and named variously. The first definition is twofold – Purusha and Prakriti, or Spiritual energy and Material energy - Spirit and Matter.

One is always experiencing (Purusha) and the other is always transforming (Prakriti).

The word Prakriti is derived from kriti – to create, and pra – meaning for, as in the Latin suffix ‘pro’ also meaning ‘for.’ Procreate is therefore linked to Prakriti, or that which creates.

Prakriti is that which is subject to being transformed, always in a state of flux – it is known as matter. Purusha is the higher nature which never changes – spirit.

The definition of dull matter is that it cannot create. Matter can only appear to create when it is touched by a higher energy. For example: Iron cannot burn unless it is touched by fire, then it acts as if it were fire.

The nature of creating anything is that there is always a lower energy which is manipulated by a higher energy with the desire to create and the will to do it. For example, to make a clay pot involves various causes:

upadana-karana- the material cause (The clay)

nimitta-karana efficient cause (The potter’s wheel, kiln, the potter’s hands) sukshma-karana – remote or subtle cause (the potter’s desire)

The real self is purusha, but we think it is prakriti – this leads to illusion and then to actions designed to extract happiness from matter, which leads to frustration followed by despair. Similarly, the supreme self is purusha or spirit and, like the potter creating his pot, possesses will to create. When we deny the supreme origin of all energy the attribute of will, we disconnect ourselves from understanding, and experiencing, the Purusha.

The supreme is not deficient in anything that can be seen in his creation. The energies coming from an energetic source are always greater closer to the source. Just as heat and light are present in the sun. The source is not deficient in what it produces.

The word purusha is also explained, by the 13th century philosopher Madhva Acharya as ‘pura shete iti purusha’ or ‘he who lives in the city of the body is the purusha.’ Purusha means ‘person’ and is also a general word for ‘man’ or ‘human.’ So, the person who lives in the body is the purusha, or soul. God is purusha and man is also purusha. They are one in quality of spirit but not in quantity. The difference is that one is dependent on the other. Therefore, God is often referred to as Parama Purusha or Purna Purusha – the ‘Supreme Purusha’ or ‘Complete Purusha.’

## Three Energies, Many Names

The Tattva-Traya - the three metaphysical elements, appear in a range of Vedic texts. According to the texts the terminology varies. The Tattva-Traya also appears in Sanskrit, Tamil, Manipravala, Bengali and now English and all the languages of the world. Here is a small, comparative sample:

![рис-004.jpg|578](<../attachments/ten-teachings-of-bhakti.-a-vaishnava-%D1%81atechism--%D1%80%D0%B8%D1%81-004--188ce68c90d21c9b.jpg>)

Krishna is potent and has potencies; He is energetic and has energies; He is shaktiman and has shakti. These three terms are interchangeable and are used throughout.

The Vedanta Sutra states: sakti-saktimatayor abhedah: ‘The shaktiman is non-different from the shakti.’ The shaktiman extends to all energies:

*sa visvakrid visvavidatma-yonih jnah kalakaro guni sarvavid yah*  
*pradhana-kshetrajna-patir gunesah samsara-moksha-sthiti-bandha-hetuh*

**He who is the support of both the unmanifested prakriti and the jiva, who is the Lord of the three gunas and who is the cause of bondage, existence and liberation from samsara, is verily the Creator of the universe, the Knower, the inmost Self of all things and their Source-the omniscient Lord, the Personification of time, the Possessor of virtues, the Knower of everything.**

**Svetashvatara Upanishad 6.16**

## The internal energy

**As we have seen, God is the source of all energies, classified as primarily three in number: internal, external, and marginal. Although He is the source of these energies, He is also the sum of them. The internal energy is He Himself, the entire spiritual world, and all His expansions and incarnations.**

There are three aspects to the internal energy of God, known classically as bliss, existence, and cognition. Srila Prabhupada comments on a famous verse from the Vishnu Purana as follows: “The Supreme Personality of Godhead has three kinds of internal potency, namely the hladini-sakti, or pleasure potency, the sandhini-sakti, or existential potency, and the samvit-sakti, or cognitive potency. In the Vishnu Purana (1.12.69) the Lord is addressed as follows:

"O Lord, you are the support of everything. The three attributes hladini, sandhini and samvit exist in you as one spiritual energy. But the material modes, which cause happiness, misery, and mixtures of the two, do not exist in you, for you have no material qualities." In his mediaeval thesis, the Bhagavat Sandarbha , Śrīla Jīva Gosvāmī explains the potencies as follows:

“The transcendental potency of the Supreme Personality of Godhead by which He maintains His existence is called sandhinР”«. The transcendental potency by which He knows Himself and causes others to know Him is called samvit. The transcendental potency by which He possesses transcendental bliss and causes His devotees to have bliss is called hlādinī”

There is a natural example to help us remember the energies as a unified whole. When we see a tree, we understand that ‘tree’ also includes the roots, trunk, branches, twigs, flowers, and fruits. There is variety in the unity, and unity in the variety. Varieties of energy are required for performing the varieties of activity within the tree. Srila Prabhupada gives another example of the machine:

“In a machine all the parts may be iron, but the machine includes varied activities. Although the whole machine is iron, one part works in one way, and other parts work in other ways. One who does not know how the machine is working may say that it is all iron; nonetheless, in spite of its being iron, the machine has different elements, all working differently to accomplish the purpose for which the machine was made.

One wheel runs this way, another wheel runs that way, functioning naturally in such a way that the work of the machine goes on. Consequently, we give different names to the different parts of the machine, saying, "This is a wheel," "This is a screw," "This is a spindle," "This is the lubrication," and so on. Similarly, as explained in the Vedas:

*parāsya śaktir vividhaiva śrūyate svābhāvikī jñāna-bala-kriyā ca*  
*Svetasvatara Upanishad 6.7.8*

The one divine potency of Parabrahman has been described in the Vedas in many ways. In the verse above the three shaktis are described as ‘jnana-bala-kriya,’ or jnana-shakti (knowledge), bala-shakti (power), and kriya-shakti (potency for activity). These terms correspond exactly to samvit-shakti, sandhini-shakti, and hladini-shakti, respectively.

These three terms also correspond to the more commonly known terms of Sat, Chit and Ananda or ‘Eternity, Knowledge and Bliss.’ All these potencies – whatever terminology is used to describe them – exist as parts of the one possessor of all potencies – the omnipotent.

![рис-005.jpg|560](<../attachments/ten-teachings-of-bhakti.-a-vaishnava-%D1%81atechism--%D1%80%D0%B8%D1%81-005--eb5ad11b27730f85.jpg>)

There are three aspects of the Purna Brahma, each greater than the previous with respect to the manifestation of Sat, Chit, Ananda.

![рис-006.jpg|400](<../attachments/ten-teachings-of-bhakti.-a-vaishnava-%D1%81atechism--%D1%80%D0%B8%D1%81-006--3b8ad6aa6b452c8d.jpg>)

## Brahman

Brahman is the nirvisesha or undifferentiated state of Bhagavan. It is devoid of distinction, homogeneous and indivisible. All powers remain within it in a potential state. It is the tanubha or bodily lustre of Bhagavan.

## Param Atma

Param Atma is the differentiated and qualified state of a partial manifestation of Bhagavan. He is Godhead in relation to the material world and the souls. He has two powers: the jiva shakti, or the souls, and the Maya shakti or external energy, the world of matter.

Param Atma is translated as the Super Self or Super Soul (Supersoul) and is also known as the Antaryami or ‘Indwelling Witness,’ the controller of all the souls in the material world.

Through the jiva shakti He is immanent in the jivas and in the world through the Maya shakti.

The Param Atma is the metaphysical foundation of the evolution of the world from prakriti and is endowed with the powers of creation, maintenance, and dissolution. Param Atma is an ontological reality. Param Atma has no part to play with those who have realised the Absolute.

A fairground comes to town and more police are drafted into the location. The fairground leaves town and the police leave. Similarly, the material world is created as a place of attraction for the rebellious souls, and Param Atma is there to supervise and to care for them.

## Bhagavan

Bhagavan is the highest and most complete manifestation of the Absolute. He is infinite in excellence, power, and sweetness. He has all divine attributes and shaktis in full. He displays a portion of his powers through Brahman and Param Atma.

Bhagavan is unaffected by Maya and is always above the modes of nature.

Bhagavan is not directly involved in creation.

Bhagavan has two aspects: Aisvarya-maya (power and majesty predominantly) and Madhurya-maya (sweetness predominantly).

*īśvaraḥ paramaḥ kṛṣṇaḥ / sac-cid-ānanda-vigrahaḥ*  
*anādir ādir govindaḥ / sarva-kāraṇa-kāraṇam*

**Krishna, who is known as Govinda, is the Supreme Godhead. He has an eternal blissful spiritual body. He is the origin of all. He has no other origin. He is the prime cause of all causes.**

**Brahma Samhita**

## The Opulence of Bhagavan

There are six principal bhaga or ‘opulences’ listed in the Vishnu Purana, an ancient text compiled by Parashara Muni, the father of Vyasa. One who possesses all of them can be known as Bhagavan, as stated in the Vishnu Purana 6.5.47:

*aiśvaryasya samagrasya vīryasya yaśasaḥ śriyaḥ*  
*jñāna-vairāgyayaiś caiva ṣaṇṇāṁ bhaga itīṅganāḥ*

1\. Aishvarya – majesty, riches, unchallenged rule over others.

2\. Virya – ‘Valor.’ Power or immutability, despite the mutability of creation.

3\. Yasas – all fame.

4\. Sri – a beauty exceeding all others.

5\. Jnana – a direct and simultaneous knowledge of all things.

6\. Vairagya – detachment, dispassion.

Bhagavan’s opulences are of two classes, aishvarya and madhurya. Omniscience, omnipresence, and omnipotence are all ‘majestic’ and too great for Bhagavan to be comprehended or approachable. Whereas friendliness, kindness, attractiveness, deep affection, eagerness to love and be loved are madhurya. It is through these ‘sweet’ attributes that Bhagavan establishes close relationships with the devotees.

## Unlimited Qualities

There are five qualities of God as the ultimate reality:

• Satyam – The Truth, free from any relativities of time or space.

• Chit – Omniscience – The Knower of the universe and all living beings.

• Anantam – Infinitude – That which is infinite and yet continues to expand.

• Anandam – Beatitude – enjoying unlimited bliss.

• Amala – Purity – free from all flaws, deterioration, and purifying.

These describe God as a metaphysical reality, whereas the ‘Opulences of Bhagavan’ describe that reality as possessing attributes, and therefore ascribing personhood. The third list of five, ‘Five Secondary Qualities,’ describe the infinitely sublime characteristics in the matter of how He interacts with his souls.

## Five Secondary Qualities

Saulabhya – Accessible – Godhead is infinite, remote, and unattainable by any powers of imagination or personal effort. However, Godhead is also as close as the heart. The Supreme Personality of Godhead is fully accessible to someone who simply chooses to turn to Him. Saushilya – ‘Perfect in Gentleness’ is how Srila Prabhupada renders this term.

Vatsalya – Loving – An affectionate mother cow licking her newly-born calf (vatsala) is the image created by this word. Just as the calf’s amniotic sac is licked away by the mother, so God kindly removes our last life samskaras.

Karunya – Compassionate – God has unlimited empathy and feels pity for the conditioned souls and their misfortune. That is why He has a strong desire to help them and extends Himself time and again in numberless avatars and saintly teachers. He is benevolent to us, despite our lack of qualification.

Audarya – Generous – Krishna come many times and is overwhelmingly charitable in each incarnation, giving the conditioned jivas just what they need to free themselves from matter.

## Where God Dwells

**The word prapya means ‘that which is sought’ and it is the first subject for consideration in the Artha Panchakam. Whether we know it or not, we are all seeking God.** Since God is the source of all love, all peace, and infinite pleasure for the soul, the soul looks for Him everywhere. But where is He and what form does He take? The Pancharatra describes five places where the form of God is manifest.

Para – the Supreme. The highest, original form of God in the Spiritual Sky. He is known as Parama Purusha, the Supreme Person, and is served by countless eternally liberated devotees. Vyuha – the Formation. God expands and the expansions are grouped for functions. Balarama is the first expansion, and then the quadruple expansions of Vasudeva, Sankarshan, Pradyumna and Aniruddha. From them come the expansions for creation of the universe, known as Mahavishnu, Garbhodakashayi Vishnu and Kshiradakashayi Vishnu.

Vibhava – the Multiplied. God incarnates as avatars such as Matsya, Rama, Nrsimha and countless others. Some of the avatars are for specific functions such as to protect their devotee at a time of need (Nrsimha), and some to re-establish dharma in the world (Rama). Most of the avatars accomplish many purposes at one time.

Antaryami – the Internal Ruler. God dwells in the heart as Paramatma, the unseen witness, the Supersoul. In the Bhagavad-gita, the Lord in the heart is known as Upadrasta - the Witness of all, The Overseer, The Omniscient One, and Anumanta – The Sanctioner, The Permitter. Archa – the Imaged. God manifests as the sacred image, the visible Deity in the temple or home altar. We cannot see God with our eyes; we can only see matter. God kindly manifests in material elements such as stone, metal, wood so that we can see Him directly, touch and serve Him. It is not that God has become matter, but that matter has become God by His mercy.

## The Divine Form

Bhagavan’s body is composed of Sat, Chit, Ananda. The human form is based upon this template. It should not be considered that the body of Bhagavan is an anthropomorphic projection of the human mind.

Those who are foolish consider that Bhagavan assumes a form of flesh and blood when appearing within this world as an avatar.

There is no contradiction between Bhagavan having a divine form and being infinite.

Bhagavan does not end where his body ends.

Vishnu (Narayana) has four hands displaying one attribute in each. The disc, conch, club, and lotus are in different hands according to the form of Vishnu. Each form has a different name. There are 24 major forms.

Krishna in Mathura or Dwaraka has either two or four hands. Krishna in Vrindavan has two hands.

The consort, or principal hladini shakti, of Narayana is known as Laxmi. The consort of Krishna in Dwaraka is Rukmini, and in Vrindavan it is Radharani.

## The Beauty of Bhagavan

The Lord’s face is perpetually very beautiful and pleasing in attitude. To the devotees who see Him, He appears never to be displeased, and He is always prepared to award benedictions to them. His eyes, His nicely decorated eyebrows, His raised nose, and His broad forehead are all very beautiful. He is more beautiful than all the demigods.The Lord’s form is always youthful. Every limb and every part of His body is properly formed, free from defect. His eyes and lips are pinkish like the rising sun. He is always prepared to give shelter to the surrendered soul, and anyone so fortunate as to look upon Him feels all satisfaction. The Lord is always worthy to be the master of the surrendered soul, for He is the ocean of mercy.

**“The Lord is further described as having the mark of Р•С™rР”«vatsa, or the sitting place of the goddess of fortune, and His bodily hue is deep bluish. The Lord is a person, He wears a garland of flowers, and He is eternally manifest with four hands, which hold a conch shell, wheel, club, and lotus flower. The entire body of the Supreme Personality of Godhead, VР”Рѓsudeva, is decorated. He wears a valuable jewelled helmet, necklaces and bracelets, His neck is adorned with the Kaustubha jewel, and He is dressed in yellow silk garments.”**

**“The Lord is decorated with small golden bells around His waist, and His lotus feet are decorated with golden ankle bells. All His bodily features are very attractive and pleasing to the eyes. He is always peaceful, calm, and quiet and very pleasing to the eyes and the mind.”**

**“Real yogР”«s meditate upon the transcendental form of the Lord as He stands on the whorl of the lotus of their hearts, the jewellike nails of His lotus feet glittering. The Lord is always smiling, and the devotee should constantly see the Lord in this form, as He looks very mercifully toward the devotee. In this way the meditator should look toward the Supreme Personality of Godhead, the bestower of all benedictions.”**

**Shrimad Bhagavatam 4.8.45 – 51**

**“Wearing a peacock-feather ornament upon His head, blue karnikara flowers on His ears, a yellow garment as brilliant as gold, and the Vaijayanti garland, Lord Krishna exhibited His transcendental form as the greatest of dancers as He entered the forest of Vrindavan, beautifying it with the marks of His footprints. He filled the holes of His flute with the nectar of His lips, and the cowherd boys sang His glories.”**

**Shrimad Bhagavatam 10.21.5**

## Krishna’s expansions

**Although Krishna is the original person and therefore the oldest of all, he appears as a youth, never more than sixteen or twenty years of age. And although Krishna is one, he expands Himself into innumerable forms, known as expansions and incarnations.**

Krishna’s innumerable forms are known as plenary expansions because they all have the full power of the Supreme Personality of Godhead. Though each of these expansions has activities, bodily features, and other attributes slightly different from the original form of Krishna, they are all identical with Krishna. They are one and the same supreme personality. The Brahma Samhita explains that as one candle can light many other candles, each with the same power of illumination, so Lord Krishna expands Himself into unlimited forms of Godhead.

In western theology this state is known as hypostasis, meaning a hierarchy of forms and manifestations, one generated from the previous one, yet all sitting within the original and omnipotent Godhead, the one substantive reality. When they become manifest, they have various powers. Each one is an individual person. They are all under the original personality of Godhead but in unity with Him.

## Three principal forms of Krishna

There are three principal forms of Krishna:

Svayam-rupa – the original form in Vrindavan.

Prabhava-prakash – expansion of many identical forms

Vaibhava-prakash is when one form or feature is manifested differently according to his emotions.

The svayam-rupa is the form of Krishna which is self-manifesting and does not depend on anything else. Sometimes it is referred to as ‘the essential form.’ Sometimes this is referred to as ‘the pastime form.’ It is in this form that Krishna remains always in Vrindavan with the inhabitants of Vrindavan. In this form Krishna is always a young cowherd boy.The svayam-rupa personal form can be further divided into the prabhava and vaibhava forms. Prabhava-prakash is when he expands himself into numerous identical forms as in during the divine circle-dance of rasa lila, or when he manifests numerous identical forms in the palaces of Dwaraka.

**“When the Lord married 16,108 wives at Dvārakā, He expanded Himself into many forms. These expansions and the expansions at the rāsa dance are called prābhava-prakāśa, according to the directions of revealed scriptures.”**

**CC Madhya 20.168**

The vaibhava-prakash of the Lord is when he is manifested differently according to his emotions, such as when he expands as his brother Balarama. The term is also used when Krishna manifests as a youthful warrior in Dwaraka.

*sei vapu, sei ākṛti pṛthak yadi bhāse bhāvāveśa-bhede nāma ‘vaibhava-prakāśe’*

**‘If one form or feature is differently manifested according to different emotional features, it is called vaibhava-prakāśa.’**

*vaibhava-prakāśa kṛṣṇera — śrī-balarāma varṇa-mātra-bheda, saba — kṛṣṇera samāna*

**‘The first manifestation of the vaibhava feature of Kṛṣṇa is Śrī Balarāmajī. Śrī Balarāma and Kṛṣṇa have different bodily colours, but otherwise Śrī Balarāma is equal to Kṛṣṇa in all respects.’**

*vaibhava-prakāśa yaiche devakī-tanuja dvibhuja-svarūpa kabhu, kabhu haya caturbhuja*

**‘An example of vaibhava-prakāśa is the son of Devakī. He sometimes has two hands and sometimes four hands.’**

**CC Madhya 171, 174-175**

The vaibhava-prakash form of the Lord expands into the tad-ekatma-rupa form.

The tad-ekatma-rupa is a form which is non-different from the svayam-rupa but appears to be different because of differing appearance and activities.

The tad-ekatma-rūpa also has two forms: vilasa and svamsa.

Vilasa is defined as ‘that which manifests almost similar qualities as svayam rupa. The vilasa form is different from the original form due to Krishna’s desire to perform different pastimes, but it is very slightly less in qualities.

Svamsa is a personal expansion which is like the original form but less important and exhibits less qualities and potency, such as the quadruple expansions of Vasudeva, Sankarshan, Pradyumna and Aniruddha.

The vilasa expansions are again divided into two: prabhava-vilasa and vaibhava-vilasa.

The prabhava-vilasa is the chatur-vyuha and from each of those four forms, three others are manifest. When we apply tilak to the body we invoke their names.

From each of the four members of the chatur-vyuha, two additional pastime forms are manifest, making a total of eight.

## Four Secondary Forms of Krishna

Krishna has four secondary forms known as the four vyuhas or divine manifestations. The word for ‘four’ is chatur and therefore they are known as the chatur-vyuha or ‘four forms.’

They are named Vasudeva, Sankarshan, Pradyumna and Aniruddha. They are identical though different in forms.

The four vyuhas have two manifestations, the first is in Dwaraka (vaibhava prakash) where they exist as Krishna, Balarama, Pradyumna and Aniruddha. They are all two-handed.

The second manifestation is in the Para-Vyoma or Spiritual Sky. They are all four-handed.

Vasudeva manifests Sankarshan, who manifests Pradyumna, who expands himself as Aniruddha.

## Three Purushas

In turn, Sankarshan expands as Maha Vishnu, also known as Karanarnava-shayi Narayana, or Karanadaka-shayi Vishnu. He is also known as the first Purusha. He lies down within the the Causal Ocean in a state of sleep. At the time of creation, all souls are manifested from him, awakened from a state of suspended animation within him. At the time of dissolution of the universe, the souls are absorbed back into him. He is the immediate cause of creation, for it is by his glance that prakriti gets energy and undergoes evolution.

After the creation of the brahmandas or universes, this first Purusha enters each universe in another form known as Garbhodaka-shayi Vishnu, a manifestation of Pradyumna and the second Purusha. He lies upon a couch of snakes and is attended by his consort Lakshmi. He gives birth to Brahma who then gives birth to Shiva.

Garbhodaka-shayi Vishnu manifests himself as the third Vishnu, known as Kshiradaka-shayi Vishnu. This Vishnu is a manifestation of Aniruddha who is the fourth vyuha, but the third Purusha.

Bhagavan is said to be both the efficient cause and the material cause of the world. He is the efficient cause through his para-shakti and the material cause through his maya-shakti. However, Bhagavan has nothing to do with the processes of the material world. All the cosmic activities are performed by Bhagavan through the four vyuhas and the Three Purushas whom he entrusts with their respective functions. Of them, Mahavishnu can be said to be the efficient and material cause of the world.

## The Spiritual World

The spiritual world is ‘three quarters’ of all reality, the material world ‘one quarter.’

There is an eternal residence of Narayana and all the forms in the Para Vyoma or Spiritual Sky. Each of the Vishnu forms has a separate planet. Collectively this spiritual world is known as Vaikuntha, or ‘the place of no anxiety.’ In the upper portion of the sky there is Krishna-loka, which is divided into three, Gokula, Mathura and Dwaraka.

The Lord is similarly situated in his murti in various locations.

A plenary expansion of Krishna presides over each of the innumerable planets in the spiritual sky. These expansions, which have four arms, are called Narayana expansions, and the spiritual sky is known as Narayana-loka.

The breadth of each spiritual planet is eight miles multiplied by one hundred, by one thousand, by ten thousand, by one hundred thousand, and by ten million. In other words, each spiritual planet is beyond our ability to measure.

Despite their infinite size and number, the planets of Narayana-loka surround Krishna’s personal abode, Krishna-loka, as petals surround the whorl of a lotus. The shape of the spiritual sky is compared to a lotus flower. The topmost region of that flower is called the whorl, and within that whorl is Krishna’s abode. The petals of the spiritual lotus flower consist of many Vaikuntha planets.

According to the Siddhartha Samhita, the Lord in his various forms is carrying different weapons in his different hands. The form of the Lord can be ascertained from the location of the weapons. The Hayashirsha Pancharatra also provides such a list.

Lord Vishnu carries a weapon in each of his four hands. The weapons are the conch-shell (shankha), the club (gada), the lotus (padma), and the discus (chakra). In describing the twenty-four forms, the order of objects in Lord Vishnu’s hands will be given as: lower right, upper right, upper left and lower left of the lord. The twenty-four names of these Lords are known as Chatur Vimsati Nama, and are used in marking the body with clay and in the process of making the upper body sacred by the ritual of nyasa, or touching the body with names and fingers.

## Creation

**How God created the world is an important topic for every religion, and there are many creation stories, the most complex of which is found in the Puranas. Matter becomes gradually manifested through a series of interstitial stages. These begin from a mass of subtle energy, pass through a stage in which all elements of matter are in a potential state, and proceed to differentiation due to the modal forces of nature and time, through to a state where material elements and organs of sense perception and action are fully manifest. No matter how allegorical the Puranic account at first appears, it is astonishingly analytical at the same time.**

## The Three Vishnus and Creation

Maya has two functions – maya and pradhana. They are both involved in creating the cosmic manifestation.

Maya is the efficient cause or ‘the agent that brings a thing into being or initiates a change.’

Pradhana is the material cause or ‘the ingredients that bring a thing into being.’

Purusha is the final cause or original cause, or ‘cause of all causes.’The general description throughout the Vedic texts is of matter becoming gradually manifested through a series of interstitial stages. These begin from a stage of subtle energy, pass through an undifferentiated stage in which all the elements of matter are in a potential state, and proceed to differentiation due to the modal elements of nature, through to a state where material elements and organs of sense perception and action are fully manifest. In the Shrimad Bhagavatam and the Teachings of Lord Chaitanya, Srila Prabhupada explains:

**“Pradhana is the subtle, undifferentiated sum total of all material elements. Although they are undifferentiated, one can understand that the total material elements are contained therein. When the total material elements are manifested by the interaction of the three modes of material nature, the manifestation is called prakriti.**

**The unmanifest material world is the subtle mahat-tattva, and from that mahat-tattva the material manifestation has emerged.”**

**Srimad Bhagavatam 3.26.9-11**

**“Material nature acts in two capacities as māyā and pradhāna. Māyā is the direct cause, and pradhāna refers to the elements of the material manifestation. When the first puruṣa-avatāra, Mahā-Viṣṇu, glances over the material nature, material nature becomes agitated, and the puruṣa-avatāra thus impregnates matter with living entities. Simply by the glance of the Mahā-Viṣṇu, consciousness is created, and this consciousness is known as mahat-tattva. The predominating Deity of the mahat-tattva is Vāsudeva. This created consciousness is then divided into three departmental activities according to the three guṇas, or modes of material nature.”**

**Teachings of Lord Caitanya, chapter 8**

As described above, when the Purusha glances over the material energy he injects the living beings. First, the total material energy is manifest (mahat-tattva) and from this arises three types of egotism:

Vaikarika – egotism in goodness (sattvika ahankara) from which the mind is manifest and whose predominating deity is Aniruddha.

Taijasa – egotism in passion (rajasa ahankara) from which the senses and intelligence are manifest and whose predominating deity is Pradyumna.

Tamasa – egotism in ignorance (tamasa ahankara) from which sound vibration (sabda-tanmatra) expands. From sound the sky is manifest and the senses beginning with the ear.

Maha-Vishnu, the primal cause of all the universes, expands himself into each universe, each of which fills half with water. He then lies down as Garbhodakashayi Vishnu. The sum total of living beings issue from the navel of the Lord as a lotus flower, atop of which sits Brahma.

This Lord is also known as Hiranyagarbha or Antaryami and is glorified by the Vedic hymn known as Purusha Sukta, a famous song of sixteen verses found in the Rig Veda Samhita 10.90.

The third expansion is known as Kshirodakashayi Vishnu and is lying within the ocean of milk within each universe. He is the indwelling witness or Paramatma (‘Supersoul’) within every living being. He is the maintainer and master of the universe.

As described in the Sātvata-tantra:

*viṣṇos tu trīṇi rūpāṇi puruṣākhyāny atho viduḥ*  
*ekaṁ tu mahataḥ sraṣṭṛ dvitīyaṁ tv aṇḍa-saṁsthitam*

**For material creation, Lord Krishna’s plenary expansion assumes three Vishnus. The first one, Maha-Vishnu, creates the total material energy, known as the mahat-tattva. The second, Garbhodaka-shayi Vishnu, enters all the universes to create diversities in each of them. The third, Kshiradaka-shayi Vishnu, is diffused as the all-pervading Super Soul in all the universes and is known as Param Atma. He is present even within the atoms. Anyone who knows these three Vishnus can be liberated from material entanglement.**

**Bhagavad-Gita 7.4**

The original Personality of Godhead (ādyaḥ), Govinda, expands Himself as the Maha-Vishnu incarnation and rests in the Causal Ocean, which He Himself creates. The Brahma-saṁhitā (5.47) confirms this as follows:

*yaḥ kāraṇārṇava-jale bhajati sma yoga-*  
*nidrām ananta-jagad-aṇḍa-saroma-kūpaḥ*  
*ādhāra-śaktim avalambya parāṁ sva-mūrtiṁ*  
*govindam ādi-puruṣaṁ tam ahaṁ bhajāmi*

**Lord Brahma says in his Brahma-samhita, "I worship the primeval Lord Govinda, who lies down in the Causal Ocean in His plenary portion as Maha-Vishnu, with all the universes generating from the pores of hair on His transcendental body, and who accepts the mystic slumber of eternity."**

## Mahat Tattva

In the spiritual sky, the effulgence of Brahman is spread all around, and the whole system is dazzling in spiritual light. From time to time, in this infinite and eternal clear sky, a cloud forms.
