This cloud is the mahat-tattva and is the via medium between the pure spirit and material existence. It is the junction of matter and spirit wherefrom the false ego of the living entity is generated.

All living entities are differentiated parts and parcels of the Personality of Godhead. Under the pressure of false ego, the conditioned souls, although parts and parcels of the Supreme Personality of Godhead, claim to be the enjoyers of material nature. The false ego is the binding force of material existence. The Lord again and again gives a chance to the bewildered conditioned souls to get free from this false ego, and that is why the material creation takes place at intervals. He gives the conditioned souls all facilities for rectifying the activities of the false ego, but He does not interfere with their small independence.

The Lord is the cause of the mahat-tattva, and the mahat-tattva is then the cause of creation itself. From the mahat-tattva twenty-four divisions of material ingredients have sprung, namely the five instruments of perception, the five working organs, the five sense objects, the five material elements, the mind, intelligence, and false ego. Consciousness is then injected into the material matrix, forming the origins of the development of the limitless living entities.

![рис-008.jpg|430](<../attachments/ten-teachings-of-bhakti.-a-vaishnava-%D1%81atechism--%D1%80%D0%B8%D1%81-008--101a62f5e1fcf899.jpg>)

## The Gunas

In Samkhya philosophy, a guna is one of three ‘tendencies or qualities.’ Nature or Prakriti consists of these three constituent forces – the three ‘modes of nature.’ These account for the diversified energies and objects of experience. Each of the three constituents has its characteristic function and the terms are used in categorizing behaviour and natural phenomena. The term guna means ‘rope’ and the understanding is that nature, made of these three ropes, has firmly tied down the living being.

Sattva-guna is the quality of illumination, balance, harmony, goodness, purity, universalizing, holistic, positive, peaceful, virtuous.

Raja-guna is the quality of passion, activity, being driven, moving, dynamic.

Tama-guna is the quality of dullness, inactivity, apathy, inertia, or lethargy.

Action that is virtuous, thought through, free from attachment, and without craving for results is considered Sattvic. Action that is driven purely by craving for pleasure, selfishness and much effort is Rajasic. Action that is undertaken because of delusion, disregarding consequences, without considering loss or injury to others or the self is Tamasik.

The gunas are the actual substances or ingredients of which prakriti is constituted. They make up the whole world, and the world evolved out of prakriti.

Sattva is derived from the word ‘sat’ or ‘that which is real or existing.’ The force that illuminates and reveals all manifestations. It manifests as light, balance, and harmony.

Rajas – derived from ‘ranj’ or ‘to be clouded, affected or moved.’ It is the force that moves the other two constituents. It is the activating and exciting potency without which the other constituents could not manifest their inherent qualities. Its function is to move things, overcome resistance, to do work. It is responsible for all motion and change that goes on throughout nature.

Tamas – meaning ‘darkness’ or ‘illusion,’ tamas guna is the quality in the universe that obscures higher consciousness and the unity of life. It represents inertia, laziness, mental dullness, procrastination, and greed.

The three gunas are described in the Bhagavad-gita, summarised as follows:

‘Nature consists of three modes – goodness (sattva), passion (rajas), and ignorance (tamas). When the living entity contacts nature, he becomes conditioned by these modes.

Goodness, being purer than the others, is illuminating, and it frees one from sinful reactions. Those situated in that mode become conditioned by a sense of happiness and knowledge. Passion is born of unlimited desires and longings, and because of this the living entity is bound to material fruitive actions.

Darkness (ignorance) is the delusion of all living entities. The results are madness, indolence, and sleep, which bind the soul. The mode of goodness conditions one to happiness; passion conditions one to fruitive action; and ignorance, covering one’s knowledge, binds one to madness.

The three modes of nature ‘compete.’

Sometimes the mode of goodness becomes prominent, defeating the modes of passion and ignorance. Sometimes passion defeats goodness and ignorance, and at other times ignorance defeats goodness and passion. In this way there is always competition for supremacy.’

When one is predominated by one of the modes, one’s destination after this current life is fixed accordingly:

‘Those situated in goodness gradually go upward to the higher planets; those in the mode of passion live on the earthly planets; and those in the abominable mode of ignorance go down to the hellish worlds.’

Prakriti means matter in a state in which none of the three qualities predominate over the others. It is matter in a state of equilibrium.

Matter exists in two conditions:

Suksma – the subtle or latent, undifferentiated condition – the ‘cause.’

Sthula – the differentiated condition. A congeries (‘a disorderly collection, a jumble’) of form – the ‘effect.’

## Evolution and the Gunas

Definition: Evolution is any process of formation, or growth, or development. An ‘evolute’ is that which the process of evolution produces. Both words come from the Latin evolutus – ‘rolled out.’

Matter, the material world, or the material nature, along with all its modifications, is known as prakriti. The word means ‘that which gives rise to various modifications.’ It refers to the primordial cosmic matter in which a certain type of evolution takes place.

Prakriti is also known as Maya, as stated in the Svetasvatara Upanishad. It is called Maya in the sense that it serves as an instrument for the creation of variegated objects in the universe.

Another word commonly used is avidya. This word means ‘that which stands as an obstruction for true knowledge of reality.’ The physical body and the sense organs are products of prakriti, and these prevent the soul from having true knowledge due to the soul’s attachment to the physical body as a vehicle for sensual pleasures.

Prakriti, or material nature, is a non-sentient substance characterised by three fundamental attributes known as sattva, rajas and tamas. Sattva stands for whatever is fine or light and causes happiness and knowledge. Rajas represents whatever is active and is the cause of suffering, attachment, and passion. Tamas is whatever is heavy and is responsible for lethargy and ignorance. These are super-normal qualities and are present in all the evolutes of prakriti, including the gross physical objects of the universe.

They are found in varying proportions, depending upon the nature of the objects. If an individual has a pleasant and friendly disposition, the quality of sattva is considered to dominate his mind. If he is too passionate or angry, it is due to the predominance of rajas. If a person is lethargic, dull, and ignorant, it is taken that tamas is predominant in him.

The three qualities are known as gunas or ‘ropes.’ Gunas remain in equilibrium in the causal state of prakriti. In the state of evolution, variation takes place in the gunas by way of one of them being dominant, while the others remain subdued. Evolution is the disturbed state of the gunas. When the equilibrium of the gunas is disturbed, evolution of prakriti begins. The process is initiated by the will of the Parameshvara, or God.

The very first evolute is known as mahat-tattva and from this evolves ahamkara or ego. Both, being products of prakriti, consist of the three gunas. The ahamkara-tattva assumes three different forms based on the three gunas: sattvika-ahamkara, rajasa-ahamkara, and tamasa-ahamkara. From this stage the process of further evolution takes place on two different lines. From the sattvika-ahamkara in which the sattva element is predominant, the sense organs and the mind evolve. From the tamasa-ahamkara evolve the five tanmatras or subtle elements – shabda or sound, sparsha or touch, rupa or colour, rasa or taste, and gandha or odour, in successive order. From each of these subtle elements come the gross elements – akash or space, vayu or air, tejas or fire, jala or water, and prithvi or earth.

## Creation

Creation is known as Sristhi or a ‘projection.’ When a spider spins a web, it creates it as a ‘projection’ from its own body. In this way the universe is ‘projected.’ There are three kinds of creation.

General Creation – Samasthi-Sristhi – the evolution of the mahat-tattva or the ingredients of the created world.

Specific Creation – Vyasthi-Sristhi – the creation of the diverse universe.

Daily Creation – Nitya-Sristhi - according to the effects of karma all jivas attain, grow, and then lose material bodies, one after the other.

The entire material world is created to supply bodies to the jivas so they may achieve their goals. Jivas include Sura – gods and angels, Nara – humans, Tiryak – animals, Sthavara – vegetables and minerals.

Just as there are three causes for the creation of a pot, so there are three causes for the creation of the world. The three causes are:

upadana-karana- the material cause (The clay)

nimitta-karana efficient cause (The potter’s wheel, kiln, the potter’s hands) sukshma-karana – remote or subtle cause (the potter’s desire)

Maya, the external energy of the Supreme Personality of Godhead, is divided into two parts. Maya is both the cause of the cosmic manifestation and the agent who supplies its ingredients. As the cause of the cosmic manifestation she is known as Maya, and as the agent supplying the ingredients of the cosmic manifestation she is known as Pradhana. That the three causes of creation are thus:upadana-karana – material cause - Pradhana nimitta-karana – efficient cause - Maya sukshma-karana – ultimate cause - The Supreme Purusha

**“The Purusha, the enjoyer, is without transformation, whereas material nature is always subject to transformation. Material nature is one, but because of the interaction of the three qualities (gunas), it can produce the total energy and the wonderful cosmic manifestation. Such transformations divide material nature into two features, namely the efficient and material causes.”**

**Sri Caitanya Caritamrta 1.6.14-15**

## The Theory of Time (Kala)

Kala or Time is another non-sentient metaphysical entity. It is an independent and real substance, having the same status as prakriti. It is not part of prakriti but exists along with it. The concept of passage of time is brought about by changes within the prakriti and its evolutes. There are immense periods of time within the cosmic manifestation:

**The Cycle of Cosmic Time**

There are four epochs of cosmic time, each one less than the preceding. They are all known as yugas or ‘ages.’ They revolve in a constant cycle, like seasons.

Satya Yuga – 1,728,000 years

Treta Yuga – 1,296,000 years

Dwapara Yuga – 864,000 years Kali Yuga – 432,000 years

The cycle of four yugas together make up one Maha Yuga lasting 4,320,000 years.

71 of these Maha Yugas is a Manvantara, the duration of the life of one Manu.

14 Manvantaras, plus the sandhis, or ‘joins,’ is 1,000 Maha Yugas and is known as a Brahma Kalpa, or one day of Brahma.

A Maha Kalpa is 360 x 100 days of Brahma or the entire life of Brahma, known as a Maha Kalpa and is equivalent to 311,040,000,000,000 years – or 311 trillion, 40 billion years.

When this period has elapsed, there is a devastation of the universe.

## The avatars

**Although now a popular word, the Sanskrit term avatar has a specific original meaning. This chapter presents the reasons for the descent of the divine incarnations, or avatars, and their different forms and characteristics.**

The concept of avatar is found in the earliest of Vedic texts. In the Purusha Sukta the hymn states: ajayamano bahuda vijayate – ‘although unborn he is born many times.’ The Satapatha Brahmana mentions the incarnation of Vishnu as a fish (Matsya) and the Taittiriya Aranyaka mentions the avatar of Vishnu as a tortoise (Kurma). The boar incarnation is described in the Taittiriya Brahmana. The Rig Veda also mentions Vamana, otherwise known as Trivikrama.

The term avatar literally means ‘coming down’ or avatarana. With reference to God it means the ‘descent’ of the Supreme Being from his divine abode to a lower level. It also means that he comes in different forms.

If we ask why the supreme would want to do this it can only be due to mercy. He is avapta-samasta-kama or ‘all his desires are already fulfilled.’ It may be argued that his descent is for the protection of the world, but he is satya-sankalpa or ‘one whose will is paramount,’ and he could achieve his wishes simply through his will. Nevertheless, his appearance in this world accomplishes a multitude of objects.

The avatar forms are real and non-material. They are made of spiritual substance. Although the English translation of avatar is ‘incarnation,’ the word incarnation comes from the Latin incarna, or ‘into the blood’ which is troublesome. Whilst it is true that the jiva-atma repeatedly incarnates and reincarnates, and surely comes again and again ‘into the flesh,’ it is a fact that Param-atma, or God himself, does not.

They do not appear and disappear according to karma. They come only by their own will, but in response to the needs of establishing dharma. Their purpose is helping the righteous (sadhus) and vanquishing the evil (duskritina). Along with these two purposes he also reveals his divine form and instructions, thus giving inspiration and purpose to those who seek him. In the beginning Krishna incarnates himself beyond the world as the purusha-avatars, the ‘Three Vishnus’ – Maha-vishnu, Garbhadaka-shayi Vishnu and Kshiradaka-shayi Vishnu. It is said that ‘He who knows these three becomes liberated from the clutches of maya.’ – from Satvata Tantra.

When the flame of one candle is expanded to light another candle, the second candle burns as brightly, still one candle remains the original.

## Six types of avatars

There are six types of avatars of Krishna:

Purusha-avatars – the incarnations of Vishnu such as Karanadaka-shayi Vishnu (Mahavishnu) Garbhodaka-shayi Vishnu and Kshirodaka-shayi Vishnu

Lila-avatars – the incarnations for performance of pastimes. No-one can count the pastime incarnations of Krishna but some of them are Matsya, Kurma, Rama, Nrsimha, Vamana and Varaha.

Guna-avatars - the incarnations for controlling the gunas (modes of nature) Vishnu, Brahma, Shiva.

Manvantara-avatars – the Manus are the progenitors of humanity. There are fourteen of them in one day of Brahma. Each one lives for a duration of 71 x 4,320,000 years.

Yuga-avatars - the incarnations for different millenniums (white, red, blue, and black, but sometimes, as in this Kali Yuga, yellow). Each one teaches the religion for the age.

Shakti-avesh-avatars - the incarnations which are empowered jivas. There are two forms, the forms of divine absorption, or bhagavad-avesh, such as Kapila and Rsabha; and the divinely empowered forms, or shakti-avesh, such as Sesha, Ananta, Brahma, the Kumars, Narada, Vyasa, Prithu and Parashurama.

“Just as hundreds and thousands of small ponds issue from great reservoirs of water, innumerable incarnations flow from the Sri Hari, the reservoir of all power.”

## Purusha Avatars

Maha-Vishnu is the creator of the total material energy.

Garbhodaka-shayi Vishnu is situated within each universe.

Kshirodaka-shayi Vishnu lives in the heart of every living being.

Mahavishnu generates countless universes from the pores of his skin, all while sleeping. He then expands himself into each universe which fills half with water and lies down as Garbhodaka-shayi Vishnu. The souls issue from the navel of the Lord as a lotus bud, and after growing into a flower, Brahma is born.

This second Purusha Avatar is also known as Hiranyagarbha or Antaryami and is glorified by the Vedic hymn known as Purusha Sukta – Rig Veda Samhita 10.90.

The third expansion is known as Kshirodaka-shayi Vishnu and is lying within the ocean of milk within each universe. He is the Super Soul within every living being. He is the maintainer and master of the universe.

## Lila Avatars

There are countless lila avatars. No-one can count the pastime incarnations of Krishna but some of them are Matsya, Kurma, Rama, Nrsimha, Vamana and Varaha. In the Srimad Bhagavatam a list of twenty-six prominent avatars is given. In popular religion, ten of these have been chosen to be the ‘Dash Avatar’ or ‘Ten Incarnations.’

Before he comes to this world, Krishna sends his devotees ahead of him to become father, mother, and others. His pastimes continue until the age of kishor or pre-youth and he remains at that age.

The same pastime continues eternally but is seen by us in an order. Just as the sun shines always somewhere at midday, or the zodiac moves, so within Krishna’s 125 years the pastimes unfold – in every universe.

## Guna Avatars

Garbhodaka-shayi Vishnu holds the accumulated rebellious jivas within his abdomen and they come out in the form of a lotus bud. The flower grows and atop the lotus the god Brahma is born. After meditation, during which he discovers his origin, Brahma is empowered to create. When he asks his four sons to join him in populating the universe they refuse, protesting that they wish to remain celibates. Brahma’s anger bursts from his forehead in the form of Shiva.

‘As the sun manifests his brilliance in a gem so the Lord empowers Brahma and expands himself to become Shiva, who oversee creation and dissolution, respectively. Milk is transformed into yogurt when it associates with a yogurt culture, thus yogurt is nothing but milk, but still it is not milk.’ So, Shiva is not Vishnu, but his own tattva.’

Vishnu oversees the mode of goodness and the maintenance of the universe, Brahma the mode of passion and creation and Shiva the mode of ignorance and destruction.

## Manvantara Avatars

Known as the Manus, these are the primordial human kings and progenitors of the universe whose lives span thousands of years each. There is always a Manu, from the dawn of creation to the annihilation. ‘In one day of Brahma there are fourteen changes of Manu, and for his entire life – 311 trillion years - there are 504,000 Manus.’

## Yuga Avatars

As humans become less qualified with the passing of the yugas, the process of yoga becomes proportionately accessible. The four processes are meditation, fire sacrifices, temple worship and congregational singing of mantras. Although all of these can be executed with spiritual profit in any of the yugas, and are often combined, the recommended process for each yuga is the one that is most spiritually beneficial for that age.

Satya – Yoga process is meditation. The avatar is white, four arms, matted hair, wears a tree bark, sacred thread, rudraksha mala and carries a rod, waterpot and black antelope skin. He was a brahmacari and taught religion and meditation.

Treta – Yoga process is agnihotra or fire sacrifice. The avatar is reddish, four arms, golden hair. His form manifested Vedic knowledge. He holds the symbols of a sacrificial spoon and ladle and so on.

Dvapara – Yoga process is temple worship. The avatar is blackish, two arms, dressed in yellow, decorated with kaustubha jewel and marks of srivasta.

Kali – Yoga process is chanting the holy names. The avatar is yellowish, two arms, propagates the yuga dharma of the chanting of the names of Krishna.

“Whatever result was obtained in Satya Yuga by meditating on Vishnu, in Treta Yuga by performing sacrifices and in Dvapara Yuga by serving the lord’s feet, can also be obtained in Kali Yuga simply by Hari Kirtan.” – SB 12.3.52

## Shakti-avesh Avatars

Avesh means ‘immersed’ and shakti means ‘power’ so these avatars are extraordinary humans who are empowered for specific tasks. Vyasa, for instance, is known as ‘the literary incarnation of God’ because he was empowered to compile the Vedic literature.

![рис-009.jpg|448](<../attachments/ten-teachings-of-bhakti.-a-vaishnava-%D1%81atechism--%D1%80%D0%B8%D1%81-009--1fe6ec3473850551.jpg>)

## The Passage of Universal Time

The chart illustrates the unlimited number of expansions and avatars, and their profusion of forms, potencies, and activities. Finally, after God comes many times to lift the fallen jivas, the entire universe is dissolved through the agency of Kala, or supreme time. As autumn turns to winter, so does the cosmic wheel move the entire universe through a cycle of seasons. Time moves on, and the universe is again manifest, and with it all those jivas who did not complete their spiritual journey the last time around.

The end of the universe is known as pralaya. There are partial devastations and complete annihilations at regular intervals, but the universal manifestation takes place again, as spring follows winter. When the universe changes from sthula back to sukshma it is said to be in Pralaya. There are four kinds of Pralaya:

Naimittika Pralaya – the universe dissolves into its constituent elements at the end of Brahma’s day, after one Brahma Kalpa.

Prakrita Pralaya – the universe dissolves at the end of Brahma’s life, after one Maha-Kalpa.

Annihilation also takes place in the life of the individual jiva, as cells of the body are destroyed each day, and finally, when the entire body perishes, marking the end of another life. Then, when we finally leave this world of matter, having attained our position in the divine abode, our connection with the material energy is destroyed.

Nitya Pralaya – every moment, bodies undergo change into a perishable condition and degenerate after death.Atyantika Pralaya – when the connection between the jiva and matter is severed or dissolved.

**After many millions of years, when Lord BrahmР”Рѓ comes to the end of his life, the annihilation of the cosmic manifestation takes place. At that time, the five elements—namely earth, water, fire, air, and ether—enter into the mahat-tattva. The mahat-tattva then enters, by the force of time, into the nonmanifested total material energy, the total material energy enters into the energetic pradhР”Рѓna, and the pradhР”Рѓna enters into You. Therefore, after the annihilation of the whole cosmic manifestation, You alone remain with Your transcendental name, form, qualities and paraphernalia.**

**Krishna Book, from Srimad Bhagavatam 10.3.25**

**"O son of Kuntī, when the kalpa, or the duration of the life of Brahmā, is ended, then all the created manifestations enter into My prakṛti, or energy, and again, when I desire, the same creation takes place by My personal energy."**

**Srimad Bhagavatam 2.6.39**

At the time of annihilation, the five gross elements—earth, water, fire, air and ether—enter into the mind, intelligence and false ego (ahaṅkāra), and the entire cosmic manifestation enters into the spiritual energy of the Supreme Personality of Godhead, who alone remains as the origin of everything. The Lord is therefore known as Śeṣa-nāga, as Ādi-puruṣa and by many other names.

## The ocean of rasa

**Krishna is a complete person. The fact that he is the supreme Brahman and, at the same time, a person, is not a contradiction. As stated in the Upanishads, the supreme is fully conscious, completely independent, and can reveal himself out of his own volition. This chapter concerns the intrinsic will of the supreme, the relationship each jiva can have with him, and the joy experienced by the bhakti-yogi. The chapter begins with a declaration that God is not impersonal or inert, but actively reveals himself in the life of a progressive soul.**

*nāyanātmā pravacanena labhyo/ na medhayā na bahunā śrutena \|*  
*yamevaiṣa vṛṇute tena labhyas/tasyaiṣa ātmā vivṛṇute tanūm̐ svām*

**The Self is not to be attained by a study of the Vedas, nor by intelligence, nor by much hearing, but the Self can be attained, only by him who seeks to know it. To him, the Self reveals its true nature.**

**Katha Upanishad 1.2.23 and Mundaka Upanishad 3.2.3**

The supreme self, or Param Atma, is vibhu, ‘the greatest,’ and the individual self, or jiva atma is anu, ‘the smallest.’ As the spark is to the fire, or the gold ring is to the gold mine, the jiva shares in the qualities of the supreme. It is one in quality but not in quantity. Volition experienced in the soul is an innate quality of freewill, and it is similarly there in the supreme to an unlimited degree.

In the Taittiriya Upanishad there is a description of levels of happiness, ascending from satisfaction, contentment, through to the highest earthly happiness, to heavenly delight and on up to divine bliss. However, by those great sages and yogis who have ascended to these heights, even the ananda of Brahman is contrasted unfavourably with the full sat-chit-ananda experience of the loving relationship with God. ‘As a drop of water compares to the entire ocean,’ they say, ‘so does brahmananda compare to love of God.’

The soul is conscious because God is conscious, and just as the soul, conditioned or liberated, experiences joy and love from a relationship, so does God. In the Upanishads it is described that the sweet taste of this relationship – the word used is rasa - is the very identity of the supreme person:

*raso vai sah rasam hy evayam labdhvanandi bhavati*

**He Himself is rasa, and certainly one who achieves this rasa becomes anandi, filled with bliss.**

**Taittiriya Upanishad 2.7.1**

In his book Laghu-Bhagavatamrita, the mediaeval philosopher Rupa Goswami (1489-1564) sets out how the relative powers of all the various incarnations can be analysed with respect to Krishna. He writes that just as the chrysoberyl gemstone can change colour, so Bhagavan himself can appear differently in varying circumstances. But we should consider in which way these aspects of Bhagavan are ‘the same’ and ‘different.’

A candle flame and a bonfire are both equal in one sense. They both manifest heat and light, and they both have burning capacity. Both sizes of flame, for instance, could start a fire that would burn down an entire city. But in the depths of winter, only a big fire can provide the warmth that we need to survive. The flames are equal if considered according their inherent qualities such as heat and light, but not if we analyse their differences and uses.

Jiva Goswami (1513-1598) follows on with an analysis of why Krishna is the same yet different from his many incarnations. He explains that God’s existential position manifests in two ways: his supreme sovereignty or aishvarya, and his supreme sweetness or madhurya. The first is manifest through his lordship and the second through his character, qualities, form, age, and lila or ‘play.’ While our measuring the sovereignty of God calls for us to apply certain criteria, assessing him as an all-attractive being, the object of love, and reciprocator of the soul’s love, require other criteria. Ultimately, he says, we must conclude that our love of God, and his love for us, is more important than his being the God of power.

Rupa Goswami lists the four ways in which Krishna is excellent and unique among all forms of Godhead. They are his lila, his flute, his form, and his devotees. All of these are madhurya or sweet, and it is in this sweetness that Bhagavan Krishna is distinguished from Bhagavan Narayan. He lists these distinguishing features as:

1\. Lila madhurya - (the ‘play’ of Krishna)

2\. Venu madhurya – (the flute)

3\. Rupa Madhurya – (the form)

4\. Prema madhurya – (the love Krishna has for his devotees)

Rupa Goswami concludes that Krishna, being the possessor of all these features, is the fullest manifestation of God, and the supreme origin of all forms of Godhead. In these features, Krishna is manifest fully in Dwaraka, yet fuller in Mathura, and fullest in Vrindavan.

That Krishna is none other than Narayana is accepted by all Vaishnava schools, and his superiority is a central element of Shrimad Bhagavatam and the theology of the Chaitanya sampradaya. The Bhagavatam describes the incarnations flowing from Krishna ‘as thousands of rivers flow from a lake.’

Rupa Goswami invokes the word rasa to explain why our estimation of Krishna must ultimately pass beyond logic and theology. The word rasa means the sweet taste of fruit. Just as the taste of fruit improves when it is ripe and when the skin, seeds or stones are removed, so true rasa is when love is ripe and when there is no longer anything mundane to impede the flavour. Rasa is regularly translated as ‘taste, flavour or relish.’ The old, mediaeval English word for the taste of ripe fruit is also used in translation: ‘mellow.’

To experience rasa therefore means the direct experience of Krishna, the highest experience of love and being loved, and the height of ecstasy for the liberated soul. Because the Shrimad Bhagavatam concerns topics of rasa the word is found throughout the text. In the beginning of the entire 18,000 verses, the speaker implores:

*nigama-kalpa-taror galitaṁ phalaṁ śuka-mukhād amṛta-drava-saṁyutam*  
*pibata bhāgavataṁ rasam ālayam muhur aho rasikā bhuvi bhāvukāḥ*

**O expert and thoughtful men, relish Shrimad Bhagavatam, the mature fruit of the desire tree of Vedic literatures. It emanated from the lips of Shri Shukadeva Goswami. Therefore, this fruit has become even more tasteful, although its nectarean juice was already relishable for all, including liberated souls.**

**Shrimad Bhagavatam 1.1.3**

To which in response, the forest sages declare:

*vayaṁ tu na vitṛpyāma uttama-śloka-vikrame*  
*yac-chṛṇvatāṁ rasa-jñānāṁ svādu svādu pade pade*

**We never tire of hearing the transcendental pastimes of the Personality of Godhead, who is glorified by hymns and prayers. Those who have developed a taste for transcendental relationships with Him relish hearing of His pastimes at every moment.**

**Shrimad Bhagavatam 1.1.19**

In the final chapter, the Shrimad Bhagavatam declares that ‘one who is satiated with its ambrosial rasa will not love anything else.’ Krishna is said to ‘possess all rasas,’ and is also called ‘he who bestows rasa,’ while his devotees are described as ‘those who know rasa,’ their minds being ‘like bees longing for the nectar of the rasa of Brahman,’ or ‘immersed in the rasa of the honey of the lords blessed lotus feet.’

Rupa Goswami defines the word rasa:

*vyatitya bhavana-vartma yas camatkara-bhara-bhuh*  
*hrdi sattvojjvale badham svadate sa raso matah*

**That which is beyond imagination, heavy with wonder and relished in the heart shining with goodness — such is known as rasa.**

**Sri Bhakti-rasamrta-sindhu: Daksina-vibhaga, 5.132**

Love is not merely an emotional condition brought about by contact with the objects of the senses, by turns exhilarating then disappointing; it is an intrinsic element of being a person. In conditioned life our love is turned towards the mundane and temporary, but on the path to liberation it is turned towards the transcendent. Full liberation is love of God expressed through divine service and felt as an overwhelming feeling of being loved coupled with an insatiable desire to be in the presence of the beloved. Victorian writer Bhaktivinode Thakur gives an example in his book Jaiva Dharma:

**“When water freezes to ice it loses its inherent nature of liquidity, and solidity becomes its assumed nature. This is a temporary condition brought about by an agent – low temperature - acting as a catalyst. When this agent is removed, the temporary state also disappears. Even if a substance is temporarily apparently transformed, its dharma remains its intrinsic nature. Its own dharma is latent within the seemingly changed substance, and by the amelioration of the alien circumstances, its dharma will reassert itself. The inherent nature of a substance – or the soul - is its nitya dharma, eternal religion –and the assumed nature of a substance is its temporary religion – its naimittika dharma.”**

True dharma is therefore to act in such a way as to understand, serve and love God. That is its destination and its greatest reward. All religions have a conception of the greatness of God. The dictionary definition of ‘God’ indicates supremacy: ‘The one Supreme Being, the creator and ruler of the universe.’ Such a being of absolute power and control, both transcendent and immanent, is unarguably great, especially when compared to a human. The relationship of the soul to such an entity is therefore at first one of awe and reverence – a sense of gratitude and humility mixed with a tinge of fear. In the beginning of the spiritual path such feelings are beneficial since the deep-rooted problem of all living beings is their mistaken self-conception as being the controller and enjoyer. This mistaken conception can only be rectified by a radical shift of perspective, and an almighty creator who is to be feared can bring that about.

It is natural, then, that the concept of God the Almighty comes first, then God the loving Father, and then God the Son or Holy Spirit as a compassionate extension of God’s almighty power. But the Vedas also describe many other ways of relating to God. There are many places where God is described as Mother, for instance, and many liberated souls have experienced God not as ‘God the Son’ but as ‘God, my son.’ Others have experienced God as the long-lost friend or even the husband. All of these are varieties of loving relationship, and all of them are possible with God.

The supreme object of the soul’s loving feelings is the youthful Krishna. When Chaitanya Mahaprabhu was teaching one of his first followers, Sanatana Goswami, in Varanasi, he told him that out of all forms of God, the form of Krishna as a youth was the supreme:

*Syamam eva param rupam puri madhu-puri vara*  
*Vayah kaisorakam dhyeyam adya eva paro rasah*

**The form of Shyamasundara is the supreme form, the city of Mathura is the supreme abode, Lord Krishna’s fresh youth should always be meditated upon, and the mellow of conjugal love is the supreme rasa.**

Krishna’s beauty exceeds even that of Vishnu, explained Chaitanya, and he is the centre of countless wonderful lilas, ‘play’ or ‘pastimes’ so that the devotees are captivated by his narrations and depictions:

“Krishna’s form as a human being is the supreme transcendental form. In this form he is a cowherd boy who carries a flute in his hand and is an expert dancer. Even a fractional understanding of his beauty can merge the universe in an ocean of love. Krishna’s body is the ornament of his ornaments. Krishna wears a pearl necklace that appears like a chain of white ducks around his neck. The peacock feather in his hair appears like a rainbow and his yellow garments appear like lightning in the sky. Krishna appears like a newly risen cloud, and the gopis appear like newly grown grains in the field. Constant rains of nectarean pastimes fall upon these newly grown grains, and it seems as if the cowherd maidens are receiving beams of life from Krishna, exactly as grains receive life from the rains.”

Krishna was once invited by King Kamsa to participate in a wrestling match, in the nearby city of Mathura. Those who only knew Krishna and his brother Balarama as two young boys were very fearful for him. They thought he would be no match for the experienced fighters.

Meanwhile King Kamsa, whose death at the hands of Krishna had been prophesied years earlier, saw him as death coming to visit him. Those who loved Krishna already saw him as an object of their eyes, and his real parents, who had not seen him at all for many years, simply saw him as their child. All the people gathered to watch the fight saw him in a uniquely different way. This is the nature of rasa.

“The various groups of people in the arena regarded Krishna in different ways when He entered it with His elder brother. The wrestlers saw Krishna as a lightning bolt, the men of Mathura as the best of males, the women as Cupid in person, the cowherd men as their relative, the impious rulers as a chastiser, His parents as their child, the King of the Bhojas as death, the unintelligent as the Supreme Lord’s universal form, the yogis as the Absolute Truth and the Vrishnis as their supreme worshipable Deity.”

The great Shrimad Bhagavatam commentator Srila Sridhara Swami (1378-1414) quotes the following verse, which explains the ten attitudes toward Krishna described here:

*raudro ’dbhutas ca srngaro / hasyam viro daya tatha bhayanakas ca bibhatsah / santah sa-prema-bhaktikah*

**“(There are ten different moods) fury (perceived by the wrestlers), wonder (by the men), conjugal attraction (the women), laughter (the cowherds), chivalry (the kings), mercy (His parents), terror King Kamsa), ghastliness (the unintelligent), peaceful neutrality (the yogis) and loving devotion (the members of the Vrishni family.”**

As Rupa Goswami elaborately explains in his textbook on rasa, the Bhakti-rasamrta-sindhu, (‘The Nectar Ocean of Devotion’) there are five main rasas — neutrality, servitude, friendship, parental love, and conjugal love — and seven secondary rasas — amazement, humour, chivalry, compassion, fury, fear, and dread. Thus, altogether there are twelve rasas, and the supreme object of them all is Sri Krishna Himself. Srila Prabhupada writes:

“As soon as one becomes a devotee of the Lord, he also has a direct relationship with the Lord. That is a very elaborate subject matter, but briefly it can be stated that a devotee is in a relationship with the Supreme Personality of Godhead in one of five different ways:

1\. One may be a devotee in a passive state; (Shanta-rasa)

2\. One may be a devotee in an active state; (Dasya-rasa)

3\. One may be a devotee as a friend; (Sakhya-rasa)

4\. One may be a devotee as a parent; (Vatsalya-rasa)

5\. One may be a devotee as a conjugal lover. (Madhurya-rasa)

Although one cannot become one of the eternal associates of Krishna such as Nanda Maharaja one may develop a parental relationship and act in that capacity. Whatever the particular rasa, the Lord fully reciprocates with His devotee, although this rasa can only be experienced when the devotee has not a tinge of attraction to the world of Maya. It is not available through a mental adjustment or by the power of the imagination.”

He continues: ‘Chaitanya Mahaprabhu taught Rupa Goswami for ten days in Prayag, an ancient town at the confluence of the Ganges and Yamuna rivers. The subject of the talks was the soul’s relationship with the supreme soul, or God. This relationship lies dormant within the heart and becomes awakened by the process of devotional service. When service becomes regular and is accompanied by hearing and chanting along with regulative principles it is called sadhana-bhakti. By regularly rendering devotional service in this way one gradually becomes attached to the supreme person, and when that attachment is intensified it becomes love of Godhead.’

During that conversation, Chaitanya explained that the gradual development of love is compared to different states of sugar: seed, cane, juice, molasses, brown sugar cake, sugar crystals, and lozenges. Each one is more refined and sweeter than the last.

The five direct relationships and seven indirect relationships with the Lord are established by the devotee’s attachment to Krishna. The five are permanently situated in the heart of the devotee, and the seven emotions appear suddenly under certain conditions.

Examples of devotees’ relationships are as follows:

Shanta Rasa – The Nine Yogendras (great sages) and the Four Kumaras (sons of Brahma).

Dasya Rasa – there are innumerable examples such as Garuda (Vishnu’s winged carrier) and Hanuman (the ape-man associate of Rama and Sita).

Sakhya Rasa – the cowherd friends Sridama and Sudama, and the warriors Bhima and Arjuna.

Vatsalya Rasa – Vasudeva and Devaki, the parents of Krishna.

Madhurya Rasa – Lakshmi Devi, consort of Narayana, the Queens in Dwaraka, wives of Krishna, the cowherd maidens or gopis in Vrindavan, girlfriends of child Krishna.

When devotees are in these rasas, they forget that Krishna is God.

A devotee in shanta rasa desires neither heaven nor liberation, he manifests the two transcendental qualities of detachment from all material desire and attachment to Krishna.

*Narayanah parah sarve na kutascana bibhyati*  
*Svargapavarga narakesv api tulyartha darsinah*

**Devotees solely engaged in the devotional service of the Supreme Personality of Godhead, Narayana, never fear any condition of life. For them the heavenly planets, liberation and the hellish planets are all the same, for such devotees are interested only in the service of the Lord.**

**Shrimad Bhagavatam 6.17.28**

These two qualities spread through the lives of all devotees in whatever rasa.However, in shanta rasa, though one’s constitutional position is realised, not even the smallest intimacy exists, rather knowledge of Brahman and Paramatma is prominent.

In dasya rasa he better understands the full opulence of the Supreme Person. Knowledge of God is revealed with awe and veneration. By rendering service, the devotee in dasya rasa gives constant happiness to the Lord. Therefore, dasya rasa is shanta rasa plus service.

In sakhya rasa the qualities of dasya rasa are mixed with the confidence of fraternity instead of awe and veneration – an intimacy devoid of the formality found in dasya rasa. Krishna sometimes serves the devotee in this rasa. In sakhya rasa Krishna is obliged to the devotees who think themselves equal to him. This rasa has three qualities: detachment, attachment, and service characterised by the confidence of fraternity.

In vatsalya rasa the qualities of the former three rasas are transformed into a form of service called palana, or maintenance, but due a greater sense of intimacy the devotee chastises and rebukes the Lord in an ordinary way. The devotee considers himself the Lord’s maintainer.

On the platform of madhurya rasa or conjugal love, attachment for Krishna, service to him, and the relaxed feelings of fraternity and the feelings of maintenance all increase in intimacy. The devotee offers his body in the service of the Lord.

Just as in the evolution of prakriti, the material energy, all the material qualities evolve one after another beginning from ether, with all five qualities found in earth, so all five feelings of the devotees are amalgamated in conjugal love.

But it is not that Krishna is only the object of rasa. Krishna is the subject of rasa also, for he experiences it unlimitedly. Krishna is known as rasika-sekhara, or the supreme enjoyer of rasa, and he is known as akhila-rasamrita-murti, the very embodiment of all the rasas. Not only does Krishna experience the highest bliss from his liberated souls in the spiritual world, he also enjoys rasa with those souls who are becoming purified. More than this, he is eager to enjoy his relationship with them.

Jiva Goswami raises a natural question on behalf of others: “If Krishna is Para Brahman, and naturally in a state of the highest bliss, never needing anything else to augment or increase this bliss, since there is nothing beyond him, how can he be ‘eager’ to enjoy rasa?”

In answer, he gives the example of a pot of milk, filled to the brim. Nothing can increase the milk within the pot because it is completely full. Yet when it is placed on the fire it can still boil over, since this is its natural tendency. Similarly, a health and happy baby, in need of nothing, bursts into spontaneous laughter as an expression of joy. So, Krishna’s desire for relishing new forms of rasa is not to be interpreted as a sign of imperfection. It is not the kind of desire that springs from any kind of want. It springs from fullness.

But why would Krishna require to experience joy from a relationship, why not simply experience the joy within himself, since he is atmarama, or ‘self-satisfied?’ Jiva replies that when a man whistles a tune, he can certainly enjoy himself, but when he plays a flute he can enjoy more. So, Krishna also enjoys more when relating to his souls, who are none other than his tatastha-shakti. Sri Krishna is thus the ocean of rasa, both in terms of the experiences the devotee may have while serving Krishna and in Krishna’s full and affectionate reciprocation with the devotee.

## The soul is part of god

**The jiva is an infinitesimal particle of consciousness. The Upanishads describe it as being one ten-thousandth the size of the tip of a hair. This chapter discusses how the soul is real and not a product of illusion, that there is no ‘unitary self’ that imagines itself as being many selves, and that individuality and personhood are non-reducible.**

**“If we divide the tip of a hair into a hundred parts and then take one of these parts and divide it again into a hundred parts, that ten-thousandth part particle is the dimension of the living entity.”**

**Svetasvatara Upanishad 5.9**

The jiva souls are like sparks coming from the flame of God. In the Brhad-aranyaka Upanishad (2.2.20 and 4.3.9) it is said:

*yathagneh ksudra visphulinga vyuccaranti evam evasmad atmanah sarvani bhutani vyuccaranti*

**"As tiny sparks fly from a fire, so all individual souls have come from the Supreme."**

The jiva is a part of Bhagavan’s tatastha-shakti, the marginal potency. It is described as tatastha because this term means ‘on the beach.’ Just as a creature walking along a beach can be alternately covered by, or uncovered from, the sea waves, so the jiva is in a marginal position and can be either covered by the material energy or uncovered from it.When the minute jiva is covered by the material energy it loses consciousness of its eternal nature and accepts a material identity defined by the limits and conditions of its assumed body and mind. These false identifications are known as ‘designations’ or upadhis. Souls in this state are known as conditioned souls. When all the false designations have been removed, the soul is known as liberated or free.

**“Although the jiva has the facility to live in either the spiritual or material world he suffers the threefold miseries because he is influenced by avidya (nescience) potency. This living entity exists in different forms in the material condition. He is proportionately freed from maya, to greater or lesser degrees.”**

**Vishnu Purana 6.7.62-63**

The jiva souls are numberless, but Bhagavan is one. The relationship between them is that the jiva is completely dependent for its existence on Bhagavan. It is never independent, at any time.

The jiva is simultaneously one with Bhagavan and yet apart. It is one with Bhagavan because it is spiritual in substance, eternal and self-luminous. Yet it cannot be completely one with Bhagavan because he is omniscient, and the jiva is not.

Sri Chaitanya told Rupa Goswami: “If we were one with God in all respects—if we were all-pervading and all-powerful—there would be no question of our being caged as we are in the material elements, forced to do battle against material nature. When we give up trying to be lord of the universe and submit ourselves as servants of the Supreme Lord, material nature begins to release us from the hard struggle for existence and we become happy.”

The jiva is different from Bhagavan yet cannot be completely different since Brahman is indivisible. Brahman is not depleted by the generation of souls, and therefore the existence of the independent yet spiritual souls point to their being simultaneously one and different from Bhagavan. This is only possible with the understanding of both the soul and matter being shaktis of Bhagavan.

Just as the conditioned soul has a distinct identity, so does the liberated soul. Liberation does not mean a merging with Bhagavan or any part of Brahman or Paramatma. Liberation means becoming free from the layers of material identification and their pernicious effect and being thence reinstated in the soul’s original position.

In the Bhagavad-gita Lord Krishna explains the permanent and lasting individuality of the soul in contra-distinction to the assumed and false individuality of the conditioned soul:

*na tv evaham jatu nasam/ na tvam neme janadhipah*  
*na caiva na bhavisyamah/ sarve vayam atah param*

**Never was there a time when I did not exist, nor you, nor all these kings; nor in the future shall any of us cease to be.**

**Bhagavad-gita 2.12**

Of special interest in this verse, so early in the Gita conversation, are the words: bhavisyamah— shall exist; sarve—all of us; vayam—we; atah param—hereafter. Krishna, who has just explained the foolishness of remaining enwrapped in material individuality, nevertheless declares that spiritual individuality persists atah param or ‘hereafter.’

His Divine Grace A. C. Bhaktivedanta Swami Prabhupada comments: “The same Vedic truth given to Arjuna is given to all persons in the world who pose themselves as very learned but factually have but a poor fund of knowledge. The Lord says clearly that He Himself, Arjuna, and all the kings who are assembled on the battlefield, are eternally individual beings and that the Lord is eternally the maintainer of the individual living entities both in their conditioned as well as in their liberated situations.

The Supreme Personality of Godhead is the supreme individual person, and Arjuna, the Lord's eternal associate, and all the kings assembled there are individual, eternal persons. It is not that they did not exist as individuals in the past, and it is not that they will not remain eternal persons. Their individuality existed in the past, and their individuality will continue in the future without interruption…

The…theory that after liberation the individual soul, separated by the covering of Maya or illusion, will merge into the impersonal Brahman and lose its individual existence is not supported herein by Lord Krishna, the supreme authority. Nor is the theory that we only think of individuality in the conditioned state supported herein. Krishna clearly says herein that in the future also the individuality of the Lord and others, as it is confirmed in the Upanishads, will continue eternally. This statement of Krishna is authoritative because Krishna cannot be subject to illusion. If individuality is not a fact, then Krishna would not have stressed it so much—even for the future.”

Writing in the 12th century, Ramanuja Acharya strongly condemns the idea that Krishna would teach that the soul loses its individuality. He says that when a man in a desert discovers that what he thought was water is nothing more than a mirage, an optical illusion, he stops seeking water in it. Similarly, if Krishna knew there was no ultimate individuality, he would have stopped teaching from that point on.

The Advaitins, those who believe in ultimate one-ness, have an answer. ‘Just as a piece of cloth, when burned to ash, continues to resemble a piece of cloth, so Krishna, even though being aware of one-ness is still aware of difference, and could still be speaking of material individuality.’ In his Gita Bhashya Ramanuja decries this as absurd:

“To imagine that Krishna, in knowledge of ‘the unitary self’ is still subject to the continuation of deceptive experiences would be in contradiction to scripture. It would also contradict his statement, only a few minutes later, that “I know, Arjuna, all beings of the past, present, and future but me no-one knows.”

Furthermore, says Ramanuja, if the perception of difference and distinction are said to persist even after the supposed ‘unitary self’ has been decisively understood, the question will arise – to whom will the Lord and the succession of teachers of the tradition impart the knowledge in accordance with their understanding? The question needs an answer. No-one who is not out of his senses would undertake to give instruction to his own reflection in a mirror, knowing that the reflection is non-different than him. He concludes by saying: ‘Enough of these unsound doctrines which have all been refuted!’

## The soul is covered by Maya

**This chapter concerns the bewildering power of Maya, how the soul forgets its spiritual identity, and of how it is covered by successive layers and entangled by karmic reactions. In the last chapter we heard how certain philosophers hold the individuality of the soul to be an illusion. They also declare that the apparent reality of Bhagavan himself, and all his avatars, is similarly the result of the bewilderment of Brahman. Because this is such a persistently popular notion, its logical errors must again be pointed out in some detail and discussed first.**

The first discussion is consequently that the illusory energy, being produced by Bhagavan, cannot itself bewilder Bhagavan. The second discussion concerns the covering and bewildering of the jiva by Maya.

## The argument of Absolute Monism (Advaita)

The proposal of the Advaita school is as follows: “Only Brahman is ultimately real. Both the jiva and Bhagavan are Maya’s imposition on Brahman.”

This is an illogical idea since an indivisible entity cannot be divided.

If Brahman is one homogeneous entity, it is all encompassing. Where is the place to divide it? In what matrix or holding space are all the parts of Brahman placed when separated? What instrument or energy can possibly divide it? How do you divide something which is infinite? Ramanuja Acharya lists seven responses to what he terms ‘the untenable arguments’ of Advaita. Together they are known as ‘The Seven Untenables.’

## The Seven ‘Untenables’

1\. The idea of avidya, or that Brahman becomes illusioned, is riddled with contradictions. For instance:

a) A flawless entity such as Brahman cannot become flawed.

b) If nothing but Brahman exists, what is Maya?

c) You have asserted that the variety of the world comes from absolute homogeneity. Even if variety is an illusion, still there must be a template for it, since variegation cannot come from homogeneity.

d) Why would Brahman become a suffering jiva? Why would Brahman make such a monumental blunder?

2\. Saying that it is ‘inexplicable’ is untenable.

3\. There is no pramana, evidence, for such a theory.

4\. The locus, or ‘sitting place’ of illusion can be neither Brahman nor jiva.

5\. It is unintelligible that illusion can obscure Brahman.

6\. That illusion can be removed merely by right knowledge is untenable.

7\. The very conception of the cessation of illusion is absurd.

## The Advaita Reply

1\. “Everything is an illusion”

2\. “The division, the divisor (that which divides), and the flaw are only illusions.”

The immediate response to the Advaita reply is as follows:

1\. You say that what divides Brahman are only illusions, but how does something unreal create a real effect?

2\. For instance, when you see a mirage, it is an illusion of water, but the mirage itself is not nothing – a mirage is something. It is a real mirage made up of light waves bent due to heat. The illusion is that the object in the mirage is close to you. But the mirage is real.

The followers of Advaita use two analogies to demonstrate that an illusion of flaw and division can affect a superior, unified, flawless object.

## Pratibimba-vada – The Analogy of Reflection.

They say that: ‘When many pots are filled each with water, a sun can be seen in each of many pots, but in truth there is only one sun.’ There is only a notion of numerous suns, but in truth there is only one sun with many reflections. Similarly, the jiva is the sun in the sky not the sun in the pot. ‘The sun reflects into many bodies of water but is not really divided or rippled.’

## Pariccheda-vada – The Analogy of Division.

They say that: ‘The space inside a pot and the space outside a pot are the same. Once the pot is broken, they will merge. Pots and walls seem to divide space, but space is not truly divided or limited by this.’

Replies to Pratibimba-vada – The Analogy of Reflection.

You say that Brahman is formless, but a formless entity cannot be reflected. You say that Brahman is everything, so what is the ‘water?’ And if Brahman has no object, who is there to look at the reflection?

Replies to Pariccheda-vada – The Analogy of Division.

This analogy is better than the previous because space is formless and indivisible like brahman, but two illogical propositions remain: Nothing besides Brahman exists, so what is the ‘pot?’ Brahman has no object, so who experiences the division? Finally, you cannot cut space, and a metaphor must agree with several points of similarity to be valid. The jiva is not a pot.

## Shastric Support for Monism

Since it is a fact that the jiva and Brahman are the same in spiritual quality, and it is also true that Brahman is ‘everything,’ there are many verses from shastra that appear to explain their nondifference in all ways. However, monists tend to cherry-pick shastric quotes and use them out of context to support their claims. For example, the Vedanta Sutra (3.2.19-20) explains the theory of reflection in its full context:

“The ‘reflection of the sun on water’ is a metaphor, not a literal statement. It illustrates that the Supreme Soul is great like the sun, and the individual soul is like the sun’s reflection - comparatively small and dependent. Thus, it establishes distinction between the two. This is the correct understanding.”

The Advaitin’s final recourse, after the sun and pot arguments, is to say: ‘Although it is apparently illogical, it only seems this way because you cannot understand it. Logic is your way of trying to understand it, but you must let go of your logic and just let God exist.’ The reply to this surprisingly unintelligible argument is: ‘Yes, God is trans-logical but what we can understand about God is not illogical. If, to understand your philosophy, I must reject logic, I would rather not, but thank you for this interesting discussion.’

A ‘modern monist,’ not caring to even refer to shastra to make an argument, may say: “Well, for God to be all powerful, he must also possess the ability to forget. If you say that he cannot forget, that will mean there is something he is incapable of, and by that statement you have incapacitated him.” But that makes no sense. You do not need to have the lack of something. Ignorance is merely the lack of knowledge, not a separate entity in itself. You do not have to possess nonexistence in order to fully exist. Non-existence is merely the lack of existence, and therefore nothing at all. Another weak argument.

## Maya cannot bewilder Bhagavan

The answers to many of these ‘slippery-soap’ arguments can be found in the writings of Srila Vyasa, the original author of the Vedanta Sutra. In his mystical meditation, in which Bhagavan and all his many potencies are revealed as persons, Srila Vyasa views Maya, a feminine energy or goddess, standing just at a little distance from Bhagavan, regarding him with awe and a little guilt. On his order she bewilders the jivas, who then behave in foolish or even evil ways, so she remains slightly embarrassed at her service. Writing in his Shrimad Bhagavatam Srila Vyasadeva explained that Maya bewilders the externally oriented soul but not the supreme soul Bhagavan, to whom she is compliant.

*tvam ādyaḥ puruṣaḥ sākṣād īśvaraḥ prakṛteḥ paraḥ*  
*māyāṁ vyudasya cic-chaktyā kaivalye sthita ātmani*

**You are the original Personality of Godhead who expands Himself all over the creations and is transcendental to material energy. You have cast away the effects of the material energy by dint of Your spiritual potency. You are always situated in eternal bliss and transcendental knowledge.**

**Shrimad Bhagavatam 1.7.23**

## Maya and ‘The Devil’

In much religious conversation over several centuries, many have thought that the soul commits bad deeds under the influence of a stronger, evil power. Since there is no ‘Devil’ in the Vedas, Maya the bewildering potency is sometimes mistakenly thought of as Satan, or a primeval, evil force. In this conception, it is therefore assumed that Maya must have a separate agenda from God.

The notion is that God wants to help us, but Maya wants to drag us down, therefore she is opposed to God. People think: “How can there be a world in which bad things happen, and in which God is good - at the same time? Either God does not care, or He is weak.” The first idea is of a capricious, whimsical Supreme, and the second a weak, helpless Supreme. Neither of these is attractive. This notion of a good God existing in the presence of evil is known as theodicy and it was a great theological problem for the early Church and remains so until the present day.

Other philosophers, who reject the idea of an opponent of God, think that nevertheless Maya can bewilder Bhagavan, causing God to fall under the illusion that he has become multiple individual selves. This is also problematic because it would make Maya greater or more powerful than God.

Although there are certainly bad things happening in the world, the world is not bad, and it is not malfunctioning. Everything is running just as it should be. The problem is our orientation, and hence our perspective.

In the height of his meditation, Srila Vyasa saw that Bhagavan had both the jiva and Maya as his manifested shaktis and that both were subordinate to him. He writes of himself in the form of a third-party narration:

*yayā sammohito jīva, ātmānaṁ tri-guṇātmakam*  
*paro ’pi manute ’narthaṁ, tat-kṛtaṁ cābhipadyate*

**He saw Maya mesmerizing individual living beings, who unfortunately forfeit their superior nature to make themselves a part of Maya due to their desire for her three mesmerizing qualities.**

**Shrimad Bhagavatam 1.7.5**

In the matter of attraction to the charms of the material world, the ‘blame’ does not rest with Maya. It is entirely the fault of the jiva for pursuing an attraction to the world of matter. Sometimes a person may criticise God for making it so hard to give up a bad habit. For instance, if someone is trying to break free from a bad habit, they may ask why God has made it so difficult – by creating poppies for instance, from which heroin can be made, or by creating tobacco. ‘Had God not created these things’ – he reasons – ‘I would not have become addicted. So, my addiction is God’s fault.’ However, the jiva’s own external focus is the source of its desire, so only the jiva is to blame for its entanglement in maya.

## The jiva becomes entangled

How the jiva becomes entangled is explained by Bhaktivinode Thakur, citing as evidence the Brihad-Aranyaka Upanishad 4.3.9:

*tasya va etasya purusasya dve eva sthane / bhavata idan ca paraloka-sthanan ca sandhyam trtiyam svapna-sthanam / tasmin sandhye sthane tisthann ete ubhe sthane pasyatidan ca paraloka-sthanan ca*

**A person has two places: the spiritual world and the place where the spiritual world meets another world. There is also a third place, a place of dreams. Standing between them, the soul sees on one side the spiritual world and on the other side the place of dreaming.**

‘In other words, the jiva is situated in a third position, which is a dreamlike condition (svapna-sthana) and is the juncture (tatastha) between the other two. Being situated at the place where the two worlds meet, he sees both the inert world (jada-jagat) and the spiritual world (cid-jagat).

**As a large fish in a river may go to one shore or the other, so a person may go to one world or another. He may go to a world where he is awake or may go to a world made of dreams.**

**Brihad-aranyaka Upanisad 4.3.18**

Just as a large fish in a river sometimes goes to the eastern bank and sometimes to the western bank, so the jiva, being situated in karana-jala (the water of cause that lies between the inert and conscious worlds), also gradually wanders to both banks, the place of dreaming and the place of wakefulness.

The jiva may develop a foothold on either side by contemplation. Just as silt may build up one side of the river, or water may erode and make the bank the riverbed, similarly the soul may move from one side to the other.

## The jiva swallowed by Maya

Bhaktivinode Thakur continues the discussion of the reasons for the jiva’s entanglement with an imagined dialogue in his book Jaiva Dharma:

Question: the jiva is of pure spiritual substance, why does he have to descend into this world?

Answer: Countless jivas emanate from Balarama, Sankarshana, and they eternally serve their object of worship. Jivas also emanate from Karana-dakashayi Vishnu. As the jivas from Karana Vishnu are the neighbours of Maya they witness her variegatedness; they are miniscule in size and situated at the margin, so their nature is to look at both the material and spiritual world.

Question: Why are some engrossed in Maya while others are elevated?

Answer: The jiva can use his minute free will. By misusing his independence, he becomes attracted to exploiting and enjoying material nature. The ‘five kinds of illusion’ then progressively cloud his pure spiritual consciousness.

## Five kinds of Illusion, Four Defects

Unless the jiva forgets its spiritual identity, it is impossible for it to attempt to enjoy matter. So, the first condition of material existence, the first stage in it becoming completely bewildered, is forgetfulness of its real identity. By forgetting its real identity, it simultaneously develops a fear of death. The false identification with material nature is the cause of a sense of false ownership of things. This becomes another feature of entrapment.

The Shrimad Bhagavatam and the Yoga Sutras of the sage Patanjali describe the ‘five kinds of nescience’ which cause an almost complete weakening of the soul’s ability to perceive reality. They are as follows:

1\) Moha - Self-deception into the illusory bodily concept.

2\) Andha Tamishra - The sense of death as the conclusion.

3\) Tamishra - Rebellious rejection of divine service.

4\) Maha Moha - False ownership and attachment to enjoyment.

5\) Tamas - Almost complete forgetfulness of one's real identity as a jiva.

Only a very faint glimmer of truth is available, so covered is the jiva. Like the occasional glimpse of a ray of sun on a dark and cloudy day, the jiva sees something from time to time and can begin, very gradually, to move towards the light. But the tendency towards the immediate sense of happiness provided by sense enjoyment is more readily available, and this fact deflects the jiva from the straight spiritual path.

The five kinds of nescience collectively cause pronounced defects in the way the jiva perceives and acts within the material world. These are often listed as follows:

Kara-napatava – Having imperfect means of gathering knowledge, to have imperfect senses.

Bhrama – The tendency to make mistakes or to accept false knowledge.

Pramada – To be in illusion.

Vipralipsa – To have a propensity to cheat others.

Note: Srila Prabhupada’s preaching presentation was that the jiva, originally with Krishna, falls into the material world due to a ‘desire to lord it over the material world.’ Originally, he presented it as ‘something like Milton’s Paradise Lost.’ John Milton was an English poet who published his epic poem Paradise Lost in 1667. The poem concerns the Biblical story of the Fall of Man, the temptation of Adam and Eve by Satan and their expulsion from the Garden of Eden.

On other occasions he presented that it was ‘impossible to trace out the origins of the jiva in the material world,’ and that the soul has been conditioned ‘since time immemorial.’

On at least one other occasion he presented that the jiva was originally ‘with Krishna’ but similar to how an embryo is with its mother. The embryo ‘knows’ its mother by dint of proximity but does not have a conscious relationship with her. This analogy suggests that the state of the soul being ‘with Krishna’ is different from that of being in one’s eternal lila with Krishna, a position from which, as he explained on other occasions, there is no falling down.

Thus, although there seems to be a slight variance in Srila Prabhupada’s presentations according to audiences and circumstances, the principle remains the same. The jiva uses its free will to move away from Godhead and becomes subsequently and progressively entangled in the material energy.

## Vidya and Avidya

Forgetfulness of the jiva’s eternal nature as a servant of Bhagavan is brought about by Maya, which has aspects or vrittis – Vidya and Avidya. The former makes Maya mercifully inclined toward the jiva, and the latter enables the giving of corrective punishment. Avidya has two vrittis – Avaranatmika, which prevents the jiva’s knowledge of its true nature, and Vikshepatmika, which gives rise to a wrong type of knowledge in it. Srila Prabhupada dubbed these two the ‘throwing and covering’ potencies.

Avidya then draws a two-fold curtain over the jiva. One is the linga-sharira or ‘subtle body’ (also known as the sukshma-sharira) and the other is the sthula-sharira or ‘gross body.’ The subtle body consists of mana (mind), buddhi (intelligence), and ahamkara (ego) but with this covering the jiva cannot act (karma) or enjoy (bhoga), so the sthula-sharira is imposed upon it, just according to its desires to enjoy and the deserves to be restricted. The gross body is composed of earth, air, fire, water, and ether or, in other words, biological and neurological combinations which, though intricate, only serve to intensify the material consciousness of the jiva.

## The Five Layers

According to different Vedic texts, the layers surrounding the jiva are counted and named in various ways. In the Taittiriya Upanishad the layers are described as pancha-kosha, the ‘five sheaths’ or ‘five layers.’

1\. Anna-maya kosha - The food sheath

2\. Prana-maya kosha - The vital energy or breath sheath

3\. Mano-maya kosha - The mind sheath

4\. Vijnana-maya kosha - The intellect sheath

5\. Ananda-maya kosha - The bliss sheath

The gross body or sthula sharira is made up of physical matter. It consists of Anna-maya kosha and Prana-maya kosha.

The subtle body or linga-sharira consists of Mano-maya kosha and Vijnana-maya kosha.

The causal body or karana sharira consists only of the Ananda-maya kosha.

The most external and visible layer is the physical body. Since this body is made of ingredients provided by eating food it is ‘composed of food’ and is described as ‘the food layer’ or anna-maya kosha.

Within this first layer, and more subtle, is the prana-maya kosha or ‘air layer.’ The vital life force is known as prana and in the Vedic medical science Ayurveda is linked to the vata dosha, or ‘the subtle wind,’ Prana moves in circles and is also referred to as ‘circles of air.’ The anna-maya kosha is an effect of the prana-maya kosha. The anna-maya kosha gets life by the prana entering it and enabling all kinds of action. Prana plus the five organs of action constitute the prana-maya kosha. In nature prana is one but is sub-divided into five according to its location within the anna-maya kosha and the bodily function it controls:

• Udana – head and throat region – speech and music

• Prana – chest region – primary prana, eating and drinking

• Samana – stomach region – digestion and peristalsis

• Apana – intestinal region – excretion, ejaculation, urination

• Vyana – moves through entire body – blood, lymph, and nervous systems, working senses

Within the prana-maya kosha is the even more subtle mano-maya kosha or ‘mental layer.’ This layer is made of the mind, which permeates the prana-maya kosha. Within the mental layer is the layer of vijnana, the ‘wisdom layer,’ the vijnana-maya kosha. Vijnana means ‘realised knowledge’ or the power of accurate discrimination, referring to the intrinsic ability to discriminate between reality and illusion. The Taittiriya Upanishad explains that ‘Those who attain wisdom are freed from sin and all their selfless desires are granted.’ The vijnana-maya kosha is made of detachment.

The final layer within it, the most subtle of all the layers and next to the jiva itself, is the ‘sheath of bliss,’ the bliss layer or ananda-maya kosha.

All of these are different densities of covering, each one more refined than the previous one, and ranging from gross elements to most subtle. As in the action of an engine shunting a train of railway carriages, each one linked to the next, they each ‘push’ the more gross layer in the hierarchy and in this way the jiva, the passenger within the layers, exploits and enjoys or suffers within the material environment.

The gross physical body is the sthula-sharira, and the linga-sharira is ‘the subtle body’ consisting of mind, intelligence, and false ego which together form the chetana or conditioned consciousness, loosely called ‘the mind.’

Since the jiva is purely consciousness it must ‘wear’ the five layers and thereby assume lower levels of consciousness to be able to move and work within the material environment. Rather like a diver or astronaut wearing a suit for the environmental conditions, the jiva must assume the identity of the suit and the commensurate level of consciousness.

The human condition is one in which, somehow or other, to be free from the conditions imposed upon it, the consciousness must be raised from the lowest level to the highest. Sometimes the elevation to the highest consciousness is thus described as ‘moving through the five phases of consciousness.’

When the consciousness is oriented towards eating, it is in the anna-maya phase. When it is oriented towards physical endeavours, it is in the prana-maya phase. When it is oriented towards mental activity, it is in its mano-maya phase. When it is oriented towards intellectual discrimination, it is in its vijnana-maya phase. When it is oriented to spiritual happiness, it is in its ananda-maya phase.

The impersonal conception of spiritual happiness is not free of mental conditioning; therefore, it is called vimukta-manina (the mental idea of liberation). That ananda-maya is therefore still a feature of the subtle body, "the last snare of Maya", as Srila Prabhupada said about merging into the Brahman effulgence. That is why the ‘covering of bliss’ so close to the jiva, is still considered a layer, and not the soul proper.

Real ananda-maya, which is beyond the subtle body and beyond impersonalism, is attainable only in the spiritual body, the svarupa of the soul. The causal body (karana-deha) is the linga-sharira that has been purified by mystic yoga. It is called karana-deha because in this subtle body endowed with mystic power one can reach the edge of the spiritual world, the causal ocean (karana-jal). But one can enter Vaikuntha only with the siddha-deha (spiritual body). The 18th century Vedanta Sutra commentator, Baladeva Vidyabhusana writes:

“The planet of Brahma is the goal of those in the ananda-maya conception. They aspire to merge into the existence of Brahma and go with him to the region of Mahavishnu (the karana-jal) when the universe is destroyed. Those who are less advanced dwell in the Jana and Tapa lokas, where the airy element predominates. Those who are more advanced reside in Satyaloka or Brahmaloka itself, where ether is the predominant element. The residents of Jana, Tapa and Satya lokas are advanced in the eight yogic perfections. They have transcended even the linga-sharira. Their forms are called karana-deha (causal bodies). The causal body is fully endowed with mystic powers. It is not engaged either in subtle or gross sense gratification, like the linga-sharira and sthula-sharira. But the karana-deha is not free of the false ego. Therefore, these mystics are generally attached to their powerful positions within the universe, and when Lord Maha-Vishnu breathes the universes out again, they return to their former posts in the Satya, Tapa and Janalokas.”Srila Prabhupada writes of the five layers – and the subsequent five levels of consciousness – in his expansion of certain verses in the Srimad Bhagavatam, known The Prayers of the Personified Vedas. In his commentary he refers to them as the ‘five stages of consciousness’ or ‘five departments of existence.’ He confirms that ‘the yogis who reach Janaloka, Tapaloka and Satyaloka are devotees of Brahma. Indeed, at the time of the maha-pralayana (great universal deluge) they merge into his karana-deha and are taken by him to have darshan of Maha-Vishnu. In the sixth canto of the Shrimad Bhagavatam he writes:

**"One who is liberated from the conceptions of annamaya, pranamaya, manomaya, vijnanamaya and anandamaya and who is perfectly aware of the subtle coverings of the soul is called panchasikha."**

**Srimad Bhagavatam 6.15.15**

The 13th century Madhva Acharya also comments: ‘The karana-deha (creative body), which is enjoyed by the residents of Janaloka, Tapaloka and Satyaloka, corresponds to the ananda-maya-kosha, the bliss-sheath next to the jiva itself. This anandamaya-kosha is used as a body by yogis in mystic trance. In explaining the need to elevate oneself through the layers of consciousness, Srila Prabhupada writes:

“Within the body there are five different departments of existence, known as anna-maya, prāṇa-maya, mano-maya, vijñāna-maya and, at last, ānanda-maya. In the beginning of life, every living entity is food conscious. A child or an animal is satisfied only by getting nice food. This stage of consciousness, in which the goal is to eat sumptuously, is called anna-maya. Anna means "food." After this one lives in the consciousness of being alive. If one can continue his life without being attacked or destroyed, one thinks himself happy. This stage is called prāṇa-maya, or consciousness of one's existence. After this stage, when one is situated on the mental platform, his consciousness is called mano-maya. The materialistic civilization is primarily situated in these three stages, anna-maya, prāṇa-maya and mano-maya. The first concern of civilized persons is economic development, the next concern is defence against being annihilated, and the next consciousness is mental speculation, the philosophical approach to the values of life.”

“If by the evolutionary process of philosophical life, one happens to reach the platform of intellectual life and understands that he is not this material body but a spiritual soul, he is situated in the vijñāna-maya stage. Then, by evolution in spiritual life, he (understands) the Supreme Soul. When one develops his relationship with Him…that stage of life is called Krishna consciousness, the ānanda-maya stage. As long as the living entities are situated in the lower four stages of life— anna-maya, prāṇa-maya, mano-maya and vijñāna-maya—they are considered to be in the material condition of life, but as soon as one reaches the stage of ānanda-maya, he is a liberated soul.”

## The Chakras

Descriptions of the chakras, or ‘vortices of subtle energy flow,’ images of which are so beloved of yoga studios and manuals, can be found in the Rudra Yamala Tantra, a tantric text wherein Lord Shiva speaks with his wife Parvati on the science of yoga. In the Shiva Samhita is found the maxim that ‘As it is in the macrocosm, so it is in the microcosm,’ meaning that the body is a miniature universe. The chakras can be thought of as a hierarchy of celestial regions within this human body universe. Within the Sushumna Nadi, a ‘river of vital energy’ corresponding to the subtle dimension of the innermost part of the spinal cord, lie the ‘seven islands’ of the chakras.
