Contemplation on, or recognition of, the chakras is normally embraced as part of the non-Vaishnava Tantric path, particularly by those attracted to gradually raising the Kundalini energy as a form of incremental enlightenment leading to Brahman realisation. This mechanical path, whereby the ego is destroyed by progressively rigorous techniques, is also challenged as the yogi is confronted by celestial temptations as each chakra is reached.

## Karma

Although the jiva has free will, when it chooses to exploit material energy it becomes bound up even more. Like a fly trapped in a web, each movement binds him tighter in the sticky trap.

Like a prisoner, the jiva is shackled in chains. Although he may move cells, or be placed in different chains, still he is never free. The three gunas are like gold, silver, and iron chains in the prison. They may vary in value, but they are chains, nonetheless.

The activities of the jiva in the prison are firstly to enjoy, and secondly to try to gain relief from the subsequent suffering. In these attempts to enjoy and prevent suffering, the jiva comes under the sway of the so-called ‘six enemies’ of the soul:

1\. Kama – Lust

2\. Krodha – Anger

3\. Lobha – Greed

4\. Mada – Intoxication

5\. Moha – Illusion

6\. Matsarya - Envy

Being covered by many layers, experiencing almost complete forgetfulness of its original spiritual nature, and coming under the sway of the ‘six enemies,’ the jiva moves the physical body, engaging in activity or karma.

Each action results in a subsequent reaction. The initial action is the cause and the reaction is the effect. But the effect then becomes the cause of the next effect, and so a chain of action and reaction is enacted. Just as a seed produces a tree which produces fruit containing seeds, so actions give rise to seeds of future actions.

Good actions cause good reactions and bad causes bad. Karma is any action that develops the future state of the material body and circumstances. Karma Phala is the reaction to karma, or action, and can be either immediate or delayed.

The chain of action and reaction - karma and karma-phala - continues life after life, the jiva being carried from one level of consciousness to another, one species to another, one situation to another. Up and down in the material world, the jiva is in effect, tied to a giant wheel of karma. Both good and bad actions create effects which must be later enjoyed or suffered. When we speak of good and bad actions, we speak of actions of the speech and limbs that will cause future good and bad reactions. Sometimes these are called virtues and sins. Papa or ‘sin’ is defined in the Mahabharata as follows:

*paropakara punyaya papaya para pidanam.*

“Virtue is that which benefits other beings. Sin is that which causes pain to others.” The entire moral theology of Sanatana Dharma or the path that is taught in the Vedas rests upon the concept of “benefit of all beings” (loka-sangraha). Motivation or intention is the deciding factor of the moral nature of an action. Motivation which centres on the welfare of others (parartha) is of the nature of holiness, goodness, and purity whereas motivation which centres upon oneself (svartha) is negative and leads to suffering.

Sin in the Vedantic context is an unskilled use of action, a lack of spiritual understanding which causes one to transgress the Cosmic Order (rita). The means of committing unskilful acts are: the mind, the speech and actions. There are ten major categories of unskilful action which need to be guarded against. The unskilful use of thought is manifest in (1) thinking harmful thoughts directed towards others (2) clinging to irrational and erroneous doctrines. The unskilful use of speech is detected in un-beneficial speech such as - (3) lying, (4) slandering, (5) gossiping, and (6) abusing others and (7) giving false teachings. The unskilful use of action is - (8) causing physical injury to other living beings, (9) sexual misconduct and (10) not rendering assistance in time of need.

All well-directed virtuous actions lead to spiritual development and happiness, all unskilful actions slow spiritual growth and produce unhappiness There are three types of Karma including both negative and positive:

1\. Sanchit Karma - the mass of accumulated results of acts which have been committed in a myriad of past lives and are stored in the sub-conscious mind waiting to come to fruition in the future.

2\. Prarabdha Karma - “operative” karma being acts done in the past which have resulted in the circumstances of the present incarnation and are causing all the joys and sorrows which we are now experiencing.

3\. Kriyamana Karma - All the actions which are now being performed; the results of which will be experienced later and will condition the circumstances of the next incarnation. The Scriptures declare that the Lord liberates His devotees from all karmic reactions. This is understood to refer to the accumulated potential effects. Prarabdha or “operative” Karma is in fact for Vaishnavas, of two kinds: abhy-upagata – accepted, and anabhy-upagata – unaccepted.

Abhy-upagata means the results of past karmas that have been accepted by the individual to be experienced. A spiritual aspirant (prapanna) resigns himself or herself to continue life and to accept the balance of merit and demerit of previous actions until the end of this present life span. A sincere aspirant does not entertain any desires to avoid the consequences of one’s previous actions, be they happiness or sorrow. One accepts them with fortitude because they are in fact the requiting of spiritual bills. Anabhy-upagata or unaccepted results of karma are karmic consequences that have not yet come to fruition and will probably lead to future births unless one takes refuge in God. The act of surrendering to Krishna destroys these accumulated reactions.

Prarabdha karma is beyond the control of the individual and the results which have produced our present conditions have to be born with patience. It is like the seed which has been planted in the past and is now flowering. Sanchit karma is like seed which has been stored and can be remitted through the grace of God.

The kriyamana karma or the present actions are entirely under the control of the individual and must be performed with the utmost awareness that each individual is the author of his or her own destiny and each and every action, no matter how trivial, will have an effect, unless all actions and their consequences are surrendered unto God, (karma phala tyaga). Once surrendered, all actions are purified and everything is done as service to God alone and thus even mundane actions become the vehicle of liberation in that surrendered actions performed without motivation for rewards does not produce any further karma.

Sanchit karma can be compared to the new shoots of a fruit tree, anabhy-upagata karma is like a green fruit and abhy-upagata karma is like a fruit ready for eating. The doctrine of karma explains the dynamic of suffering. There are three factors in suffering: the person who is suffering, an agent of suffering such as a person, thing, condition and so on, and the degree of suffering.

Once we understand the self as it is, and the dynamic of actions and their consequences, then the agent of suffering becomes incidental and secondary. We no longer bear any anger or resentment towards the person or thing that is seen to be causing our suffering because they are merely agents of our own karma. It is on this basis and this basis alone that we can truly love our “enemy” and return love for hurt, compassion for thoughtlessness. When the experience of suffering is thus understood it becomes an opportunity for self-development and spiritual transformation and its emotional and psychological intensity is greatly diminished.

Some jivas are moving up to heaven (svarga) and some moving down to hellish worlds (naraka). Even the gods are also jivas who, when their stock of good reactions to virtuous deeds are exhausted, will have to return to the earthly worlds.

Jivas on this earthly plane can also become gods in the next life. This is the goal of the activities recommended in the karma-kanda section of the Vedas.

Another classic analogy for the different types of karma-phala is that of the archer:

“An archer holds his bow and over his shoulder is a quiver full of arrows. He is an expert bowman and can shoot several arrows in quick succession. As we watch him, he has just shot one arrow which is speeding away, forming a curve through the sky. It has left his bow, and he cannot recall it. He is fixing another to his bow string, and his quiver is filled with even more, ready to be shot.

The arrow already in flight is the prarabdha.

The arrows in the quiver yet to be shot is the sanchit.

The arrow he is about to shoot is the kriyamana.”

![рис-010.jpg|660](<../attachments/ten-teachings-of-bhakti.-a-vaishnava-%D1%81atechism--%D1%80%D0%B8%D1%81-010--b4ba15046d257221.jpg>)

## Cosmic colours

**Like primary colours of red, blue, and yellow, the three gunas, or ‘modes of nature’ mix with each other, producing endless hues of Maya. Each of these cause the soul to forget its original position in a certain way.**

Goodness is tinged by passion, becoming passion-goodness; and passion deepened by ignorance, becoming an ignorant form of passion. In this way, swirling and mixing throughout the cosmos, the three modes become nine modalities; the nine each become tinged by three more producing twenty-seven modal combinations. Those then mix to produce seven hundred and twenty-nine. The mixing eventually reaches 8,400,000 unique shades.

Although each tiny individual soul is at the same level of original purity, there are 8,400,000 shades - degrees of forgetfulness - by which it can be covered. 8,400,000 levels of occluded consciousness. In each case the soul’s ability to perceive reality is reduced; its vision, hearing, sense of smell all restricted according to the modal mix. This proportional inability to comprehend the reality of its situation produces a dysfunctional consciousness through which it physically engages with, and conceptually processes, the environment.

From the single-celled amoeba up through four-legged creatures, humans, angels and half-gods, the entrapped soul asks only one question: how can I find pleasure and avoid pain? The Sanskrit terms are sankalpa and vikalpa, and they follow each other in quick succession. Either intensely selfish or selflessly giving to others, the soul seeks a continuum of pleasure. Through the particular mixture of the three gunas, exercised at its current biological birth as prarabdha karma, the soul is impelled toward combinations of material energy where it seeks out those sensual experiences which seem to promise a lasting happiness uninterrupted by loss, fear or pain.

At the lowest levels of consciousness, the trapped soul thinks only of biological survival through immediate gratification and a quick response to danger. Its questions of existence extend only to: ‘Will it eat me?’ ‘Can I eat it?’ ‘Should I run or fight?’ ‘Can I mate with it?’ At higher levels of consciousness, the soul can exercise delayed gratification, working with others in shoals, flocks, herds, packs, or tribes for greater sustainability of food and security. It can exercise the beginnings of extended self-interest, placing the social group before itself. Later, a sense of emotional happiness is found to be greater than physical gratification. Later still, a less calculated altruism begins, as even higher levels of consciousness allow the soul to become genuinely concerned for the welfare of others, even at the risk to its own biological discomfort.

Still higher levels of consciousness allow the soul to comprehend that it is not, in truth, merely a biological or emotional entity, and that happiness will not be ultimately found by gratification of these lower selves. Something more is required to secure a longer-lasting happiness. And so, the struggle for self-realisation begins. Through many lifetimes of struggle with the remnants of sensuality, emotional attachment and conceptual confusion, the soul moves on in its journey, eventually coming to the platform of full self-realisation followed by God realisation. In the classic text, the Vishnu Purana, it is said:

*jala-jā nava-lakṣāṇi*  
*sthāvarā lakṣa-viṁśati*  
*kṛmayo rudra-saṅkhyakāḥ*  
*pakṣiṇāṁ daśa-lakṣaṇam*  
*triṁśal-lakṣāṇi paśavaḥ*  
*catur-lakṣāṇi mānuṣāḥ*

**There are 900,000 species living in the water. There are also 2,000,000 non-moving living entities (sthāvara ), such as trees and plants. There are also 1,100,000 species of insects and reptiles, and there are 1,000,000 species of birds. As far as quadrupeds are concerned, there are 3,000,000 varieties, and there are 400,000 human species.**

Srila Prabhupada writes that not all these species may be currently living on this planet, but that there are many other planets where they live. He adds:

“The material energy acts on the living entity in different degrees, according to how he acquires the association of the three modes of material nature. There are 8,400,000 species of life, some inferior, some superior and some mediocre. The gradations of the bodies are calculated according to the covering of material energy. In the lower categories — including aquatics, trees, plants, insects, birds and so forth — spiritual consciousness is almost non-existent.”

“In the mediocre category — the human form of life — spiritual consciousness is comparatively awakened. In the superior life forms, spiritual consciousness is fully awakened. Then the living entity understands his real position and tries to escape the influence of material energy by developing Krishna consciousness.”

![рис-011.jpg|600](<../attachments/ten-teachings-of-bhakti.-a-vaishnava-%D1%81atechism--%D1%80%D0%B8%D1%81-011--dd3ae5dd5efd4d39.jpg>)

## Freedom of the soul

**The jiva is an eternal particle of consciousness, unchangeably personal, yet distracted from its original nature and purpose. It passes through the material world untouched by time or space, yet engrossed in both by dint of attachment, false identity, and consequent layers of covering. It is in permanent transit.**

Always trying to achieve a sense of happiness, but never finding permanency; always seeking to minimise distress but never succeeding, it yearns for the sweet fruit of eternal existence while mistakenly grasping only the cold ripples of a reflection. Through life after life, in millions of species, it tries to break free from its limitations, deep down suspecting its own eternity but never realising it in one temporary body after another.

Eventually it comes again to the human form of life, the precious form in which some dim recollection of its sat-chit-ananda nature can be felt. Seeking some form of relief, it augments that initial human life with a search for knowledge, practice of moral behaviour, or a spiritual journey of elevation of consciousness. In this he sometimes acts alone, and sometimes joins with others in various groups and styles of religion. In his discussions with Rupa Goswami, Shri Chaitanya described the trillions of living beings and the minority who take to any kind of spiritual search.

He told him that the inquisitive human being is only a tiny portion of the universal population. The population can be divided in two: those that can move and those that cannot. Trees and plants are non-moving living entities, while aquatics, birds, and animals move in the water, in the air, and on land. Among the millions and trillions of living beings moving on land, human beings are a numerically minuscule section. Then:

“In this small human community, most members are completely ignorant of spiritual life and have no faith in the existence of God. Among those few who do have faith in God and the scriptures, at least half give only lip service while engaging in all kinds of activities against religious principles, or even against basic moral standards. The activities of the faithless and the lip servers eventually degrade to the point where they fully resemble the activities of animals or worse, and these living entities, their consciousness again covered by their own will, descend again to the lower species in their next lives to start over in the evolutionary cycle.”

“Above the lip servers, among the sincere followers of religious principles, most people aspire to profit from the business of piety. These people are called fruitive workers, because they want to enjoy the results, or fruits, of their good work. They want something back for their devotion: wealth, fame, a comfortable life for themselves and for those they love, either here or in heaven. Out of millions of fruitive workers one is wise enough to see that no matter how rich the results of our piety, we continue suffering birth and death in the material universe without any true satisfaction of the soul. These rare wise persons have a preliminary understanding that they are eternal spirit, not matter, and they desire liberation from material suffering by losing themselves in eternal spiritual existence.”

With rudimentary knowledge, people who follow a moral, religious path seek to have happiness in the here and the hereafter. The Earth is only one of many possible destinations in the next life, and both Heaven (Swarga), and Hell (Patala) are factual realms of experience for the soul. Good karma takes the soul upwards, and bad karma guarantees a downward path. As Krishna explains: ‘Lust, greed, and anger are the three gates leading to Hell.’

If only to guarantee a peaceful, healthy, and progressive society, human beings must practice morality and altruism. The fact that such activities can ultimately earn them a long life on a heavenly planet is a benefit and, for some, a distinct goal. There are many sacrificial practices described in the Vedas toward this end, and those who subscribe to them, with their goal of purifying their karma and reaching riches in this life, or a heavenly next birth, are known as karmis.

Then there are those who, after hearing from the Upanishads, or through learning from the hard experience of many lifetimes, decide that only the freedom of the soul will satisfy them. Their goal is to break free from the never-ending cycle of birth and death and to achieve liberation from matter. They become even more attracted to jnana, the path of knowledge. By a discipline of long and scrupulous study they enhance their abilities to discriminate between matter and spirit. They are known as jnanis.

But these spiritualists are generally inclined towards monism, the idea of Brahman as the ultimate, and they conceive that merging with the oneness of the eternal absolute is the destination of the soul. There is a gulf of difference between the individual soul and God, despite the spiritual similarities, and the monist makes futile attempts to dissolve this distinction in favour of oneness, eventually falling back in frustration to activities for material enjoyment.

Very similar to them are the yogis, who take to the path of ashtanga-yoga or the eightfold path. By practising moral discipline, mastering self-control, and engaging in postures (asanas) to help them drill the respiration in preparation for deep meditation, they also aim towards Brahman. A few develop their relationship with the Param Atma and a few of them eventually have a vision of him. In this way, there is a hierarchy of human aspiration.

## Dharma

**According to its level of consciousness, the embodied soul will have a set of urgent needs, the first of which is survival. From basic survival, physical security, shelter, education, social standing all the way to transcendence can be a long and challenging journey over many lifetimes. How we deal with our basic needs, in a way that disentangles us from Maya and gradually moves us toward the transcendent, is the purpose of Dharma.**

In 1954, when psychologist Abraham Maslow devised his ‘hierarchy of needs’ he got it pretty much right. At the base of his pyramid, he put our most basic needs to survive and to prolong our survival by planning for food, water, and security. He reasoned that only when these needs are met could we legitimately progress upwards towards less physical and selfish needs. And that to have those needs met we would have to create family, society, and social networks. Being a psychologist chiefly concerned with the mind, it took him a few years to finally add ‘transcendence’ but eventually it was added. He did not define it according to the Vedas, but to him it seemed to be a human need that could not be denied.

But Maslow’s concept has two immediate flaws. We will not hold it against him, but we need to understand what they are. The first was his conclusion that: ‘the easiest technique for releasing the organism from the bondage of the lower, more material, more selfish needs is to gratify them.’ As a purely biological organism that would be true, of course, but sense gratification does not actually release us; the same desire for sense gratification, with often greater intensity, reappears the very next day.

Building a society in which every biological or selfish need is gratified? Well, we are seeing the results around us – it is not working. It does not work for the individual and it is devastating for the environment. There are, quite simply, not enough material resources for us to live a life of sense gratification whenever we want it. And for everyone on Earth to live like that would take three entire Earths – a choice we do not have. Besides which, gratifying every need will only lead to actions that generate karma-phala – reactions that will have to be enjoyed or suffered in the future.

The second flaw in his presentation is that the need for transcendence, as he understood it, comes only after everything else has been dealt with. But rampant selfishness leads to exploitation of others, mistrust of social enterprises, reduction in co-operation in society, corruption and bad government, social breakdown, and war. The point he missed – the point that everyone misses – is that the human being is not merely a biological organism but a spiritual being seeking to be happy through a biological organism. We are not, to paraphrase Teilhard de Chardin, human beings that sometimes have transcendental experiences, but transcendental beings having a human experience. Transcendence is not the goal once all our thousands of perceived human needs have been met; it is the main thing we crave. Just as a fish stranded on land craves the water, being a creature of the water, so we crave transcendence because we belong to it. We do not aspire to transcendence just because we want it; we aspire to it because we desperately need it.

However, our bodies and minds do need air, food, water, homeostasis, excretion, security, sex, respect, and love, too. But we need to gratify those needs in a way that creates less exploitation and violence, uses less precious resources, helps control our senses so we’ll need less not more, and provides our legitimate needs in a way that allows us to move gradually towards transcendence. How do we do that? It is called Dharma.

The word has many translations, but the main meaning is ‘that which sustains the existence of something.’ The dharma of a flame is heat and light, for instance; it is that which is inseparable from it, and that which therefore sustains it. The flame cannot exist without heat and light. The dharma of the human being is its inner nature, its higher nature, and its real nature. It is the soul that sustains the human being, so it is the human being’s dharma to always act as if the soul was the most important ‘self’ – because that is the reality.

When we work in the world, but not as if we were of the world, as all great spiritual leaders tell us, we will be on our way to transcendence. We will be practising dharma.

By working with this always in mind we will begin to consume less because the soul does not need to consume matter. To the degree that we are situated more on the spiritual platform, seeing the world with spiritual vision, to that same extent we will see things less materially. We won’t need to gratify the senses to satisfy our basic needs because our needs have lessened. To the degree that we experience spiritual happiness, to the same degree we’ll need less material happiness, seeing it for what it is: fleeting and temporary.

This will help shape a new set of values for us, a new set of ideas and subsequent behaviours that we consider to be of value. It will not always be easy to make the transition to a new set of values, but it will happen by degree. From values comes morals, both personal morals – how we regard and treat ourselves in accordance with our new personal code of behaviour – and social morals – how we behave towards others.

By placing transcendence first in our hierarchy of needs – and walking the walk of dharma – we surprisingly achieve every other aspect of everything we need. All that we need will come to us with less effort. By being in touch with the universal agencies that supply everyone’s needs we will have all our needs met. The classic expression for this law is dharma, artha, kama, moksha. First comes dharma then comes ‘everything valuable’ (artha), pleasure and joy (kama) and finally transcendence or ‘ultimate freedom’ (moksha).

## Analysing a religion

**According to one’s level of material attachment, the Vedas offer different approaches to spiritual practice. Yet the aim is the same: yoga. Saints and sages, gurus, and popes all speak in different ways, in different countries throughout history, and amass followers whenever their messages strike a chord. We then have what we describe as ‘different religions.’ But there should be no confusion. There is only one God and hence only one religion. Yet some ‘gurus’ teach fallacious and misleading ideas, so Vaishnavas should be astute and analytical.**

Devotees of Krishna are often asked what they think of ‘other religions’ and how the path of bhakti-yoga compares to them. The answer is surprising at first, and then very revealing. Firstly, Shri Krishna Himself has absolutely nothing to say about Buddhism, Hinduism, Judaism, Christianity, Islam, or Sikhism as none of them were existent at the time the Bhagavad-gita was spoken.

However, he has much to say about the principles of religion per se and describes in detail the influences that cause the soul to become deviated from the path of yoga – the path of perfection. Reassuringly for the apprehensive, Krishna even says that every single soul is on the same path – his path – and that as they surrender to him, he will reveal Himself to them accordingly.

All souls want Krishna, ultimately, because Krishna is the goal of the soul. But along the way there are distractions due to temptation. And temptation comes because the soul is carrying a heavy burden of karmic baggage from many thousands of previous lifetimes. This baggage keeps it weighed down and intensely attracted to the world.

So, there are no ‘different religions,’ merely different levels of purity of the same religion. There may be those who describe heaven to be a certain way; or that being a good person means a certain type of behaviour. There may be others who profess faith in their guru, saint, messiah, priest, or prophet; and others who have their preferred language for speaking to God and a set of rules for their community. But all of these are variations on some quite simple themes.

Just as we can understand whether some fruit is ripe by examining it, so we can determine the nature of a religious path by examining a few key elements. By looking at these elements we can identify clearly what sort of religious path we have.

Firstly, what is the goal? Is it a desire to feel virtuous, or a wish for wealth and health in this world? Or is the goal a heaven after death where even more personal wealth is available? Perhaps there is some concept of coming back to this world to take birth again in improved circumstances. While these goals are laudable, they are still very much in the world.

Secondly, what of the means to reach this goal? Do they involve discipline, self-restraint, study? Do they involve service to others, good deeds, charity and so on? Does the religious path call for daily spiritual activity or short bursts of pious behaviour on special weeks or months? Analysed according to the Bhagavad-gita’s framework of behaviour in the gunas, the former is in sattva-guna and the latter in raja-guna (goodness and passion).

What about universal principles such as non-violence, cleanliness, austerity, and mercy toward others? What are their views on distractions such as intoxication and how do they moderate gambling, sexual relations and animal killing?

Is the path one where followers join for regular worship and celebration, or does the path involve more individual pursuits such as monastic living, rigorous discipline, contemplation, and study? If people congregate, do they have a head of the community and methods of electing future leaders? How hierarchical is the governing structure of their religion, and who interprets the scripture and teaches it?

What is the result of this religious path? Is the result tolerance, peacefulness, simplicity, and patience? Are the followers becoming free from selfish desire, greed, and anger? Do they behave amicably toward one another or is there tribalism, party spirit, sectarianism, nationalism, intolerance, and discord?

Finally, what is the concept of God? Some religions believe in no God at all, and others that God is real but a ‘perpetual mystery,’ and forever beyond our understanding. Some say that there are many gods or that God is ‘one with many faces.’ Others say that because they themselves do not know the identity of God then no-one else can possibly know. Still others say that ‘we used to believe but we have now lost our faith.’

All these varieties of religious thought and experience have always existed, and analysed according to them, any religion can be understood, as it is.

Krishna’s charama-sloka, His concluding instruction to Arjuna, was that his warrior friend (who was previously trained in many paths of righteous and noble behaviour) should simply abandon ‘all varieties of religion’ and simply surrender to him. In this way, the Lord describes the conclusion of all religion to be simply to love him with unalloyed devotion, and he promises that Arjuna that, if he does so, he would find ultimate protection and lasting reward (Bhagavad-gita 18.66).

## The four paths

**The problem for the soul is illusion, and the solution is yoga. Yoga consists chiefly of controlling the mind so that the soul’s true nature can be experienced. There are several classical methods of making spiritual advancement, removing karma and attachment to matter, and attaining moksha: The Bhagavad-gita validates four paths of yoga but sees their culmination in just one.**

There are four paths of yoga (ashtanga, jnana, karma, bhakti) which are meant to free the soul from this predicament. Each one has a different approach to overcoming the mind.

There are two paths of extrication, or giving up home, occupation, and social duties, and two paths of integration, or maintaining home, occupation, and social duties.

## Extrication

Ashtanga Yoga – Stills the mind through rigorous discipline and concentration. Through that stillness one can experience the true self and see God everywhere. The process is difficult. Ashtanga cultivates internal renunciation through external renunciation.

Jnana Yoga – Breaks the mind’s attachments. Through analysis and discernment between reality and illusion, aided by austerity. The process is troublesome. Jnana cultivates internal renunciation through external renunciation.

## Integration

Karma Yoga – Transforms the false sense of ego. By working with knowledge and detachment and turning all action into sacrifice. Karma cultivates internal renunciation through renouncing the fruits of one’s work and cultivating service.

Bhakti Yoga – Harmonises the mind with the self. Through mantra meditation, hearing from sacred texts, ritual, service, and cultural arts. Bhakti cultivates internal renunciation through transmuting all acts into acts of one-pointed devotion.

## Karma Yoga

When the idea of gradual elevation to the platform of divine service to God (instead of elevation to the heavens) becomes the goal, but when there is still attachment to material goals and favoured kinds of work, the path is known as Karma Yoga. Karma Yoga is ‘the yoga of work,’ and consists of remaining in one’s work / family / life situation but offering all the fruits of one’s work to the Divine. Karma yoga is said to be for those who are beginners since it involves attachment. Karma Yoga, the ‘Yoga of Action’ involves:

Japa – The recitation of mantras, both audible and private.

Homa – Oblations into a sacrificial fire with libations of clarified butter.

Yajna – Discharging of debts to parents, gods, sages, society, environment\*

Dana – Charity and generosity to those in need.

Tapas – Self-discipline and control of the mind and senses.

\*Yajna means the ‘Five Great sacrifices’ and they must all be accompanied by sincere feeling of gratitude and demonstration of that gratitude. They are as follows:

Pitri Yajna – Parents and ancestors Deva Yajna – Demigods

Brahma Yajna – Saints and Sages Bhuta Yajna – Animals

Manushya Yajna – Human beings

Demonstration of gratitude means caring for parents and making offerings to ancestors; making offerings to the gods; studying and teaching what the sages gave us; feeding animals and protecting their environment; offering hospitality to guests and strangers, with at least a seat, water and kind words.

Dana – Generosity

Compassion in action. Empathy and a desire to alleviate suffering to the extent possible. Dana is service, the giving of one’s resources and self to help others.

Tapas - Austerity

Tapas or tapasya means austerity or ‘heat,’ and indicates a voluntary acceptance of something difficult for the purposes of moving forward in spiritual life. There are three kinds of tapas.

1\. Kayika Tapas – Physical self-discipline – Adoration of elders, gurus, gods and saints; personal and environmental hygiene; integrity – the harmony of thought and action; sexual restraint and avoidance of exploitation or objectification of others; non-injury.

2\. Vachika Tapas – Discipline of speech – using words that do not disturb; words that are true, agreeable, and beneficial. The practice of recitation of scripture.

3\. Manasika Tapas – peace of mind, the absence of desire, anger, delusion, pride, and greed. Benevolence, being devoted to the good of others. Silence, control of expression. Self-restraint, focusing the flow of thoughts to the object of contemplation. Mindfulness, constant awareness of God.

Ramanuja Acharya says that this threefold self-discipline should be practised with utmost faith in action, speech and in thought; having no consideration of reward, and with the conviction that one is doing it for God.

## Ashtanga Yoga

Classic ashtanga yoga, is the ‘yoga of eight limbs.’ The yogi retires to a peaceful, secluded place with no disturbance, moderates his eating and sleeping, and practises lengthy meditation to reduce bodily identity and elevate consciousness. In the Bhagavad-gita Krishna describes the first requirements of the path:

**There is no possibility of one's becoming a yogi, O Arjuna, if one eats too much, or eats too little, sleeps too much or does not sleep enough. He who is temperate in his habits of eating, sleeping, working and recreation can mitigate all material pains by practicing the yoga system.**

**Bhagavad-gita 6.16-17**

The classic yoga of ‘eight limbs’ is fully described in the Yoga Sutras of the sage Patanjali. He begins by defining that yoga means ‘the stilling of the changing states of the mind,’ and that when this is accomplished ‘the seer abides in its own true nature.’

That cause of the changing states of mind is the klesha, various forms of conditioning which creates intense bodily identity which creates desires or raga, then contact between sense and sense object, which again creates further entanglement, bondage in samsara, and future suffering. The upward path of yoga consists largely of breaking attachment to the world by realising that the mind itself is the obstacle and controlling it. The ‘eight-fold path’ consists of two sadhanas or means of achievement, one executed after the other:

Bahiranga Sadhana – 5 external practices

Yama (self-restraint), Niyama (self-development), Asana (sitting posture), Pranayama (breathing discipline), Pratyahara (withdrawal from external stimuli)

Antaranga Sadhana – 3 internal practices

Dharana (focusing the mind), Dhyana (unbroken concentration), Samadhi (absorption)

Yama and Niyama are the first two elements. Yama means ‘reining in’ and the five principles of yama are a series of self-restraints or abstentions. On the other hand, niyama means ‘positive observances.’ Together they comprise ten principles and are listed as follows:

**Yama**

1\. Ahimsa – non-violence in thoughts, words, deeds.

2\. Satya – telling the truth to others, honesty with oneself.

3\. Asteya – refraining from stealing.

4\. Brahmacharyam – celibacy.

5\. Aparigraha – refraining from acquisition or coveting, renunciation of unnecessary possessions.

**Niyama**

6\. Saucham – cleanliness.

7\. Santosha – contentment.

8\. Tapah - austerity and perseverance.

9\. Svadhyaya - Veda study.

10\. Ishvara-pranidhana – devotion to God.

If successful, the yogi can persist in his determined striving until he reaches the state of samadhi in which he is blessed by a strikingly beautiful vision of Ishvara, the previously unseen controller of all time and space, Shri Vishnu himself. If, however, like many yogis, he only succeeds in attaining the Brahman he may achieve the long, but ultimately temporary, realisation of his eternal nature then eventually returns to samsara, the cycle of birth and death.

Many on the yoga path are unsuccessful since it requires many years, even many lifetimes, of extreme dedication. In that case they are born again, somewhere on Earth, into a pious or wealthy family, and unconsciously drift towards yoga practice, even from a relatively young age.

![рис-012.jpg|407](<../attachments/ten-teachings-of-bhakti.-a-vaishnava-%D1%81atechism--%D1%80%D0%B8%D1%81-012--76beef3bdd7fa8d9.jpg>)

## Jnana Yoga

Jnana Yoga is the yoga of knowledge and consists of renunciation, a frugal lifestyle, a deep study of the Vedic texts, and meditation. Classical Advaita Vedanta emphasises the path of Jnana Yoga to attain moksha. It consists of fourfold attitudes or behavioural qualifications:

1\. Discrimination (NityР”Рѓnitya vastu viveka or simply viveka) — the ability to correctly discriminate (viveka) between the unchanging, permanent, eternal (nitya) and the changing, transitory, temporary (anitya).

2\. Dispassion of fruits (IhР”РѓmutrР”Рѓrtha phala bhoga virР”Рѓga or simply viraga) — The dispassionate indifference (viraga) to the fruits, to enjoyments of objects (artha phala bhoga) or to the other worlds (amutra) after rebirth.

3\. The Six Virtues (Samadhi ṣatka sampatti or simply satsampat) —

• Shama, temperance of mind.

• Dama, temperance of sense organs (voluntary self-restraint).

• Uparati, withdrawal of mind from sensory objects.

• Titiksha, forbearance.

• Shraddha, faith.

• Samadhana, concentration of mind.

4\. Drive or longing (Mumukshutva) — intense yearning for moksha from the state of ignorance.

Jnana yoga consists of three practices, a three-step methodology rooted in the teachings of chapter four of the Brihad Aranyaka Upanishad.

Shravana literally means hearing, and broadly refers to perception and observations typically aided by a counsellor or teacher wherein the Advaitin listens and discusses the ideas, concepts, questions, and answers.

Manana refers to thinking on these discussions and contemplating over the various ideas based on svadhyaya and sravana.

Nididhyasana refers to meditation, realization and consequent conviction of the truths, nonduality, and a state where there is a fusion of thought and action, knowing and being.

These practices, with the help of a guru are believed to lead to correct knowledge, which destroys avidya, psychological and perceptual errors related to Atman and Brahman.

## Different and non-different

**The Upanishads sometimes describe the soul as being one with God, and in other places as different from God. Since everything is generated from, and is part of, God, the soul can be said to be ‘one with God.’ Yet the soul can perceive God, and perception immediately infers subjectivity. One consciousness is perceiving another consciousness. In addition to the ‘one-ness’ there seems also to be a ‘two-ness.’ This sounds like a paradox.**

The answer to the age-old question is provided in the mystic meditation verse of Srila Vyasa in which he describes the ultimate reality as advaya jnana. Advaya means ‘not-two-ness,’ and jnana indicates both a ‘knower’ and ‘that which is known’ which infers ‘two-ness.’ Taking these definitions into consideration, the term ‘advaya jnana’ at first seems like a contradiction of terms.

Yet, as we have discussed earlier on, Vyasadeva saw in his vision that the ultimate reality consisted of Brahman, Paramatma and Bhagavan. What we term ‘God’ is three levels of reality in a unified existence. In the Brahman, perception is suspended (sushupta), so distinction is not perceived. At this stage the soul perceives itself as ‘one with God.’ But in the Paramatma and Bhagavan conception, there is perception and distinction is experienced.

Considering this tiered experience of higher reality, the Veda sometimes emphasizes the unity (advaya), and sometimes emphasizes the plurality inherent in perception (jñāna).  To do both of these statements justice, we must synthesize a concept from both. The great mediaeval commentator, Śrī Jīva Goswami, says:

“In non-duality, unity and plurality coexist.”

## Plurality in Non-Duality

The plurality inherent in perception is the distinction between “seer” and “seen.”

Although as seekers of our divine source, we are looking for just a small glimpse of God, almost anything to confirm that he is there, we must also understand that God himself is also the seer. He also desires to see, and not simply to be seen. The soul’s relationship is that God is the seer of the soul, for his pleasure. God as the seer wants to see, so he manifests the seen. Being the source of the seen, the seer is called shaktiman, and the seen is called shakti.

The seen exists to be seen by the seer, and to see the seer seeing it. Shakti exists to fulfil the purposes of Shaktiman. Shakti is that which creates objects of perception for the Seer.

They are distinct parts of one entity, like the sun (shaktiman) and sunshine (shakti). ‘Shine’ is inherent in the sun. It is inseparable but distinguishable.

*Shaktih – karana nisthah karyotpadana-yogyo dharma visesah*

**The definitive nature of ‘energy’ is the ability to accomplish the effects of a cause.**

**From the Tattva Dipika by Baladeva Vidyabhushana.**

Baladeva Vidyabhushana, the 18th century author of the Govinda Bhashya commentary on the Vedanta Sutra, attempts to establish the achintya bheda-abheda tattva of Shri Chaitanya as the best school of philosophical darshan, taking all other systems as incomplete arguments. He cites as introductory evidence the Upanishad text, explaining that Brahman is one unit with two distinct parts: Shaktiman and shakti.

*nityo nityānāṁ cetanaś cetanānām eko bahūnāṁ yo vidadhāti kāmān*

**Eternal to eternals. Consciousness to consciousnesses. The singular supports and maintains the plural.**

**Katha Upanishad 2.2.13**

Advaitins, or those who contend only that ‘all is unity’ do not like the notion of ‘shakti’ and ‘shaktiman’ since they say, ‘there are no parts in Brahman.’

## Unity in Non-Duality

One reason the Veda stresses the unity between the soul and God is that unity is the basis of relationship and love. Paramatma and Bhagavan conceptions, though aware of both seer and seen, are not ignorant of their unity. Another reason the Veda stresses unity is to provide a way to understand what is otherwise beyond our comprehension. Unity is important to understand first. Jiva Goswami says: ‘Imagine a person sitting in a cave, who has never seen the sun, yet there is a sliver of light coming through a crack in the wall. And the person who has come to free him says: “That is the sun.” Yes, it is the sun, although a partial manifestation, but we describe what is known to understand what is not known.’

So, Brahman is the ‘known’ because it can be conceptualised and understood first. As in the famous maxim of the Upanishad:

*tat tvaṁ asi*

**You are that.**

**Chandogya Upanishad 6.8.7**

There are many such texts that stress unity over duality, but the Vedanta Sutra, which reconciles apparent differences, explains why there seems to be an apparent contradiction:

*anyārtha parāmarṣa*

**Understand one, understand the other.**

**Vedanta Sutra 1.3.20**

This means that to understand something we can first understand something else. For instance, we may ask an American: ‘Do you know the game of cricket? You don’t? Well, it’s just like baseball.’ The American has never watched or played cricket before in his life, but he knows baseball, so cricket is explained as a variant of baseball. He understands one thing by understanding another.

In his Dasha Moola, Bhaktivinode Thakur explains that, just as the Advaitins hold a philosophy of transformation, so do the Vaishnavas. The difference is that the Advaitins believe in the transformation of Brahman, and the Vaishnavas in the transformation of the energies, the shaktis, of Brahman. He writes:

Parinama Vada - the philosophy of transformation - may be divided into two concepts: Brahma-parinama-vada – the transformation of Brahman.

Tat-shakti-parinama-vada – the transformation of the energies of Brahman.

The first idea is that Brahman transforms itself into the jivas and the world, considering that since Brahman is one and indivisible it is the only known entity. The second idea is that Brahman does not accept transformation but that the achintya-shakti of Brahman is transformed, partly into jiva shakti and partly into Maya shakti.

The generated entity is distinct from its origin. This transformation is known as vikara. Just as yogurt is distinct from milk, although originating from it. This is vikara. If Brahman is one, singular entity without qualities or distinction how can it be transformed? And if you argue that it is transformable then Brahman is robbed of its absolute and detached nature. Shakti-parinama-vada does not suffer from these logical deficiencies.

Bhaktivinode’s argument was that Brahman’s energy is transformed but Brahman remains unaffected. But some might say that a proposition that Brahman desired to expand is in itself a transformation, because ‘desire’ is a change in the eternal, changeless reality. Bhaktivinode replied: “You may say that for Brahman to desire to expand is a transformation, but you would be comparing his desire to ours, which is brought about by external forces. His desire is part of his intrinsic nature. Although his desire is non-different from his shakti, it is simultaneously also distinct from his shakti.”

The word Brahman means ‘the greatest’ or brihat. If we only say that He is ‘untransformed’ and do not say anything else about Him, that is not a very great accolade, and we fail to comprehend the inconceivable power of Brahman, who is also a person. In the Taittiriya Upanishad the Vedas have used the Sanskrit instrumental case (by), the ablative (from), and locative (in) cases to describe his relationship with the material world:

*yato va imani bhutani jayante / yena jatani jivanti yat praty abhisamvishanti / tad vijajnasva tad brahma*

**Please know that Brahman is He from whom all living beings are born, by whose power they remain alive, and into whom they enter at the end.**

**Taittiriya Upanishad 3.1.1**

In the Bhagavat Sandarbha, using a brilliant natural example, Jiva Goswami writes that: “The parama-tattva is one. He controls inconceivable potencies intrinsic to his nature. These potencies manifest his personal form, expansions, jivas, and the pradhana. These can correspond to:

The inner core of the sun

The surface of the sun

The rays emanating from the sun

The light of the sun reflected from another surface far away.

The Advaitins, as explained earlier, rely a lot on their understanding of the word vivarta or bewilderment. Somehow, they reason, Brahman becomes bewildered and conceives of itself as multiple jivas. When true knowledge arises, the bewilderment is ended. But vivarta has a specific meaning, as explained by the sage Sadananda, a fellow Advaitin, and author of the Vedanta Sara:

*atattvato nyatha-buddhir vivarta ity udahrtah*

**To mistakenly perceive any object – that is contrary to, and different from, the actual real object – to be the real object is vivarta.**

**Vedanta Sara 49**

Using this definition, for the jiva to falsely identify with the body is vivarta. To mistake a rope for a snake, or to mistake a glistening oyster shell to be silver, is vivarta. Vivarta is therefore ‘false perception.’ However, the ‘vivarta-vadis’ have ignored this definition and formed their own notion. They say that ‘I am Brahman’ and that ‘I am jiva’ is the illusion.

Their arguments are:

1\. In a deluded state Brahman thinks himself to be jiva and thus jivas are created.

2\. The jivas are the bewildered reflections of Brahma.

3\. Brahman is only dreaming that he has created jivas and the universe.

Advaitins have thus withheld the eternal identity from the jivas, saying that it is a dream only, and that only Brahman is real. The entire Vedic scriptures decry this philosophy but the Advaitins have found four statements which seem to support them. They call them maha-vakya or ‘great statements’:

Sarva khalv idam brahma – ‘this world is Brahman’ – Chandogya Up. 3.14.1 Prajnanam brahma – ‘pure knowledge is Brahman’ – Aitareya Up. 1.5.3 Tat tvam asi – ‘you are that Brahman’ – Chandogya Up. 6.8.7

Aham brahmasmi – ‘I am Brahman’ – Brihad-Aranyaka Up. 1.4.10

Let us exam these four famous statements and see what they mean.

## “Sarva khalv idam brahma.”

What does the first of these maha-vakyas teach? It teaches that the material world and the individual souls are all Brahman, that there is nothing but Brahman. In the Upanishad it is said:

*na tasya karyam karanam ca vidyate*  
*na tat-samash cabhyadhikash ca vidyate*  
*parasya shaktir vividhaiva shruyate*  
*svabhaviki jnana-bala-kriya ca*

**The Supreme has nothing to do. Nothing is equal to him or greater than him. He acts in different phases by manifesting his parts and parcels which are all simultaneously differently situated by his unlimited, variegated potencies. Each potency acts quite naturally in sequences, providing him full knowledge, power, and pastimes.**

**Shvetashvatara Upanishad 6.8**

In these words, Brahman and Brahman's potency are accepted as one. Here it is said that the potency is the property (svabhaviki) of Brahman, and it is also said that the potency is manifested in many ways (vividha). Because the potency and the master who possesses the potency are not different, it may certainly be said that nothing is different from Brahman. However, in the Katha Upanishad 2.13 and Shvetashvatara Upanishad 6.10 appears the ‘nityo nityanam’ verse which balances the apparent complete identity of the jiva and Brahman. In these words, variety is accepted as an eternal fact. In the ‘na tasya karyam; verse just quoted, the Lord's potency and His knowledge, power, and pastimes are also considered, in one sense, to be different from Him.

## “prajnanam brahma”

These words appear in Aitareya Upanishad (1.5.3) and mean "Brahman is consciousness." The nature of consciousness is described in these words:

*tam eva dhiro vijnaya prajnam kurvita brahmanah*

**A wise man who understands the Supreme falls in love with Him.**

**Brihad-aranyaka Upanishad (4.4.21)**

Here the word "prajna" does not mean merely "consciousness". Here it means "love and devotion".

## “tat tvam asi”

These words appear in the celebrated conversation between father and son, Uddalaka and Shwetaketu, in the Chandogya Upanishad (6.8.7): tat tvam asi shwetaketo "O Shwetaketu, you are that."

These words supposedly teach that the Supreme Lord and the individual spirit soul are identical. However, the proper explanation of these words is found in this passage from the Brihad-aranyaka Upanishad (3.8.10): yo va etad aksharam gargy aviditvasmal lokat praiti sa kripanah – ‘One who departs from this world without knowing the Unchangeable, is miserable. But he who departs from this world after knowing the Immutable, is a knower of Brahman.’

## “aham brahmasmi”

These words of Brihad-aranyaka Upanishad 1.4.10 mean "I am Brahman.” Some thinkers say that the knowledge described in these words does not culminate in realisation of Bhagavan but as stated previously, there are many statements in the Veda describing the abheda, the inherent similarity, between Brahman and the shakti but there are also statements describing the distinction between Brahman and shakti such as:

*pradhana ksetrajna patir gunesah*

**He is the controller of Maya and is beyond the influence**

**Svetashvatara Upanishad. 6.16**

**He is attained only by one whom he himself chooses. To such a person he manifests his own form**

**Katha Upanishad 2.23**

**He has fulfilled the desires of everyone since time immemorial**

**Isha Upanishad 8**

**I could not realise who this great personality is**

**Kena Upanishad 3.6**

The following verses confirm that Brahman does indeed have shaktis, even though the Advaitins are reluctant to admit it; that even though emanations stream from Brahman, still Brahman remains unchanged; and that when the Maya-shakti is removed the jiva shakti will be able to see him again.

*parāsya śaktir vividhaiva śrūyate svābhāvikī jñāna-bala-kriyā ca*

**The one divine potency of Parabrahman has been described in the Vedas in many ways. In the verse above the three shaktis are described as ‘jnana-balakriya,’ or jnana-shakti (knowledge), bala-shakti (power), and kriyashakti (potency for activity).**

**Svetashvatara Upanishad 6.7.8**

*oṁ pūrṇam adaḥ pūrṇam idaṁ / pūrṇāt pūrṇam udacyate*  
*pūrṇasya pūrṇam ādāya/ pūrṇam evāvaśiṣyate*

**The Complete Whole is perfect and complete, and because He is completely perfect, all emanations from Him, such as this phenomenal world, are perfectly equipped as complete wholes. Whatever is produced of the Complete Whole is also complete. Because He is the Complete Whole, even though so many complete units emanate from Him, He remains the complete balance.**

**Isha Upanishad Invocation**

*hiraṇmayena pātreṇa satyasyāpihitaṁ mukham*  
*tat tvaṁ pūṣann apāvṛṇu satya-dharmāya dṛṣṭaye*

**O my Lord, sustainer of all that lives, your real face is covered by your dazzling effulgence. Kindly remove that covering and exhibit yourself to your pure devotee.**

**Isha Upanishad 15**

*pūṣann ekarṣe yama sūrya prājāpatya / vyūha raśmīn samūha tejo*  
*yat te rūpaṁ kalyāṇa-tamaṁ tat te paśyāmi / yo ’sāv asau puruṣaḥ so ’ham asmi*

**O my Lord, O primeval philosopher, maintainer of the universe, O regulating principle, destination of the pure devotees, well-wisher of the progenitors of mankind, please remove the effulgence of your transcendental rays so that I can see your form of bliss. You are the eternal Supreme Personality of Godhead, like unto the sun, as am I.**

**Isha Upanishad 16**

## Conclusion

Other siddhantas, or philosophical conclusions based upon the Upanishads and Vedanta Sutra, are not wrong. They are right, but incomplete. The teaching of Chaitanya Mahaprabhu sides neither with non-dualism nor dualism but says that “unity and plurality coexist.” Without either, a relationship between the individual and the supreme is impossible. Unity is the basis of love and relationship, yet distinction allows the very existence of lover and beloved.

The beginning of Chaitanya Mahaprabhu’s teaching is therefore that the jiva is simultaneously the same yet different from God. However, the jiva is eternally related to God in a serving capacity:

*jīvera 'svarūpa' haya--kṛṣṇera 'nitya-dāsa'*  
*kṛṣṇera 'taṭasthā-śakti' 'bhedābheda-prakāśa*  
*sūryāṁśa-kiraṇa, yaiche agni-jvālā-caya*  
*svābhāvika kṛṣṇera tina-prakāra 'śakti' haya*

**It is the living entity's constitutional position to be an eternal servant of Krishna because he is the marginal energy of Krishna and a manifestation simultaneously one and different from the Lord, like a molecular particle of sunshine or fire. Krishna has three varieties of energy.**

**Madhya lila 20.108-109**

## The concept of ultimate origin

We all have an individual consciousness and yet we are all participating in a group consciousness since there are factors common to all of us, making it a shared experience. The question may be raised: What links us? How?

*ābhāsaś ca nirodhaś ca yato ’sty adhyavasīyate*  
*sa āśrayaḥ paraṁbrahma paramātmeti śabdyate*

**The Supreme Brahman, celebrated as the Supreme Being, is the ultimate origin of the cosmic manifestation as well as its shelter and sustainer, and its winding up. Thus, He is the fountainhead.**

**Shrimad Bhagavatam 2.10.7**

When we use the term ‘ultimate origin’ we indicate the entity upon whom reality exists, and by whom it becomes manifest and unmanifest to our perception. It is known by words like Brahman and Paramatma. In this key verse, there are other words that help to explain the relationship of the individual consciousness to the supreme consciousness.

Abhasas – manifests to our perception.

Nirodhas – disappears from our perception.

Ashraya – is what sustains reality through its creations and destructions.

For instance, when you go for a walk and it starts raining and you open an umbrella. The umbrella becomes your ashraya. For a child, the parents are the ashraya. For adults, the house is an ashraya. Ashraya means ‘that which shelters and sustains.’ The place one rests, or upon which one depends. The place one returns to. In the ultimate sense, ashraya is what sustains reality through its creations and destructions.

Creation and destruction happen by the moment. Consciousness sustains and connects one moment to the next. Consciousness is perception. Perception involves subjectivity and individuality therefore consciousness or ashraya is conscious and personal.

## Components of a ‘Person.’

Sometimes the statement ‘God is a person’ is challenged. The argument is that if we are persons, and we are suffering, how could the supreme, who is beyond suffering, ever be a person? But the argument rests on the faulty understanding of what a person is. The next verse in the series explains what a person is. It both helps define the atma and the Paramatma. Because these three verses are in a series it becomes easy to understand and clear.

*yo ’dhyātmiko ’yaṁ puruṣaḥ so ’sāv evādhidaivikah*  
*yas tatrobhaya-vicchedaḥ puruṣo hy ādhibhautikaḥ*

**The individual person possessing different instruments of senses is called the adhyР”Рѓtmic person, and the individual controlling deity of the senses is called adhidaivic. The embodiment seen on the eyeballs is called the adhibhautic person.’**

**Shrimad Bhagavatam 2.10.8**

From this, we may say that ‘A person consists of identity, tangibility and, linking them, divinity.’

Tangibility is the body, composed of physical senses.

Divinity is the power to utilize the body and senses.

Identity is the seer possessing and utilizing those powers of perception and action.

Bhauta means something that has been produced. Since from our last life we have brought with us our mind intelligence and ego, and since this subtle package factually helped to create our present physical incarnation, we say that: ‘the mind creates the senses.’

The senses of the body have been produced by our mind, the continuation of all the desires of our previous life. It is a projection of our consciousness at the time of our last death. We are provided parents as part of this projection. It is important to understand our agency in this. If we do then we can use our consciousness to reform our projections and senses. We can also understand that we ourselves - and everything we see around us - are a projection of the original consciousness.

Divinity also refers to ‘devas,’ celestial beings who give the power of the perception and actions of the senses (‘senses’ means both perception and action – exploring the world and interacting with it).

Identity means the self that sustains the consciousness through all the circumstantial perceptions and actions. In the case of the individual it is the atma, and in the case of the universe the param-atma.

**Tangible** **Divinity** **Identity**   Eyes Sight Seer   Ears Hearing Hearer   Hands Grasping Grasper

## The Ultimate Person

The next verse in the series continues the description of the term ‘person.’

*ekam ekatarābhāve yadā nopalabhāmahe*  
*tritayaṁ tatra yo veda sa ātmā svāśrayāśrayaḥ*

**All three of the above-mentioned stages of different living entities are interdependent. In the absence of one, another is not understood. But the Supreme Being who sees every one of them, as the shelter of the shelter, is independent of all, and therefore He is the supreme shelter.**

**Bhāgavata Purāṇa 2.10.9**

Or, to put another way: “In the absence of one, the others cannot function. The one who knows these three is the true self. Its origin has no other origin.”

## A series of logical conclusions:

None of the three aspects of a person can be the true origin of consciousness, for they are interdependent. Consciousness is the origin of the three aspects.

The origin of consciousness is the ultimate consciousness, which is its own origin.

Consciousness is the origin and sustains reality and is personal.

Personal consciousness is distinct from its three interdependent components which manifest reality: identity, divinity, and tangibility.

Individual consciousness (atma) is the ultimate origin of its own three components, but not of them all, therefore, there is an ultimate origin beyond individual consciousness.

That consciousness (Paramatma), is the ultimate Origin of Reality.

## The breakthrough moment

**As spiritual souls we live thousands of lifetimes, and in each of them we learn precious lessons. Eventually, if sufficient wisdom prevails, we undergo a spiritual awakening, a moment of clarity that opens our inner eyes to a new panorama of reality.**

In Western Christianity that moment is sometimes explained as ‘being saved.’ It has given birth to outpourings of the spirit in hymns with words such as:

“Tis mercy all, immense and free; for O my God, it found out me. Fast bound in sin and nature’s night; I woke, the dungeon flamed with light; my chains fell off, my heart was free, I rose, went forth, and followed thee.”

Stories abound of such moments when a life was changed after a religious ‘conversion.’ Notable historical figures such as Saint Francis in Europe, and Mahavira and Gautama Buddha in India, tell of their own experiences and of how their lives took a new direction afterwards.

The Upanishads explain that it is like someone who has lost their memory. The amnesia is so profound that there is no recollection of the years before. A man forgets who he is and lives under an assumed identity. A story is told to convey the idea.

“Once there was a king who took his young son out hunting with him. Riding through a deep forest, a skirmish with some local bandits broke out, and the child became separated from his father. He was taken in by some kindly pig farmers who lived in the forest and the child grew up to become a pig farmer himself. He completely forgot who he was, and every day thought: “I am a pig farmer.” When he was grown, he one day happened to meet the king’s minister who recognized him. “You are not a pig farmer,” he told him, “you are the beloved son of the king.” Thereupon, the young man gave up the idea that he was a pig farmer and accepted his royal identity.

In this tale, remembrance comes through the agency of someone who reminds the forgetful young man who he was. Before this life-changing moment arrives, and one converts or ‘turns around,’ to a new life, a person may be looking at his situation and thinking: “I don’t quite fit in here,” or “There must be more to life than this.” This attitude of doubt and questioning will make it easier when the messenger of God arrives on the scene.

In the Shrimad Bhagavatam, the great epic of such conversion stories, we find a description of the sadhu, the self-realized person whose every movement is filled with wisdom, compassion, and grace, and who tries to help others on their path to enlightenment. The saint and spiritual master, Narada Muni, tells of his own moment, when he met those who changed his life. They were travelling sadhus who, during the rainy monsoon season, took shelter at the grass hut he shared with his poor mother. They spoke to him kindly, and his mother cooked for them:

“Once only, by their permission, I took the remnants of their food, and by so doing all my sins were at once eradicated. Thus, being engaged, I became purified in heart, and at that time the very nature of the transcendentalist became attractive to me.”

The sadhu’s words and kindness are so liberating, they are compared to nothing less than being set free from captivity. There is another story from the Chandogya Upanishad.

“There was a man from the city of Gandhara (modern day Kandahar, Afghanistan) who was captured by robbers and taken to a remote place in the desert. He awoke to find himself blindfolded, with his hands tightly bound. “Where am I?” he thought, “and how shall I now find my way home?”

“To the south or the north of me there might be wild animals and I may be set upon and killed. To the east and west of me there may be more desert, or deep canyons into which I may fall. How shall I find my way back home?” Thinking in this way he despaired and wept. Then there came to that place a traveller who, filled with compassion at the poor man’s plight, and being equipped with a sharp knife, cut the blindfold, and freed the man’s hands. The traveller then gave him directions, and the man rejoiced and made his way back home, to the beautiful city of Gandhara.”

In this ancient tale, the blindfolded and bound man is the spirit, temporarily housed in the body; the compassionate traveler is the sadhu who sets him free; and returning home to Gandhara is the spirit finding its way home to the eternal destination. Just as someone with amnesia is presented with a photograph album to remind them of their life, or has old songs sung to them to bring back their lost memories, so the sadhus remind us of our eternal home by creating word pictures and singing songs.

But to make the most of the moments of remembrance, we must perpetuate them by daily application. By repeating sacred descriptions and songs, the keys to regaining our memory, we can regain clarity each day. Yet another story is told to illustrate this point:

“In the deep ocean there lived a giant turtle. Every thousand years he would come to the surface of the water and fill his lungs with air. Then he went down again, to be submerged for another thousand years. On the surface of the vast ocean there floated a plank of wood. The waves took it here and there, and the wind blew it for many miles. It was always moving. In this plank of wood, there was one hole. Once, when the turtle rose to the surface, its head poked through that hole, and it could not retract it. From then on, the turtle was carried along on the surface of the water.”

This fable compares the soul to the turtle, and the ocean to the world of samsara, of repeated birth and death. Once in thousands of years, the soul rises to the level of human consciousness and receives a human body, just fit for self-realization. The chances of the turtle putting its head through one hole, in one piece of wood, in such a vast ocean, once in a thousand years, is astronomically vast. But it happened. Similarly, the chances for the soul in the human form of life, meeting a sadhu and being carried along through successive human lives – without having to descend to the animal species ever again – seems unimaginably unlikely. Yet still it happens.

The ancient method for reminding the inner self of its eternal nature and the joy of the ultimate destination, is known as sadhana or ‘the means of attainment.’ While the practical techniques vary, the process is ultimately one: gentle reminders of our higher, joyful inner nature by meditation on the name, form and characteristics of Krishna and our relationship with him.

## Foundations of bhakti

**Throughout history, those seeking the spiritual life have been impelled by an intense desire to escape material life. Material life offers limited enjoyment at a great price, with the inevitability of increased disappointment and anxiety. Spiritual life offers increased happiness and a sense of well-being, clarity of purpose, and freedom from future entanglement in karmic reaction. But even before bhakti-yoga can begin, the aspirant must put in place moral and ethical foundations.**

It is for this reason that men and women have taken themselves to solitude, in forests, deserts and mountains, hermitages and monasteries, and have undertaken what externally appears to be a life of rigorous discipline, but is in fact a life of increasing enlightenment and joy. Early attempts at the contemplative life almost always take the form of attempts at controlling the wandering mind. In classical literature, the mind is said to be like a wildly out of control chariot, pulled by five runaway horses, the senses. The terrified passenger is the helpless inner self.

The mind is the focal point in all spiritual systems, in all religions. If the idle mind is the Devil’s workshop, then a peaceful mind is the beginning of the upward path. It might even be said that the mind is the problem, and yoga, the solution. Thoughts come and go within the mind due to attraction and aversion, both of which arise from the movements of the tri-guna, or three modes of nature. Since the mind will go wherever the attention focuses, a desire pushes the mind to dwell on a particular object, to meditate on it, and imagine a future state of enjoyment that might be possible by engagement with that object.

When desire gives way to physical speech and action, the individual is immediately caught in a cycle of wish-fulfilment followed by disappointment, frustration, anger, and bewilderment. Then the cycle of desire begins again. In this way, the mind is bound to this world by a network of desires, cravings, foolish attachments, and addictions.

Krishna declares that, ‘Lust, greed, and anger are the three gates leading to Hell,’ and the Upanishads mention the ari-shad-varga or ‘the group of six enemies.’ The mind is compared to a traveller through life who is always stalked by six bandits. Although they seem to be friendly, travelling companions, they mean nothing but harm. They are as follows:

1\. Kama – Lust or craving, intense desire.

2\. Krodha – Anger, hatred.

3\. Lobha – Greed, miserliness.

4\. Moha – Delusory emotional attachment, temptation.

5\. Mada – Pride, arrogance, stubborn-mindedness.

6\. Matsarya – Envy, jealousy, vanity, pride.

When these ‘six bandits’ push us towards the pleasures of the world, they ultimately steal from us. Gains results in losses. Pleasures gives rise to pain. Krishna cautions in the Gita: “An intelligent person does not take part in the sources of misery, which are due to contact with the material senses. Such pleasures have a beginning and an end, and so the wise man does not delight in them.”

So, when our actions and their subsequent reactions amount to nothing but frustration, we look to the causes. That is why the first step in spiritual life is always to understand the mind and its ‘six friends.’

In the early days of Christianity, in the year 375, the monk Evagrius, living in the desert, compiled his ‘eight terrible thoughts from which all sins arise,’ and his list reveals that the ‘enemies’ of the soul, those that interfere with our deepest spiritual aspirations, are not Indian or Hindu, but universal.

## The Removal of Enemies

Knowing that bandits are on our path, we can either take steps to avoid them by walking in the opposite direction or have them removed by a stronger power. Bringing the mind under control is thus the beginning of the spiritual life, and the first step in yoga.

Spiritual adepts of all religious paths first develop inner determination to avoid temptation, and then they remove their favoured objects of temptation from their daily life. This may mean moving to an environment suitable for peaceful contemplation or choosing to move in very different circles of friendship. The yoga system is designed so that the mind can be freed from agitation or chitta-vritti, just as a lake can be freed from ripples. In the Shrimad Bhagavatam, the great sage and empowered incarnation Kapila provides a classic description of steps to free the mind:

*ahiṁsā satyam asteyaṁ yāvad-artha-parigrahaḥ*  
*brahmacaryaṁ tapaḥ śaucaṁ svādhyāyaḥ puruṣārcanam*

**One should practice nonviolence and truthfulness, should avoid thieving and be satisfied with possessing as much as he needs for his maintenance. He should abstain from sex life, perform austerity, be clean, study the Vedas and worship the form of the Supreme Personality of Godhead.**

**Shrimad Bhagavatam 3.28.4**

And in his Yoga Sutras, the sage Patanjali Muni provides the preparatory rules for his path of ‘eight-limbed yoga,’ the famous Yama and Niyama. After declaring that the state of yoga is one in which the changing states of mind are stilled, when the mind is no longer disturbed by the pushing and pulling of the ‘six enemies,’ he proceeds to explain how it can be done:

*ahimsa satya asteya brahmacarya aprigraha yamah*

**The abstentions are nonviolence, truthfulness, refrainment from stealing, celibacy, and renunciation of unnecessary possessions.**

*saucha santosh tapah svadhyaya ishvara pranidhana*

**The observances are cleanliness, contentment, austerity, study of scripture, and devotion to God.**

**Yoga Sutras 2.30 and 2.32**

These Yama and Niyama are the codes of thought and living for the aspiring yogi. By practising self-discipline and engaging in purifying acts of virtuous and ethical conduct, a break with the habits of the past can be made and a new future created. The yama and niyama principles work together to enhance health and positive mental attitude. Applied in a social setting, they are the foundations of living a life of dharma.

Yama means ‘virtuous self-restraint’ and involves harnessing the outgoing senses and restraining the mind from contemplation or acting on anything that causes further engagement in the cycle of sense enjoyment. They are the ‘don’t do these things’ of yoga practice.

Niyama means the positive mental attitudes and precepts that lead to peace of mind, tranquillity, and a higher state of awareness. From that position spiritual advancement can be made. Niyamas are the ‘do these things’ of yoga practice.

The yama and niyama are not simply codified behaviour for yoga practice; they are also statements of an ethical and dharmic life. For instance, someone who is not inclined to the rigours of yoga discipline but simply wishes to lead a good and virtuous life, in a peaceful community, would still do well to follow yama and niyama, both privately and socially. Yama and Niyama can thus be called ‘universal principles.’

The yoga concepts of actions that lead us to spiritual freedom and other actions that imprison us have their reflection in the notions of good and evil, pious action and sin, so prevalent in the religions of the world.

The old word ‘sin’ is a mediaeval archery term invoked when the bowman does not reach his target; “Oh, I have sinned!” The word also means when the actions of a human do not hit the target set up by God. To sin means to shoot the arrow of life and miss the mark. The Sanskrit equivalent is papa, the opposite of punya or ‘good deeds.’ When a person engages in bad deeds, or papa, they create ‘bad karma,’ and when they engage in punya they create ‘good karma.’ The life of dharma means to avoid papa and to do punya; and the life of a yogi means to follow yama and niyama which are, not surprisingly, synonymous with papa and punya, good and bad karma, virtue and vice.

The ‘four pillars’ of virtue, the four supports that every other act of goodness rests on are cleanliness, mercy, austerity and truth, or saucam, daya, tapa and satyam. Conversely, a life of vice is built upon foundations that are quite the opposite: dirtiness, cruelty, indulgence, and deceit.

## Snakes and Ladders

The game of snakes and ladders was played throughout the ancient world. The board, just as today, was divided into squares with depictions of snakes dragging the player down, and ladders taking the player up. In ancient India, and in many Buddhist countries, by the side of each snake’s head was a colourful depiction of a classical sin, such as theft, lying or adultery. Next to the bottom of the ladders were depictions of ‘good karma’ such as feeding the poor, helping the blind, or planting a fruit tree. In this way, players, especially the young, would be educated to know what was good or bad.

The Shrimad Bhagavatam tells of the Emperor Parikshit touring his vast kingdom. He came upon a man mercilessly beating a bull. When he threatened to punish the man for his cruelty, he discovered that the man was none other than the personification of the age, Kali. Ready to personally chastise the offender, Parikshit’s heart was softened when Kali fell to his knees and pleaded for mercy. The emperor relented and allowed Kali to dwell wherever gambling, drinking, prostitution, and animal slaughter was going on. But since none of those activities were going on in the kingdom, he also gave him permission to reside wherever gold was being stored, ‘because wherever there is gold there is also falsity, intoxication, lust, envy and enmity.’

The ‘four regulative principles’ recited by modern Vaishnavas when they formally begin their spiritual lives at the time of initiation – ‘no intoxication, no meat-eating, no gambling, no illicit sex’ - are particularly connected to avoidance of these principles of adharma. Intrinsic in that vow is the corollary promise – to follow the four principles of dharma: Austerity, Cleanliness, Mercy and Truth. Other virtues can be traced to these four. Ethics, morality, and freedom of the spirit can largely be said to be found in these ‘universal principles of freedom.’ And care not to be tempted by the ‘love of money’ will help to preserve them.

Foundations such as these will help the aspiring Vaishnava to always be influenced more by sattva guna, and in a social setting, help his interactions with others.
