Inter-personal morals and ethical behaviour are more easily achieved when internal morality is practised as a habit, and important choices are made at moments free from consuming selfishness.

Abstaining from impure foods, practising restraint, remaining peaceful, and being disciplined in eating and sleeping are one aspect of the Vaishnava life. When dealing with others, maintaining himself by an honest occupation, becoming free from anger, harshness, and the tendency to lie or cheat are others.

Another aspect, and perhaps a ‘seventh bandit’ along the road less travelled, is the thought that, since spiritual dynamics are above moral and ethical choices, the Vaishnava can jettison ethical behaviour at a certain point. This is a grand mistake and arises from a lingering conceit that affects the practitioner at a crucial time on his journey. It tends to happen after spiritual advancement has been experienced, but inadequately processed. It is hard to remove. Known in western theology as antinomianism or avoidance of religious rules on the pretext of serving God ‘on a higher level,’ it must be scrupulously avoided. The path of the Vaishnava cannot feature such claims of premature transcendence.

To keep the devotee firmly grounded in practice, to develop sattva-guna within him, and to help him always remember that everything in this world is owned and controlled by God, the ‘regulative principles’ form a moral and ethical foundation for the rest of the practice. Avoidance of meat, fish, eggs, or anything containing their parts or ingredients, and fasting from food or grains on holy days; refraining from illicit sexual relationships; freedom from consumption of intoxicating drinks or other substances; and avoiding gambling are four. A traditional fifth principle involves the exercise of great care regarding money, and especially accumulated wealth which may tempt the Vaishnava away from his dedicated life.

With this foundation in place, the next level of the transformed life can take place. Writing in the 11th century, Ramanuja Acharya offers ‘Seven Sadhanas’ for the foundational life of an aspiring bhakti-yogi:

Viveka – purification of the mind for concentration by taking only pure food.

Vimoka – relinquishing all self-referent, or self-cherishing, desires. Rather, the opposite should be cultivated – loka-sangraha – the desire for the welfare of all sentient beings.

Abhyas – steady and continuous practice of contemplation on one object.

Kriya – doing one’s duties, both religious and secular.

Kalyana – the cultivation of good qualities such as compassion, mercy, charity etc.

Anavasada – to avoid grieving over suffering or loss. Not becoming depressed when something disagreeable happens.

Anuddharsa – contentment and satisfaction with what one already has. To avoid exultation over achievement and obtaining what is agreeable.

## Varna and ashram

**If we cannot move to the forest or monastery, our spiritual life must be practised in a community. To have our basic needs met with a minimum of difficulty we form families, groups, societies, and nations. Because our needs are numerous, we depend upon others acting in different capacities. Those persons also form groups to better provide us with their respective commodities and services.**

One group grows food and another group transports and sells it. Another group educates our children, and another protects us from harm. Each person in a society is a part of an interconnected and inter-dependent social network. Maintenance of the society as an integrated whole – without which the society collapses - depends very much on mutually shared values and the citizens agreement to relate to each other based on those common values. And yet many values we hold will be different from one another, often because of the work we perform.

For instance, the doctor makes a vow to preserve life at all costs, whereas a soldier is trained to protect by taking a life if necessary. They both may use a sharp blade to do their work. Somehow, they must honour each other’s contribution to society and see their mutual value.

The Vedas provide for varying value systems by maintaining that, although each person is equally a spark of the divine, the load of karma-phala that each carries is different, and will propel them towards certain qualities of work.

Our inherited psycho-physical nature gives us an aptitude and innate ability - and often a real love - for physical and mental tasks. Therefore, what we consider most important in life – our personal value system - will be different from our neighbours. Given our differences, how can we all get along? The Vedas say that society can work as one peaceful, functional, harmonious unit if we all agree on three things:

1\. We are all trying to make spiritual progress to the best of our capacity.

2\. Although spiritually equal, we are diverse in our modes of values and work.

3\. Being different from each other is fine and helps us meet our needs.

4\. We must agree to follow some common principles that harmonise us.

Although there are thousands of different occupations, Vedic culture provides four umbrella classifications, each of which will have general rules. These are known as the varnas. There is the brahmana or teacher, the kshatriya or executive, the vaisya or business person, and the shudra or those in directed employment. These are divisions of labour based on the modes of nature dominant in the individual’s psychology, physiology, and work tendency.

The brahmanas are those predominantly influenced by the mode of goodness; the kshatriyas by passion; the vaishyas by mixed passion and ignorance; and the shudras by ignorance.

When the brahmanas are educating and offering guidance to society, the kshatriyas protecting and serving society by executing that guidance; the vaishyas producing and trading, and the shudras employed in the service of the other three, society functions well. When the positions and roles in society are taken up by those unqualified for the task society becomes weak and may divide and collapse. Therefore, having the person with the correct qualities for the job is one of the main keys to a peaceful and productive society.

Society organised according to the four varnas is not the so-called ‘caste system’ where oppression, exploitation and social immobility are the hallmarks. Rather it is the reverse. The varnas simply give recognition to the inherent tendencies of an individual and therefore the occupation they would be most suited for.

The modern, industrial society often allocates ‘jobs’ regardless of the person’s fitness or real love for the work. The citizens have been herded together in cities that benefit the industrialist but threaten the quality of life. Executive leadership is in the hands of merchants instead of true statesmen; guidance and social leadership is in the hands of those who are unfit and have no vision; education is dominated by those who prefer fashionable theories rather than tried and tested principles; and people who would love to be in the countryside peacefully farming are labouring in factories producing useless objects.

## Four stages of life

In life each of us is born, grows, reproduces, dwindles, and dies. The life cycle can help us to better our chances of spiritual progress if we use our main strengths in each phase. Taking consideration of our needs at each point, the ‘four stages of life’ offer appropriate modes of living that help our progress. Because they offer us ashraya or ‘shelter,’ they are known as ashrams and are summed up as Learn, Live, Withdraw, Detach.

Learn: To study the Vedas under the instruction of a qualified teacher, a guru, one who has knowledge and experience, and can educate others with practical guidance, encouragement, and patience. This period is known as brahmacharya, or ‘walking in the ways of the divine,’ and is characterised by learning, discipline, and celibacy. The period includes spiritual education and vocational training. For brahmanas the formal period may last for many years, for others a year or two.

Live: To marry, have children, work to support the family and society. This period is known as grihasta-ashram or the ‘householder’ phase. It is characterised by charity to members of the other three ashrams. Marriage is considered a binding commitment, and children the natural fruit of marriage. Working honestly according to one’s varna (occupational duties) and then performing yajna or sacrifice through godly conduct and good deeds for the benefit of others are the mark of the householder.

Withdraw: With the birth of the grandchildren some, but not all, may retire from active work to reflect and share their experience and life-learning with younger members of society. This stage is characterised by a reduction in the pursuit of ephemeral enjoyment and an increase in striving for spiritual progress. The stage may be accompanied by husband and wife travelling to holy places together and a re-dedication to sacred learning. Because this travelling previously involved taking the pilgrimage path through the countryside, it is known as vanaprastha-ashram or ‘entering the forest.’

Detach: Only the minority of husbands will take the final step of separating from family and circle of friends to become a wandering ascetic. This stage involves shaving the hair, wearing the saffron robe, and taking up the begging bowl; staying for only a few days in each town and eating only after midday. There are strict vows for this stage of life, known as sannyasa, or ‘laying it all down.’ Once the husband has left home, he must never return to his wife, leaving her in the care of his son and family. He remains strictly celibate, not even allowing himself to think of the former pleasures of family life. The sannyasi is again a monk, as in his youth, but this time without a monastery or a fixed abode. Although he is considered civilly dead, the sannyasi is regarded, wherever he walks, as the living witness of God’s grace. Rather than a lonely existence he is said to have made the entire world his family and is regarded as the guru of all the varnas and ashrams.

When the citizens of a nation aspire for self-realisation and follow the Vedic injunctions, and when they organise themselves into four occupational orders (varnas) and act according to the four stages of life (ashrams) they are known as members of Vedic society.

The Vedic cultural matrix is not an artificial imposition, or the culture of a long-dead civilisation. Neither is it a curb to the freedom that everyone seeks. Rather it is a framework for peaceful living and for realising the greatest dreams and hopes of the civilised human being.

## Scoring the goals of life

**What is the goal of life? We want physical and mental pleasures as a reward for our labour – without waiting too long for them. We want a sense of happiness and contentment, and the respect, friendship and love of family and society. We want to do good works too and help others in need or crisis. But what does it all lead to?**

Aspiring for more, we want all these things in the afterlife, if there is one. We want God if he is there too, but our desire for God gets mixed up with desires for all the other things we think will make us happy. That jumble of earthly and divine aspirations creates mundane religion. Later, if we begin to awaken spiritually and cannot find happiness in worldly pleasure – either here or in the hereafter - our goal becomes liberation from the cycle of birth and re-birth, or moksha.

In the Sanskrit language, the goals of life are known as the purusarthas. The first three, dharma, artha and kama usually come as a package, one the result of the one before. When the soul realises that lasting happiness is not on offer in the world, he progresses towards a desire for selfrealisation. But even this upward move is tainted with selfish motive. Only until the soul places God as the object of his life will bhakti or love of God begin.

Dharma – personal restraint, ethical principles, and good works for sentient creatures.Dharma can be summed up in the following three virtues: -

**The Eternal Duty (Sanatana Dharma) towards all creatures is the absence of malevolence towards them in thought, deed, or word, and to practice compassion and charity towards them.**

**Mahabharata Vana Parva 297.35.**

Adroha is the absence of all negative feelings which result from being prejudiced. All judgement must be suspended before one can begin the practice of unconditional love and compassion - anugraha. The presence of sincere compassion will naturally lead to charity in the form of self-giving and service, or danam.

Artha – possessions acquired through honest work within the codes of the varna-ashram social system. Everyone must work according to their psycho-physical nature. The fruits of that work are used for maintenance of family, position in society and reputation, and the surplus then given in charity. Artha does not mean to exploit those around us, or to achieve needless profit at the expense of our own health.

Kama – enjoyment in this life and the next. Pleasure and power, health, and happiness. (Note: the word kama, or enjoyment, has nothing to do with the word karma, which means action).

Moksha - Atmanu-bhava – freedom from the attachments and aversions which come because of kama. Eventual release from the cycle of birth and death.

Moksha - Bhagavad-bhava – awakening of the dormant love for Krishna. In the Bhagavad-gita, Krishna says that progress towards Moksha while denying the personhood of God and the cultivation of devotion will take a long time. But after many births and deaths that soul finally comes to realise that Vasudeva sarvam iti – that ‘Vasudeva is everything.’ Whereas the initial platform of progress is self-realisation, it is an inferior stage to that of God-realisation. The first means coming to know one’s spiritual identity, the second coming to know of one’s eternal relationship with God. This is achieved in progressive stages beginning with a special type of curiosity in the figure of Krishna or the scriptures that speak of Him.

There are five kinds of liberation. The first is an impersonal liberation and the remaining four are personal, involving some relation with the Personality of Godhead.

1. Sayujya - Sāyujya means merging into the Brahman effulgence of the Lord.
2. Sālokya means that after material liberation one is promoted to the planet where the Supreme Personality of Godhead resides.
3. Sāmīpya means remaining an associate of the Supreme Personality of Godhead.
4. Sārūpya means attaining a four-handed form exactly like that of the Lord.
5. Sārṣṭi means attaining opulences like those of the Supreme Lord.

Srila Prabhupada comments on the hierarchy of goals of the soul:

“The less intelligent persons in the material world are engaged by the four principles of benediction called dharma, artha, kāma and mokṣa. Generally, they prefer to take to religious life to achieve some material benediction, the purpose of which is to satisfy the senses. When, by that process, they become confused or frustrated in fulfilling the maximum amount of sense enjoyment, they try to become one with the Supreme, which is, according to their conception, mukti, (moksha) or liberation.

There are five kinds of liberation, the least important of which is called sāyujya, to become one with the Supreme. Devotees don't care for such liberation because they are actually intelligent. Nor are they inclined to accept any of the other four kinds of liberation, namely to live on the same planet as the Lord, to live with Him side by side as an associate, to have the same opulence, and to attain the same bodily features. They are concerned only with glorifying the Supreme Lord and His auspicious activities.”

Sayujya moksha is a great temptation for the soul. It bestows a freedom from the repetition of birth and death, a release from the never-ending cycle of birth, old age, dwindling and disease; and negates the troublesome aspects of reconciling oneself to God. There is no real attempt to understand the personal nature of God, to serve or love Him. Although the soul seeking sayujya will recite hymns and wave lamps before the image of Vishnu and ask for his blessings, he does so with only one purpose: so that he never has to do it again.

If he is successful in his determination for this Godless form of moksha the soul becomes kevala or ‘isolated,’ and enjoys the brahma-nirvana, the apparent oneness of the divine Brahman. He experiences a universal awareness, the relief of freedom from death, and a feeling of boundless joy. But after countless thousands of years, the soul again becomes restless. The joy of impersonal Brahman cannot satisfy him and again he takes birth within the world. It is then that he has another chance of achieving his heart’s true desire: unalloyed love for Krishna.

Therefore, great Vaishnavas describe impersonal liberation as ‘the last snare of maya.’ Having struggled through all the 8,400,000 species of life, gradually elevating our consciousness lifetime by lifetime, we fall at the last hurdle after many, many thousands of years. We are warned therefore to guard against this, and to regard it as the chief virodhi or spiritual obstacle of the soul. Śrī Prabodhānanda Sarasvatī, a great devotee of Lord Caitanya, said, kaivalyaṁ narakāyate : "The happiness of becoming one with the Supreme Lord, which is aspired for by the Māyāvādīs, is considered hellish."

## Bhakti

**The opening verse of the fourth century Sankhya Karika explains that an awareness of the suffering and frustration inherent in embodied existence is a prerequisite for seeking higher truth. “It is because of the threefold sufferings that the desire to know the means to counteract them arises.”**

The very first dawning of insight, viveka, whereby the jiva takes to any kind of yoga is the realisation that ‘all is frustration’ when one is under the influence of avidya, or ignorance.

## The soul’s nature

Although the soul is covered by the material energy, its intrinsic and natural qualities are all preserved, however non-manifest they may be. The process of yoga serves to reduce Maya’s influence and to progressively reveal these innate characteristics. The sage Gautama lists the natural qualities of the soul:

Compassionate

Forgiving

Without envy, greed, jealousy, or anger

Pure

Peaceful

Tolerant

Generous

Auspicious

Like clouds that apparently cover the sun, concealing its brilliance, the energies of Maya serve to conceal the illumination of both God and the brightness of the soul. As winds blow the clouds away, so do the Vedic hymns and mantras remove the obstacles to the soul as it re-discovers its relationship with God.

The jiva is by nature eternally related to Godhead and aspires only for unity in everlasting loving service in full bliss and knowledge. However, due to the countless lifetimes of its long sojourn in the material world, the soul also has doubts, confusions, and attachments. Like the scent of a wild beast lingers in the forest long after it has left the spot, so the fragrance of its thousands of previous lives still hovers in the air. Although it really thirsts only for bhakti, under the spell of upadhis, or ‘restricting qualifiers,’ the jiva engages in other endeavours such as karmic elevation, the path of worldly pleasure, whether this life or the next, and the search for impersonal liberation. In the former path, the jiva mistakes this world as being his possession to enjoy, whereas in the latter his mistake is that this world is his to renounce. Both karma (ritual acts) and jnana (gnosis) arise from mis-identifying the world.

These tendencies or practices mix with bhakti and serve to weaken its effect. Mixed bhakti is known by the name of the disposition and practice it is mixed with. Thus, bhakti mixed with karma becomes karma-mishra-bhakti and mixed with jnana becomes jnana-mishra-bhakti. Practicing either of these will render the goal of pure bhakti unachievable. Jiva Goswami writes: “How can one travelling east obtain an object moving west?”

Only bhakti is recognised in the Shrimad Bhagavatam as parama dharma or the highest and most satisfying function of the soul. In giving his first answer to the forest sages Suta Goswami said:

*sa vai puṁsāṁ paro dharmo / yato bhaktir adhokṣaje*  
*ahaituky apratihatā / yayātmā suprasīdati*

**The supreme dharma for all humanity is that by which men can attain to loving devotional service unto the transcendent Lord. Such devotional service must be unmotivated and uninterrupted to completely satisfy the self.**

**Shrimad Bhagavatam 1.2.6**

Narada Muni describes bhakti as ‘indescribable love,’ or anirvachaniyam prema swarupam and the grandest and most sublime of all experiences. Even one of history’s most famous Advaitins, Madhusudana Saraswati, (1540-1640) according to whom non-duality is the highest truth, concedes that ‘bhakti is one hundred times superior to liberation.’ He says that ‘one realises at the dawn of true knowledge that duality is even more beautiful than non-duality.’

The paths of karma, jnana and yoga are not eternal. They cease as soon as the goal is reached. Bhakti is the eternal and supreme function of the soul. It is both the means and the end. Bhakti is independent (nirpeksha) of karma, jnana, and yoga. They are all dependent on bhakti (bhakti sapeksha) for their efficacy. They cannot lead to happiness, liberation, or bliss without the aid of bhakti. Yoga cannot even begin without bhakti as it involves ishvara pranidhan or surrender to God and has Paramatma realisation as its goal. Even jnana yoga relies on bhakti. The great Adi Shankara (AD 788-820) explained in his commentary on the Bhagavad-gita that:

Jnana-nishtha, or fidelity to knowledge, without which liberation is not possible, is itself the result of Archana-bhakti, which consists in the ceremonial worship of deities.

Commentary to Gita 8.56

The realisation of brahma-nirvana through jnana is also not permanent without bhakti. Shri Chaitanya speaks of two kinds of men who follow the path of jnana, those who do not have faith in Bhagavan and those who do. The former attains liberation and the state of immersion in Brahman after a great deal of effort, but there is every possibility of their again falling prey to Maya. The latter, who have faith in Bhagavan, attain brahma-nirvana more easily due to the grace of Bhagavan. He lets them enjoy this state for some time, but ultimately lifts them up so that they may enjoy a much higher pleasure. This is natural because bhakti, which conditions their jnana, is after all a potency of Bhagavan himself.

There is no fruit of karma, jnana, and yoga which cannot be attained by bhakti without the help of any other means. Mukti or liberation comes as a necessary accompaniment of bhakti, and jnana or knowledge, as well as vairagya (detachment) are themselves natural concomitants of bhakti. Jnana and vairagya as independent means of self-realisation are redundant to bhakti.

Although the paths of karma, jnana and yoga are hard and can only be done by those with resolve, determination, and the means at their disposal, bhakti can be performed by all.

## Definition of Bhakti

The word bhakti is derived from the Sanskrit root bhaj which means ‘division, a portion, to share.’ The sense is of sharing what one has with someone else. The word yoga is derived from the root yuj or union, so the joint term ‘bhakti-yoga’ indicates a union with God achieved by sharing what one has. While unity destroys a relationship, sharing establishes it. Without the deep understanding that God is other than oneself, no love can arise, but once arisen, that love leads to a sense of complete unity.

*bhaj ity esa vai dhatuh / sevayam parikirtitah*  
*tasmat seva budhaih prokta / bhaktih sadhana-bhuyasi*

**The root bhaj is used to convey service. Therefore, the wise explain bhakti as engagement in the service of the lord, and as a yet better practice.**

**Garuda Purana 231.3**

Srila Prabhupada would customarily translate bhakti not as ‘love’ but always ‘devotional service.’ As he would say, “If you love me, you do something for me. Bhakti is not just a matter of feelings.” On another occasion he explained:

“Devotional service is not a matter of sentimental speculation or imaginative ecstasy. Its substance is practical activity. Bhakti is a sort of cultivation. As soon as we say “cultivation,” we must refer to activity. Cultivation of spirituality does not mean sitting down idly for meditation, as some pseudo yogis teach. Such idle meditation may be good for those who have no information of devotional service, and for this reason it is sometimes recommended as a way to check distracting materialistic activities. Meditation means stopping all nonsensical activities, at least for the time being. Devotional service, however, not only puts an end to all nonsensical mundane activities, but also engages one in meaningful devotional activities.”

Under the instruction of his master, Shri Chaitanya Mahaprabhu, Rupa Goswami wrote his devotional handbook, the Bhakti-Rasamrita Sindhu. In the first few verses of the first chapter, he sets out his definitions:

*anyābhilāṣitā-śūnyaṁ / jñāna-karmādy-anāvṛtam*  
*ānukūlyena kṛṣṇānu-śīlanaṁ bhaktir uttama*

**One should render transcendental loving service to the Supreme Lord Kṛṣṇa favourably and without desire for material profit or gain through fruitive activities or philosophical speculation. That is called pure devotional service.**

**Bhakti Rasamrita Sindhu 1.1.11**

In the very next verse, he quotes from the Narada Pancharatra another definition of pure bhakti:

*sarvopadhi-vinirmuktam tat-paratvena nirmalam*  
*hrsikena hrsikesa-sevanam bhaktir ucyate*

**Bhakti, or devotional service, means engaging all our senses in the service of the Lord, the Supreme Personality of Godhead, the master of all the senses. When the spirit soul renders service unto the Supreme, there are two side effects. One is freed from all material designations, and one’s senses are purified simply by being employed in the service of the Lord.**

**Bhakti Rasamrita Sindhu 1.1.12**

Sometimes, those who are egoistic cannot fully appreciate the idea of service. They think that the path of bhakti is for those who are intellectually weak or temperamentally submissive. They cannot understand that in the spiritual world, where love reigns supreme, to serve is to love, and to love is to rule. In love, self-sacrifice is self-realisation, and self-effacement is self-fulfilment.

Though karma and jnana have no place in bhakti, still they are implied in it. One must have jnana, knowledge, of Krishna and act, karma, for his pleasure. Vairagya or detachment is involved, not in renouncing the objects of the world, but in purifying the senses in service to him.

It does not imply complete eradication of cravings and impulses, but a complete transformation or purification of them under the subordination of the central impulse of service to Krishna.

People speak of there being many different yogas, or paths to God realisation, but in truth there is only one. All paths are angas of bhakti, not different paths. While they cannot be successful if pursued without bhakti, the results of all of them are accrued by the practitioner of bhakti. In the Bhagavad-gita Shri Krishna explains the powerful position of bhakti:

*daivi hy esa guna-mayi / mama maya duratyaya*  
*mam eva ye prapadyante / mayam etam taranti te*

**This divine energy of mine, consisting of the gunas, is difficult to overcome. But those who have surrendered to me can easily cross beyond it.**

**Bhagavad-gita 7.14**

Later in the Gita, after revealing to his friend the reality of his divine nature, Krishna again explains that it is only by bhakti that he can be understood:

*bhaktya tv ananyaya sakya / aham evam-vidho 'rjuna*  
*jnatum drastum ca tattvena / pravestum ca parantapa*

**My dear Arjuna, only by undivided devotional service can I be understood as I am, standing before you, and can thus be seen directly. Only in this way can you enter the mysteries of my understanding.**

**Bhagavad-gita 11.55**

But bhakti leads not merely to ‘crossing beyond Maya,’ or even an ‘understanding’ of Krishna. It leads to much more. Bhakti in the devotee’s heart is a result of Krishna placing his hladini-shakti in him. This gives bliss to Krishna as well as to the devotee. Krishna is the supreme relisher of bliss, rasika-sekhara, and he relishes it more when it is placed in the hearts of his devotees (shaktyananda) than when it flows from the nature of his own self (swarupananda). Because of the gravitational force of the hladini-shakti the bhakta is drawn towards Bhagavan and Bhagavan surrenders himself to the bhakta. Grace is the surrender of Bhagavan to the bhakta.

God is of the nature of pure bliss and it is not possible for him to have a direct experience of the jiva’s suffering. Grace or mercy is extended to the jivas through the saintly persons who are themselves recipients of grace.

## The effects of bhakti

**What has been said above describes the essential nature (svarupa-lakshana) of bhakti as a function of the svarupa-shakti of Bhagavan. This section considers some of the non-essential characteristics (tatastha-lakshana) or effects which flow from it. In this signatory verse, Rupa Goswami follows his description of what bhakti is, with a description of the effect of bhakti:**

*klesaghni subhada moksha laghutakrt sudurlabhha*  
*sandrananda visesatma sri krishnakarshini ca sa*

**Bhakti removes suffering, bestows auspiciousness, makes light of liberation, is very rare, is composed of condensed bliss, and attracts Sri Krishna.**

**Bhakti Rasamrita Sindhu 1.1.17**

1\. Klesaghni (brings immediate relief from material distress, removing suffering

2\. Subhada (all auspicious)

3\. Moksh laghutakrit (derides the conception of liberation)

4\. Sudurlabha (pure devotional service is very rarely achieved)

5\. Sandrananda visesatma (is composed of condensed bliss)

6\. Sri Krishnakarshini (is the only means to attract Krishna)

Bhakti rids one of distress or klesha. Klesha is of three types: papa - sin, papa-bijam – seeds of sin, and avidya – ignorance that blinds one to the consequences of bad deeds and propels one toward them. Bhakti removes all three. Bhakti bestows good fortune. The word subha – good fortune or auspiciousness – means to ascend to a position of attracting the love and respect of everyone, to have available all varieties of pleasures and comforts that give joy, and to be adorned with all good qualities. When bhakti dawns, humility, compassion, humility, and magnanimous respect decorate the character of the jiva. As a result, the jiva is loved and respected by all. Bhakti derides even the concept of liberation. Even a touch of the ecstasy makes the jiva disinterested in dharma, artha, kama and moksha. Bhakti is a rarely attained goal. It is given by the lord to one whose bhajan is devoid of discrepancy. There are many other effects of bhakti mentioned in the sacred texts. Among them are the following:

1\. Bhakti leads to the fulfilment of all desires.

2\. It destroys all evil.

3\. It removes all impediments.

4\. It instantly relieves one of all fears and anxieties.

5\. It satisfies everyone.

6\. It is the breeding ground for all good qualities.

7\. It is above the three modes of nature.

8\. It is self-manifest.

9\. It is identical with supreme bliss

10\. Bhakti even subjugates the Lord.

## Sadhana Bhakti

Bhakti is either sadhana-bhakti or sadhya-bhakti. Sadhana-bhakti is the ‘means of achievement,’ and sadhya-bhakti is the end, the actual achievement. Sadhana means to repose the mind in Krishna by every conceivable means, and sadhya means the stage where this happens automatically. Sadhana bhakti consists of regulative devotional service, and the sadhya, or end, of the process is prema – devotional service imbued with divine love, at the elevated stage of perfect spiritual consciousness. In between sadhana-bhakti and sadhya-bhakti there is an ‘awakening’ stage. Bhaktivinode Thakur comments:

“Bhakti has three stages viz sadhana-bhakti, bhava-bhakti, and prema-bhakti. Sadhana-bhakti is that stage of culture when the feeling has not yet been roused. In bhava-bhakti the feeling awakes, and in prema-bhakti the feeling is fully set to action. Bhakti is a spiritual feeling towards the spiritual object of love.”

Of the ‘means of achievement’ or sadhana, there are two forms. Both are very similar, and have the same goal, but have different applications and prerequisites. They are known as vaidhi-sadhana and raganuga-sadhana. When bhakti is inspired by scriptural stipulations, known as vidhi, it is called vaidhi-bhakti. When bhakti is inspired by raga, or innate attraction to Krishna, it is known as raganuga-bhakti.

Sadhana-bhakti is also sometimes described as being either saguna - endowed with the three modes of nature,’ or nirguna – ‘devoid of the three modes of nature.’ These are different terms, but the idea is the same. Nirguna bhakti is said to be when the devotee’s mind, on hearing the qualities of the Lord, flows continuously without any interruption towards him. Bhakti is, of course, always nirguna, it only appears otherwise when the mind of the aspirant is reflected in it. In this stage bhakti is ahaituki – not related to a desire for anything else. It is also described as avyavahita – not interrupted by karma and jnana. It is also known as the ‘highest devotional service’ or uttama-bhakti.

Although there are many rules of bhakti practice, everything there is to know can be condensed into one simple maxim: “Always remember Krishna, and never forget him.”

*smartavyah satatam vishnur / vismartavyo na jatucit*  
*sarve vidhi-nishedhah syur / etayor eva kinkarah*

**Vishnu should always be remembered and never forgotten at any time. All the rules and prohibitions mentioned in the shastras (scriptures) should be the servants of these two principles.**

**Padma Purana, Uttara 42.103 and Narada-Pancharatra 4.2.23**

Every act of good karma can bring the actor benefit. Good action is also known as sukriti. When those previous good activities involve some small act of bhakti, such as a temple visit or charity given to a Vaishnava, the actions are known as bhakti-sukriti. When the auspicious results of such bhakti-sukriti is accumulated it gives the jiva the opportunity to come into the company of the Vaishnavas. Bhaktivinode Thakur comments:

“From bhakti-sukriti comes the opportunity to listen to advanced Vaishnavas; from this comes sraddha (faith), and from sraddha develops sharanapatti, the spirit of surrender to Krishna.”

## Finding an advanced Vaishnava

From these statements it can be understood that keeping company with advanced Vaishnavas is essential to developing the necessary faith and the spirit of surrender to Krishna. The Vaishnavas are alive to their relationship with Krishna and their solid conviction and joyful devotion are infectious.

In the 11th canto of the Shrimad Bhagavatam Krishna himself explains to his friend Uddhava the three-steps on the journey to finding such persons. He describes that the seeker’s own mind is the first ‘guru.’ After considering mentally the world around him, the seeker considers the nature of suffering and the need for escape. The second step is when the entire world seems to be his ‘guru.’ The trees, mountains, creatures of the forest, the humble bee, the bear, all teach him by their actions. Finally, he meets one person who embodies all the teachings he needs and reposes his faith in him.

## Surrender

**The word surrender means ‘to yield to the power or possession of another.’ It normally means that there has been a competition for resources, land, or desirable objects, and that one party has lost. Surrender to Krishna is a very different experience.**

The process of surrendering to Krishna begins with serious consideration of what one has heard from the Vaishnavas. This is followed by a series of deep internal adjustments, and then by applying oneself physically. In the 11th century, Ramanuja Acharya wrote:

“Learning of all the other three means to moksha, (karma, jnana and yoga) and contemplating one’s own inability to practise any of them, and knowing that God is the sole redeemer, the sole saviour, and promising to keep his laws unto death, contemplating his six qualities of bliss (ananda), compassion (daya), empathy (vatsalya), easy accessibility (saulabhya), affability (saushilya), and knowing that both chit and achit are his body (sharira), one surrenders to Him.”

Lord Krishna’s final instruction to Arjuna was ‘mam ekam sharanam vraja,’ – “surrender to me only.” With this concluding instruction, known as the charama sloka, Arjuna is asked to abandon all other paths to attain God and to simply turn to Krishna for surrender. In one sense, the word surrender is an unusual one to use. Krishna and Arjuna were not enemies, and there was no question of surrendering weapons, the normal procedure when one party in a conflict surrenders to the other. In this case, Arjuna’s surrender to Krishna was to pick up his weapon and to engage in battle, this time under Krishna’s order.

The word surrender means ‘to yield to the power or possession of another.’ It normally means that there has been a competition for resources, land, or desirable object, and that one party has lost. Part of the reluctant acceptance of their loss is that the losing party abandons their claim, their power, and disengages from the conflict completely.

Surrender for the soul is an entirely spiritual adjustment. It means to finally comprehend that the struggle for enjoyment in the world of matter is over, that it is now a lost cause, and a new path is to be followed. The soul comes to the point of comprehending that the world of matter belongs to someone else and that trying to enjoy someone’s property without their permission is theft, making them liable for punishment.

It also means to accept that they have a relationship with God and that there are consequences inherent in that relationship. Yet unlike a conquering opponent, God does not exactly force the soul into submission. He simply leaves that up to the material energy – over a long period of many lifetimes. When a soul senses that there is such a thing as a higher power, invisible but nonetheless real, perhaps a being of infinite power and grace, the soul responds and life changes.

## Six Aspects of Surrender

Surrender to God is therefore one of the core teachings of Pancharatra. It is known as Sharanagati, Prapatti or sometimes Sharanapatti. Traditionally there are six internal adjustments and corresponding external changes that must be made for the surrender to be complete. They are described in many Pancharatra samhitas (compilations) such as Ahirbudhnya

Samhita, Lakshmi Tantra etc., though the order of the limbs may vary. The Lakshmi Tantra provides this well-known verse describing them:

*anukulyasya sankalpah pratikulyasya varjanam*  
*raksisyatiti visvaso goptrtve varanam tatha*  
*atma-niksepa-karpanye sad-vidha saranagatih*

**The six divisions of surrender are: accepting those things favourable to devotional service, rejecting unfavourable things, the conviction that Krishna will give protection, accepting the Lord as one's guardian or master, full selfsurrender, and humility.**

**Lakshmi Tantra 17, 59-61**

A slightly variant translation of the six elements is as follows:

1\. a resolution to yield ('anukulasya sankalpah')

2\. the avoidance of things opposed to God ('pratikulasya varjanam')

3\. faith that God will protect ('raksisyati ity visvasah')

4\. acceptance of him as saviour ('goptrtva-varanam')

5\. throwing one's whole soul upon him ('atmaniksepah'), and

6\. a sense of helplessness ('karpanya').

Anukulya-nishcaya – Acceptance of everything favourable to devotional service. The resolve to do use every opportunity to please God. “I will do whatever is favourable for devotional service to Lord Krishna.”

Pratikulya-vivarjana - Giving up any conception, attachment, habit, or practise which is not favourable to devotional service. Abandoning everything not in keeping with the will of God. “I will avoid whatever is not favourable for devotional service to Lord Krishna.”

Vishvasa - Irrepressible and great faith in Godhead. Involves the understanding that God is the benefactor of all beings, and His compassion is always and readily available. The firm conviction that He will not fail to protect us is 'vishvasa'. This by itself can eradicate all sins. “Lord Krishna is my only protector.”

Goptr - The deliberate choice of Krishna as the sole protector. Even when it is intellectually understood that He is the sole protector, the Lakshmi Tantra points out that what is involved in this limb is the conscious and mindful selection of Him, the acknowledgment that we need protection, and that he only can protect us. “I am confident that Lord Krishna will always maintain me.”

Karpanya - This refers to absolute humility bordering on lowliness and honest awareness of one's own natural ignorance, impurity etc., partly because of stains acquired through countless births. “I am very poor and worthless.”

Atma-nikshepa - Placing oneself completely and directly under God's care. This involves giving up independent ownership in matters of actions or fruits thereof. “It is not good that I desire to be independent. I should do whatever Lord Krishna desires."

Some scriptures say that atma-nikshepa, or complete self-surrender, is the main act and that the other elements are its five limbs. Other scriptures list the limbs in a different order. The great Vaishnava commentator Vedanta Deshika has addressed this potential confusion in his NyР”Рѓsa Vimsati, verse twelve, and concludes that those who know the scriptures explaining the concept of surrender agree that there are five limbs to surrender, and the sequences given really say the same thing in the end: sarva vākya eka kanthyam.

Therefore, he says:

“Oh, my Master! I surrender at Your lotus feet the total responsibility for my protection. I have no other means. I will perform only the duties that please You (anukulya sankalpam). I will not transgress the codes of conduct prescribed by You in Your shastras (prathikulya varjanam).

I am totally unfit for pursuit of any paths of deliverance: hence I stand before you with utter humility about my helplessness (karpanyam). I have total and enduring faith in your coming to my protection (Maha vishvasam) and I entrust my protection entirely to you with this prayerful appeal (Goptrutva varanam). Here Swami Sri Deshikan performs prapatti with its five limbs.”

From Vedanta Deshika’s Nyasa Dasakam

Jiva Goswami concludes that actively choosing Bhagavan as one’s sole protector is the central element in the state of sharanapatti, and the other elements are auxiliaries. Next in importance is the attitude of resignation, which implies the conviction on the part of the devotee that he is not the doer and that an invisible power moves him and makes him do what it wills. Karpanya is a state free from self-conceit or ego. In this state the devotee thinks: “Oh Lord! There is none so merciful as you, and none so low and deserving of your mercy as me.” Writing in his Bhakti Sandarbha, Jiva Goswami clarifies this point:

“God is not so close to anyone as he is to a person free from conceit, while he is not so far away from anyone as he is from a man who is full of conceit.”

A devotee surrenders to the Lord just as a small kitten totally ‘surrenders’ to her mother’s mouth and is carried everywhere. Even though the mother’s teeth could crush her, the kitten feels safe. Krishna also carries His devotees like this – but only if the surrender is sincere. A struggling kitten cannot be carried very well. Similarly, any reluctance in any one of the limbs of sharanagati is akin to ‘struggling.’ But when the surrender is complete there is such a spiritual force flowing through the devotee there can be no lack of inspiration and devotional activity. In another poem, Vedanta Deshika addresses the Lord saying:

“My dear Lord, you are like a bird-seller and I am like a bird with a string tied around its leg. You pull me towards you, and I keep flapping my wings trying to escape. I may always try to fly away from you my Lord, but please keep pulling me towards you.”

Despite its elevated spirit of surrender, Bhaktivinode Thakur says that ‘sharanagati is the threshold of the house of bhakti.’

## The guru

**The process of sharanagati and sadhana-bhakti is taught by a guru, and when the student formally surrenders to Krishna, it is the guru who accepts that surrender on the Lord’s behalf.**

The guru knows that he is merely a representative of Krishna and considers himself as sishya or ‘one under discipline,’ a disciple. He regards himself only as the Lord’s menial servant, and a servant of his own guru. The disciple also knows that the guru is Krishna’s representative, but regards him as prabhu or ‘master.’ In this classical and sacred relationship, respect flows upwards to the guru, and grace flows downwards to the disciple.

It is important that the guru is one who represents a lineage of teachers and conveys the accumulated wisdom of thousands of years of experience to his students. He must be learned in the scriptures and have realised their conclusion. He must visibly demonstrate detachment from the enjoyments of the world and be fixed in his enjoyment of the higher world. He must live simply and be affectionate and generous with his disciples. Above all, Krishna explains, “He must be my surrendered devotee.”

The classical description of how to approach such a guru is that the prospective student must bring with him some firewood in his hands, gathered as a gift to help the guru with his daily ceremonial fire sacrifices. In this way he demonstrates that, in return for the priceless knowledge he will be learning, and for the guidance and discipline he will receive, he stands ready to reciprocate with humble service.

A person who has a strong attraction to the message of the scriptures, who has developed faith, who wishes to dedicate his life, is qualified to engage in unalloyed devotional service. When a person becomes qualified in this way, he becomes very eager to learn the science of devotional service. To learn it, he accepts the shelter of the lotus feet of a bona fide spiritual master. It is written:

*tad-vijnanartham sa gurum evabhigacchet*  
*samit-panih shrotriyam brahma-nishtham*

**To learn transcendental subject matter, one must approach the spiritual master. In doing so, he should carry fuel to burn in sacrifice. The symptom of such a spiritual master is that he is expert in understanding the Vedic conclusion, and therefore he constantly engages in the service of the Supreme Personality of Godhead.**

**Mundaka Upanishad 1.2.12**

The Chandogya Upanishad 6.14.2 declares that: acharyavan purusho veda - "One who approaches a bona fide spiritual master can understand everything about spiritual realisation."

And the Shrimad Bhagavatam declares that the guru will give the disciple real happiness providing he is learned, realised, and convincing in his presentations and guidance. The main qualification is that they have taken complete shelter of God:

*tasmād guruṁ prapa dyeta jijñāsuḥ śr eya uttamam*  
*śābde pare ca niṣṇātaṁ brahmaṇy upaśamāśr ayam*

**"Any person who is seriously desirous of achieving real happiness must seek out a bona fide spiritual master and take shelter of him by initiation. The qualification of a spiritual master is that he must have realized the conclusion of the scriptures by deliberation and arguments and thus be able to convince others of these conclusions. Such great personalities, who have taken complete shelter of the Supreme Godhead, leaving aside all material considerations, are to be understood as bona fide spiritual masters."**

**Shrimad Bhagavatam 11.3.21**

In his teachings, Shri Chaitanya Mahaprabhu described many elements of the life of bhakti, and the first five of those concern the guru:

(1) One must accept a bona fide spiritual master. (2) Accept initiation from him. (3) Serve him.

(4) Receive instructions from the spiritual master and make inquiries to learn devotional service.

(5) Follow in the footsteps of the previous acharyas and follow the directions given by the spiritual master.

Further explaining to one of his first followers, Sanatana Goswami, how to write about this stage in a Vaishnava handbook, he explained:

“In the beginning one must take shelter of a bona fide spiritual master. In your book there should be the characteristics of the guru and disciple. Then, before accepting a guru, one can be assured of the guru’s position. Similarly, the guru can be assured of the disciple’s position.”

A disciple must also have a minimum of qualifications. In the Mantra-Muktavali it is said: "A genuine disciple is faithful, humble, pleasing to the eye, truthful, pious, intelligent, honest, not a hypocrite, free of lust and anger, devoted to his spiritual master's feet, and kind. A person who has these and other virtues is qualified for initiation.

In Shrimad Bhagavatam (11.10.6) it is said: "A genuine disciple is free from pride, non-envious, diligent, free of false-possessiveness, always active, never lazy, a sincere friend of his spiritual master, eager to learn the truth, not hostile to others, and averse to useless conversation."

## Relations between guru and disciple

The guru and disciple are meant to have a relationship of firm friendship, where the guru is always thinking of the spiritual welfare of the disciple, and the disciple thinks of the physical welfare of the guru, particularly if he is old or infirm. Although they are friends the guru’s responsibility is to occasionally remind the disciple that he is the student.

Disciples, especially those in their youth and brahmacari years, traditionally bring firewood and water for the guru, collect vegetables and grains, and cook meals for the guru. They carry his water-pot, blanket, and books. They may organise his transport or arrange medicines for him. Although the disciple is a menial servant in the ashram, the time comes eventually for his graduation. The samavartana ceremony marks the day when the disciple steps forward into a new life. The guru blesses him with kind words and the disciple promises to use all the guru’s teachings to improve his life and the good of the world. Later, after achieving some prosperity, the disciple returns to the ashram and offers the guru a dakshina, a tuition gift.

## Offences to the guru

Offences against the Vaishnava, and particularly the guru, is considered the worst of behaviours. They are known as being unbearable to the Lord. They are the hardest of all to forgive because the gurus have abandoned everything and dedicated their lives for alleviating the sufferings of others. These offences, which are enumerated in the shastras, include:

• Addressing the guru by his personal name, issuing instructions to him, requesting service of him, or treating him as an equal.

• Speaking to him whilst reclining on a bed, eating or with the face turned away.

• To consider the guru to be an ordinary person.

• To sit on a seat higher than him.

• Using the guru’s seat, bed, books, or other implements without permission.

• Mimicking his walk, speech, or deportment.

• Saluting him from a seat or carriage without having descended.

• Stretching out the feet towards him, holding one's knees, or clasping the hands behind the back in his presence.

• Arguing with the guru.

## A sacrament of reformation

**In the beginning of the bhakti path there must first be faith, a deep curiosity in the goal of Krishna consciousness, and a trust in the scriptures that explain it. This is known as shraddha. The next stage is to gain the association of advanced devotees and to learn from them. Then one takes shelter of one of them and receives mantras and instructions on the life of bhakti. This takes place in ‘sacrament of reformation,’ a ceremony known as diksha.**

Srila Prabhupada describes the process of bhakti, step by step:

"In the beginning there must be faith. Then one becomes interested in associating with pure devotees. Thereafter one is initiated by the spiritual master and executes the regulative principles under his orders. Thus, one is freed from all unwanted habits and becomes finally fixed in devotional service. Thereafter, one develops taste and attachment. This is the way of sadhana-bhakti, the execution of devotional service according to the regulative principles. Gradually emotions intensify, and finally there is an awakening of love. This is the gradual development of love of Godhead for the devotee interested in Krishna consciousness."

At some stage in the early part of this journey a devotee may identify a senior Vaishnava from whom he can take guidance and instruction. Requesting initiation from him, he becomes a disciple. The ceremony of initiation involves five sacramental elements which are also derived from the Pancharatra. Srila Prabhupada followed the tradition established by his own spiritual master and gave three of those elements at the first initiation and the remaining at the second.

The traditional diksha ceremony within any traditional Vaishnava community will generally consist of the following elements. The five reformatory sacraments are 4-8:

Udaka Shanti – Sanctification of the spot where diksha will take place, and the temporary establishment of the Deity for the duration of the ceremony.

Punyaha Vacana – Purification of the accessories of the ritual.

Homa – Fire sacrifice.

Tapa – marking of the disciple’s right and left upper arms or shoulders with heated silver brands shaped as Vishnu’s discus and conch, the chakra and shankha. (See \* below for modern-day version)

Pundra – giving the disciple the clay markings of tilak on twelve points of the body.

Dasya Nama - giving the disciple a new name consisting of one of the names of God plus the suffix Dasa meaning ‘servant.’

Mantra Upadesha – giving the disciple the mantra and explaining how to chant it.

Yaga – telling the disciple of his new duties as a servant of God. According to the Pancharatra there is a framework of five actions that a Vaishnava is supposed to perform throughout the day, one at each of five times of day, from morning to night. These are known as pancha-kala-kriya or ‘five timely actions.’

All of them are provided for in every ISKCON temple or home, in what devotees know as ‘the morning programme’ and ‘the evening programme.’ Srila Prabhupada explained that either one does these activities himself or can watch someone else do them, or support someone who does them. Either way counts as participating in the activity.

## Abhigamanam – Approaching the Lord.

The Sanskrit word abhigam means ‘to approach,’ in the sense of approaching Him by visiting the temple or home shrine. It also means to approach the Lord and ask for His blessings for the day ahead, and that you may serve Him without hindrance.

In the old days in India, every village had a public temple within walking distance of each home. A city was constructed in such a way that the temple was in the very centre of the grid of streets, and there would be a morning ‘rush hour’ before dawn as residents made their way through the still dark streets to approach the Lord’s residence. Most would not eat before their daily darshan, or ‘audience,’ with the Lord.

Every home would also have a smaller version of the temple, either in a dedicated small room or a corner of a room. At the very least, lamps would be lit and perhaps incense offered with the first prayer of the day.

Vaishnavas also arrange their month in such a way that they visit a major temple regularly. Public temples are enlivening places, being ‘Vaikuntha on earth,’ and uplifting because of the inspirational darshan available there, the high standards of archanam, and the presence of the Vaishnavas.

## Upadhan – ‘Obtaining.’

This means to acquire all the ingredients, collectively known as samagri, for the daily worship. This includes assembling the articles to be offered such as flowers, incense, lamps, and food. It also means to acquire a pure mind through recitation of mantras and prayers.

Upadhan can be done by shopping for the items, or by picking flowers or Tulasi leaves from the home garden. Gardening can be done in such a way as to ensure a small supply of flowers and fruits all year round. Traditional Vaishnavas always keep a Tulasi bush in the home and pick the leaves daily for making an offering of food to the Lord. Cotton wicks for lamps can be made at home and soaked in ghee. A wide variety of fragrant incense can be purchased in bulk.

Quantities of food supplies can be purchased easily locally, setting aside some items for the Lord’s enjoyment in addition to the food to be offered for one’s daily meals. In several places in shastra, it is mentioned that the Lord is pleased with the devotee who offers even a fruit, flower, leaf or water, so the Vaishnavas always try to something every day on their home altar.

## Ijya – Worship.

This Sanskrit word arises from the root Yaj. It indicates the aradhanam or worship of the Lord according to scripture. This normally involves offering a welcome (svagata) sitting place (asana), bath (snana), clothing and decoration (alankara), food (naivedya), incense (dupa), lamps (dipa), and salutations, prayers, and requests for forgiveness (vandana).

There are many items that can be offered, and they are traditionally grouped together in lists of 5, 10, 12 or 16. According to the facilities available to the individual, one of these lists can be selected. The main ingredient is bhakti.

## Svadhyaya – Study.

This word is found in the Taittiriya Upanishad, Siksha Valli, section 9. It means to personally read and teach the Vedic literature. Reading may be done best in the morning, before work, but also a few minutes can be spent during lunch. Explaining the Vedas to others can be done through informal conversation or by preparing and delivering formal classes to the devotees or newcomers.

Although there are almost unlimited scriptures, it will be found more helpful to limit one’s reading to a smaller number of texts. Memorization of passages, key words, and important verses all help to develop the mind and clarity of understanding. Recitation and taking notes, repeating what one has read, along with discussion are all aids to retention. Vaishnavas are particularly recommended to study the bhakti shastras - scriptures such as the Upanishads, Bhagavad-gita and the Puranas.

## Yoga – Meditation.

This means meditation on the Lord, His divine form, and excellent attributes, particularly before the end of the evening. In the context of daily activity, this means the meditation on what we have done for the Lord during the day, or a recollection of our omissions, and what services we could improve. The end of the day is a good time for meditation on his divine lila or eternal activities, particularly through narrations from the Shrimad Bhagavatam. Finally, it can take the form of a prayer that we can be in His care while we sleep.

At the ceremony for first initiation, the harinam-grahana, the disciple vows to follow the four regulative principles and chant sixteen rounds of the Hare Krishna mantra daily. At the second initiation, traditionally known as mantra-diksha, the now purified disciple vows to chant a series of mantras three times daily, at the join (sandhya) of night and day. Traditionally this is just before sunrise, at the zenith and just after sunset before the stars become visible. These prayers are known as sandhya-vandanam.

Chanting the sandhya mantras necessitates that the chanter is clean, has sipped water for purification, faces east in the morning and at midday, and west in the evening. The morning and midday require a standing posture and the evening sitting. Timing is important and lateness is not allowed.

The chanting of the Hare Krishna maha-mantra is the primary practice for awakening our dormant awareness of Krishna, our direct experience of His presence, and our love for Him. For this mantra, by contrast, there are no similar rules. The mantra can be chanted at any time, in any situation, facing any direction, and in any state of cleanliness.

\*By the decree of Chaitanya Mahaprabhu himself, the branding of the disciple’s upper arms with a hot metal stamp was dispensed with in favour of cooling sandalwood paste designs made by wooden blocks carved into the same shapes. Later, in the 18th century, the practise was dropped completely as a diksha requisite within the Gaudiya-Brahma community. It is retained within the Shri sampradaya as a one-time event, and within the Madhva-Brahma community as an annual event.

Bhaktivinode Thakur comments, however, that tapa (translated as ‘austerity’ or ‘heat’) must still be present as part of the process of the disciple becoming purified. Some pains must be undergone by the disciple so that he becomes purified of his attachment to ego and bodily pleasure. Sinful acts must be abandoned, and the disciple must rise early in the morning, undergoing any discomforts needed to serve the guru’s order. Through the heat of this personal austerity, tapa can be preserved as an element of diksha.

## Practices of devotion

**There are nine classical elements of bhakti, any one of which can lead to perfection if performed sincerely. In practical spiritual life, these are combined by the guru to form a disciple’s daily routine. The acharya of devotion, Rupa Goswami, collects and lists sixty-four ways in which these nine can be combined. Chaitanya Mahaprabhu selected five of those as the ‘most potent’ forms of devotional practice. They are all reproduced in this chapter.**

One of the most celebrated gurus and teachers of bhakti is Narada Muni, spiritual master of Srila Vyasa and author of the Narada Pancharatra, a treatise on devotion. Narada is also the guru of the young boy saint, Prahlada, and taught him everything. In the Shrimad Bhagavatam’s seventh book, young Prahlada gives the definitive description of how bhakti is practiced:

*śravaṇaṁ kīrtanaṁ viṣṇoḥ / smaraṇaṁ pāda-sevanam*  
*arcanaṁ vandanaṁ dāsyaṁ / sakhyam ātma-nivedanam*  
*iti puṁsārpitā viṣṇau / bhaktiś cen nava-lakṣaṇā*  
*kriyeta bhagavaty addhā / tan manye ’dhītam uttamam*

**Hearing and chanting about the transcendental holy name, form, qualities, paraphernalia and pastimes of Lord Vishnu, remembering them, serving the lotus feet of the Lord, offering the Lord respectful worship with sixteen types of paraphernalia, offering prayers to the Lord, becoming His servant, considering the Lord one’s best friend, and surrendering everything unto Him—these nine processes are accepted as pure devotional service. One who has dedicated his life to the service of Krishna through these nine methods should be understood to be the most learned person, for he has acquired complete knowledge.**

**Shrimad Bhagavatam 7.5.23-24**

The recitation, chanting, singing, and reading of Krishna’s names, forms, qualities, characteristics, and activities constitute the first and most important of the nine elements of devotion. In particular, the congregational singing of Krishna’s names is the most powerful and immediately pleasing way of meditating on, and remembering him, and is the recommended method of self-realisation and God-realisation in the present ‘Age of Quarrel and Hypocrisy.’ The shastras are quite emphatic on this uniquely helpful principle of yoga:

*kaler dosa-nidhe rajann asti hy eko mahan gunah*  
*kirtanad eva krsnasya mukta-sangah param vrajet*  
*krte yad dhyayato visnum tretayam yajato makhaih*  
*dvapare paricaryayam kalau tad dhari-kirtanat*

**The most important factor in this Age of Kali, which is an ocean of faults, is that one can be free from all contamination and become eligible to enter the kingdom of God simply by chanting the holy names. The self-realisation that was achieved in the Satya millennium by meditation, in the Treta millennium by the performance of different sacrifices, and in the Dvapara millennium by serving the Lord’s lotus feet, can be achieved in the Age of Kali simply by chanting the holy names of Hari.**

**Shrimad Bhagavatam 12.3.51-2**

**The supreme goal which was attained in Satya-yuga by years of prolonged meditation; in Treta-yuga by performing extensive yajnas; in Dvapara-yuga by opulent and scrupulous Deity worship; in Kali-yuga the same results are easily had simply by the chanting of the holy names of Keshava.**

**Vishnu Purana**

*harer nama harer nama / harer namaiva kevalam*  
*kalau nasty eva nasty eva / nasty eva gatir anyatha*

**In this age of quarrel and hypocrisy the only means of deliverance is chanting of the holy name of only Lord Hari. There is no other way. There is no other way. There is no other way.**

**Brihad Naradiya Purana**

Explaining that the goal is to ‘always remember Krishna and never forget him,’ Bhaktivinode Thakur comments on the powerful remembrance of Krishna induced by the chanting of his names: “Remembering Lord Krishna's name, form, qualities, and pastimes is called "smaranam" (remembering). Remembering is of five kinds:

1\. when one searches for something, that is called "smarana".

2\. When one withdraws the mind from other things and fixes it on one object, that is called "dharana".

3\. When one meditates on a particular form, that is called “dhyana".

4\. When meditation is unbroken and becomes like a flood of nectar, that is called "dhruv-anusmriti".

5\. When only the object of meditation and nothing else is present in the heart, that is called "samadhi".

## Shravanam, Kirtanam, and Smaranam

‘Shravanam (hearing), kirtanam (chanting), and smaranam (remembering) are the three most important limbs of devotional service. All the other limbs are included within these three. Of the three limbs hearing, chanting, and remembering, the most important is chanting, for hearing and remembering are naturally included within it.’

## Pada Sevanam

Pada-sevanam (serving the feet) or paricarya (service) is the fourth limb of devotional service. Serving the Lord's feet should be performed along with hearing, chanting, and remembering. When one serves the Lord's feet one should think oneself worthless and unqualified to serve, and one should think of the object of His service as a person who is eternal and full of knowledge and bliss. To think in these ways is essential. When serving the Lord's feet one should think that he is seeing the Lord's handsome face, touching Him, circumambulating Him, following Him, and seeing His temple, the Ganges, Jagannatha Puri, Dvaraka, Mathura, Navadvipa, and other holy places. Serving the Tulasi plant and serving the Lord's devotees are included within this limb of serving the Lord's feet.

## Archanam

The fifth limb is archanam (worship). If, after being engaged in hearing, chanting, and remembering, one becomes attracted to the path of worship, one should take shelter of the lotus feet of a guru, accept mantra-diksha initiation from him, and become engaged in the activities of worship. The holy name of the Supreme Lord is the life and soul of all mantras. By adding the word ‘namah’ (obeisances) and other like words to the Lord's holy names, the sages have given us various mantras glorifying the Lord, mantras that each possess specific powers. The holy name of the Lord has nothing to do with the material world. To withdraw the mind from thoughts of the material body and other material things, mantras containing the Lord's holy names are given.

To be able to chant mantras, materialistic persons require to accept initiation (diksha). To chant Krishna-mantras there is no need to accept any other customary purificatory processes. Initiation into the chanting of Krishna-mantras is very beneficial for the living entity. Krishna-mantras are the most powerful mantras in the entire world. When he accepts a Krishna-mantra from a bona-fide spiritual master, a qualified person gets strength from Lord Krishna Himself. The spiritual master then describes the path of archana (worship) to the inquisitive disciple.

It may be said that the observance of Shri Krishna-janmashtami, Karttika-vrata, Ekadashi-vrata, Magha-snana, and other vows are included in this path of worship. One thing especially should be noted about the path of worshiping Lord Krishna: One must worship Lord Krishna's devotees along with Lord Krishna Himself.

## Vandanam

Vandana (offering obeisances) is the sixth limb of devotional service. Although it is included within pada-sevanam, kirtanam, and the other limbs of devotional service, vandanam is also considered a separate limb. Vandana means ‘offering obeisances.’ There are two ways to offer obeisances:

1\. ekanga-namaskara (offering obeisances with one limb), and

2\. ashtanga-namaskara (offering obeisances with eight limbs).

To offer obeisances with one hand, with the body completely covered with cloth, directly before, on the left side, or behind the Lord, or very close to the Lord, are all considered offenses.

## Dasyam

Dasyam or service is the seventh limb of devotional service. ‘Dasya’ means to think "I am a servant of Lord Krishna". The best kind of worship is performed when the worshiper thinks that he is a servant of Lord Krishna. Offering obeisances, reciting prayers, offering all of one’s actions, serving, offering honour, remembering, hearing about the Lord, and other like activities are included within dasya.

## Sakhyam

Sakhya (friendship) is the eighth limb of devotional service. "Sakhya" means to act as a friend to Lord Krishna. There are two kinds of such friendship: 1. vaidhanga (friendship in vaidhi-bhakti), and 2. raganuga (friendship in raganuga-bhakti). The first is possible when the devotee engages in Deity worship. That is called "vaidha-sakhya".

## Atma-Nivedanam

Atma-nivedana (surrendering everything) is the ninth limb of devotional service. To offer the pure soul, the material body, and everything else to Lord Krishna is called "Atma-nivedana". To act only for Lord Krishna's benefit and not for one's own benefit is the special feature of atma-nivedana. As a cow that is sold to someone does not do anything to protect or maintain herself, so the surrendered soul acts only according to Lord Krishna's desires. Such a soul desires whatever Lord Krishna desires. The activities of atma-nivedana are described in the story of the great King Ambarisha, told in the Shrimad Bhagavatam, and beginning sa vai manah krishna-padaravindayor / vachamsi vaikuntha-gunanuvarnane:

**Maharaja Ambarisha always engaged his mind in meditating upon the lotus feet of Krishna, His words in describing the glories of the Lord, his hands in cleansing the Lord's temple, and his ears in hearing the words spoken by Krishna or about Krishna. He engaged his eyes in seeing the Deity of Krishna, Krishna's temples, and Krishna's places like Mathura and Vrindavan, he engaged his sense of touch in touching the bodies of the Lord's devotees, he engaged his sense of smell in smelling the fragrance of tulasi offered to the Lord, and he engaged his tongue in tasting the Lord's prasada.**

**He engaged his legs in walking to the holy places and temple of the Lord, his head in bowing down before the Lord, and all his desires in serving the Lord, twenty-four hours a day. Indeed, Maharaja Ambarisha never desired anything for his own sense gratification. He engaged all his senses in devotional service, in various engagements related to the Lord. This is the way to increase attachment for the Lord and be completely free from all material desires.**

**Shrimad-Bhagavatam 9.4.18-20**

## The 64 limbs of Devotion

There are 64 limbs of devotional activities, expanded from the nine principal limbs of shravanam, kirtanam and so on. Taken together, these comprise the entirety of bhakti-yoga. The first 10 form a group which may be called the ‘initial activities.’ The next 10 are actions that must be avoided. The first three of these 20 – taking shelter of, being initiated, and instructed by, a guru with faith and devotion – are the most important. The remaining 44 limbs are observed to increase one’s bhakti. The last five (60-64 inclusive) are the most potent forms of bhakti. They have been explained elsewhere but have been repeated for emphasis. Items 21-49 concern initiation into the worship of Krishna. The 64 limbs are as follows:

1\. accepting the shelter of the lotus feet of a bona fide spiritual master, 2. becoming initiated by the spiritual master and learning how to discharge devotional service from him, 3. obeying the orders of the spiritual master with faith and devotion, 4. following in the footsteps of the great acaryas (teachers) under the direction of the spiritual master, 5. inquiring from the spiritual master how to advance in Krishna consciousness, 6. being prepared to give up anything material for the satisfaction of the Supreme Personality of Godhead, Shri Krishna (this means that when we are engaged in the devotional service of Krishna, we must be prepared to give up something which we may not like to give up, and also we may have to accept something which we may not like to accept), 7. residing in a sacred place of pilgrimage like Dwaraka or Vrindavan, 8. accepting only what is necessary, or dealing with the material world only as far as necessary, 9. observing the fasting day on ekadashi, and 10. worshiping sacred trees like the banyan tree.

The next ten limbs of devotional service are prohibitions that should be carefully followed. They are:

11\. One should rigidly give up the company of non-devotees. 12. One should not instruct a person who is not desirous of accepting devotional service. 13. one should not be very enthusiastic about constructing costly temples or monasteries. 14. One should not try to read too many books, nor should one develop the idea of earning his livelihood by lecturing on or professionally reciting Shrimad-Bhagavatam or Bhagavad-gita. 15. One should not be neglectful in ordinary dealings. 16. One should not be under the spell of lamentation in loss or jubilation in gain. 17. One should not disrespect the demigods. 18. One should not give unnecessary trouble to any living entity. 19. One should carefully avoid the various offenses in chanting the holy name of the Lord or in worshiping the Deity in the temple. 10. One should be very intolerant toward the blasphemy of the Supreme Personality of Godhead, Krishna, or His devotees.

These twenty limbs are the gateway to devotional service. Among them the first three, beginning with accepting the shelter of the lotus feet of a bona fide spiritual master, are the most important. The next limbs are:

21\. One should decorate the body with tilak, which is the sign of the Vaishnavas. 22. In marking such tilak sometimes one may write Hare Krishna on the body. 23. One should accept flowers and garlands that have been offered to the Deity and the spiritual master and put them on one's body. 24. One should learn to dance before the Deity. 25. One should learn to bow down immediately upon seeing the Deity or the spiritual master. 26. As soon as one visits a temple of Lord Krishna, one must stand up. 27. When the

Deity is being borne for a stroll in the street, a devotee should immediately follow the procession. 28. A devotee must visit a Vishnu temple at least once or twice every day, morning, and evening. 29. One must circumambulate the temple building at least three times. 30. One must worship the Deity in the temple according to the regulative principles. 31. One must render personal service to the Deities. 32. One must sing. 33. One must perform sankirtan. 34. One must chant. 35. One must offer prayers. 36. One must recite notable prayers. 37. One must taste maha-prasada (food from the very plate offered before the Deities). 38. One must drink charanamrita (water from the bathing of the Deities, which is offered to guests). 39. One must smell the incense and flowers offered to the Deity. 40. One must touch the lotus feet of the Deity. 41. One must see the Deity with great devotion. 42. One must offer arati at different times. 43. One must hear about the Lord and His pastimes from Shrimad-Bhagavatam, Bhagavad-gita, and similar books. 44. One must pray to the Deity for His mercy. 45. One should remember the Deity.

46\. One should meditate upon the Deity. 47. One should render some voluntary service. 48. One should think of the Lord as one's friend. 49. One should offer everything to the Lord.

50\. One should offer a favourite article (such as food or a garment). 51. One should take all kinds of risks and perform all endeavours for Krishna's benefit. 52. In every condition, one should be a surrendered soul. 53. One should pour water on the tulasi tree. 54. One should regularly hear Shrimad-Bhagavatam and similar literature. 55. One should live in a sacred place like Mathura, Vrindavan, or Dwaraka. 56. One should offer service to Vaishnavas. 57. One should arrange for one's devotional service according to one's means. 58. In the month of Karttika (October and November), one should plan for special services. 59. During Janmashtami (the time of Krishna's appearance in this world) one should observe a special service.

The last five items are the most potent forms of bhakti.

60\. One should do whatever is done with great care and devotion for the Deity.

61\. One should relish the pleasure of Shrimad Bhagavatam reading among devotees and not among outsiders.

62\. One should associate with devotees who are considered more advanced.

63\. One should chant the holy name of the Lord.

64\. One should live in the jurisdiction of Mathura.

## Srila Prabhupada on ‘the 64 limbs’

‘Of the sixty-four items mentioned above, Lord Chaitanya instructed that five are the most important due to the present conditions of civic life:

One should associate with the devotees. Association with devotees is made possible by hearing them attentively, by asking them relevant questions, by supplying them food and by accepting food from them, and by giving them charity and by accepting from them whatever they offer.” In the Srimad Bhagavatam 9.4.57 the Lord explains:

*aham bhakta-paradhino hy asvatantra iva dvija*  
*sadhubhir grasta-hrdayo bhaktair bhakta-jana-priyah*

**The devotees are always in My heart, and I am always in the heart of the devotees. The devotee does not know anything beyond Me, and I also cannot forget the devotee. There is a very intimate relationship between Me and the pure devotees. Pure devotees in full knowledge are never out of spiritual touch, and therefore they are very much dear to Me.**

One should chant the holy name of the Lord in all circumstances. The chanting of the Lord's name is an easy and inexpensive process of realization. One can chant any of the innumerable names of the Lord at any time. One should try to avoid offenses. There are ten offenses which one can commit while chanting the transcendental names, and these should be avoided as far as possible, but in any event, one should try to chant the holy names of the Lord at all times. Lord Chaitanya Mahaprabhu has written:

*namnam akari bahudha nija-sarva-shaktis*  
*tatrarpita niyamitah smarane na kalah*  
*etadrishi tava kripa bhagavan mamapi*  
*durdaivam idrisham ihajani nanuragaha*
