**O my Lord, your holy name alone can render all benediction to living beings, and thus You have hundreds and millions of names like Krishna and Govinda. In these transcendental names You have invested all Your transcendental energies. There are not even hard and fast rules for chanting these names. O my Lord, out of kindness You enable us to easily approach You by Your holy names, but I am so unfortunate that I have no attraction for them.**

One should hear the transcendental topics enunciated in the Shrimad Bhagavatam. “This hearing is made possible through platform lectures by bona fide devotees and by authorized translations of the Bhagavatam.”

**O Shrimad Bhagavatam! I offer my respectful obeisances unto you. By reading you one attains transcendental bliss, for your syllables rain pure love of God upon the reader. You are always served by everyone, for you are an incarnation of Lord Krishna.**

**Sanatana Goswami**

One should make his home at Mathura, the birthplace of Lord Krishna. “Or one may make his home as good as Mathura by installing the Deity of the Lord to be worshiped by all members of the family after proper initiation from the spiritual master.”

One should worship the installed Deity with attention and devotion “so that the whole atmosphere of one's home becomes the replica of the Lord's abode. This is made possible by the direction of the spiritual master who knows the transcendental art and can show the candidate the proper method.”

“By worshiping the archa-vigraha, one can at once approach the Lord, who accepts the service of a devotee by His omnipotent energy. The archa-vigraha of the Lord descends at the request of the acharyas, the holy teachers, and works exactly in the original way of the Lord by virtue of the Lord's omnipotence. Foolish people who have no knowledge ...consider the archa-vigraha, which is worshiped by pure devotees, to be made of material elements. This form may be seen as material by the imperfect eyes of foolish people…but such people do not know that the Lord, being omnipotent and omniscient, can transform matter into spirit, and spirit into matter, as He desires.”

“The above five items can be adopted by any man in any part of the world. Thus anyone can prepare himself for returning home, back to Godhead, by the simple method recognized by authorities such as Lord Sri Chaitanya Mahaprabhu, who specifically advented Himself to deliver the fallen souls of this age...The above-mentioned five items of devotional activities are so spiritually powerful that their performance by a devotee, even in the preliminary stage, can very quickly promote the sincere executor to the stage of bhava (the stage just prior to love of Godhead), or emotion on the spiritual plane, which is transcendental to mental and intellectual functions.

A complete absorption in bhava, or love of God, makes one fit to be transferred to the spiritual sky just after leaving the material tabernacle. The perfection of love of God by a devotee actually situates him on the spiritual platform, even though he may still maintain a gross material body. He becomes like a red-hot iron which, when in contact with fire, actually ceases to be iron and acts like fire.”

“These things are made possible by the Lord's inscrutable and inconceivable energy, which material science has not the scope to calculate. One should therefore engage himself in devotional service with absolute faith, and to make his faith steadfast one should seek the association of the standard devotees of the Lord by personal association (if possible) or by thinking of them. This association will help one develop factual devotional service to the Lord, which will cause all material misgivings to disappear like a flash of lightning.

All these different stages of spiritual realization will be personally felt by the candidate, and this will create in him a firm belief that he is making positive progress on the way to the spiritual sky. Then he will become sincerely attached to the Lord and His abode. Such is the gradual process of evolving love of God, which is the prime necessity for the human form of life."

These five spiritually potent activities were taught by Srila Prabhupada to his disciples from the very beginning, and they form the basis of the Krishna consciousness movement.

## Eleven Elements of Bhakti

In the Bhakti Sandarbha, one of his series of six books dealing with the philosophy and practice of bhakti, Jiva Goswami summarises the orientation and practise of bhakti-yoga into eleven elements.

Reasoning that everything begins with the inspiration, teaching and guidance of an advanced Vaishnava he writes that service to such a person is the primal element of the spiritual life.

Second, because this inspires the aspirant and gives him the strength to continue, the next step will be to surrender, both internally as in choosing Krishna as master, and formally through diksha under the auspices of an authentic guru. The six aspects of sharanapatti are a constant factor in the life of such a devotee, and not merely a primary orientation. They must, of course, be there in the beginning, but they must also be preserved through his life, too.

Third, the nine principles of devotional life – shravanam, kirtanam, etc form the remaining nine elements of his summary of the spiritual life, thus bringing the total to eleven. Jiva Goswami’s list remains a handy and useful summary of the key elements of bhakti-yoga. Each of the subpoints can be expanded upon limitlessly.

## In Summary

1\. Guruseva – service to the guru

A guru is one who knows the shastra, practices the same, and teaches it to sishyas who also practice it. He appears in an authentic parampara and is fixed in spiritual consciousness. He is friendly, compassionate, and always helping others to understand and take shelter of God.

2\. Sharanagati – resorting to God as the only refuge

(a) Accepting things favourable for God’s service (b) Rejecting things unfavourable (c) Having confidence in His protection (d) Embracing His guardianship (e) Self surrender (f) Humility.

3\. Shravanam – listening to the name, qualities, character, and activities of God - through personal japa meditation, kirtan, classes, discussions, reading.

4\. Kirtanam – chanting and/or describing the name and qualities of God

\- through chanting japa, kirtan, giving classes, teaching, preaching.

5\. Smaranam – remembering God - through many methods.

6\. Padasevanam – service to the feet of God

\- performing tasks joyfully as a menial servant and becoming a servant of the devotees of the Lord, who are already situated at his feet.

7\. Archanam – worship

\- rituals, ceremonies, personal home puja, and temple worship and festivals.

8\. Vandanam – ‘salutations,’ prayer and paying homage

\- offering respect through bowing and prostration, personal prayer and recitation of the prayers and songs of great devotees.

9\. Dasyam – feeling of servitude

\- understanding and developing an inner mood of your natural place as a servant.

10\. Sakhyam – a sense of friendship to the Lord

\- understanding and developing the mood and behaviour of a friend.

11\. Atmanivedanam – complete surrender of the self

\- making a ‘deposit’ of oneself, as if making a deposit in a trusted bank / making a ‘consignment’ of the self as in delivering a precious package. Still further, understanding that He owned you always, and that the power of giving yourself to Him also came from Him.

## Obstacles along the path

If spiritual life were easy, it would not require so many books, the company of spiritual friends, and a guru to help. It could all be accomplished by the lone spiritual traveller. But on the spiritual path, as in life generally, there are many obstacles.

Srila Bhaktivinode Thakur said that they are like bandits hiding behind a rock. ‘If they catch you, they will strangle you.’ Spiritual life, it would seem, is not for the faint-hearted. That’s why the guru also explains about the obstacles to his disciple, sometimes to the point where the disciple feels daunted by the prospect of moving forward. But then the guru also explains how to deal with each obstacle, thus restoring the disciple’s enthusiasm. Obstacles are called virodhi and when they prevent us reaching our desired target, they are known as Prapti-Virodhi. Here are the five categories mentioned in the Artha Panchakam:

Anti-soul (svarupa-virodhi) - Doing things for the short-term pleasure of the body and not the long-term happiness of the soul.

Anti-God (paratva-virodhi) - Thinking the demigods as equal. Thinking of the avatars as human, or that the Deities in the temple are simply statues made of wood, stone or metal and therefore useless.

Anti-Goal (purusartha-virodhi) - Wanting to enjoy things along the way and therefore reducing the goal from ultimate and eternal to a goal within the world, therefore delaying the goal.

Anti-Means (upaya-virodhi) - Engaging in processes other than bhakti. Forgetting the techniques of bhakti or abandoning them in favour of something which seems easier or quicker.

Anti-Gain (prapti-virodhi) - Sinning against God or the godly devotees. Repetitive sinning. Making progress in spiritual life but then rebelling when progress is experienced.

## The essence of all advice

Srila Rupa Goswami considered the virodhi, or hindrances to spiritual life, and he then selected those external behaviours that would ruin spiritual practise. He also enumerated those items that would help progress towards spiritual perfection.

He provided the essence of both in his Upadeshamrita, rendered by Srila Prabhupada as The Nectar of Instruction.

**Six Unfavourable Principles**

*atyāhāraḥ prayāsaś ca*  
*prajalpo niyamāgrahaḥ*  
*jana-saṅgaś ca laulyaṁ ca*  
*ṣaḍbhir bhaktir vinaśyati*

**One’s devotional service is spoiled when he becomes too entangled in the following six activities:**

**1. eating more than necessary or collecting more funds than required.**

**2. over-endeavouring for mundane things that are very difficult to obtain.**

**3. talking unnecessarily about mundane subject matters.**

**4. practicing the scriptural rules and regulations only for the sake of following them and not for the sake of spiritual advancement. Or rejecting the rules and regulations of the scriptures and working independently or whimsically.**

**5. associating with worldly-minded persons who are not interested in Kṛṣṇa consciousness.**

**6. being greedy for mundane achievements.**

**Six Favourable Principles**

*utsāhān niścayād dhairyāt*  
*tat-tat-karma-pravartanāt*  
*saṅga-tyāgāt sato vṛtteḥ*  
*ṣaḍbhir bhaktiḥ prasidhyati*

**There are six principles favourable to the execution of pure devotional service:**

**1. being enthusiastic.**

**2. endeavouring with confidence**

**3. being patient.**

**4. acting according to regulative principles \[such as śravaṇaṁ kīrtanaṁ viṣṇoḥ smaraṇam – hearing, chanting, and remembering Krishna\].**

**5. abandoning the association of non-devotees.**

**6. following in the footsteps of the previous ācāryas.**

These six principles undoubtedly assure the complete success of pure devotional service.

Srila Prabhupada comments:

“Devotional service is not a matter of sentimental speculation or imaginative ecstasy. Its substance is practical activity. Śrīla Rūpa Gosvāmī, in his Bhakti-rasāmṛta-sindhu (1.1.11), has deﬁned devotional service as follows:

*anyābhilāṣitā-śūnyaṁ*  
*jñāna-karmādy-anāvṛtam*  
*ānukūlyena kṛṣṇānu-*  
*śīlanaṁ bhaktir uttamā*

**“Uttamā bhakti, or unalloyed devotion unto the Supreme Personality of Godhead, Śrī Kṛṣṇa, involves the rendering of devotional service in a way that is favorable to the Lord. This devotional service should be free from any extraneous motive and devoid of fruitive karma, impersonal jñāna and all other selﬁsh desires.”**

Bhakti is a sort of cultivation. As soon as we say “cultivation,” we must refer to activity. Cultivation of spirituality does not mean sitting down idly for meditation, as some pseudo yogР”«s teach. Such idle meditation may be good for those who have no information of devotional service, and for this reason it is sometimes recommended as a way to check distracting materialistic activities. Meditation means stopping all nonsensical activities, at least for the time being. Devotional service, however, not only puts an end to all nonsensical mundane activities, but also engages one in meaningful devotional activities.

## Nine stages of bhakti

**Just as there are nine main practices of bhakti, there are nine progressive stages through which the successful practitioner passes. They were described by Shri Chaitanya Mahaprabhu and recorded by Rupa Goswami. Two hundred years later, a great spiritual master named Vishvanatha Chakravarti wrote about them and explained the obstacles that even a very sincere practitioner might encounter on the path. They have been combined in this chapter.**Srila Rupa Goswami writes:

**adau sraddha tatah sadhu-sango 'tha bhajana-kriya**  
**tato 'nartha-nivrttih syat tato nistha rucis tatah**

**athasaktis tato bhavas tatah premabh-yudancati**  
**sadhakanam ayam premnah pradurbhave bhavet kramah**

**In the beginning there must be (1) faith. Then one becomes interested in (2) associating with pure devotees. Thereafter one is initiated by the spiritual master and executes the (3) regulative principles under his orders. Thus, one is (4) freed from all unwanted habits and becomes finally (5) fixed in devotional service. Thereafter, one develops (6) taste and (7) attachment. This is the way of sadhana-bhakti, the execution of devotional service according to the regulative principles. Gradually emotions intensify, and finally there is (8) an awakening of love. This is the gradual development of (9) love of Godhead for the devotee interested in Krishna consciousness.**

The nine stages are as follows:

1\. Sraddha – a curiosity for spiritual life, yoga, the Vedas, and the very beginnings of faith in a supreme being is the first step in faith. By hearing from devotees the aspirant’s faith will increase to the point where he has faith that by serving Krishna alone everything else is accomplished, as by pouring water at the root of a plant all its branches, twigs and leaves are fed. This firm faith or sraddha is the very seed of bhakti. The primary level of faith (curiosity) develops into the subsequent levels of faith in the company of sadhus.

2\. Sadhu-sanga – the company of sadhus or saints. This means an individual’s eager desire to be in the company of a sadhu to learn the methods of spiritual practices for God-realization alone and for no other purpose. A genuine sadhu can be understood according to the list of qualities provided by Krishna in discussion with his friend Uddhava, recorded in the eleventh canto of the Shrimad Bhagavatam. To keep company with such a person in thought, physically, and through classes discussions is Sadhu-Sanga.

3\. Bhajana-kriya - spiritual practices conducted under the guidance, direction, and encouragement of the sadhus. Spiritual initiation is included in this stage. This stage comprises early, unsteady practise followed by later, steadier practise. Slackness in spiritual practices may be due to a variety of causes including sins and offences to God from a previous life. The result of these sins leads to weaknesses such as pride of early achievement, sporadic endeavour, worldly temptation, vacillating determination, and stalling at the first genuine spiritual experience.

4\. Anartha-nivritti – a cessation (nivritti) of the obstacles and the defects (anarthas) that cloud Bhakti. When one conquers over the ‘five persistent obstacles’ one has achieved this stage:

(i) Laya-to feel drowsiness while singing, listening to and remembering the glories of the Godhead.

(ii) Vikshepa-to maintain mundane associations while practicing even the external forms of Bhakti.

(iii) Apratipatti-disinclination for bhajana or spiritual endeavors even when realizing one's Laya and Vikshepa.

(iv) Kasaya-instinctively prone to anger, avarice, vanity, etc.

(v) Rasasvada- to think of sensual enjoyment and worldly pleasures while engaged in bhajana or practices of Bhakti.

5\. Nishtha – is a firmness in the striving for the unfolding of Bhakti. When the five obstacles mentioned above are almost removed from the path of the aspirant, the steadiness that he then attains is called Nishtha. This Nishtha or firmness in Bhakti can be of two different forms. The first steadfastness is that which directly concerns Bhakti, and the second type concerns things which are favourable to bhakti. Firmness in bhakti is of three kinds:

1\. Physical, such as worship in the temple, listening to the scriptures, prostrated obeisances before the Deities in the temple and before the guru, circumambulation of the temple of God and the sacred places of pilgrimage associated with his divine descents into this world.

2\. Verbal, such as chanting of the name of God, chanting on beads and reading aloud His glories.

3\. Mental, such as remembrance of and meditation on the divine name, transcendental form and beauty, qualities, his spiritual realm, associates and entourage, and pastimes.

Then there is steadiness in relation to things which are favourable to bhakti. For instance, having no desire for the respect of others, being respectful to others, friendliness, compassion, gratitude, self-control, mental tranquillity, tolerance, and such other qualities as are helpful to the development of constancy in bhakti.

6\. Ruchi – a strong liking or ‘taste.’ When a strong taste for listening to the glories of the Lord becomes much more agreeable than talks on mundane things then such a mental taste of the devotee is called ruchi. This means a taste for all aspects of bhakti without paying any particular attention to the less important aspects; and to feel agreeably affected by the various traits of bhakti, keeping in view the more important and the less important aspects.

7\. Asakti - Attachment. When taste for services of the Lord develops into full-grown maturity and he alone becomes the sole object of life, then such an intensity of spiritual practice creates a glue-like attachment for the Lord in the devotee’s heart. This asakti cleanses the heart of the devotee and makes it so transparent that the Lord's reflection appears in it, in such a way as if the Lord has appeared before the devotee in person. In the beginning, the devotee in the early stages of spiritual practice purposely turns his mind towards the Supreme Person and soon his mind is spontaneously and constantly fixed on Him. In this stage of asakti, if the devotee is even involved in worldly gossip, his mind will turn unawares to the Lord and be attached to thinking about Him. His soul now begins to relish the supreme sweetness of the incomparable beauty of Krishna, the Lord. All worldly talks and worldly affairs become boring and unbearable to him.8. Bhava - When the above explained asakti or attachment along with ruchi or spontaneous taste softens the heart, mind, and ego, and all reasoning faculties reach a point of melting, it is called bhava. This bhava is the innate nature of the self-same potency (swarupa-shakti) or internal potency (antaranga-shakti) of the all-powerful supreme person and is like the aurora immediately preceding the rise of the sun of prema. Rupa Goswami describes that when a devotee attains the stage of bhava the following nine traits are fully developed in him:

**“When the seed of ecstatic emotion for Krishna fructifies, the following nine symptoms manifest in one’s behaviour: forgiveness, concern that time should not be wasted, detachment, absence of false prestige, hope, eagerness, a taste for chanting the holy name of the Lord, attachment to descriptions of the qualities of the Lord, and affection for those places where the Lord resides – a temple or a holy place. These are all called anubhava, subordinate signs of ecstatic emotion. They are visible in a person in whose heart the seed of love of God has begun to fructify.”**

**Bhakti Rasamrita Sindhu 1.3.25-26.**

9\. Prema - when bhava develops to such a pitch that it completely softens the mind of the most advanced devotee and it creates an intense of ownership of Krishna, that ‘Krishna is mine,’ which gladdens the devotee’s heart beyond all measure, it is called prema.

## Challenges to bhakti

Bhakti produces kleshaghni – relief from material distress – and shubhada – the beginning of all auspiciousness. Klesha means suffering or affliction but also the causes of suffering. There are five different kinds, or causes, of ignorance. From them arises the tendency to right or wrong action, causing religion or irreligion, and thus sinful or pious actions. From the reactions to these one meets with fortune or misfortune. A description of the five klesha is found in Patanjali’s Yoga Sutras: Avidya (ignorance), Asmita (egoism), Raga (attachment), Dvesha (aversion or hatred), and Abiniveshah (clinging to life and fear of death).

The four effects of sin, prarabdha (mature), aprarabdha (not yet fructified), kuta (lying as seed) and bija (germinating seed) are also included in klesha.

Disappearance of undesirable qualities and the appearance of auspicious qualities both occur, but there is a rate of difference between them. This creates gradual stages in the development of bhakti which can be ascertained by certain symptoms that become manifest.

First, the aspiring devotee develops shraddha, or faith. This means faith in the scriptures dealing with bhakti, and an enthusiasm to begin the activities described in them. These two types of faith may be either spontaneous (svabhaviki) or brought about by the forceful preaching of another (bala-dutpadita).

Through meeting a guru, the aspiring devotee gains sadhu sanga – or through sadhu-sanga he meets guru – which leads him to bhajana-kriya, being engaged in activities of devotional service. At first the aspirant will be unsteady in the activities of devotional service. Six different stages mark the progression of unsteady devotional service:

1\. Utsaha-mayi – ‘Enthusiasm in prideful practice.’ Due to false confidence and the audacity of thinking he has mastered everything quite early on in his spiritual life, the aspirant becomes filled with a childish enthusiasm.

2\. Ghana-tarala – ‘Condensed-dilute.’ The aspirant is sometimes diligent yet sometimes negligent in practice, and his endeavour is therefore sporadic. Thus, he is sometimes productive in his service and sometimes inattentive.

3\. Vyudha-vikalpa – ‘Extensive speculation but indecisive.’ The aspirant vacillates in considering different options. He is experiencing a ‘parade of possibilities.’

4\. Vishaya-sangara – ‘A struggle with the senses.’ The aspirant tries to abandon addictions yet often falls for them. He sometimes experiences victory, sometimes defeat, and thus engages in a battle with sense pleasure.

5\. Niyama-kshama – ‘An inability to uphold vows.’ He makes resolutions but is unable to carry them out. Whereas vishaya-sangara is the inability to give up sense enjoyment, niyama-kshama is the inability to improve one’s devotional service, otherwise known as ‘unfulfilled commitment.’

6\. Taranga-rangini – ‘enjoying the small waves’ – the nature of bhakti is attractive, and people become attracted to the devotee. Therefore, bhakti offers the opportunity for material gain, respect, and position (labha, puja, pratistha). This stage is also known as ‘exploiting the benedictions of bhakti.’ Like paddling in the surf on a beach, if the swimmer progresses no further, he may remove his chances of enjoying the ocean.

After bhajana-kriya comes anartha-nivritti, or the clearing of unwanted habits that obstruct the path of bhakti. Anartha means ‘unwanted’ and nivritti means ‘nullification.’ Anarthas are of four types according to their origin:

1\. Duskritotha – arising from past sins.

2\. Sukritotha – arising from previous pious works.

3\. Aparadhottha – arising from offenses.

4\. Bhaktyuttha – arising from bhakti.

Sin or papa means any act condemned by scripture for which there is a prescribed atonement. Though it is easy to understand why the reactions to sin in a previous life might interfere with one’s practice of bhakti in this life, it is also a fact that even the reactions to good deeds create obstacles. Pious actions or punya done either in this life or in previous births, create opportunities for enjoyment here in this world or hereafter in heaven (svarga). Enjoyments in this world or in heaven resulting from pious deeds are obstacles to the attainment of bhakti. They are distractions and are like deep clouds that cover the moon of bhakti.

Then there are those obstacles arising out of ten offenses done to the name of Krishna and thirty-two offenses committed in the services of archana, the worship of the deities in the temples. One should know them in detail so that spiritual practices of bhakti might not be clouded over.

There are also obstacles arising out of bhakti itself, that is, from a non-fixed state of sadhana of bhakti. It is possible that one is carried away by the desire for bhukti, the enjoyment of pleasures of heaven as a minor god, or by the desire for mukti, or self-annihilation by way of a desire to merge into the absolute Brahman. Or by desires for worldly gains, name, and fame. One has got to be very careful about these subtle temptations that may lead one astray from unalloyed bhakti. Anarthas arising from bhakti are like weeds that retard the growth of the main plant.

The greatest obstacle to bhakti is the aparadha, an offence for which the only recourse is to ask forgiveness from Krishna or the Vaishnava. The greatest offence to Krishna is to ignore, insult, openly criticize, or physically hurt the Vaishnava or guru. This is known as Vaishnava aparadha.

The only recourse is to beg forgiveness from the Vaishnava concerned. One should not think:

1. I shall nullify my offence against the devotee by chanting the holy name.
2. He is not an advanced Vaishnava therefore my offence is negligible.
3. He has defects and sometimes offends me; therefore, my offence is nullified.

Each of the four types of anarthas have five grades of nivritti or nullification:

1. Eka-desa-vartini – limited or partial
2. Bahu-desa-vartini – pervasive
3. Prayiki – almost complete
4. Purna – complete
5. Atyantiki – absolute

Partial: The first level of nullification of anarthas is according to the ancient logical maxim: gramo-dagdhah pato bhagnah - ‘The town burned; the cloth is torn.’ This means that although we may say that the town burned, still some of the town was left unburned; and although the cloth was torn, still the pieces of cloth remain. So, at this early stage of nullification of anartha, although it is true to say that the removal of the pernicious effects of anartha have begun, yet much remains to be nullified.

Pervasive: When nistha, or steadiness in devotional life, appears it can be said that nullification of anartha has reached the stage of ‘pervasive.’

Almost complete: has been achieved with the appearance of rati or bhava.

Complete: has been achieved with the appearance of prema.

Absolute: has been achieved with the attainment of the Lord’s direct association.

The eradication of anarthas arising from past sins is as follows:

When the stage of bhajana kriya has been reached the anarthas from past sins is almost complete

When the stage of Nistha has been reached the removal of anarthas from past sins is complete. When the stage of Asakti has been reached the removal of anarthas from past sins is absolute.

The eradication of anarthas arising from bhakti is as follows:

Bhajana kriya – partial.

Nishtha – complete.

Ruchi – absolute.

In the Shrimad Bhagavatam there are two key verses related to the progress in bhakti: The first verse refers to unsteady bhajana-kriya, then the cleansing of inauspicious elements (abhadrani vidhunoti) and then steady bhajana-kriya. The word nishtha means to have the quality of steadiness (from the word naiscalyam or non-movement)

*śṛṇvatāṁ sva-kathāḥ kṛṣṇaḥ puṇya-śravaṇa-kīrtanaḥ*  
*hṛdy antaḥ stho hy abhadrāṇi vidhunoti suhṛt satām*

**Shri Krishna, the Personality of Godhead, who is the Paramatma in everyone’s heart and the benefactor of the truthful devotee, cleanses desire for material enjoyment from the heart of the devotee who has developed the urge to hear His messages, which are in themselves virtuous when properly heard and chanted.**

*naṣṭa-prāyeṣv abhadreṣu nityaṁ bhāgavata-sevayā*  
*bhagavaty uttama-śloke bhaktir bhavati naiṣṭhikī*

**By regular attendance in classes on the Bhagavatam and by rendering of service to the pure devotee, all that is troublesome to the heart is almost completely destroyed, and loving service unto the Personality of Godhead, who is praised with transcendental songs, is established as an irrevocable fact.**

**Shrimad Bhagavatam 1.2.17-18.**

Though a devotee strives for steadiness every day, while the anarthas are still present he will not attain it. This is due to the ‘five persistent obstacles’:

Laya means the tendency to sleep; vikshepa, distraction toward mundane topics; apratipatti, the occasional inability to do kirtan despite the absence of sleepiness or distraction; kasaya, the tendency toward anger, greed and pride, rasasvada, the inability to become absorbed in kirtan if one gets the opportunity for sense gratification. Nistha appears in the absence of these faults.

## Bhava

In his handbook on the path of bhakti, the Madhurya Kadambini, the author Vishvanatha Chakravarti Thakur writes eloquently on what happens to the devotee after all obstacles have been removed and bhava has been achieved:

“Bhava is like a flower which forcibly attracts Krishna, who is like a bee. The Lord is overwhelmed by enjoying the bhava of his devotee. Even the gods adore such a devotee in bhava, however lowly the society in which he might have been born.

When bhava is awakened in the heart of a devotee, showers of tears drench his soul, being overfilled with emotions of inexplicable joy due to his experiencing and realizing the suppleness of the dark-blue body of Shri Krishna, the ruddiness of the corners of His eyes and lips, his gentle smile and the yellowness of His robes.”

“He feels, at intervals, that he has listened to the soothing music of the Lord’s flute, the sweet jingling of the tinkling anklets of his feet, and His own charming and fascinating voice. He remains ever eager to taste them again and again.

He feels an immensely delightful sensation, of occasional touch of the tender hands and the feet of the Lord, and he is thrilled to smell the gladdening odour of his body. He sometimes feels his presence and is drowned in the stream of ecstasy. At another time, he feels that the Lord has forsaken him, and he is thrown into the depth of the pang of separation.”

The author continues his explanation by saying that: “The devotee in bhava experiences the above-mentioned conditions not only when wide awake, but also in deep sleep as also in dreams. In the stage of bhava, the devotee strongly identifies himself with his siddha-deha or eternal, supramundane, all-spiritual and eternal body, and he forgets the egoism of the physical body. Now he has attained bhava, the stage of absolute freedom from the bondage of the gross physical body and the subtle mind and perverse egoism.”

“Though such a devotee tries to hide his spiritual experiences from worldly-minded folks, they become manifest in the company of genuine, freed, and passionate devotees. To the ordinary people, expressions of such supramundane experiences appear like madness. This bhava, which is otherwise called sthayi-bhava, is a permanent trait of relation between the individual atma and the Parama-atma or Godhead and is comprised of five primary and seven secondary types.”

## Prema

When bhava develops it is called prema. This may manifest itself in a devotee in two different ways, (a) as a development of bhava, and (b) as arising from the grace of the Lord.

In the first manifestation of prema, it becomes normal and spontaneous for the devotee to be completely and constantly engaged in listening to, chanting and singing the endearing name of the Lord; he is deeply attached to his most beloved Lord and a profound fondness for Him overwhelms him. All his reasoning faculties are dissolved and melt into divine emotions; he then forgets all social decorum and gives way like a mad person sometimes to shrieks of loud laughter, sometimes to bitter weeping, sometimes to shouting, sometimes he is merged in the melody of music of singing the Lord's limitless qualities, and sometimes to dancing in ecstasy.

In the manifestation of prema, that resulting from mercy, the cowherd maidens of Vraja had not read the Vedas or served saints, too no religious vows or performed any austere penances, yet they achieved prema for the supreme Lord Shri Krishna, because of His causeless grace only.

Prema has two more important aspects: its intrinsic nature is a dense, consolidated and ecstatic bliss or ananda. Prema once awakened never ceases even if there be strong grounds of a break or cessation. Prema possesses the supreme power to attract even Shri Krishna, who is the allattractor. When the sun of prema dawns in the perfectly cleansed and transparent heart of the prema-bhakta the following characteristics become natural and normal with him:

All forms of the sense of ‘I’ and attachment of a prema-bhakta for his body-mind-soul and those related to the body-mind are completely directed to Shri Krishna alone. His sense of identity is attracted by Sri Krishna like a magnet drawing a clean piece of iron.

As a greedy and hungry man’s desire for palatable dishes never ends even if he has eaten much of them, so also a prema-bhakta's burning desire for Krishna have no end when once he has attained him, rather the longing increases all the more.

“As a tired traveller crossing a vast desert in the scorching rays of the summer sun, suddenly finds a densely shaded banyan tree, in this way the devotee feels relief and pleasure.”

To a prema-bhakta in his pangs of separation from his beloved Lord, his own dear friends appear like a dry well without water and his own home pricks him like a house of thorns. Food tastes like severe beating, praise seems like the venom of a snakebite, and the different limbs and organs of his body become a heavy burden to him. The solacing words of friends sound platitudinous and even bitter. Sleep is death to him, and existence of the body seems to him as God's punishment. What is more, even meditation on the beloved by such a prema-bhakta in the agonies of separation, feels as if his very soul was breaking apart. Like a covetous thief, the prema-bhakta loses all powers of distinguishing between good and evil.

As the soothing rays of the moon cool the scorching heat of a thousand suns, so also the union with Shri Krishna removes all the intense pangs of separation from the heart of a devotee who has acquired this wealth of prema.

Overpowered by these anxieties of prema of His devotee, Shri Krishna reveals to him His matchless beauty, extraordinary loveliness, limitless magnanimity, and fathomless compassion and such other divine qualities.

The devotee who has ascended the ladder of prema and has thus been blessed by the Lord, experiences a divinely wondrous and luminous pleasure that far outweighs any other pleasures. As a tired traveller in the scorching rays of the summer sun, in the middle of a vast desert, suddenly finds a densely shaded banyan tree, full of thick foliage with all its branches and twigs, or a wild elephant enjoys being drenched in a cool waterfall after it has escaped half-burnt from the huge forest fire, so the devotee feels pleasure.

After an individual soul has attained prema by his or her spiritual practices, he or she now beholds the Lord face-to-face and realizes that this luminous experience far excels all the experience he or she had at the stage of bhava. This experience takes place through the soul’s spiritual eyes, nose, ears, touch, and sense of taste.

Out of His magnanimity Shri Krishna occasionally makes the devotee experience in his prema the beatitude of his beauteous sweetness all at once with all his supra-mundane and transcendental senses. At the time of revealing his person before his devotee in the stage of prema, the Lord responds favourably to the devotee's own nature and spiritual practice.

## Raganuga bhakti

**Raga is defined as a ‘strong thirst, a deep attraction.’ The ultimate object of raga is Krishna, and the very pinnacle of raga is felt by the liberated souls. Since their very self (atma) is made of spontaneous attraction (raga), their service to Krishna is known as raga-atmika or ragatmika bhakti. Reawakening of spontaneous attraction is, of course, the goal of bhakti, and comes at an advanced stage. This chapter discusses what raganuga bhakti is, and the next chapter explains what it is not.**Srila Rupa Goswami defines raga:

**“Materialists fall in love with the objects of the material senses. This love may be called "raga". In this way the eyes become excited when they see beauty and other attractive qualities. In this way one is attracted to the material sense objects and the mind develops ‘raga,’ or love for them. When that love (raga) is placed in Lord Krishna as its only object, that is called raga-bhakti.”**

**Bhakti Rasamrita Sindhu 1.2.17-18.**

When a devotee in this world, having worked diligently through all the preparatory levels of bhakti, becomes attracted to a particular liberated devotee (a ragatmika devotee) and their service to Krishna, and wishes to follow (anuga) them in their particular form of spontaneous service (raga) they become known as raga-anuga or raganuga bhaktas. Thus, a raganuga-bhakta is one who ‘follows in the wake’ of the perfect devotees, earnestly endeavouring to achieve their mood of spontaneous love. And because raganuga-bhakti is something that is practiced as a ‘means of attainment,’ it is also a sadhana, hence the term raganuga-bhakti sadhana. Srila Prabhupada explains:

**“The original inhabitants of Vrindavan are attached to Krishna spontaneously in devotional service. Nothing can compare to such spontaneous devotional service, which is called ragatmika bhakti. When a devotee follows in the footsteps of the devotees of Vrindavan, his devotional service is called raganuga bhakti.”**

**Madhya 22.149**

Spontaneity is not something which can be imitated or arrived at prematurely. It gradually awakens within the devotee after carefully adhering to the behaviour of vaidhi bhakti for some time. In the Chaitanya Charitamrita, Srila Prabhupada describes the basic qualification for raganuga-bhakti sadhana:

“When an advanced, realized devotee hears about the affairs of the devotees of Vṛndāvana — in the mellows of śānta, dāsya, sakhya, vātsalya and mādhurya — he becomes inclined in one of these ways, and his intelligence becomes attracted. Indeed, he begins to covet that particular type of devotion. When such covetousness is awakened, one’s intelligence no longer depends on the instructions of śāstra \[revealed scripture\] or on logic and argument.”

The basic qualification for raganuga-bhakti is eagerness, or ‘intense covetousness,’ known as lobha, to achieve the service of a particular ragatmika devotee, but this is not easy to attain. Lobha does not awaken until steadiness in devotional practice is reached, when the heart is fully purified. In his book Nectar of Devotion, Srila Prabhupada explains that: “We must always remember, however, that such eagerness…is not possible unless one is freed from material contamination.” He defines such freedom as: “There is a stage called anartha nivritti, which means the disappearance of all material contamination.”The acharyas describe that vaidhi-sadhana-bhakti is like an unripe mango and that raganuga-sadhana-bhakti is like a ripe mango. They are the same fruit in different stages of ripening. Therefore, Srila Prabhupada always wrote of raganuga sadhana growing out of vaidhi sadhana as follows:

**“An advanced devotee is attracted by the service rendered by an eternal servitor of the Lord. This attraction is called spontaneous attraction. Technically it is called svarР•«pa-upalabdhi. This stage is not achieved in the beginning. In the beginning one must render service strictly according to the regulative principles set forth by the revealed scriptures and the spiritual master. By continuously rendering service through the process of vaidhi bhakti, one's natural inclination is gradually awakened. That is called spontaneous attraction, or raganuga bhakti.”**

**CC Madhya 22.153**

Srila Prabhupada explains that the internal motivation of one performing raganuga-bhakti is enthusiasm and spontaneity. The devotee is no longer motivated by a sense of duty or religious regulation. That inner enthusiasm comes ‘step by step’ as the devotee follows the principles of devotion. Even though it might be difficult at first, if the devotee has patience the higher stage will come, eventually:

“The mood of surrender during the stage of vaidhi-bhakti (devotional service under strict rules and regulations) is different from that in the stage of raganuga-bhakti (spontaneous devotional service). In the spontaneous stage, the mood of surrender is the natural expression of the self. When the process of surrender is followed step by step, one patiently executes the Lord's orders and gradually becomes enthusiastic. Such a devotee follows the regulative principles of hearing, chanting, remembering, and so on, and emulates previous saintly preceptors. In the association of devotees, he becomes more and more proficient in rendering devotional service. Gradually his service becomes easier. Thus, constant remembrance of the Lord comes simply by developing enthusiasm and patience in devotional activities.”

Renunciation through Wisdom chapter 15

From these statements it can be understood that, since raganuga bhakti ripens from it, vaidhi bhakti is a much-required ‘apprenticeship,’ as Srila Prabhupada explains:

“The more you are engaged in devotional service, the more your senses become pure or uncovered. And when it is completely uncovered, without any designation, then you are capable to serve Kṛṣṇa. This is apprenticeship. Vaidhi-bhakti, that is apprenticeship. Real bhakti, para-bhakti, that is raganuga-bhakti. This raganuga-bhakti, we have to come after surpassing the vaidhi-bhakti. In the material world, if we do not try to make further and further progress in devotional service, if we are simply sticking to the shastric regulation process and do not try to go beyond that... Shastric process also regulation, that is required. Without shastric process you cannot go to that platform.”

Lecture on Shrimad Bhagavatam 1.2.33, November 12, 1973

The external behaviour of the devotee does not change once bhava has appeared within. Rather, the hearing, chanting and menial services all continue as before. But now they will be performed without any trace of mental resistance or effort, and they will be done with joy. In his Nectar of Devotion, Srila Prabhupada explains:

“It is said by Śrī Rūpa Gosvāmī, “When one is actually liberated from material contamination, he can always remember an eternal devotee in Vṛndāvana in order to love Kṛṣṇa in the same capacity. And developing such an aptitude, one will always live in Vṛndāvana, even within his mind.” The purport is that if it is possible one should go and physically be present at Vraja-bhūmi, Vṛndāvana, and be engaged always in the service of the Lord, following the devotees in Vraja-dhāma, the spiritual realm of Vraja. If it is not possible, however, to be physically present at Vṛndāvana, one can meditate anywhere on living in that situation. Wherever he may be, one must always think about life in Vraja-dhāma and about following in the footsteps of a particular devotee in the service of the Lord.”

Srila Prabhupada takes care that his readers understand that a devotee who has reached such a level of purity should not behave in such a way as to reveal his good fortune to others. Similarly, even though the absolute requirement for stringent regulations has been surpassed by the onset of the very goal of those regulations, the development of spontaneous attraction, the devotee should remain externally the same:

“A devotee who is advanced in Krishna consciousness, who is constantly engaged in devotional service, should not manifest himself, even though he has attained perfection. The idea is that he should always continue to act as a neophyte devotee as long as his material body is there. Activities in devotional service under regulative principles must be followed even by the pure devotee. But when he realizes his actual position in relationship with the Lord, he can, along with the discharging of regulative service, think within himself of the Lord under the guidance of a particular associate of the Lord, and develop his transcendental sentiments in following that associate.”

The question may be raised: How does a person in raganuga sadhana bhakti act? He no longer needs to follow the regulative principles in the same way as before, so what does that mean for his daily practice? Again, Srila Prabhupada, paraphrasing Rupa Goswami, clarifies:

“Śrī Rūpa Gosvāmī says that learned acharyas recommend that we follow the regulative principles even after the development of spontaneous love for Kṛṣṇa. According to the regulative principles, there are nine departmental activities, as described above, and one should specifically engage himself in the type of devotional service for which he has a natural aptitude. For example, one person may have a particular interest in hearing, another may have a particular interest in chanting, and another may have a particular interest in serving in the temple. So these, or any of the other six different types of devotional service (remembering, serving, praying, engaging in some particular service, being in a friendly relationship or offering everything in one’s possession), should be executed in full earnestness. In this way, everyone should act according to his particular taste.”Several years later, when translating the Chaitanya Charitamrita, he reiterates the same point, highlighting the difference between the internal and external service of such an advanced devotee:

**“There are two processes by which one may execute this raganuga bhakti—external and internal. When self-realized, the advanced devotee externally remains like a neophyte and executes all the shastric injunctions, especially those concerning hearing and chanting. But within his mind, in his original, purified, self-realized position, he serves Krishna in Vrindavan in his particular way. He serves Krishna twenty-four hours a day, all day and night.”**

**CC Madhya 22.156-157**

The Six Goswamis of Vrindavan are the perfect examples of internal and external bhakti. Externally, they were performing all the prescribed activities of regulated devotional activities. They reduced eating and sleeping, rested in a different place each night to maintain their detachment, chanted many malas of Hare Krishna japa, wrote books, excavated holy places, counselled commoners and kings, and built beautiful temples. They guided disciples and engaged them in missionary work. Internally, however, they were engaged in the highest service as associates of the Queen of Vrindavan, Srimati Radharani.

The advanced devotee never becomes lazy, apathetic, or distracted in his execution of devotion. In the ‘apprenticeship period’ he is using every moment to serve Krishna, albeit with motivation born from a desire for purification. In the advanced period of devotion, when love for Krishna awakens in the heart, the devotee feels as if he is not a devotee at all, and that his only hope now is to become a servant of a servant’s servant of Krishna, many times removed, and nothing more, so his acts of external devotion do not decrease, they tend to increase. Yet in this state of abject humility, he also feels spontaneously attracted to a liberated devotee who serves Krishna in one of the relationships. This attraction compels him to hear about this devotee’s service to Krishna, and the devotee then feels motivated to behave similarly:

“Spontaneous devotional service…can be executed in the shanta-rasa when one aspires to be like Krishna’s cows or the stick or flute in the hand of Krishna, or the flowers around Krishna’s neck. In the dasya-rasa one follows in the footsteps of servants like Citraka, Patraka or Raktaka. In the friendly sakhya-rasa one can become a friend like Baladeva, Śrīdāmā or Sudāmā. In the vatsalya-rasa , characterized by parental affection, one can become like Nanda Mahārāja and Yaśodā, and in the madhurya-rasa , characterized by conjugal love, one can become like Srimati Radharani or her lady friends such as Lalita and her serving maids (manjaris) like Rupa and Rati. This is the essence of all instruction in the matter of devotional service.”

Nectar of Instruction 8

This does not mean that the devotee becomes one of Krishna’s personal entourage such as Nanda or Yashoda, but that he moulds his serving desires upon theirs, and is very attracted to assisting them in their service. Krishna is served in the spiritual world by liberated devotees, both those who were never conditioned at all, and who are with Krishna eternally, and those who were gradually elevated through the practice of regulated bhakti until they were pure enough to return to him. The devotee aspires to be in the latter category:

“These beings don't have to learn anything about devotional service; they are already perfect in all regulative principles and have achieved the spontaneous loving service of the Supreme Personality of Godhead. For example, the cowherd boys who are playing with Krishna do not have to learn by austerities or penances or yogic practice how to play with him. They have passed all tests of regulative principles in their previous lives, and as a result they are now elevated to the position of direct association with Krishna as his dear friends.”

Nectar of Devotion chapter 15

## Divine service and dangerous deceptions

**In spiritual life we have two options: to follow the great saints or to imitate them. The first is known as anukaran, the second anusharan. These two words may sound similar, but one leads us upwards and the other pulls us down. One leads to serving Krishna in our eternal form, the other to an imaginary liberation. This chapter discusses both.**

Vishnu smaranam is one of the nine processes of bhakti. It means to ‘remember’ Krishna by directly harnessing the wandering mind and thinking of his form, beauty, nature, and activities. Since love of Krishna is eternally situated in the heart, this process gradually awakens what is there already, but which currently lies dormant. Smaranam is not practiced in isolation from the other eight processes such as kirtan, rather it is achieved through them. Although it is listed as an individual process it is performed in conjunction with the others and arises because of them. In the advanced stage of devotion, however, smaranam is achieved effortlessly by already having awakened one’s spontaneous attraction to Krishna. The devotee is completely drawn to Krishna, and Krishna reciprocates by flooding the devotee’s mind with thoughts of him. In this case, it is impossible for the devotee to pull his mind away from Krishna, even if he wants to.

## Chaitanya Mahaprabhu’s instructions

Sri Chaitanya Mahaprabhu taught that smaranam, and everything else desired by the devotee, could be found within the chanting of the holy names of Lord Krishna:

*nitya-siddha krsna-prema sadhya kabhu naya*  
*sravanadi suddha-citte karaye udaya*

**Ecstatic love for Krishna is eternally perfect and cannot be achieved through any effort; however, it manifests in the heart that has been purified by the acts of devotion such as hearing and chanting.**

**Chaitanya Charitamrita Madhya 22.107**

In his only known written work, the Sikshastakam, he explains the ascending realisation and experience of the devotee in eight verses. In effect, the verses explain the entire philosophy in a nutshell. The first verse mentions seven results to be gained from the chanting of the names. These include a thorough cleansing of the heart from the effects of many previous lives, the dawning of pure knowledge, and the experience of spiritual bliss. The poem culminates in the highest sentiments for Krishna, feelings that are often accompanied by physical symptoms such as incessant joyful tears, but also intermittent feelings of being abandoned by Krishna.

The poem closely mirrors Lord Chaitanya’s life as he went from starting the sankirtan revolution in Navadwipa, to becoming almost a recluse in his last years in Puri. During the last eighteen years he experienced increasing levels of ecstatic trance, with a wide range of emotional transformations and physical symptoms. His instructions to his countless followers included the variety of messages he wanted them to share with successive generations.

To Nityananda he gave the instruction to create a popular movement in Bengal, and to make sure his Harinam ministry included the poor, women, and the uneducated. Advaita Acharya was given a similar instruction. Rupa Goswami and Sanatana Goswami were asked to rediscover the lost holy places of Vrindavan, create pilgrimage places and temples, and to write books. Specifically, Rupa was requested to explain the principles of rasa, and Sanatana was asked to delineate the principles of vaidhi-bhakti, and the relationship between vaidhi-bhakti and raga-bhakti. Gopala Bhatta Goswami was told to write treatises by which one can easily detect and expose aberrant philosophies and practises adulterating the principles of pure madhurya-rasa. Simultaneously he was to ensure the importance of vaidhi-bhakti was understood and followed. Swarupa Damodara Goswami was asked to disseminate rasa-upasana, the process of worship of Krishna inculcated with rasa.

## Siddha Deha

The terms siddha deha (spiritual body), siddha rupa (spiritual form) and swarupa (one’s ‘own form’), all indicate the eternal body of the devotee in which he serves Krishna in the spiritual sky, a form achieved at the conclusion of the process of self-purification. The terms are often used interchangeably, and are found in the Chaitanya Charitamrita:

*siddha-dehe cinti’ kare tāhāṅñi sevana*  
*sakhī-bhāve pāya rādhā-kṛṣṇera caraṇa*

**After thinking of Radha and Krishna and their pastimes for a long time and after getting completely free from material contamination, one is transferred to the spiritual world. There the devotee attains an opportunity to serve Radha and Krishna as one of the gopis.**

**Chaitanya Charitamrita Madhya Lila 8.229**

In his commentary to this verse, words spoken to Chaitanya Mahaprabhu by Ramananda Raya, Srila Prabhupada carefully explains the meaning of siddha-deha, beginning with a reference to his own guru’s words:

“Srila Bhaktisiddhanta Thakur comments that the word siddha-deha, ‘perfected spiritual body,’ refers to a body beyond the material gross body composed of five elements and the subtle astral body composed of mind, intelligence, and false ego. In other words, one attains a completely spiritual body fit to render service to the transcendental couple Radha and Krishna.

“When one is situated in his spiritual body, which is beyond this gross and subtle material body, he is fit to serve Radha and Krishna. That body is called siddha-deha. The living entity attains a particular type of gross body in accordance with his past activities and mental condition. In this life the mental condition changes in different ways, and the same living entity gets another body in the next life according to his desires. The mind, intelligence and false ego are always engaged in an attempt to dominate material nature. According to that subtle astral body, one attains a gross body to enjoy the objects of one’s desires. According to the activities of the present body, one prepares another subtle body. And according to the subtle body, one attains another gross body. This is the process of material existence. However, when one is spiritually situated and does not desire a gross or subtle body, he attains his original spiritual body.

“In Vaikuntha the consort is Lakshmi and in Goloka Vrindavan the consort is Srimati Radharani. In the spiritual body, free from material contamination, one can serve Radha Krishna and Lakshmi Narayana. When one is thus spiritually situated, he no longer thinks of his own personal sense gratification. This spiritual body is called siddha-deha , the body by which one can render transcendental service unto Radha and Krishna. The process is that of engaging the transcendental senses in loving devotional service. This verse specifically mentions, sakhī-bhāve pāya rādhā-kṛṣṇera caraṇa: only transcendentally elevated persons in the mood of the gopīs can engage in the service of the lotus feet of Radha and Krishna.”

According to the teachings he directly heard from Lord Chaitanya, the definition of siddha rupa is given by Rupa Goswami:

*sevā sādhaka-rūpeṇa siddha-rūpeṇa cātra hi*  
*tad-bhāva-lipsunā kāryā vraja-lokānusārataḥ*

**Following the inhabitants of Vraja, one should perform service in one’s physical body (sadhaka rupa) and in one’s perfect body (siddha rupa) with a desire for a particular bhava.**

**Bhakti Rasamrita Sindhu 1.2.295**

From this statement, it appears that an advanced devotee is practicing bhakti-yoga on two levels simultaneously. The 18th century saint, Vishvanatha Chakravarti, explains how one person can operate both as a practitioner of bhakti in the physical body, while simultaneously serving Krishna in the eternal body:

**“Mental performance with the siddha rupa (perfected body) is to be done in a manner that follows Sri Radha, Vishakha, Lalita, Sri Rupa Manjari, and other gopis. But physical performance, with the sadhaka-rupa (physical body), is to be done in a manner that follows Sri Rupa, Sanatana, and other Vrindavan Goswamis.”**

**Raga Vartma Chandrika**

## A ‘mentally conceived’ siddha deha

In their commentaries to Rupa Goswami’s ‘sadhaka-rupena, siddha-rupena’ verse, both Jiva Goswami and Vishvanatha Chakravarti comment that even before the disciple achieves a vision of his actual eternal body, he may mentally conceive of his desired spiritual form and serve Krishna within his mind in this body. They both write:

**“In ‘the siddha rupa’ means in an internally conceived, desired body suitable for His service.”**

The question may be raised as to whether this ‘internally conceived’ siddha deha is merely overactive imagination on the part of the disciple, or an actual reality. According to the acharyas, if the devotee has become purified, reached the level of transcendental greed to serve the Lord in his spiritual form, and is experiencing the symptoms of attachment, he may approach his guru and explain his desire. After consultation with his disciple the guru may agree to the disciple’s proposal and confirm his meditational identity. The mutual agreement to meditate on a siddha deha will not be an imposition on the actual eternal form of the disciple if given at the right time when the feeling has awakened. Krishnadas Kaviraja comments:

**“On the raganuga-path, even the sadhaka bhaktas whose rati (dominant loving emotion) has not yet arisen, mentally conceive of their desired siddha dehas and thus serve the Lord. When rati has arisen, that body is perceived automatically. In the arisen rati, one gradually attains the stage of anuraga; then that siddha deha will be always perceived.”**

**Krishna das Kaviraja Goswami´s commentary on Krishna Karanamritam verse 2**

Given that the devotee has yet to attain the body of an associate in Vraja, the question may be asked as to whether it is possible to contemplate on a siddha-deha prior to the attainment of perfection. From these comments it can be understood that the practitioner of bhakti may, at the appropriate time, and before being completely awakened to his eternal identity, meditate on a spiritual body, and imagine himself serving Krishna in that form. If he is advanced enough, the desire to do so will have sprung from a pure, awakened consciousness, and there will be a correspondence between his ‘mentally conceived’ form and his actual, eternal form. If not, it will be an artificial imposition, his interaction with Krishna will be nothing but imagination, and his bhakti will consequently suffer. Therefore, it is crucial to have a guru who has realised his own siddha deha and to consult him. In the beginning of his section on raganuga bhakti in his Bhakti Sandarbha, Srila Jiva Goswami describes how one can practice raganuga bhakti before one has realized one’s eternal relationship with Krishna:

**“When a person develops a taste for the aforesaid specific raga (attraction), even though that raga itself has not yet arisen in him, the heart becomes like a crystal, shining as it reflects the rays of the moon of that raga. By hearing about this raga from scripture or one’s teacher, one develops a taste for the actions of the ragatmika associates that are expressions of this ragatmika bhakti. Then by adhering to the raga of a particular associate according to one’s taste, one executes devotion, which is called raganuga.”**

**Bhakti Sandarbha, anuccheda 310.**

Srila Bhaktivinode Thakur explains:

**“When the sadhaka’s lobha (greed) for raganuga bhakti is awakened, he approaches his gurudeva with great humility and asks for instruction. If he does so, the bona fide guru first examines the disciple’s taste and then, along with devising the appropriate bhajan (form of worship) introduces him to his own siddha identity.”**

**Sri Chaitanya Shiksamrita part 6, chapter 5**

To aid this process, the guru may also discuss with the disciple characteristics of the eternal body to which the disciple aspires. The disciple, if qualified, may already have an idea of his siddha, and the guru then ensures it is within acceptable parameters. In other cases, the guru may speak himself on the bodily description, name, service, and forest location of the siddha deha of the disciple. Just how such a spiritual identity is then implemented is described by the Thakur as follows:

**“The sadhana…is executed in five progressive stages: shravana dasa – the stage of hearing, varana dasa – the stage of accepting, smarana dasa – the stage of remembering, apana dasa – the stage of maturing, and sampatti dasa – the stage of attainment.”**

Incrementally, step by step, the aspirant progresses from simply hearing about service to Krishna in an eternal form, through thinking, conceiving, then actualising a form in which to serve.Being introduced to one’s own siddha identity often comes many years after mantra diksha, or initiation. In all cases, it depends on the mercy of the guru and the eligibility of the disciple. Vishvanatha Chakravarti Thakur writes:

**“The mercy of Krishna and His devotees is the only cause of attaining raganuga-bhakti. Some call this type of devotion pushti-marga.” “There are two causes for the appearance of sacred greed: The mercy of God or the mercy of another anurР”РѓgР”« devotee. There are again two kinds of mercy bestowed by a devotee: prР”Рѓktana and Р”Рѓdhunika; prР”Рѓktana means mercy bestowed by a raganuga bhakta in a previous life, and Р”Рѓdhunika is mercy bestowed in the present birth. The prР”Рѓktana-devotee takes shelter of the lotus feet of a raganuga guru after the sacred greed has arisen in him, the Р”Рѓdhunika will get that sacred greed only after having surrendered to the feet of such a guru.”**

**Srila Vishvanatha Chakravarti in Raga Vartma Chandrika**

## Siddha Pranali

Lord Chaitanya’s instructions to Swarupa Damodar included the request to disseminate knowledge of ‘worship inculcated with rasa.’ He was the Lord’s secretary and was in constant association with him during his last years. He kept detailed diaries which were composed in two sections: antah-pantha – the esoteric, internal means of attainment, and bahih-pantha – the exoteric, external means of attainment. Acting as a guru, Swarupa Damodar’s knowledge of the method of worshiping was passed down through two different lines: one through Raghunath Das Goswami which was then carried on to Krishna Das Kaviraj Goswami, the other through Vakreshvara Pandit whose disciple was Gopal Guru Goswami.

Gopal Guru wrote two books on the subject, Smarana-krama-paddhati , ‘A Guidebook to the Steps to Remembering,’ also known as Seva-smarana-paddhati , ‘A Guidebook to Remembering and Service,’ and Gaura-Govinda Archana-paddhati , ‘A Guidebook to Gaura-Govinda’s deity worship.’ In turn, Gopal Guru Goswami passed on his knowledge to his disciple, Dhyanachandra Goswami, who wrote another guidebook named Shri Gaura-Govinda Archana Smaraṇa Paddhati .

Vaishnavas who have regained their siddha deha (the actual one, not the ‘mentally conceived’ one) are sometimes known as siddha, a general Sanskrit term for ‘perfected.’ After the time of Chaitanya Mahaprabhu there were those who were marked as siddha by the Vaishnava community. As one siddha guru gave initiation to disciples who then themselves became siddha, there grew the recognition of a lineage of such siddhas. The Sanskrit word for ‘channel’ as in a channel of flowing water, is pranali, and the ‘flow of perfected gurus’ became known as the siddha-pranali.

The term siddha pranali is not mentioned in any of the books of the Six Goswamis, although the word pranali and the concept of a lineage of realised teachers would, of course, have been well known to them. The term seems to have been first mentioned in the small handbook of Dhyanachandra Goswami.

For certain Vaishnava communities it became the custom, at some point in history, (and exactly when is not generally known), for the siddha to reveal his spiritual identity to his disciples and then, at an appropriate time, and at the disciple’s sincere request, to reveal the disciple’s own form. Generally, the revealed form of the disciple would place him in an appropriate cohort, serving in the eternal Vrindavan alongside his guru.In the siddha-pranali system, the guru privately tells a qualified disciple the eleven aspects of his siddha deha known as ekadasha-bhava, which include the disciple’s eternal spiritual name in Krishna lila, age, dress, service, location, mood, group leader and so on. As learned from his own guru, the guru would then teach the disciple the same method to realize his/her eternal spiritual identity, or siddha deha. In the last chapter of his Harinam Chintamani, Thakur Bhaktivinode gives the list of eleven characteristics:

**“In order to fulfil one’s ambitions for attaining madhurya rasa there are eleven items: relationship, age, name, form, group, dress, order, residence, service, highest ambition, and the friend of Srimati Radharani one serves under.”**

**Harinam Chintamani 15.58**

In addition, the introduction of one’s siddha deha includes the siddha deha identities of each guru in one’s individual parampara. The disciple would then meditate on his own, and the previous gurus’ forms, while mentally serving Radha-Krishna in the eternal Vrindavan. The disciple would be able to trace his lineage, not only a chain of gurus through several hundred years of history, but also a chain of associates of the cowherd maidens or cowherd boys, each beginning with an associate of Chaitanya Mahaprabhu such as Nityananda Prabhu or Swarupa Damodara Goswami. Each lineage of siddhas came to be known as a parivara or ‘retinue.’

## Problems in the Pranali

One immediate problem, which might easily be anticipated, arises when the guru cannot ascertain the disciple’s spiritual identity. Until the disciple has ascertained it himself, by virtue of an intense spiritual taste – devoid of mundane conceptions, sentiment, or imagination – the guru, even though qualified, will have difficulty offering guidance. Srila Bhaktivinode Thakur highlights the possible disconnect between guru and disciple:

**“When the spiritual master is ascertaining the aspirant’s pure personal inclinations (ruchi), the aspirant should also help the spiritual master by speaking his mind about his own preferences. As long as he has not clearly established the disciple’s inclinations, the guru’s directions are not flawless.”**

**Hari Nama Chintamani 15.72.**

Another problem arises if the disciple imagines that he can perform internal meditation without simultaneously continuing the standard practices of hearing and chanting. If constant smaranam is the goal of bhakti, and if smaranam arises only when the mind is free from materialistic thoughts, then the practitioner should adhere to the advice given by one of the greatest followers of Lord Chaitanya. In his monumental work on bhakti, the Bhakti Sandarbha, Sri Jiva Goswami writes: “Smaranam is dependent on the purity of the mind.” He adds that even though an advanced devotee may find it easy to remember Krishna, he should not abandon any of the processes that helped him reach that point: suddhantah-karanas cet nama-kirtana-parityagena smaranam kuryat – “If the mind is pure, one should engage in smaranam without abandoning nama-kirtan.” (Bhakti Sandarbha 275).Sanatana Goswami explains that there is a synergistic relationship between smaranam and kirtan:

**“By congregational singing, the joy of meditation grows, and by meditation the sweet joy of congregational singing grows. In our experience, the two methods fortify one another and are therefore actually one.”**

**Brihad Bhagavatamrita**

The tendency to impose ekadasha-bhava upon neophyte devotees still in the early stages of sraddha, sadhu-sanga, bhajan-kriya, and anartha-nivritti, and for those devotees to then cast aside the standard processes of devotional practice, imagining themselves to be beyond them, have been two grand mistakes in the history of the Vaishnava sampradayas coming from Lord Chaitanya. The first has been a deception employed to attract the ignorant masses and collect disciples, the second an illusion stemming from arrogance.

In the early 1700s there was an errant Vaishnava named Rupa Kaviraja who taught predominantly in the Assam region of eastern India. His teachings then spread to the Vrindavan communities. He taught of a natural divorce between the internal and external methods of bhajan, the smaranam and kirtan, reasoning that if the gopis themselves did not study or count thousands of names of Krishna on beads, then neither should those who had already realised their siddha deha. And furthermore, if someone has reached this level, there must also be an imperceptible transition of his physical identity towards the nature of the siddha deha. The result of this teaching was men who neither studied nor chanted and, in some cases, emulated female mannerisms and dress. Things progressed to the point where he was subjected to a trial and excommunicated at a gathering of sampradaya leaders in Jaipur in 1727. The effect of his teaching, and that of several others, would be to open the glorious parampara to even more speculative gurus and deluded disciples. Their combination almost wrecked the entire community.

By the time of Bhaktivinode Thakur there were thirteen separate apa-sampradayas, or ‘blind sampradayas,’ practising one or more forms of deviation. Presenting themselves as members of the lineages of the great devotees of Chaitanya Mahaprabhu, or in the biological family tree of Nityananda, they attracted disciples by promising them quicker access to spiritual perfection, or claimed they were the only ones authorised to do so, taught invented mantras, and even engaged in sordid practices with their surrendered but gullible students, usually female.

## A Lineage of Siddhas

Although the Thakur had been initiated into one siddha pranali through his guru Bipin Bihari Goswami, and even initiated one of his sons by the same method, he was pained by the spiritual and moral abuses he saw around him. He campaigned against it, much to the consternation of those who were making good business from it. His son, Bhaktisiddhanta Thakur, took up his father’s campaign, revitalising the Vaishnava community to the point of identifying a new parampara, a lineage of genuine siddhas, connected not by mantra-diksha or the giving of siddha deha, but purely on the basis of their purity, knowledge-sharing or siksha, and their contribution to the welfare and upliftment of the world. He called this the bhagavata parampara, and it included siddhas from several different lineages.

This incensed those who maintained that the flow of spirituality could only come through a siddha pranali lineage, unbroken and connected one to the other by diksha. They declared that the parampara of Bhaktisiddhanta Thakur was not a flowing pranali but ‘a dead canal.’The teaching of Bhaktisiddhanta Saraswati on raganuga-bhakti was that those practitioners who are ajata ruci (without taste) should adopt the methods of raganuga sadhana only that they are qualified to adopt in proportion to their development of sacred greed (lobhamayi sraddha), while following the angas (limbs) of vaidhi bhakti (regulated devotional service). In making this statement he was perfectly following Jiva Goswami's explanations in the Bhakti-sandarbha.

**"One in whom this taste (ruchi) has not arisen but who has come to appreciate raganuga-bhakti only on account of appreciation for a particular saint or scripture (sat), may still practice raganuga-bhakti but with an admixture of vaidhi-bhakti. In the same way, for the sake of preaching (loka-sangrahartaham) one who is advanced and in whom taste has manifested should also practice raganuga with an admixture of vaidhi. Such mixing of the two kinds of bhakti means that one practices vaidhi-bhakti by uniting it with whatever raganuga practices one is capable of."**

**Bhakti Sandarbha 311**

In the opinion of Sri Bhaktisiddhanta Saraswati, such mixing of the two kinds of bhakti means that one embrace the angas or ‘limbs’ of vaidhi-bhakti with a view to attain greater eligibility for raganuga-bhakti and its sadhya (goal) by uniting them with whatever raganuga practices one is eligible for (yatah-yogyam). In his opinion this did not include an emphasis on meditating on the pastimes of Radha-Krishna from the vantage point of one's siddha deha for those who had not attained the stage of nistha, based on the reasoning that dhyana (meditation) requires a pure heart, whereas kirtanam (chanting) does not. He reasoned, as has Sri Sanatana Goswami in Brihad-bhagavat-amrita, that smaranam (remembering) arises naturally out of kirtanam.

Thus, he emphasized nama smaranam for beginners, stressing inoffensive chanting that would lead naturally to meditation on Krishna’s form (rupa samaranam), qualities (guna smaranam), and pastimes (lila smaranam), the remembrance of one's lila in Krishna's pastimes. Of course, he also emphasized mantra dhyana of the Gayatri mantras, and these mantras were given to sadhakas only after they had attained a degree of steadiness in nama smaranam (japa).Bhaktisiddhanta Saraswati was reacting to what he perceived as 'a sleight of hand' in the name of giving, or in some cases making a business out of and selling, siddha pranali. Thus, his position was that sadhakas, while aspiring for Vraja bhakti, should do kirtanam and that as this kirtanam qualified them they should combine it with smaranam. Furthermore, he maintained that one's eternal identity would be glimpsed in the stages of ruchi and asakti, at which time effective and meaningful lila smaranam from the vantage point of one's siddha deha could take place propelling the sadhaka into bhava bhakti. He told his own disciples a story of his guru’s proclamation of how to become siddha:

**“Once a grihastha follower of Gaura-kisora dasa Babaji asked for siddha-pranali (ekadasa-bhava initiation). Babaji Maharaja replied, “The Hare Krishna maha-mantra is a siddha-mantra. Within this mantra are the siddha-rupa of the Supreme Lord Krishna and the siddha-rupas of all living entities.**

**If you chant the maha-mantra purely, the syllables of the maha-mantra will gradually reveal the spiritual form, qualities, and pastimes of Shri Krishna.**

**This chanting will also reveal your eternal spiritual form, service, and the eleven particulars of your spiritual identity.”**

Two hundred years before the Thakur, the standards within some communities that practised siddha pranali diksha were that the disciple had to first purify himself by chanting sixty-four rounds of the Hare Krishna mantra every day for twenty years. Another community required the disciples to chant 192 rounds every day for one year. By the time of the Thakur, in the early 20th century, that had given way to much lower standards. (And now in the 21st century, some groups only ask for four rounds per day for a period of six weeks.)

The siddha pranali gurus would write out the eleven characteristics on paper, sometimes alongside a printed map of Vrindavan indicating where the eternal associate was to serve Krishna. Sadly, as the years passed by, the culture of giving ekadasha-bhava at the point of initiation, and requesting funds for the privilege, in the name of guru-dakshina, became gradually widespread.

## ‘Imitators and cheap devotees’

It is not known whether Bhaktisiddhanta Thakur ever gave siddha deha identities to his disciples. Although there is some anecdotal evidence, it is scanty, there is nothing in writing, and that generation is now deceased. What is very clear is that his foremost disciple, A. C. Bhaktivedanta Swami Prabhupada, spoke and wrote about it with great clarity on numerous occasions. While explaining that such states of love of God would also awaken knowledge of one’s eternal form, he sharply criticised those who were, as he called them, ‘imitationists,’ ‘pseudo-devotees,’ and sahajiyas or ‘those who want things cheaply.’ A selection of his statements on this important area is given below.

In the first extract, Srila Prabhupada differentiates between vaidhi bhakti and raganuga bhakti, or as he terms it here, raga marga. Although the initiated neophyte may worship Radha-Krishna in the temple, he says, because the neophyte is not advanced, the worship is accepted byLakshmi Narayana, a subsidiary form of Krishna. Any attempt to bypass the stage of vaidhi bhakti, or to curtail it prematurely, will result in degradation of the practitioner of bhakti, and render them as sahajiyas, or ‘cheap devotees.’

**“Without serving Krishna according to the vidhi-marga regulative principles of the pancharatrika-vidhi, unscrupulous persons want to jump immediately to the raga-marga principles. Such persons are called sahajiya. There are also demons who enjoy depicting Krishna and his pastimes with the gopis, taking advantage of Krishna by their licentious character. These demons who print books and write lyrics on the raga-marga principles are surely on the way to hell. Unfortunately, they lead others down with them. Devotees in Krishna consciousness should be very careful to avoid such demons.**

**One should strictly follow the vidhi-marga regulative principles in the worship of Lakshmi-Narayana.
