Although the Lord is present in the temple as Radha-Krishna, Radha-Krishna includes Lakshmi-Narayana; therefore, when one worships the Lord according to the regulative principles, the Lord accepts the service in the role of Lakshmi-Narayana. In the Nectar of Devotion full instructions are given about the vidhi-marga worship of Radha-Krishna or Lakshmi-Narayana. Although there are sixty-four kinds of offenses one can commit in vidhi-marga worship, in raga-marga worship there is no consideration of such offenses because the devotees on that platform are very much elevated, and there is no question of offense. But if we do not follow the regulative principles on the vidhi-marga platform and keep our eyes trained to spot offenses, we will not make progress.”**

**Shrimad Bhagavatam 4.24.45-46**

**“The siddha-pranali process is followed by a class of men who are not very authorized and who have manufactured their own way of devotional service. They imagine that they have become associates of the Lord simply by thinking of themselves like that. This external behaviour is not at all according to the regulative principles.**

**The so-called siddha-pranali process is followed by the prakrta-sahajiya, a pseudo-sect of so-called Vaishnavas. In the opinion of Rupa Goswami, such activities are simply disturbances to the standard way of devotional service. Sri Rupa Goswami says that learned acharyas recommend that we follow the regulative principles even after the development of spontaneous love for Krishna.”**

**Nectar of Devotion 16**

Nevertheless, it is not that Srila Prabhupada decries the very idea of the siddha deha, since to do so would place him in conflict with all the acharyas. The main argument in all his discussions on this essential point is that the eternal identity is only revealed at the appropriate stage of purity, and this stage cannot be artificially accelerated. In an early letter, he explains to a disciple:

“In further reference to your question about the form of the spirit soul of the conditioned living entity, there is a spiritual form always, but it develops fully only when the living entity goes back to Vaikuntha. This form develops according to the desire of the living entity. Until this perfectional stage is reached, the form is lying dormant like the form of the tree is lying dormant in the seed.”

Letter to Rupanuga — Los Angeles, 8 August 1969Sometimes it is observed that, without waiting for siddha pranali at all, a neophyte practitioner, and a deceptive one at that, will simply imitate the symptoms of an ecstatic trance in order to win appreciation or favours from the public. This unscrupulous behaviour was very common in Bengal, and resulted in many becoming deceived:

**“The bhava stage is manifested by eight transcendental symptoms, namely inertness, perspiration, standing of hairs on end, failing in the voice, trembling, paleness of the body, tears in the eyes, and finally, trance. The Nectar of Devotion, a summary study of Srila Rupa Goswami’s Bhakti Rasamrita**

**Sindhu explains those symptoms and vividly describes other transcendental developments, both in steady and accelerating manifestations. Srila Vishvanatha Chakravarti Thakur has very critically discussed all these bhava displays in connection with some unscrupulous neophyte’s imitating the above symptoms for cheap appreciation. Not only Vishvanatha Chakravarti, but also Srila Rupa Goswami treated them very critically.**

**Sometimes all the above eight symptoms of ecstasy are imitated by the mundane devotees (prakrita-sahajiyas), but the pseudo symptoms are at once detected when one sees the pseudo devotee addicted to so many forbidden things. Even though decorated with the signs of a devotee, a person addicted to smoking, drinking or illegitimate sex with women cannot have all the above-mentioned ecstatic symptoms. But it is seen that sometimes these symptoms are wilfully imitated, and for this reason Srila Vishvanatha Chakravarti accuses the imitators of being stonehearted men. They are sometimes even affected by the reflection of such transcendental symptoms, yet if they still do not give up the forbidden habits, then they are hopeless cases for transcendental realization.”**

**Shrimad Bhagavatam 2.3.24**

Throughout the 1800s and the 1900s, the prakrita sahajiyas used to give siddha pranali diksha to anyone and everyone, not knowing whether they were Vaishnavas or not. These countless and indiscriminate initiations meant that many pseudo-devotees would move to Vrindavan and there, in the dress of Vaishnavas, engage in illicit activities. In conversation with his young disciples, Srila Prabhupada distilled his many decades of experience of this phenomenon into some pithy and pointed observations. Here, instead of the term siddha deha, he uses the word svarupa:

Srila Prabhupada: “First of all, anartha-nivṛtti . You are accustomed to so many bad habits. First of all, try to rectify it, then talk of svarūpa. Where is your svarūpa? Simply wasting time. A man is diseased, he's thinking, "When I shall be cured, I shall eat, go to this hotel, I shall eat like this." First of all, cure, then talk of eating this and that. Svarūpa, when you are cured, that is svarūpa. So long you are not cured, what is the use of talking svarūpa? First business is cure yourself. Anartha-nivṛtti , that is anartha-nivṛtti . Then svarūpa will come. That is the bābājīs. In Vṛndāvana, you have seen? Siddha-praṇālī .

Disciple: “Ah, siddha-praṇālī, siddha-deha?”

Srila Prabhupada: “They are smoking and having illicit sex with one dozen women. Svarūpa. Rascal. This is called sahajiyā , a rascal. Condemned. Where is your svarūpa? Don't talk unnecessarily. First of all come to svarūpa, then talk of svarūpa.”

Conversation - June 23, 1976, New Vrindavan, USA

On another occasion, Srila Prabhupada was informed that two of his disciples had taken leave of their posts to speak with other gurus in Vrindavan, India. One of them had a question to ask. He wanted to know whether siddha-praṇālī was bona fide . Srila Prabhupada’s secretary at the time, Hari Sauri Das, tells the story:

“Prabhupāda's anger flared up at the very mention of it. He demanded to know why his disciples had been associating with the "nonsense bābājīs who proclaim such things." "Their siddha-deha," he said, "is smoking beedis and keeping three or four women. And our men are becoming polluted by such things. This is very dangerous."

"One has to come to that highest stage. It is not forbidden. That may be ideal, but not for the neophytes. One who does not know ABCD, what he will know about M.A. degrees? That they do not know. They think that they have already passed M.A. degree. That is their fault."

When a devotee explained that the disciples had read that they could desire to have a relationship with Krishna, he replied:

"That's all right, he can desire. I already explained: first deserve, then desire. You are rascal, how you can desire? You have no qualification; you desire to be High Court judge. What is this nonsense?"

But should they at least try it?

"How you can try it?" Prabhupāda challenged. "First of all, be qualified, a big lawyer, then you become High Court judge. Where is that qualification? You are after illicit sex and beedi , and you want to be associated with the gopīs."

Conversation, Los Angeles, June 7th, 1976

These strong comments by the founder of the Hare Krishna movement may seem harsh to the contemporary reader, but it was because of the sharp demarcation between bhakti and pseudobhakti that the entire sampradaya had been revived. Gaura Kishor Das Babaji, himself an undisputed siddha mahatma, had once described a sahajiya wailing for Krishna to be like a woman who is not pregnant crying in labour pains. “There is nothing there, but still she is crying.” In this way, Srila Prabhupada’s pithy remarks had a long pedigree.

The other concern, and a very serious one, was that premature, reclusive contemplation (even without immoral activities) coupled with the abandonment of efforts to help others would mean the death of the sampradaya as a missionary movement. “The preaching will be finished,” Srila Prabhupada commented.

“In 1935, our guru mahārāja, Bhaktisiddhānta Sarasvatī Ṭhākura, he went to Rādhā-kuṇḍa for Kārtika-vrata. So, at that time he was reading Upaniṣads. So, first of all, these bābājīs they were coming. Bhaktisiddhānta Sarasvatī had come at Rādhā-kuṇḍa. He's giving some class. So, they used to come. But as soon as they saw that he was reading Upanishads, they stopped coming. They saw: 'They are jñānīs, they are not bhaktas…so Prabhupāda condemned them that: 'They are not living in Rādhā-kuṇḍa. They're living in Nara-kuṇḍa \[hell\].' I heard it, 'They're living in Nara-kuṇḍa.'"

## Radha Kunda

This tendency of western-born disciples to become lured away to receive their siddha deha, and of unscrupulous gurus only too eager to give it to them without any consideration of their qualifications, is a mutual deception that persists until the time of writing (2020). In recent years, devotees have arrived at the sacred lake of Radha Kunda, Vrindavan, the hub of such activity, and received their ekadasha bhava within a week. But this modern manifestation of a centuries-old phenomenon has not been without disappointment and, it must be said, some black humour.

A few years ago, three Russian devotees in Radha Kunda became god-brothers. After initiation, they shared the “secret” details of their siddha-pranali diksha and discovered that they all had the same siddha deha, the same ekadasha bhavas. Feeling dissatisfied that they were all the same person in the spiritual world, they returned to their guru to air their doubts and voice their complaint. Their guru told them, “I have given this pranali just to give you some idea of your identity. You have to do bhajan. When your realization begins to appear, we can discuss again and change it accordingly.”

In another incident, a devotee received siddha-pranali diksha from one guru in Radha Kunda. Some time later, out of curiosity, he again received siddha-pranali diksha from another guru. To his surprise, the other guru gave him a completely different identity.

Another devotee, after receiving siddha-pranali-diksha, left the path of bhakti for some years. Later she became inspired again, but she had lost her paper certificate of siddha-pranali and could not clearly remember her ekadasha-bhava. So, she went back to her guru and asked to receive siddha-pranali again. To her surprise, she received a different siddha deha this time, resulting in complete confusion and loss of faith.

So here we have cases of several devotees with the same siddha deha, cases of devotees receiving different siddha dehas from different gurus, and even a devotee receiving different siddha dehas from the same guru. This cannot be what the Six Goswamis intended for successive generations of Vaishnavas.

## The vaishnavas

**In many sacred texts it is explained that finding, befriending, and serving an advanced Vaishnava is of utmost importance on the path of bhakti. This is because the pure Vaishnava not only carries knowledge and experience, but also the grace of God. If the pure Vaishnava’s compassion is enhanced by the aspirant’s service, the result will bestow many blessings.**In teaching Sanatana Goswami, Sri Chaitanya spoke as follows:

**“The root cause of devotional service is association with advanced devotees. Even when dormant love for Krishna awakens, association with devotees is still most essential.”**

**Chaitanya Charitamrita Madhya Lila 22.83**

Such a devotee can offer a living example of devotion, along with guidance and affectionate encouragement. And yet there are many who appear to be Vaishnavas on the outside but who are not capable of offering such guardianship. So, the first question of spiritual life is - who is a real Vaishnava?The earliest record of a reply to this all-important question is given in the eleventh canto of the Shrimad Bhagavatam. The Nava Yogendras, nine great yogis, had appeared in the palace of a certain King Nimi. The king addressed one of the yogis by the name of Havir, and had just asked him two questions: “What is the nature of devotional service to the Lord?” and “What are the specific duties of the Vaishnavas?”Havir replied that there are three categories of Vaishnava and began by describing them, beginning with the highest:

**“The most advanced devotee sees within everything the soul of all souls, the Supreme Personality of Godhead, Shri Krishna. Consequently, he sees everything in relation to the Supreme Lord and understands that everything that exists is eternally situated within the Lord.”**

**An intermediate or second-class devotee, called madhyama-adhikari, offers his love to the Supreme Personality of Godhead, is a sincere friend to all the devotees of the Lord, shows mercy to ignorant people who are innocent and disregards those who are envious of the Supreme Personality of Godhead.”**

**A devotee who faithfully engages in the worship of the Deity in the temple but does not behave properly toward other devotees or people in general is called a prakrita-bhakta, a materialistic devotee, and is considered to be in the lowest position.”**

**Shrimad Bhagavatam 11.2.45-47**

Enlivened by the king’s receptivity, Havir continued to describe many symptoms of the most advanced Vaishnava, the uttama-adhikari: The description is long but one of the most important sections of the Shrimad Bhagavatam dealing with this subject:

“Even while engaging his senses in contact with their objects, one who sees this whole world as the energy of Lord Vishnu is neither repelled nor elated. He is indeed the greatest among devotees.”

“Within the material world, one’s material body is always subject to birth and decay. Similarly, the life air (prana) is harassed by hunger and thirst, the mind is always anxious, the intelligence hankers for that which cannot be obtained, and all of the senses are ultimately exhausted by constant struggle in the material nature. A person who is not bewildered by the inevitable miseries of material existence, and who remains aloof from them simply by remembering the lotus feet of the Supreme Personality of Godhead, is to be considered bhagavata-pradhana, the foremost devotee of the Lord.”

“One who has taken exclusive shelter of the Supreme Lord, VР”Рѓsudeva, becomes free from fruitive activities, which are based on material lust. In fact, one who has taken shelter of the lotus feet of the Lord is freed from even the desire to enjoy material sense gratification. Plans for enjoying sex life, social prestige and money cannot develop within his mind. Thus, he is considered bhagavat-ottama, a pure devotee of the Lord on the highest platform.”

The yogi continued: “Birth in an aristocratic family and the execution of austere and pious activities certainly cause one to take pride in himself. Similarly, if one enjoys a prestigious position within society because his parents are highly respected members of the varnashram social system, one becomes even more infatuated with himself. But if despite these excellent material qualifications, one does not feel even a tinge of pride within himself, he is to be considered the dearest servitor of the Supreme Personality of Godhead.”

“When a devotee gives up the selfish conception by which one thinks “This is my property, and that is his,” and when he is no longer concerned with the pleasures of his own material body or indifferent to the discomforts of others, he becomes fully peaceful and satisfied. He considers himself simply one among all the living beings who are equally part and parcel of the Supreme Personality of Godhead. Such a satisfied Vaishnava is at the highest standard of devotional service.”

“The lotus feet of the Supreme Personality of Godhead are sought even by the greatest of demigods, such as Brahma and Shiva, who have all accepted the Supreme Personality of Godhead as their life and soul. A pure devotee of the Lord can never forget those lotus feet in any circumstance. He will not give up his shelter at the lotus feet of the Lord for a single moment — indeed, not for half a moment — even in exchange for the benediction of ruling and enjoying the opulence of the entire universe. Such a devotee of the Lord is to be considered the best of the Vaishnavas.

“How can the fire of material suffering continue to burn the hearts of those who worship the Supreme Lord? The Lord’s lotus feet have performed innumerable heroic deeds, and the beautiful nails on His toes resemble valuable jewels. The effulgence emanating from those nails resembles cooling moonshine, for it instantly relieves the suffering within the heart of the pure devotee, just as the appearance of the moon’s cooling light relieves the burning heat of the sun.

The Supreme Personality of Godhead is so kind to the conditioned souls that if they call upon Him by speaking His holy name, even unintentionally or unwillingly, the Lord is inclined to destroy innumerable sinful reactions in their hearts. Therefore, when a devotee who has taken shelter of the Lord’s lotus feet chants the holy name of Kṛṣṇa with genuine love, the Supreme Personality of Godhead can never give up the heart of such a devotee. One who has thus captured the Supreme Lord within his heart is to be known as bhagavat-pradhana, the most exalted devotee of the Lord.”

Many thousands of years later, Lord Chaitanya instructed Sanatana Goswami for several days. During his instructions he explained the path of devotional service to Krishna and the symptoms of the different kinds of devotees of Krishna. He first explained as follows:

“A faithful devotee is a truly eligible candidate for the loving service of the Lord. According to one’s faith, one is classified as a topmost devotee, an intermediate devotee, or an inferior devotee. One who is expert in logic, argument, and the revealed scriptures and who has firm conviction and a deep faith in Krishna that is not blind, is classified as a topmost devotee. He can deliver the whole world.

One who is not very expert in argument and logic based on the revealed scriptures but who has firm faith is considered a second-class devotee. He also must be considered most fortunate. He who does not know scriptural argument very well but who has firm faith is called an intermediate or second-class devotee.

One whose faith is soft and pliable, whose faith is not very strong, who is just beginning, is called a neophyte, but by gradually following the process he will rise to the platform of a first-class devotee.”

After this, the Lord explained to Sanatana Goswami the qualities developed by the devotee who is advanced in love of Krishna. This is the well-known list of twenty-six qualities. Then he gave him a very pertinent instruction:

“The root cause of devotional service to Lord Krishna is association with advanced devotees. Even when one’s dormant love for Krishna awakens, association with devotees is still most essential.”

What happens when the devotee hears from more advanced devotees? Sri Chaitanya explained by quoting a verse from the third canto of the Shrimad Bhagavatam:

“The spiritually powerful message of Godhead can be properly discussed only in a society of devotees, and it is greatly pleasing to hear in that association. If one hears from devotees, the way of transcendental experience quickly opens, and gradually one attains firm faith that in due course develops into attraction and devotion.”

Several of Lord Chaitanya’s dear devotees lived in a village named Kulina-gram. They travelled to see him every year and he met with them and blessed them. When they asked him for advice, he told them to simply take up the company of Vaishnavas and to serve them. “A person who is always chanting the holy name of the Lord is to be considered a first-class Vaishnava, and your duty is to serve his lotus feet.” When they asked the same question the following year he explained further: “A first-class Vaishnava is he whose very presence makes others chant the holy name of Krishna.”

## The Qualities of a Vaishnava

In the Shrimad Bhagavatam 11.11.29-32 Lord Krishna himself lists the qualities of the best of his devotees:

**“The Supreme Personality of Godhead said: O Uddhava, a saintly person is merciful and never injures others. Even if others are aggressive, he is tolerant and forgiving toward all living entities. His strength and meaning in life come from the truth itself, he is free from all envy and jealousy, and his mind is equal in material happiness and distress. Thus, he dedicates his time to work for the welfare of all others. His intelligence is never bewildered by material desires, and he has controlled his senses.**

**“His behaviour is always pleasing, never harsh and always exemplary, and he is free from possessiveness. He never endeavours in ordinary, worldly activities, and he strictly controls his eating. He therefore always remains peaceful and steady.**

**“A saintly person is thoughtful and accepts Me as his only shelter. Such a person is very cautious in the execution of his duties and is never subject to superficial transformations because he is steady and noble, even in a distressing situation. He has conquered over the six material qualities — namely hunger, thirst, lamentation, illusion, old age, and death. He is free from all desire for prestige and offers honour to others.**

**“He is expert in reviving the Kṛṣṇa consciousness of others and therefore never cheats anyone. Rather, he is a well-wishing friend to all, being most merciful. Such a saintly person must be considered the most learned of men. He perfectly understands that the ordinary religious duties prescribed by Me in various Vedic scriptures possess favourable qualities that purify the performer, and he knows that neglect of such duties constitutes a discrepancy in one’s life. Having taken complete shelter at My lotus feet, however, a saintly person ultimately renounces such ordinary religious duties and worships Me alone. He is thus considered to be the best among all living entities.”**

## How to serve Vaishnavas

To associate with advanced Vaishnavas means to learn from them, and to serve them by helping them. Vaishnavas represent Krishna because they are close to Him. When it came time for Srila Rupa Goswami to give the best of all advice on this subject he wrote as follows:

*dadāti pratigṛhṇāti / guhyam ākhyāti pṛcchati*  
*bhuṅkte bhojayate caiva / ṣaḍ-vidhaṁ prīti-lakṣaṇam*

**“Offering gifts in charity, accepting charitable gifts, revealing one’s mind in confidence, inquiring confidentially, accepting prasР”Рѓda and offering prasР”Рѓda are the six symptoms of love shared by one devotee and another.”**

This verse is taken from the Upadeshamrita. It is immediately followed by a verse that explains how to have a relationship with different levels of Vaishnava. Srila Prabhupada writes in his commentary:

“In order to intelligently apply the six-fold loving reciprocations mentioned in the previous verse, one must select proper persons with careful discrimination. Shrila Rupa Goswami therefore advises that we should meet with the Vaishnavas in an appropriate way, according to their particular status. In this verse he tells us how to deal with three types of devotees – the kanishtha-adhikari, madhyama-adhikari, and uttama-adhikari. The kanishtha-adhikari is a neophyte who has received the hari-nama initiation from the spiritual master and is trying to chant the holy name of Krishna. One should respect such a person within his mind as a kanistha Vaishnava.

A madhyama-adhikari has received spiritual initiation from the spiritual master and has been fully engaged by him in the transcendental loving service of the Lord. The madhyama-adhikari should be considered to be situated midway in devotional service. The uttama-adhikari, or highest devotee, is one who is very advanced in devotional service. An uttama-adhikari is not interested in blaspheming others, his heart is completely clean, and he has attained the realized state of unalloyed Krishna consciousness. According to Srila Rupa Goswami, the association and service of such a maha-bhagavata, or perfect Vaishnava, are most desirable.”

Once having located the Vaishnavas and identified their level of advancement, Srila Rupa Goswami recommends that we serve them appropriately as follows:

“One should mentally honour the devotee who chants the holy name of Lord Krishna, one should offer humble obeisances to the devotee who has undergone spiritual initiation (diksha) and is engaged in worshiping the Deity, and one should associate with and faithfully serve that pure devotee who is advanced in undeviating devotional service and whose heart is completely devoid of the propensity to criticize others.”

Serving Vaishnavas is a very practical matter. Those who have spent their lives in Krishna’s service usually require some assistance as their bodies grow old. Cooking, cleaning, transport, secretarial support, and medicines are all ways that help may be offered. The tradition is that the senior always thinks of the spiritual welfare of the junior, and the junior always thinks of the physical welfare of the senior.

## The holy name

**The holy name of Krishna is non-different from Krishna, and the meditative, prayerful murmuring or exuberant, musical singing of the names brings the chanter directly into his presence. There are immense benefits to be had from chanting, and different levels on which to chant. It all begins with understanding the purifying effect of the sound of the name.**

In discussing the central practice of bhakti yoga, we can begin with the idea of purification. The word ‘purification’ means:

to make pure; to free from anything that debases, pollutes, adulterates, or contaminates; to free from foreign, extraneous, or objectionable elements.

In order that the originally pure soul can become free from thousands of lifetimes of being debased, polluted, adulterated, and contaminated, there is a need for purification. The material mind and body are foreign to the jiva, and there is need of a process, a series of actions, that free it from all extraneous and objectionable elements. Only after such a process of purification can the soul be once again established in its eternal position.

Shri Chaitanya Mahaprabhu, when teaching his student Sanatana Goswami, was quite categorical:

*nitya-siddha kṛṣṇa-prema ‘sādhya’ kabhu naya*  
*śravaṇādi-śuddha-citte karaye udaya*

**Pure love for Kṛṣṇa is eternally established in the hearts of the living entities. It is not something to be gained from another source. When the heart is purified by hearing and chanting, this love naturally awakens.**

**Chaitanya Charitamrita Madhya 22.107**

It is not that the soul is merely ‘spiritual’ and, once having been liberated from conditioned existence and reinstated to its eternal nature, will simply rest in some ‘nirvana’ state without purpose or activity. The soul is dynamic, and service is how that dynamism is expressed. The connection with Krishna, and the loving attraction for Krishna, is intrinsic to the soul’s very existence. Once purified this is restored:

*jivera svarupa haya – krsnera nitya dasa*  
*krsnera tatastha sakti, bhedabheda-prakasa*

**The living entity’s constitutional position is to be an eternal servant of Krishna because he is the energy of Krishna, like a molecular particle of sunshine or fire.**

**Chaitanya Charitamrita Madhya 20.109**

Although karma, jnana and yoga can elevate or help the soul to different degrees, only bhakti can restore the soul to its original position as a lover of God. And of all the processes of bhakti, chanting of the holy names is the most efficient process. The reason for this is that the jiva is eternally related to Krishna, and the names of Krishna are non-different from Krishna, and directly place the soul in the company of Krishna.

*nama chintamani krishnas chaitanya-rasa-vigrahah*  
*purnah shuddho nitya-mukto'bhinnatvan nama-naminoh*

**The name of Krishna is transcendentally blissful. It is Krishna himself, the reservoir of all pleasure. Krishna’s name is complete, and it is the form of all transcendental mellows. It is no less powerful than Krishna himself. Krishna’s name is always liberated and spiritual; this is because the name of Krishna and Krishna himself are identical.**

**Padma Purana**

In his Harinam Chintamani, Bhaktivinode Thakur declares that there is nothing to compare to the purifying process of chanting the holy names of Krishna:

"One must take to the name, for in this material world there is nothing comparable to it. It is the ultimate treasure in Krishna's storehouse, since it contains within it the whole spiritual realm. The particles of spirit who are parts and parcels of Krishna, have in illusion come into this world of dull matter, and the holy name, containing all aspects of Krishna and the full power of Krishna, has come into this world just to deliver those spirit souls. One should know that everything in the world other than the jiva and the holy name, are without substance.”

There are four ages of the universe, eons of time that rotate like the four seasons. They are almost incomprehensibly long, and the total length of the four ages is 4.32 million years. In each of the yugas there are opportunities for God realisation, according to the capabilities of the humans who live at that time. As the cycle of ages progress there is a commensurate weakness in aptitude for the disciplines of spiritual activity. The last of the ages, Kali yuga, is marked by diminished life span, distractions, lack of determination, and regular disturbances due to quarrels and the actions of hypocritical leaders.

Nevertheless, the Age of Kali provides all human beings with a precious opportunity. Whatever results that could be gained in other yugas can still be gained by the comparatively easy act of recitation of the names of Krishna. In the Shrimad Bhagavatam (12.3.51) it is said:

*kaler doṣa-nidhe rājann / asti hy eko mahān guṇaḥ*  
*kīrtanād eva kṛṣṇasya / mukta-saṅgaḥ paraṁ vrajet*

**My dear King, although Kali-yuga is an ocean of faults, there is still one good quality about this age: Simply by chanting the name of Krishna, one can become free from material bondage and be promoted to the transcendental kingdom.**

*kṛte yad dhyāyato viṣṇuṁ / tretāyāṁ yajato makhaiḥ*  
*dvāpare paricaryāyāṁ / kalau tad dhari-kīrtanāt*

**Whatever result was obtained in Satya-yuga by meditating on Vishnu, in Treta-yuga by performing sacrifices, and in Dvapara-yuga by serving the Lord’s lotus feet can be obtained in Kali-yuga simply by chanting Hari kirtan.”**

**Shrimad Bhagavatam 12.3.52**

Within the ancient Yajur Veda, the book of the Vedas providing details on the execution of elevating rituals and sacrifices, there is one philosophical portion known as the Kali-Santaran Upanishad. In a recorded conversation between Brahma and his son Narada, a question is raised as to what is the specific mantra which will be of most benefit during the harsh winter age of the universe, the Kali yuga. The answer is clear:

*hare krishna hare krishna krishna krishna hare hare*  
*hare rama hare rama rama rama hare hare*  
*iti sodasakam namnam kali-kalmasa-nasanam*  
*natah parataropayah sarva-vedesu drsyate*

**hare krishna hare krishna krishna krishna hare hare, hare rama hare rama rama rama hare hare - these sixteen names destroy all the inauspiciousness of the Age of Kali. This is the conclusion of all the Vedas.**

Within the Vedas, and certainly within their corollaries such as the Pancharatra texts, many rigorous directions are given as to how to chant sacred mantras. Ideally the reciter should sip purified water before chanting a mantra and must face east or north. His mouth must be clean, and he must first practice pranayama or breathing techniques. He cannot walk while chanting or be seated in a moving vehicle such as a boat, bullock cart or horse.

For the chanting of the name of Hari there are no similar restrictions or prohibitions. Indeed, one who chants the name of Krishna is awarded good results even if the names are chanted with an unclean mouth, recited neglectfully, while walking, or pronounced incorrectly, still there will be benefit. Even if the name of God is said to indicate something else, or if the syllables are inserted in another word, still the chanter will make spiritual advancement.

## Primary and Secondary Names

The holy names of God are known as Hari Nama or Harinam. In the form of his own names, Shri Hari, or Bhagavan Krishna, descends to this mundane sphere to remove ignorance. Because of this there is no better friend and well-wisher. God has millions of names, according to his activities. Nevertheless, basing his words on the explanation of Rupa Goswami, Bhaktivinode Thakur explains the distinction between the names which relate to God’s function as the Creator, and those that indicate God as the supreme enjoyer of rasa:

“The Supreme Personality of Godhead has two kinds of names:

1\. Mukhya (primary) and 2. Gauna (secondary). Names that show His relation to the material creation and the material modes of nature are all secondary names. ‘Shrishti-karta’ (the creator of the material world), ‘Jagat-pati’ (the maintainer of the material world), ‘Vishva-niyanta’ (the controller of the material world), ‘Vishva-palaka’ (the protector of the material world), and ‘Param-atma’ are examples of some of these many secondary names.

When they are chanted, these secondary names bring many kinds of benefits, but they do not directly or quickly bring the highest spiritual benefit. The names that describe the Lord as He is manifested in the spiritual world, which is beyond material time and space, and where time is manifested as an eternal present (nitya-vartamana) are names that are all spiritual and primary. ‘Narayana,’ ‘Vasudeva,’ ‘Janardana,’ ‘Hrishikesha,’ ‘Hari,’ ‘Achyuta,’ ‘Govinda,’ ‘Gopala,’ and ‘Rama’ are examples of these primary names. All these primary names are identical with the form of the Supreme Lord Himself, and therefore they exist in the eternal present in the spiritual world.”

The fortunate souls attract the attention of mukhya nama by their bhakti, and Harinam dances on their tongues.

*harer nama harer nama / Harer namaiva kevalam*  
*kalau nasty eva nasty eva / nasty eva gatir anyatha*

**The holy name, the holy name, the holy name of Lord Hari. In this age of quarrel and hypocrisy, the only means of deliverance is the chanting of the holy names of the Lord. There is no other way. There is no other way. There is no other way.**

**Brihan Naradiya Purana**

The two elements of successful bhakti yoga, as was explained in the Padma Purana, is to always remember Krishna and to never forget him. Chanting the holy name of Krishna is the guaranteed way to do this. The relationship between smaranam and kirtanam is explained in the Vaishnava Chintamani where it is said:

*agha-cchit smaranam vishnor / bahv-ayasena sadhyate*  
*oshtha-spandana-matrena / kirtanam tu tato varam*

**By working very hard to always remember Lord Vishnu, one can destroy all sins. However, one can attain an even greater benefit simply by moving the lips to chant the Lord's holy name."**

The immediate question which springs to the mind of many as they are introduced to the claims of bhakti yoga is: ‘How can a name, which is after all a series of syllables, be different from other sounds in the world?

The answer is that the name of Krishna does not take its birth in the material world. The soul is a tiny particle of spirit, and only when it is re-established in its eternal position is it eligible to pronounce the pure name of Lord Hari. The material senses of a soul imprisoned in Maya's world have no power to pronounce it. However, by the mercy of the Lord's hladini-shakti, a soul can be situated in his original form. Then the holy name will appear before him. The holy name enters the devotee's thinking process, and then descends to the tongue. On that tongue the holy name dances. The holy name is not a series of material syllables.

*ataḥ śrī-kṛṣṇa-nāmādi na bhaved grāhyam indriyaiḥ*  
*sevonmukhe hi jihvādau svayam eva sphuraty adaḥ*

**Therefore, material senses cannot appreciate Krishna’s holy name, form, qualities, and pastimes. When a conditioned soul is awakened to Krishna consciousness and renders service by using his tongue to chant the Lord's holy name and taste the remnants of the Lord's food, the tongue is purified, and the soul gradually comes to understand who Krishna really is.**

**Bhakti-rasāmṛta-sindhu 1.2.234.**

Out of all the millions of names of the Lord that may be chanted the Brahmanda Purana states that: ‘The pious results derived from chanting the thousand holy names of Vishnu three times can be attained by one repetition of the holy name of Krishna.’ It may be suggested that the effects of the chanting of Harinam have been slightly exaggerated, perhaps out of some sectarian sentiment, for how could simply chanting purify the soul from thousands of lifetimes of bad karma? The answer is provided by the decidedly non-sectarian Garuda Purana:

*avashenapi yan-namni / kirtite sarva-patakaih*  
*puman vimucyate sadyah / simha-trastair mrigair iva*

**If one chants the holy name of the Lord, even in a helpless condition or without desiring to do so, all the reactions of his sinful life depart, just as when a lion roars, all the small animals flee in fear.**

One of the side-effects of this great purification is that the person suffering, as the ancient text puts it, ‘in a blazing forest fire of sins,’ becomes relieved of suffering. This happens not in some end-of-life heavenly state, but during the lifetime of the serious chanter. Almost immediately the person who takes the holy name starts to feel relief from suffering and a lightness of heart leading to a sense of happiness. A person who chants the holy name of Lord Hari also purifies his family, friends, and all others. In the Brahmanda Purana it is said:

‘Even if he has committed many great sins, a person who chants the holy name of the Lord day and night become pure at heart. He purifies all his relatives.’

Another common question that is raised is that: “If we chant incessantly, how can we do the other limbs of bhakti?” The answer is that this proposal does not present a contradiction, for the sixty-four divisions of bhakti are included within the main nine limbs, and that the nine limbs of bhakti can be executed through Harinam. Furthermore, of those nine limbs, chanting is the most potent.

## Three levels of chanting

There are three levels of chanting of the holy name according to the faith and purity of the practitioner. At first, the aspirant will inevitably chant what is known as nama-aparadha, which is chanting the holy name while committing some sort of offence. For instance, although even negligent or inattentive chanting of the holy name will render some benefit, it is nonetheless offensive to Krishna, who is non-different than his name, to call him by chanting and then not pay him full attention.

Next is the level of nama-bhasa, which is ‘a semblance of the name.’ This is compared to the sun rising in the morning. The full sun cannot be seen, but still there is some light. Finally, there is shuddha-nama or the pure chanting of the name. Bhaktivinode Thakur explains: “Pure chanting, suddha-krishna-nama, may be attained only when sraddha fully matures into ananya-bhakti, single-minded devotion. Any chanting at a lower level is either nama-bhasa – the semblance of nama, or nama-aparadha – offensive chanting.”

Then he makes several related points: “When nama is chanted at the level of sadhana-bhakti it is sadhana, but when at the stage of bhava-bhakti it is sadhya, the goal.”

“The three types of adhikaris (levels of devotees) are categorised by how much they chant or inspire chanting: The first level of Vaishnava chants with sincere faith, if not incessantly; the second level chants incessantly, and the topmost level of Vaishnava is he that inspires others to chant by his presence.”

“Some may ask if Krishna in his original form, swarupa, and his name are ‘non-different’ from each other, are they actually the same, or are there differences? I say that there are no differences, but nama is more merciful than swarupa.”

“The purpose of counting the names we chant is to know whether or not the number of names chanted is gradually increasing. Tulasi is very dear to Lord Hari, therefore touching tulasi beads as one chants the holy name brings a better result. When one chants, one should be conscious that the name of Lord Krishna is not different from Lord Krishna Himself, so one must carefully avoid the offenses to the name.”

## The immense benefits of chanting

*ceto-darpaṇa-mārjanaḿ bhava-mahā-dāvāgni-nirvāpaṇaḿ*  
*śreyaḥ-kairava-candrikā-vitaraṇaḿ vidyā-vadhū-jīvanam*  
*ānandāmbudhi-vardhanaḿ prati-padaḿ pūrṇāmṛtāsvādanaḿ*  
*sarvātma-snapanaḿ paraḿ vijayate śrī-kṛṣṇa-sańkīrtanam*

**“Chanting the holy name of Krishna cleanses the mirror of the heart of all the dust accumulated for years and extinguishes the great forest fire of conditional life, of repeated birth and death. At that time real auspiciousness begins for the soul. Just as the evening lotus blooms in the moon’s cooling rays, the heart begins to blossom in the nectar of the name, and at last the soul awakens in full knowledge of its real inner treasure – a life of love in the highest mellows of devotion. Repeatedly tasting nectar, the soul dives and surfaces in the ever-increasing ocean of joy. In this way, all phases of the self are fully satisfied and purified by bathing in the nectarean mellows of the name. Therefore, let the sankirtan of the holy name be victorious.”**

**Sikshastaka verse 1**

In this verse, the first of eight verses personally handwritten by Shri Chaitanya Mahaprabhu, there are seven benedictions offered by the chanting of the holy name. Sometimes they are known as the seven flames of the sankirtan fire yajna:

1\. Ceto darpanam marjanam – it cleanses the mirror of the heart of all the dust accumulated for years

2\. Bhava-maha-davagni-nirvapanam – it extinguishes the great forest fire of repeated birth and death.

3\. Sreyah kairava candrika vitaranam – the lotus of the soul begins to bloom.

4\. Vidya vadhu jivanam – true knowledge develops.

5\. Anandam buddhi vardhanam – the soul is plunged into an ocean of bliss.

6\. Sarvatma snapanam – all aspects of the person become purified.

7\. Param vijayate shri krishna sankirtanam – the entire world becomes blessed.

The teachings of Chaitanya Mahaprabhu are completely taken from the Shrimad Bhagavatam. From the meditation of Srila Vyasa and his vision of Bhagavan, Paramatma and Brahman, all through the relationships between the bhaktas and Bhagavan, through to the one method of God realisation for Kali yuga. Shrimad Bhagavatam is consistent in this, from the beginning through to the final verse. In the very first chapter we find: “Living beings who are entangled in the complicated meshes of birth and death can be freed immediately by even unconsciously chanting the holy name of Krishna, which is feared by fear personified.” (1.1.14) and at the very end of the book, thousands of verses later, this verse:

*nama-sankirtanam yasya / sarva-papa pranasanam*  
*pranamo duhkha-samanas / tam namami harim param*

**I offer my respectful obeisances unto the Supreme Lord, Hari, the congregational chanting of whose holy names destroys all sinful reactions, and the offering of obeisances unto whom relieves all material suffering.**

**Shrimad Bhagavatam 12.13.23**

Shrimad Bhagavatam ends, concludes, culminates with this glorification of the sankirtan movement and in the Gaudiya Vaishnava sampradaya it is understood that Chaitanya Charitamrita begins where Shrimad Bhagavatam ends. Srila Prabhupada’s godbrother B.R. Sridhara Swami comments:

“The concluding verse of the Shrimad Bhagavatam says, ‘Krishna’s Holy Name can relieve us from all undesirable sinfulness, all filthy characteristics, and all miseries. Let us all bow down to Him.’ Uttering this verse, the Shrimad-Bhagavatam stops; that great treatise becomes silent. The last word in the Bhagavatam is nama-sankirtan. The Bhagavatam has given such great importance to chanting the Holy Name of Krishna, and Shri Chaitanya Mahaprabhu developed it from there.”

Shri Chaitanya Mahaprabhu has given his personal advice on how to chant the holy names of Krishna, and how to maintain such chanting. The names must be chanted respectfully, with great attention and with respect, not only for Krishna himself, but for all other living beings:

*trinad api sunichena / taror iva sahishnuna*  
*amanina manadena / kirtaniyah sada harih*

**One who is humbler than a blade of grass, more forbearing than a tree, who gives due honour to others without desiring honour for himself, is qualified to always chant the Holy Name of Krishna.**

**Sikshastaka verse 3**

## Incorrect chanting

**Since the holy name is Sri Krishna himself, the aspirant’s relationship to the holy name is directly his relationship to God. Therefore, the aspirant’s attraction to chanting, or his reluctance to chant, is directly proportionate to his affinity to the person of Krishna.**

For example, the name of Krishna may be chanted with attention and absorption, or it may be chanted inattentively and without faith in its divine and personal nature. The former is correct, the latter incorrect.

Furthermore, since one’s relationship with both Krishna and his name is mediated through those who represent him, the Vaishnavas, relationships with such devotees can also be correct or incorrect. Any disrespect to a compassionate and learned Vaishnava, from verbal insult to physical assault, is debilitating to spiritual progress and deemed the greatest of all offences against Krishna. Offences, known as aparadhas, or that which is ‘against worship,’ are almost inevitable in the early stages of bhakti-yoga, and there are ten of them listed in the Brahma Kanda portion of the Padma Purana, Chapter 25, verses 5-18.

In this section they are given along with comments from Bhaktivinode Thakur. Although chanting offensively will never result in love of God, the only solution to offensive chanting is renewed and observant practice – and more chanting. Therefore, it is said that chanting is the only way to become free from aparadha:

*namaparadha-yuktanam / namany eva haranty agham*  
*avishranti prayuktani / tany evartha-karani ca*

**The chanting of the holy name is recommended for persons who commit offenses, because if they continue chanting, they will gradually chant without offence. Even if in the beginning one chants with offences, one will become free from such offenses by chanting again and again.**

**Padma Purana, Svarga Kanda 48.49**

## Three Stages of chanting

There are three stages of chanting, the offensive stage, the ‘clearing stage’ and the ‘pure stage,’ or nama-aparadha, nama-abhasa, and shuddha-nama, respectively. Working hard on diligent and absorbed chanting, and carefully avoiding the offences, the aspirant comes gradually to the ‘clearing stage’ and ultimately to the ‘pure stage’ or shuddha-nama. The symptom of shuddha-nama is chanting without any offences at all. With offences, shuddha-nama does not appear. Bhaktivinode Thakur writes: “The chanting may be spoiled in two ways, small or great – samanya or brihat. The first is nama-abhasa and the second nama-aparadha.”

Abhasa means ‘semblance, shadow, reflection.’ The radiance from a lustrous object creates two effects: Chaya – shadow, and Pratibimba – reflection. It is just like this with the holy name of Krishna, there is a shadow and a reflection. This is nama-abhasa.When chanting is contaminated by pramada, ignorance, and bhrama, illusion, or by lack of attention or comprehension that is nama-abhasa. When chanting is contaminated by mayavada, the desire for impersonal liberation, moksha, or sensual enjoyment, bhoga, this is nama-aparadha.

**"One who chants the holy name of the Lord is immediately freed from the reactions of unlimited sins, even if he chants sanketa indirectly, to indicate something else, parihasa jokingly, stobha antagonistically, for musical entertainment, or even hela, disrespectfully or neglectfully. This is accepted by all the learned scholars of the scriptures."**

**Shrimad Bhagavatam 6.2.14**

## The Ten Offences

*satam ninda namnah paramam aparadham vitanute*  
*yatah khyatim yatam katham u sahate tad vigariham*

**First offence: ‘Blasphemy of the great saintly persons who are engaged in preaching the names is the worst offence. The nama-prabhu will never tolerate such blasphemous activities, even from one who passes as a great devotee.’**

Comments: “The name of Krishna is Krishna, and he cannot tolerate any criticism of those who have surrendered to him, especially those who are glorifying his name.”

Offences against the devotees include:

• Confiscating or appropriating the property of a devotee.

• Wrongfully accusing a devotee of a crime.

• Abusing a devotee.

• Assaulting a devotee.

• Neglecting or showing disrespect to another devotee.

• Inquiring into the past of a devotee and finding some fault.

• Regarding a devotee as higher or lower status because of birth or occupation.

The offences against the devotees are worse than the offences against the Lord Himself.

*shivasya shri-vishnor ya iha guna-namadi sakalam*  
*dhiya bhinnam pashyet sa khalu hari-namahita-karah*

**Second offence: ‘The holy name of Vishnu is all-auspicious. Vishnu's name, form, qualities, and pastimes are all transcendental, absolute knowledge. Therefore, if one tries to separate him from his holy name or his transcendental form, qualities, and pastimes, thinking them to be material, that is offensive. Similarly, to think the names of demigods such as Shiva to be as good as the name of Vishnu is also blasphemous.**

Comments: “Thinking that the names of the gods are independent from, or equal to, the name of the lord is polytheism. He is ishvara and they get their powers from him. The second explanation is to think that the nama is different from the original form of Krishna, or Krishna swarupa.”

*guror avajna shruti-shastra-nindanam / tathartha-vado hari-namni kalpanam*  
*namno balad yasya hi papa-buddhir / na vidyate tasya yamair hi shuddhih*

**Third offence: ‘To consider the spiritual master to be material, and therefore to envy his exalted position.’**

Comments: “To imagine the guru to only understand about the nama and not Vedanta is an offence.”

**Fourth offence: ‘Blasphemy of Vedic literature, such as the four Vedas and the Puranas.’**

Comments: “The shrutis (Vedas) are the origins of the knowledge of the nama. Thinking that what the shrutis and Puranas say about nama is exaggeration will send one to hell.”

**Fifth offence: ‘To give some interpretation on the holy name of the Lord.’**

Sixth offence: ‘To consider the glories of the holy name to be imaginary.’

Comments: “To consider that the name is an imaginary and fictitious proposition. An offender will think that the lord is impersonal, and that the appearance of Krishna is only temporary, to accomplish a certain purpose, therefore the name is only something given to a temporary manifestation.”

**Seventh offence: ‘To think that since the name can counteract all sinful reactions one may therefore go on with his sinful activities and then at the same time chant to neutralize them is the greatest offense at the lotus feet of Hari-nama. One who thinks in this way cannot be purified by any means, such as by austerities or by the various punishments of Yamaraja.’**

Comments: “To commit sins and then rely on the name to absolve one of the future sinful reactions. Even nama-abhasa will absolve sins, it is so powerful, but if the devotee continues sinning out of premeditation or nonchalance, exploiting the sin-eradicating potential of the nama, then his sins escalate to aparadha.”

*dharma-vrata-tyaga-hutadi-sarva / shubha-kriya-samyam api pramadah*  
*ashraddadhane vimukhe 'py ashrinvati / yas copaddeshah shiva-namaparadhah*

**Eighth offence: ‘It is a great offence to consider the chanting of the Hare Krishna mantra to be equal to the performance of religious ceremonies, following austere vows, practicing renunciation, and fire sacrifices, which are all materialistic auspicious activities.’**

Comments: “Pious activities or sat-karma are only an upaya, a means to an end. Such altruistic actions must never be compared to the perfection of chanting which yields the ultimate.”

**Ninth offence: ‘It is an offense to preach the glories of the holy name to those who will not hear, to those who are atheistic and those who have no faith in the chanting of the holy name.**

Comments: “The confidential knowledge of nama must not be disclosed to those who do not have firm faith. Only they are eligible candidates. They should not be initiated for name and gain. But that chanting nama is the most beneficial spiritual practice and that they will achieve complete benediction is good to teach.”

*shrutvapi nama-mahatmyam / yah priti-rahito 'dhamah*  
*aham-mamadi-paramo / namni so 'py aparadha-krit*

**Tenth offence: ‘That lowest among men, who, even after hearing the glories of the transcendental holy name of the Lord, continues in a materialistic concept of life, thinking, \`I am this body, and everything belonging to this body is mine (aham mameti), and does not show respect and love for the chanting of the Hare Krishna maha-mantra is an offender to the holy name.’**

Comments: “One who receives knowledge from a pure devotee but who then turns back to the world of attachment is an aparadhi, an offender.”

## Love for Krishna is the final goal

**The original form and nature of Godhead is Krishna, the supreme person. Bhakti is devotional service to Krishna, a dynamic love that develops to perfection and continues unlimitedly and eternally. Since love has many levels, even in the state of perfection, those levels are discussed in this chapter.**

Krishna appeared within this world and displayed his pastimes, or lilas, for one hundred and twenty-five years. During that time there were countless persons who loved him. Their love was expressed in an infinity of ways, and Krishna reciprocated with them by returning their love. Krishna was attractive to all, and everyone could have a relationship of their choice with him. Krishna’s appearance, clothing and ornamentation, his food preferences, his chivalry and martial skill, teachings, conversation, and jokes, were all recorded. That is why, thousands of years later, we know what he liked and did not like, and we can still love him.

Yet if Krishna were only an historical character, discussing his preferences of so many centuries ago would be interesting only for the artist or scholar. They would be intriguing pieces of information, no doubt, but of no more relevance than discovering the personal tastes of any other important historical figure. Krishna is a person, but different from every other person. The fact that he appeared within human history does not relegate him to the past nor demean him to a mere historic figure. He enters human history to transform it, not to be bound by it. Those who imagine Krishna to have only appeared on earth once, or who consider him to be a mere philosopher or king or think his body to be made of flesh and blood, do not understand him.

Krishna is a person, but not limited by time, space, or the frailty and impermanence we have come to understand by the word ‘person.’ Krishna is the beginning of all, the sustainer of all, and the end of all, and yet is perfectly capable of accepting even a child’s offering of a solitary daisy. He is everything that exists, and yet completely a person. Those who engage in bhakti, loving devotional service, find that the reciprocation from Krishna comes to them in waves of gratitude, experienced as tangible pleasure. This pleasure takes them through all the range of human emotion, with profound physical symptoms, all the way to joyful tears of love and ecstatic trance.

Krishna is the focus of an infinite number of liberated souls in the spiritual world, and a multitude in the material world who are trying to reawaken their love for him. Since he reciprocates with them all in an infinite variety of ways, the loving feelings between the supreme and his souls create numerous categories of loving feelings known as ‘tastes’ or rasas. These categories, and how to experience them, are the subject of Srila Rupa Goswami’s Bhakti Rasamrita Sindhu. Since both Bhaktivinode Thakur and Srila Prabhupada follow the general layout of Rupa Goswami’s masterpiece in their Jaiva Dharma and The Nectar of Devotion respectively, it would be worth analysing it in brief before we proceed.

The Bhakti Rasamrita Sindhu consists of 2,127 Sanskrit verses, divided into four sections. Since the title translates as ‘the nectar-ocean of devotion,’ the four sections in the book are listed as four ‘oceans’ - the eastern, southern, western, and northern. Inside each of these sections are ‘waves’ which deal with the different topics within each section.

As its title indicates, the book is about bhakti-rasa. The term rasa literally denotes ‘juice.’ Sanskritic thought considers juice or liquid to be the element responsible for flavour and, therefore, enjoyment. Thus, the term rasa connotes the pleasure relished in relating with various persons and objects. Bhakti-rasa is the pleasure relished from bhakti – loving exchange with Godhead.

Rupa Goswami borrowed much of his terminology from existing books on aesthetics. One thousand years before him the great writer on theatrics, Bharata Muni, wrote his Natya Shastra or ‘A Treatise on Drama.’ His text analyses all aspects of drama, from plot development, costuming, and dance, but no element is more important in his dramaturgy than rasa. “Without rasa,” Bharat declares, “no significance arises.” According to Bharat, emotions are central to drama, and the performance of the dramas narrative through acting, words, costuming, music and dance leads to a single goal: to properly depict and develop the emotions of the plays protagonists. Bharat took the term rasa and identified various emotions, or bhavas, that serve to stimulate and enhance the central feeling. Later writers such as Bhoja and Vishvanath also used these terms, so that by the time of Rupa Goswami they were in common usage.

His book was not, therefore, written in a highly technical language of his own invention, or in religious terms known only to fellow practitioners, but in words that would be immediately understood by the courtly poets and playwrights of his day. Rupa Goswami was familiar with these circles due to his former membership of the political and social elites, and he wanted his book to be read by them. But chiefly it was a work which invited the readers to take their rightful place as actors in the supreme drama of the spiritual world. Each reader could experience the highest possible rasa by taking their role in the very real play being performed eternally in Goloka Vrindavan.

In the first division, the Eastern Ocean, Rupa Goswami begins with a definition of bhakti, and specifically what he describes as bhakti of the highest order, uttama-bhakti, and describes the six hallmarks of such bhakti. He states that there are three classes of uttama-bhakti: (1) uttama-bhakti practiced by the physical senses (‘sadhana-bhakti’), (2) uttama-bhakti realized internally (‘bhava-bhakti’), and (3) perfected uttama-bhakti (‘prema-bhakti’). He gives a general overview of the practices, techniques, and characteristics of bhakti, and then the eligibility required for it.

In the second division, the Southern Ocean, he describes what happens within the state of prema-bhakti, as the individual soul and the Supreme Soul relish the exchange of love. He terms this ‘rasa,’ of which there are five components:

1\. the ecstatic stimulants of love (vibhava).

2\. the expressions of love, the subsequent ecstasies (anubhava).

3\. the involuntary expressions of love (sattvika-bhava).

4\. the contrary expressions of love, or ‘transitory ecstasies’ (vyabhichari-bhava).

5\. the basic loving mood, or ‘constitutional ecstasy’ (sthayi-bhava).

Rupa Goswami identifies twelve categories of loving mood, five of which are ‘direct’ (mukhya) and seven of which are ‘associated’ (gauna). Each mood has specific stimuli, expressions, involuntary expressions, and contrary expressions suitable to intensify and transform it into a relishable loving experience (rasa).

The third division, the Western Ocean, is comprised of five waves. Each wave takes one of the five ‘direct’ loving moods and defines and illustrates the specific stimuli, expressions, involuntary expressions, and contrary expressions appropriate to transform that basic mood into rasa: (1) relish of purity, or ‘neutral affection’ – shanta-rasa, (2) relish of servitude – dasya-rasa, (3) relish of friendship – sakhya-rasa, (4) relish of parenthood – vatsalya-rasa, and (5) relish of romance, or consorthood – madhurya-rasa.

The fourth and final division, the Northern Ocean, similarly defines and illustrates the specific stimuli, expressions, involuntary expressions, and contrary expressions appropriate to transform an ‘associated’ loving mood into rasa. Thus, its first seven waves concern:

1\. relish of humour, or ‘laughing ecstasy’ – hasya-rasa.

2\. relish of amazement, or astonishment – adbhuta-rasa,

3\. relish of heroism or ‘chivalry’ – vira-rasa,

4\. relish of compassion – karuna-rasa,

5\. relish of anger – raudra-rasa,

6\. relish of fear, worry or dread – bhayananka-rasa,

7\. relish of disgust or ‘ghastliness’ – vibhatsa-rasa.

Rupa Goswami calls these seven moods ‘associated’ loving moods (gauna-bhakti-rasa) because they are always enjoyed in association with one of the five ‘direct’ loving moods. For example, humour is relished primarily in association with friendship. The eighth wave of this ‘ocean’ describes which ‘associated’ and ‘direct’ moods are compatible, and which are incompatible. For example, disgust is very seldom compatible with romantic love. Incompatible association of moods produces a distasteful, less relishable state Rupa Goswami calls virasa.

Finally, the ninth and final wave describes a similar situation: If the stimuli, expressions, involuntary expressions, or contrary expressions appropriate for one loving mood appear in connection with a different loving mood, that mood may not fully develop into a relishable, loving experience. Rupa Goswami gives the term rasa-abhasa for this semi-developed experience.

In this way, providing colourful examples of different kinds of devotees in situations with Krishna or his associates, Rupa Goswami analyses all twelve rasas (5+7) by identifying the components that make them up, such as the four bhavas and their subdivisions.

The subject is broad and the categories and sub-categories numerous, and the reader will consequently benefit from slowly reading, considering, and digesting the subject matter. Only the most important and essential features have been included in this chapter. In his Jaiva Dharma, Bhaktivinode Thakur, echoing Rupa Goswami’s detailed descriptions, begins by reminding his readers of salient principles previously discussed.

## The Five Rasas

Pure devotional service is causeless and cannot be obstructed. It is like a river. However, the desire for earthly enjoyment and its opposite, liberation from earthly enjoyment - karma and jnana - are said to be like two witches in the heart. For as long as they are present, there is no possibility of enjoying the bliss of bhakti.

There are five direct relationships with the Lord and seven indirect relationships. These are established by the devotee’s attachment to Krishna. The five are permanently situated in the heart of the devotee, and the seven emotions appear suddenly under certain conditions. Examples of devotees’ relationships are as follows:

Shanta – (peaceful or neutral) the Nine Yogendras and the Four Kumaras.

Dasya – (servitude) innumerable examples such as Garuda and Hanuman.

Sakhya – (friendship) Sridama and Sudama, Bhima and Arjuna.

Vatsalya – (parental) Vasudeva and Devaki.

Madhurya – (consort) Laxmi, the Queens in Dwaraka, the gopis in Vrindavan.

When devotees are in these rasas, they forget that Krishna is God.

A devotee in shanta rasa desires neither heaven nor liberation, he manifests the two transcendental qualities of detachment from all material desire and attachment to Krishna. These two qualities spread through the lives of all devotees in whatever rasa. However, in shanta rasa, though one’s constitutional position is realised, not even the smallest intimacy exists, rather knowledge of Brahman and Paramatma is prominent.

In dasya rasa he better understands the full opulence of the Supreme Person. Knowledge of God is revealed with awe and veneration. By rendering service, the devotee in dasya gives constant happiness to the Lord. Therefore, dasya rasa is shanta rasa plus service.

In sakhya rasa the qualities of dasya rasa are mixed with the confidence of fraternity instead of awe and veneration – an intimacy devoid of the formality found in dasya rasa. Krishna sometimes serves the devotee in this rasa. In sakhya rasa Krishna is obliged to the devotees who think themselves equal to him. This rasa has three qualities: detachment, attachment, and service characterised by the confidence of fraternity.

In vatsalya rasa the qualities of the former three rasas are transformed into a form of service called palana, or maintenance, but due a greater sense of intimacy the devotee chastises and rebukes the Lord in an ordinary way. The devotee considers himself the Lord’s maintainer.

On the platform of madhurya rasa or conjugal love, attachment for Krishna, service to him, and the relaxed feelings of fraternity and the feelings of maintenance all increase in intimacy. The devotee offers his body in the service of the Lord.

Just as in the evolution of prakriti, the material energy, all the material qualities evolve one after another beginning from ether, with all five qualities found in earth, so all five feelings of the devotees are amalgamated in conjugal love.

## Ingredients of Rasa

The gradual development of love is compared to states of sugar: sugarcane seed, sugarcane, cane juice, molasses, brown sugar loaf (gaur), crystals and lozenges. It is the same substance but progressively refined and by stages purer and sweeter. Similarly, the successively sweeter stages of love, when regarded as a continuum, are collectively known as sthayi-bhava, (‘steady ecstasy’), or continuous love of Godhead. When that ecstatic love increases, it becomes krishna-bhakti-rasa (the mellow of devotional service to Lord Krishna).

Ingredients of a mixture are known as samagris. Just as there might be five ingredients to bake a cake, so there are five kinds of ingredients, or samagris, of rasa, each of which has different symptoms. They are:

1\. Vibhava (special symptoms or causes of ecstasy).

2\. Anubhava (subsequent ecstasy).

3\. Sattvika-bhava (constitutional or existential ecstasy).

4\. Vyabhichari-bhava (aggressive ecstasy).

5\. Sthayi-bhava (continuous ecstasy).

Now these will be explained in order:

## Vibhava – ecstatic stimulants

Vibhava is that which awakens and stimulates our eternal affection to Krishna, heightening it to the point of relishable intensity. An example may help to clarify the concept of vibhava. A mother has constant affection for her child, but sometimes her affection remains in the background of her thoughts. If she sees the child’s shoe it is a stimulus to her love, and she experiences a heightening of motherly affection. Srila Prabhupada translates vibhava as ‘causes for ecstasy,’ so seeing the shoe is the stimulant or the vibhava. In the case of love for Krishna, these stimulants are of two kinds:

1\. Alambana – foundational ingredients for rasa to take place, features that directly cause love.

2\. Uddipana – the impetus for rasa to be experienced, features that enhance the love or emotion.

Then, Alambana is itself of two kinds:

1\. Vishaya (the object of love).

2\. Ashraya (the possessor or giver of love).

The object of love is the vishaya-alambana, and the person who possesses the love is the ashraya-alambana. Thus, the person who is the object of attachment or love is the rati-vishaya, and the person who is attached or who loves is the rati-ashraya. Thus, a devotee who in his heart loves Lord Krishna is the rati-ashraya, and Lord Krishna, who is the object of His devotee's love, is the rati-vishaya. When Lord Krishna loves His devotee, then Krishna Himself is the rati-ashraya (a person who loves someone) and the devotee is the rati-vishaya.

Since Krishna is the foundational ingredient for love of Krishna, his different aspects give rise to various kinds of love. When Krishna is in Dvaraka He is perfect. When He is in Mathura, He is more perfect. When He is in Gokula He is most perfect. His transcendental qualities are manifested in this way in different degrees.

Although Krishna is independent of everyone, out of His causeless mercy He is dependent upon Garga Rishi for religious instruction, for learning the military art He is dependent upon Satyaki, and for good counsel He is dependent upon His friend Uddhava.

Devotees who are situated in vibhava are the ones experiencing the rasas. A person who in his heart has ecstatic love (bhava) for Lord Krishna is considered a devotee situated in the rasas. Two kinds of devotees are qualified to take part in rasas:

1\. Sadhaka (those who are cultivating devotional service in order to enter into the transcendental kingdom)

2\. Siddha (those who are already in the perfect stage of devotional service).

A sadhaka is a person who has attained the stage of attraction for Krishna and who is not freed from the material impasse, but who has qualified himself to enter the kingdom of God is called a sadhaka. A sadhaka means one who is cultivating devotion in Krishna consciousness.

When a devotee is never tired of executing devotional service and is always engaged in Krishna conscious activities, constantly relishing the transcendental mellows in relationship with Krishna, he is called perfect (siddha). There are two kinds of perfect devotees:

• they who attained perfection (samprapta-siddha)

• they who were always perfect (nitya-siddha)

The perfectional stage (samprapta-siddha) can be achieved in two ways: by gradual progress in devotional service (sadhana-siddha), or by the causeless mercy of Krishna, even though he has not executed all the details of devotional service (kripa-siddha). Regarding siddhas, Rupa Gosvami writes:

*atma-koti-gunam krishne pramanam paramam gatah*  
*nityananda-gunah sarve nitya-siddha mukunda-vat*

**Persons who have achieved eternal, blissful life exactly on the level of Shri Krishna and who are able to attract Lord Krishna by their transcendental loving service, and who love Lord Krishna millions of times more than they love themselves, are called eternally perfect (nitya-siddha).**

**Bhakti-rasamrita-sindhu 2.1.290**

In the Padma Purana, Uttara-khanda it is said:

“Just as Lord Ramachandra descends along with Lakshmana (an incarnation of Sankarshana) and Bharata (an expansion of Pradyumna), so the members of the Yadu dynasty and the cowherd men of Vrindavan also descend with Lord Krishna in order to join in the transcendental pastimes of the Lord. When the Supreme Lord returns to His eternal abode, His associates return with Him to their respective places. As such, these ever-liberated Vaishnavas are not bound by the material laws of birth and death.”

The foregoing was a description of the alambana, the foundational ingredients for rasa to take place. Uddipana is the impetus for rasa to be experienced.

Some things that give impetus (uddipana) or stimulation to ecstatic love of Krishna are his transcendental qualities, his uncommon activities, his smiling features, his apparel and garlands, his flute, his buffalo-horn, his leg-bells, his conchshell, his footprints, his places of pastimes (such as Vrndavana), his favourite plant (tulasi), his devotee, and the periodical occasions for remembering him. One such occasion for remembrance is ekadashi.

As far as Krishna's transcendental qualities are concerned, they can be divided into three groups: qualities pertaining to his transcendental body, qualities pertaining to his transcendental speech, and qualities pertaining to his transcendental mind.

Krishna's age is considered in three periods: kaumara, pauganda, and kaishora. These are described in the following words:

*kaumaram pancamabdantam paugandam dashamavadhi*  
*ashodashac ca kaishoram yauvanam syat tatha param*

**Krishna's age is considered in three periods: from his appearance day to the end of his fifth year is called kaumara, from the beginning of the sixth year up to the end of the tenth year is called pauganda, and from the eleventh to the end of the fifteenth year is called kaishora. After the beginning of the sixteenth year, Krishna is called yauvana, or a youth, and this continues with no change.**

**Bhakti-rasamrita-sindhu 2.1.309**

Krishna's kaishora age may be divided in three parts. Among his many transcendental qualities manifested in this age, his handsomeness is very prominent. Lord Krishna's limbs are graceful and appropriately shaped. This feature is called is called saundarya (handsomeness). Lord Krishna's is dressed in exquisite garments, and decorated with ornaments, and his hair is also decorated.

There are three kinds of flutes used by Krishna:

Venu: very small, not more than six inches long, with six holes for whistling.

Murali: about eighteen inches long with a hole at the end and four holes on the body of the flute. This kind of flute produces a very enchanting sound.

Vamshi: about fifteen inches long, with nine holes on its body.

The right-turning conch shell Krishna holds in His hand is called “Panchajanya". All these uddipanas increase the devotee’s attraction and love for Krishna and fill the devotee with bliss. The result is that rati (attraction) and sthayi-bhava (continuous ecstasy) turn into rasa (the transcendental mellows of a personal relationship with Krishna).

## Anubhava – subsequent ecstasy, ‘ensuants.’

The second of the four ingredients of rasa is known as anubhava or ‘subsequent ecstasy.’

Anubhava is an action which results from heightened affection, a physical response to love. That action then further increases the affection. In the example of the mother, when she sees her child’s shoes, she may pick them up and clasp them to her chest in affection. The holding or embracing of the shoes is anubhava.

When one is attracted (rati) to Krishna, it is called vibhava. The bodily symptoms manifested by a devotee in expressing that ecstatic love for Krishna are called anu-bhava. (‘What follows bhava’).

Practical examples of anubhava are as follows: dancing, rolling on the ground, singing very loudly, stretching the body, crying loudly, yawning, breathing very heavily, neglecting the presence of others, drooling, laughing like a madman, wheeling the head, and belching.

When the heart is agitated by the ecstatic feelings of vibhava, many extraordinary and wonderful feelings are manifested. These feelings blossom in many ways in the heart. When these feelings blossom in the heart, they eventually bear fruits of ecstatic symptoms that are manifested in the external body. The fruits of ecstatic symptoms are manifested as dancing and in many other ways also. Thus, when the heart dances, the body dances also. When the heart sings, then the tongue sings also.

The vibhava in the heart manifests the anubhavas, and the anubhavas in turn also influence the heart. Because the anubhavas have this effect they are considered separate samagris, or ingredients of rasa.

## Sattvika-bhava – involuntary expression

The third component of rasa is called sattvika-bhava, or an involuntary reaction that results from heightened affection, which then serves to further enhance that affection. A sattvika-bhava is a special type of anubhava.

Both anubhava and sattvika-bhava are similar, but there is an important distinction between them. Anubhava involves a conscious decision to suitably express one’s affection, whereas sattvika-bhava is an unpremeditated, involuntary expression arising directly from heightened spiritual consciousness. There are eight sattvika-bhavas such as becoming stunned, crying, trembling, hair standing on end and so on.

When a non-devotee is filled with wonder and bliss by hearing the description of the great wonder that is Lord Krishna's sweetness, the ecstatic symptoms are called ‘ruksha’ (dried up). Standing of the hairs on the body is the symptom of ruksha-sattvika-bhava.

When the sadhaka devotee's vital force of life is in contact with the various external elements, agitation and transformation result. From this agitation the various ecstatic symptoms, such as becoming stunned, are manifested.

There are eight symptoms of sattvika-bhava (existential ecstatic love): becoming stunned, perspiring, standing of the hairs on the body, faltering of the voice, trembling of the body, changing of bodily colours, shedding of tears, and devastation. When the vital force is in contact with the five material elements, different kinds of ecstatic symptoms are manifested.

in contact with the earth one is stunned.

in contact with water, shedding of tears.

in contact with fire, perspiration.

in contact with sky, complete devastation.

in contact with air, trembling, failing of the voice, and standing of the hairs on the body.

In this way the different ecstatic symptoms are manifested. These symptoms are sometimes manifested internally and sometimes externally. Sometimes they are called ‘bhavas’ and sometimes they are called ‘anubhavas.’

Ecstatic symptoms are sometimes mimicked as a means of deception, especially in eastern religious circles where such manifestations may be appreciated. Bhaktivinode Thakur warns us not to be deceived: “Some wicked-hearted persons exhibit artificial ecstatic symptoms as a sort of dramatic show. They may put on an artificial show of crying in ecstatic love. These persons are not pure in heart.”

## Vyabhichari-bhava – transitory ecstasy

The fourth component of rasa is known as vyabhichari-bhava, an emotional experience that results from intensified affection for Krishna, that serves to embellish and further stimulate that affection.The word charanti means ‘to move,’ while abhi means ‘forward,’ and vi – a ‘specific way,’ therefore vyabhichari-bhava is a transient emotion that moves one forward in a specific way. It arises when the mind is overcome with a specific emotion.

In the example of the mother, after she sees the shoes, she continues walking but then sees her child walking along the edge of a cliff. Her affection intensifies dramatically, embellished by a sudden rush of extreme anxiety. She scoops her child into her arms, saves her from calamity, and her feelings of anxiety gradually subside. The feeling of anxiety is transitory, but because it ‘pushes’ her affection for a limited period it is known as ‘aggressive.’
