With regards to Krishna, there are thirty-three vyabhicharis or ‘transitory or aggressive ecstasies.’ These thirty-three bhavas work ‘aggressively’ to increase the love of a devotee. That is why they are called vyabhichari. They are manifested in the words, limbs, and thoughts. They are like waves in the ocean of nectar, that rise from, and then again disappear, back into the ocean of bhava.

The thirty-three vyabhichari-bhavas are: disappointment, lamentation, humility, guilt, fatigue, intoxication, pride, doubt, apprehension, intense emotion, madness, forgetfulness, disease, confusion, death, laziness, inertness, bashfulness, concealment, remembrance, argumentativeness, anxiety, thoughtfulness, endurance, happiness, eagerness, violence, haughtiness, envy, impudence, dizziness, sleepiness, and alertness.

## Sthayi-bhava – constitutional ecstasy

The fifth and final component of rasa is known as sthayi bhava. This refers to our predominant and eternal mood of love toward Krishna such as a passive admirer, servant, friend, parent, or lover. It is the basic loving mood, which Srila Prabhupada also translates as ‘permanent’ or ‘continuous’ ecstasy. It is the one bhava that controls all the others, and for this reason is also known as the ‘king’ of bhavas.

We relish rasa with Krishna when various emotional components heighten our affection for him. As discussed in this chapter, these components are known as bhavas. Our mood of affection for Krishna is the permanent foundation of our spiritual heart and is therefore the most important element of rasa. To help understand this, here is an analogy:

Rasa consists of an interaction among five bhavas. Similarly, the ocean consists of an interaction between sea water, wind, waves, tides, and foam. Sthayi-bhava is comparable to the sea water, and the other four bhavas are comparable to wind, waves, tides, and foam. All these elements are essential parts of the ocean, and they are visible whenever we see the ocean. But the sea water is the most important and indispensable element since without it the others would not exist.

## Primary and secondary rasas

**This chapter concerns the five primary rasas and the seven secondary rasas. Further treatment of this exalted subject matter in more detail can be found in Srila Rupa Goswami’s Bhakti Rasamrita Sindhu, given to the western world by Srila Prabhupada as The Nectar of Devotion.**

As sunlight is divided into different colours when it passes through a prism, so attraction to Krishna assumes different features according to the different kinds of persons who contain it. Pure attraction to Krishna, known as rati, is of three kinds: samanya-rati (ordinary), svaccha-rati (clear), and shanta-rati (peaceful).

Samanya-rati or ‘general attraction’ is the love the people in general feel for Lord Krishna. When attraction to Krishna is manifested in different ways in the different kinds of devotees, as light assumes different colours as it passes through coloured glass, that kind of rati is called svaccha or ‘clear.’ In this way some devotees offer prayers to Krishna and call him ‘master,’ others joke with Krishna and call Him ‘friend,’ still others protect Krishna and call him ‘son,’ still others, overcome with bliss, call Krishna their ‘beloved,’ and still others meditate on Krishna and call Him ‘the Supersoul.’ A devotee in shanta-rati (neutrality) meditates with a peaceful mind on the unchanging Supreme.

When the devotee thinks, ‘Krishna is the master, and I am His servant,’ he loves Krishna in the dasya-rasa. When he is attached to Krishna in this way, a devotee is not attracted to or pleased by anything outside this rasa of servitorship.

When a devotee is firmly convinced that Krishna is his equal, his love for Krishna is called sakhya-rati. In sakhya-rati there are joking, laughing, and other like activities.

The kindly love that His elders give to Krishna is called vatsalya-rati. Embracing and fondling Krishna, performing auspicious rituals for His benefit, pronouncing blessings upon Him, and affectionately touching His chin are some of the activities of vatsalya-rati.

When the gopis love for Krishna is prompted by the eight types of sambhoga, enjoyment, such as smarana, remembering, Krishna and darshana, gazing at Krishna, these are called the activities of priyata (love) or madhura-rati (sweet love). Included in these activities are sidelong glances (kataksha), moving the eyebrows (bhru-ksepa), speaking sweet words (priya-vani), and speaking joking words (hasya). Beginning with shanta and extending up to madhura-rati, the devotees' love, relish, and bliss is increasingly relishable and blissful. These are the five kinds of mukhya-rati or primary rasas.

## The Secondary Rasas

The super-excellent secondary rasas, known as gauna-bhava, which belong to the transcendental lover and beloved, are known as gauna-rati. There are seven of them: laughter (hasya), astonishment (vismaya), chivalry or eagerness (utsaha), lamentation (shoka), anger (krodha), dread or fear (bhaya), and scorn, ghastliness, or disgust (ninda).

In the first six of these rasas the devotee always meditates on Krishna. When the devotee attains pure love (shuddha-rati), then he finds his material body and material activities to be very distasteful. He is horrified by them. That is the seventh rasa, ghastliness.

Between these symptoms and the primary symptoms there are inherent differences. Yet because they are in contact with the primary rasas, these seven secondary emotions are included among the rasas, and the word rati (attraction, or love) is used to describe them.

For some devotees one or more of these secondary rasas remain a permanent part of their relationship with Krishna. But this is not true for every devotee. For most devotees, these secondary rasas are manifested only temporarily. In some situations, one or more of these secondary rasas become so strong that they eclipse the natural primary rasas. Attachments or ratis cause certain emotions to take place within the heart of a devotee:

Hasya-rati: Certain unusual words, appearances, or activities cause laughter to appear in the heart. In laughter the eyes may open wide, and the nostrils, lips, and cheeks may tremble. This laughter is also generated by love of Krishna combined with the emotion of hesitation.

Vismaya-rati: When one sees something extraordinary, astonishment and wonder is manifested in his heart. Some of the symptoms of astonishment are opening the eyes wide, speaking words of praise, and standing up of the body's hairs.

Utsaha-rati: When the heart is determined to perform the noble deeds that are glorified by great saints, that is called chivalry. Promptness, impatience, great exertion, fervour, and enthusiasm are among the symptoms of chivalry.

Krodha-rati: When actions of an enemy make the heart burst into flames, that is called anger. Harshness, callous intemperance, knitting the eyebrows, and redness in the eyes are among the symptoms of anger.

Bhaya-rati: When the sight of horrible things makes the heart wish to flee, that is called dread. Hiding oneself, dryness of the heart, and fleeing from the scene are among the symptoms of dread.

Jugupsa-rati: When one is averse to contemptuous things, that is called ghastliness. It leads to disgust. Spitting, curling the lips, and speaking harsh criticisms are among the symptoms of ghastliness. When this emotion is in relation to Lord Krishna, then it is considered one of the spiritual rasas. Otherwise it is merely another material emotion.

Readers attracted to poems, plays, and other books describing Lord Krishna know that the vibhavas are causes of devotional service. In truth these activities of rati (pure love) are inconceivable. They are very great pastimes of devotional service. In Mahabharata and other scriptures, it is said that these activities of devotional service are very exalted. They are beyond the touch of ordinary, material logic. In the Mahabharata it is written that these bhavas are beyond the understanding of the material mind, and therefore material logic cannot be employed to understand them. Whatever is beyond the world of matter cannot be understood by the material mind. One who attains sweet love for Lord Krishna's form and other features attains vibhava. This vibhava does not remain static. It spontaneously increases, growing greater and greater.

Rati (attraction for Krishna) enables one to directly perceive Lord Krishna, who is the resting place of transcendental sweetness and other virtues. On the other hand, when he directly sees Lord Krishna's form and other features, the devotee loves Lord Krishna more and more. In this way vibhava, anubhava, sattvika-bhava, and vyabhicari-bhava all increase rati, and rati increases them.

## Vipralambha - Separation

Material love is part of the material world. Love for Krishna is beyond the material world, and it is also the origin of many wonderful things. Material love is pleasant when the beloved is present and unpleasant when the beloved is absent. When a person loves Krishna, and Krishna is present, then the lover experiences the taste of a certain kind of rasa, the rasa of sambhoga-sukha (the happiness of enjoying with Krishna). However, when Krishna is absent, the lover experiences the rasa of vipralambha (separation), which itself produces a specific kind of wonderful transcendental bliss.

In answer to one of Chaitanya Mahaprabhu's questions, Ramananda Raya described (Chaitanya-caritamrita Madhya 8.194) the wonderful bliss the devotee feels in separation from Krishna, a bliss he called ‘vivarta.’ Therefore, what seems here to be suffering is in truth the highest spiritual bliss.

## Qualification to discuss rasa

Rupa Goswami explains that those who are not pure, who are interested in other aspects of spiritual development, or whose love of God is covered by formality, should not take part in discussions on rasa; and that devotees who do speak about it should do so only to a qualified audience:

“There are others who are not qualified, and the devotee should be very careful not to describe rasa to them, just as one does not describe the glories of the holy name to the faithless. Renunciation that has nothing to do with pure devotional service is called phalgu-vairagya (false renunciation). Persons who are in the fire of false renunciation, the dry speculative habit, and who neglect devotional service are in a lamentable situation. Persons who are attached to the ritualistic ceremonies recommended in the Vedas and to the impersonal Brahman cannot relish the transcendental pleasure of devotional service.

Therefore, devotees who have already tasted the nectar of devotion should be very careful to protect devotional service from such dry speculators, formal ritual elevationists, and impersonal salvationists. Devotees should protect their valuable jewel of spiritual love from the clutches of thieves and burglars. In other words, a pure devotee should not describe devotional service and its different analytical aspects to speculators and false renouncers.”

## Shanta Rasa

The features of the different rasas are known collectively as rasa-vichara, and the first is shanta-rasa (neutrality). In this rasa the sthayi-bhava or constitutional mood, is shanta-rati. The happiness the yogis find in the impersonal Brahman is very slight. The happiness attained in a relationship with the Supreme Person is much greater than that impersonal happiness. The cause of that personal happiness is the direct perception of the Supreme Personality of Godhead's spiritual form. The alambana (object of worship) in shanta-rasa is the four-armed form of Lord Narayana. This form of the Lord manifests qualities like supreme power and opulence. In this way the vishaya and anubhava are manifested in alambana. Peaceful persons situated in shanta-rasa are the ashraya of shanta-rati. Persons who are transcendentally self-satisfied or atmarama, and persons who have faith in the Supreme Personality of Godhead are qualified for shanta-rasa.

The four sages headed by Sanaka and Sanandana are prominent among the atmaramas. These four sages are boy sannyasis. At first, they were attached to the impersonal Brahman. Later they became attracted to the sweetness of the form of the Supreme Person. Then they began to worship the Lord's spiritual form. Persons who are austere and renounced, but who have not yet thrown far away the desire for impersonal liberation, are eligible for shanta-rasa.

Hearing the important Upanishads, living in a secluded place, meditating, searching for the truth, study, seeing the universal form, associating with persons engaged in devotional service mixed with impersonal speculation (jnana-mishra-bhakti), and studying the Upanishads with others of equal scholarship are the uddipanas that inspire this rasa. The fragrance of tulasi offered to the Supreme Lord's lotus feet, the sound of the conch shell, sacred mountains, sacred forests, sacred places, the Ganges, being free of material desires, and the awareness that time will eventual destroy everything material, are also uddipanas that inspire shanta-rasa. The anubhavas of this rasa are as follows:

Staring at the tip of the nose, acting like an avadhuta, eyes to the ground while walking, looking ahead a distance of four hands as one walks, fondness for showing the jnana-mudra (joining the forefinger and thumb), not hating the enemies of the Supreme Personality of Godhead, being slightly inclined to the devotees dear to the Lord, being attracted to the soul's liberation and the end of his residence in the material world, being impartial and aloof, being selfless, free from material possessiveness, and material pride, and feeling a sort of cool-tempered love for the Supreme are included among the anubhavas of shanta-rasa. Yawning, swelling of the limbs, teaching about devotional service, bowing down before Lord Hari, and offering prayers to Lord Hari are also included among these anubhavas.

Shukadeva Gosvami and Bilvamangala Thakura are examples of two persons who renounced shanta-rasa and became expert in tasting the bliss of the rasas of direct devotional service. The great scholar Sarvabhauma Bhattacarya is also an example of that.

## Dasya Rasa

This rasa is also termed prita-bhakti-rasa, the pleasurable devotional mellow. The Lord’s servitors receiving his favour are of two kinds: dasya, those who consider themselves as his subordinate servitors; and lalya, those who consider themselves personally maintained and watched over by him. So those in dasya-rasa feel themselves to be either:

1\. anugrahya-patra-dasya (the object of mercy)

2\. lalya-patra-dasya (the object of affection).

Prita-rasa is also divided into these two kinds:

1\. sambhrama-prita (with pride) and

2\. gaurava-prita (with submission).

Persons who are proud to be servants of Krishna have sambhrama-prita for Krishna, the son of Nanda. When this rasa develops it is called sambhrama-prita. In this rasa Krishna and Krishna's servants are both the alambanas.

In Gokula sambhrama-prita, Krishna has two arms. In the other parts of this rasa he sometimes has two arms and sometimes four arms. In Gokula Krishna is a cowherd boy, has two arms, holds the flute, and is decorated with a peacock-feather and other ornaments. When he is manifested outside of Gokula, Krishna is decorated with jewels and his feature is that of aishvarya (power and opulence). Srila Rupa Gosvami (Bhakti-rasamrita-sindhu 3.2.11-15) explains:

Krishna possesses the following qualities: 1. Many millions of universes come from the pores of His body, 2. He is an ocean of mercy, 3. He has inconceivable potencies, 4. He is served by all mystic powers. 5. He is the seed from which all incarnations have come. 6. His transcendental qualities attract the hearts of the liberated souls. 7. He is the supreme controller. 8. He is the supreme object of worship. 9. He knows everything. 10. He is determined. 11. He has all wealth. 12. He is tolerant and forgiving.

13\. He protects the surrendered souls. 14. He is happy. 15. He is truthful. 16. He is expert. 17. He brings all auspiciousness. 18. He is powerful. 19. He is religious. 20. He sees through the eyes of the scriptures.

21\. He is the best friend of the devotee. 22. He is generous. 23. He is glorious. 24. He is grateful. 25. He is the resting place of fame. 26. He is the best. 27. He is powerful. 28. He is controlled by love.

In this way Lord Hari is the alambana (the object of love) for His four kinds of servants, namely:

1\. prashrita (who humbly look down when they stand before their master),

2\. ajnanuvarti (who humbly carry out their master's orders),

3\. vishvasta (who have full faith in their master), and

4\. prabhu jnane namra-buddhi (who are humble and very conscious of their master's superiority).

These four are also called: 1. adhikrita (they who are qualified), 2. ashrita (they who have taken shelter), 3. parishada (associates), and 4. anugata (followers).

Brahma, Shiva, Indra, and the other demigods and goddesses are adhikrita servants and maidservants. By fulfilling various duties in the material world, they eventually become qualified (adhikrita) to serve the Supreme Lord directly.

The three kinds of ashrita servants are: 1. sharanagata (surrendered souls), 2. jnani (wise philosophers), and 3. seva-nishtha (intent on serving).

Kaliya-naga and the kings imprisoned by Jarasandha are counted among the sharanagata servants. Shaunaka and the other sages who have rejected the desire for impersonal liberation and taken shelter of Lord Hari are counted among the jnani servants. Because they were attached to worshiping the Lord from the very beginning of their lives, Candradhvaja, Harihara, Bahulashva, Ikshvaku, and Pundarika are counted among both the seva-nishtha and sharanagata servants.

Uddhava, Daruka, Satyaki, Shrutadeva, Shatrajit, Nanda (this is another Nanda, not Lord Krishna's father), Upananda, and Bhadra are counted among the parishada servants. Although these persons give advice to Lord Krishna, at the appropriate times they also serve Him. Among the Kauravas, Bhishma, Parikshit, Vidura, and others are counted among the parishada servants. Among them all, Uddhava, who is filled with pure love (prema) is the best.

The anugata devotees, whose hearts are always eager to serve the Lord, are of two kinds: 1. the servants who stay in Dvaraka, and 2. the devotees who stay in Vraja. Suchabdra, Mandala, Stambha, and Sutamba are included among the anuga devotees in Dvaraka. Raktaka, Patraka, Patri, Madhukantha, Madhuvrata, Rasala, Suvilasa, Premakandha, Makarandaka, Ananda, Candrahasa, Payoda, Bakula, Rasada, and Sharada are included among the anuga servants in Vraja. Raktaka is the best of the anuga servants in Vraja. The parishada servants are of three kinds: 1. dhurya (leaders), 2. dhira (philosophers), and 3. vira (heroes). The ashrita servants are also of three kinds: 1. nitya-siddha (eternally perfect), 2. siddha (perfect), and 3. sadhaka (aspiring for perfection).

The sound of Krishna's flute, the sound of His buffalo-horn bugle, His smiling glance, hearing the narration of His glories, His lotus footprints, His bodily limbs splendid like a new monsoon cloud, and the fragrance of His transcendental body.

The anubhavas of this rasa are to perform one's duties in every situation, to follow orders, to be free of envy, to make friendship with devotees sincerely surrendered to Lord Krishna, and to have faith in Lord Krishna are included among the extraordinary anubhavas of this rasa. Dancing, respect for Lord Krishna's personal friends, and renunciation of everything outside the realm of Krishna consciousness are also included among these anubhavas.

## Sakhya Rasa

In sakhya-rasa, Krishna and His friends are the alambanas. Krishna, who is the prince of Vraja, and who holds the flute in His two hands, is the vishaya. Krishna's friends are the ashraya.

Their forms, qualities, and garments are like those of Krishna's servants. However, Krishna's friends are confident to approach Krishna as equals. They are not filled with awe and veneration like Krishna's servants. These friends are of two kind: 1. friends in Dvaraka, and 2. friends in Vraja. Arjuna, Bhimasena, Draupadi, and Shridama Vipra are among the friends in Dvaraka and the other cities. Among them, Arjuna is the best. The friends in Vraja are always eager to see Krishna. Krishna is their very life. They are the best of Krishna's friends.

In Vraja, Krishna has four kinds of friends:

1\. suhrit (well-wishers),

2\. sakha (friends),

3\. priya-sakha (confidential friends), and

4\. priya-narma-vayasya (intimate friends).

Krishna's well-wisher friends are a little bit older than Krishna, and they have some parental affection for Him. Because of their being older than Krishna, they always try to protect Him from any harm. As such, they sometimes bear weapons so that they can chastise any mischievous persons who want to do harm to Krishna. Counted among the well-wisher friends are Subhadra, Mandalibhadra, Bhadravardhana, Gobhata, Yaksha, Indrabhata, Bhadranga, Virabhadra, Mahaguna, Vijaya, and Balabhadra. Among these friends Balabhadra and Mandalibhadra are the best.

Friends who are younger than Krishna, who are always attached to Him, and who give Him all kinds of service are called ordinary friends, or simply friends. Such ordinary friends are called sakhas, and the names of some of them are Vishala, Vrishabha, Ojasvi, Devaprastha, Varuthapa, Maranda, Kusumapida, Manibandha, and Karandhama. Among them Devaprastha is the best.

The more confidential friends are called priya-sakhas and are almost Krishna's age. Because of their very confidential friendship, their behaviour is based on pure friendship. Some confidential friends are as follows: Shridama, Sudama, Dama, Vasudama, Kinkini, Stoka-krishna, Amshu, Bhadrasena, Vilasi, Pundarika, Vitanka, and Kalavinka.

The uddipanas of this rasa are Krishna's age, his beauty, his bugle, his flute, his conch shell, his pleasing attitude, his exceptional joking abilities, his great power, and his playful activities all provoke love in friendship for Him.

Krishna has four distinct periods in his age. From his appearance day to the end of His sixth year is called kaumara; from the beginning of the sixth year up to the tenth year is called pauganda; and from the tenth to the sixteenth year is called kaishora; after the beginning of the sixteenth year, Krishna is called a yauvana or a youth, and this continues with no change.

Krishna’s pastimes are mostly executed during the kaumara, kaishora and pauganda periods. His affectionate pastimes with his parents are executed during his kaumara age. His friendship with the cowherd boys is exhibited during the pauganda period. And his friendship with the gopis is exhibited during the age of kaishora. Krishna’s pastimes in Vrindavan are finished by the end of His fifteenth year, and then He is transferred to Mathura and Dwaraka, where all other pastimes are performed.

When Krishna is spending His days as a cowherd boy, he is in the kaumara and pauganda ages. In the kaishora age, when Krishna appeared in Gokula, He acted as a cowherd boy, and then, when He was sixteen, He went to Mathura.

Krishna played with His intimate friends sometimes by fighting or wrestling with their arms, sometimes by playing ball, and sometimes by playing chess. Sometimes they carried one another on their shoulders, and sometimes they exhibited their expertness at whirling logs. And the cowherd friends used to please Krishna by sitting together with Him on couches or on swings, by lying together on their beds, by joking together, and by swimming in the pool, by playing with the monkeys and other animals, and by dancing, singing, and other activities. All these activities are called anubhava.

The suhrit friends used to sit together and advise one another what to do, sometimes inducing one another to be engaged in welfare work. The sakha friends would offer betelnuts to one another, decorate one another's faces with tilaka, or smear pulp of chandana, or sandalwood on one another's bodies. The priya-sakha friends wanted to defeat Krishna. Sometimes they used to snatch His clothing or snatch away the flowers from His hands. Sometimes they would try to induce one another to decorate His body for Him, and failing this, they were always ready to fight, challenging one another to combat in wrestling. The priya-narma-sakha friends would plan for Krishna's madhura-lila. Like the servants, these friends also decorated Krishna with forest flowers and fanned Him. The stages of sakhya-rasa are classed as: prema, sneha, raga, and pranaya.

Prema – pure affection free from fear and awe.

Sneha – love, unable to bear separation from Krishna.

Raga – intense love, in which even sorrow brings happiness.

Pranaya – friendship without formality, a love fortified by unflinching faith and trust.

In pranaya, awe and veneration are completely absent, even in situations where they might be appropriate. Sakhya-rasa is wonderful and unprecedented. Sakhya-rasa is very different from either dasya-rasa or vatsalya-rasa. Among all the rasas, sakhya-rasa is especially dear to Krishna. Krishna and his friends have a very close relationship. In some ways the spiritual ecstasy of sakhya-rasa is almost like the ecstasy of madhurya-rasa.

## Vatsalya Rasa

This is the rasa of parental feeling, where Krishna is the recipient of his devotee’s protective love. In vatsalya-rasa, Krishna is the vishaya, and His elder relatives and superiors are the ashraya. Krishna's body is dark and handsome. He has all virtues. He is gentle, a pleasing speaker, honest, sincere, shy, humble, respectful, and generous. The king and queen of Vraja, Rohini, the elder gopis, Devaki, Kunti, Vasudeva, and Sandipani Muni are included among the devotees in vatsalya-rasa. Nanda and Yashoda are the best among them. The uddipanas include Krishna's kaumara age and other ages and his form, garments, childhood, restlessness, talking, joking, and pastimes.

The anubhavas include smelling Krishna's head, massaging His limbs, offering blessings to him, giving orders to him, caressing him, protecting him, and giving good advice to him. Kissing Krishna, hugging him, and calling his name are also included among these activities.

The sattvika ecstatic symptoms in this rasa include milk flowing from the breasts and the eight ecstatic symptoms that begin with becoming stunned.

Lord Balarama's rasa is a mixture of dasya-rasa and vatsalya-rasa. King Yudhishthira's rasa is a mixture of vatsalya, dasya, and sakhya. King Ugrasena's rasa is a mixture of vatsalya and sakhya. The rasa of Nakula, Sahadeva, Narada Muni, and many others is a mixture of sakhya and dasya. The rasa of Shiva, Garuda, Uddhava, and many others is a mixture of dasya and sakhya.

## Madhurya rasa

**Madhurya-rasa is the mood of the romantic consort, also known as conjugal love. Srila Bhaktivinode Thakur writes: “Madhurya-rasa is said to be the most important of the bhakti-rasas. It is very difficult to understand. It is not easy to find a person qualified to understand it. This rasa is very confidential. Therefore, it is appropriate to keep it hidden from general view.”**

The priya-narma-sakha, or intimate friends, of Krishna are to a certain extent aware of the activities of madhura-rasa or madhurya-rasa. In this rasa the vishaya is Shri Krishna, the hero whose handsomeness has no equal. The transcendental ashraya is the devotees who relish pastimes with Him. Therefore, the vraja-gopis are the ashraya in this rasa.

Of all the dear gopis, Shri Radha is the best. Of the many uddipanas in this rasa, the sound of Krishna's flute is the first. The anubhavas of this rasa include sidelong glances and smiles. All the sattvika-bhavas are gloriously present in this rasa. All the vyabhicharis, except for laziness and ferociousness, are also present in this rasa. Madhurya rasa is of two kinds:

1\. Vipralambha (separation).

2\. Sambhoga (meeting).

Vipralambha is of many kinds, including purva-raga (the beginning of love), mana (jealous anger), and pravasa (when the beloved goes far away).

Purva-raga is said to be the situation before the lovers have met. Mana is the state of being offended by the lover. Pravasa is when the lover loses the association of the beloved. When the two lovers meet and enjoy (bhoga) together, that is called sambhoga.

There are four types of nayaka, or ‘hero’ and Krishna is all of them – he can play all these roles. In madhurya rasa Krishna assumes two distinct roles as nayaka: pati (husband) and upapatti (paramour). Within madhurya the parakiya – or paramour mood – is superior to the svakiya – or married mood.

Two other aspects of Krishna as purusha are: atmaramata – his state of completeness and selfsatisfaction where the enjoyment of rasa is solely reposed within Krishna, taking no assistance from any other entity. Krishna is eternally self-satisfied.

The other aspect is pararamata – Krishna exhibits the nature of enjoying rasa by the charming assistance of other entities. This is also eternal. These elements are opposing but exist harmoniously within his personality.

The highest mood of the pararamata nature is displayed in the pastimes of parakiya-bhava.

Parakiya-bhava is produced when the nayaka (hero) and nayika (heroine) are brought together by raga, spontaneous love. The atmaramata nature is at the bottom of the ‘rasa barometer.’

Bhaktivinode Thakur explains that although the affairs of Krishna and Radha seem to the worldly-minded just like the affairs of an ordinary young boy and girl, the position of this rasa should not be misunderstood. Just as the sweetest fruit can be found at the top of the tree, while in the upside-down reflection of the tree it is at the bottom, so the parakiya rasa of Radha and Krishna is the sweetest and topmost state of existence, while the so-called “parakiya rasa displayed by the conditioned soul is illicit and despicable.”

Krishna’s consorts in Dwaraka, his queens, are svakiya, or ‘married,’ while those in Vraja, the cowherd girls, are parakiya or unmarried. Krishna manifests his lila or ‘pastimes’ in two worlds, the spiritual and material. In the spiritual world they are known as aprakata-lila or ‘unmanifest pastimes’ and in this world the ‘manifest pastimes’ or prakata-lila. The first is known as Goloka and the other Gokula.

There is no difference between them, only the perception of the viewer creates differences. The eligibility to see these places is directly the result of Krishna’s mercy. In Goloka all perception is based on pure spiritual consciousness, all mundane perception is absent. Bhava creates abhimana, spiritual self-conception born of love. Although Krishna is unborn, Nanda and Yashoda feel themselves to be parents. However, in Gokula Krishna is born. All this is brought about by Krishna’s yogamaya.

All of Krishna’s prakata-lila are constantly present for perception, a cyclic continuum of manifestation progressing through the different material universes, just as the Sun is always at midday somewhere in the world, or a travelling show appears in different towns but the performance is the same in each. In the aprakata-lila, all pastimes are manifest in the nitya-vartamana, the transcendental eternal present.

## Further details of Madhurya Rasa

Out of lobha – greed – for relishing parakiya-rasa Krishna is allured into transgressing his inner dharma of being infinitely self-satisfied.

The site of the rasa-lila, the Yamuna, Govardhan and innumerable bowers are the places of Krishna’s amorous play. Parakiya bhava churns rasa from the ocean of pastimes.

The unmarried gopis have the firm conviction that Krishna will be their husband, therefore their love is svakiya-bhava.

Those married gopis who go to see Krishna leave another form behind them, therefore seeing them at home, their husbands never become envious of Krishna.

Sadhakas who are eligible to practise madhurya-rasa cultivate their identity as parodha – another’s wife who was given in marriage. This mood is present in all the gopis. Were it not there it would be impossible for the lila to take place.

There are three categories of gopi in Vraja. These are present in groups known as yuthas. Every group has a leader or yutheshvari. Within every group there is a gana or sub-group. Within the groups various positions are assigned, each with its own service.

1\. Sadhana-para – gopis by sadhana. These are two types: yauthiki - linked in yuthas or groups (of which there are two: muni-gana – those gopis who were previously male sages, and upanishad-gana – the personified Upanishads). There are also ayauthiki – those who are not linked in groups, of which there are two kinds:

prachina - established, those who attained salokya (elevation to the same abode as the Lord) a long time ago, and navina – new, those who are taking their initial birth in Vraja through the species of devas and humans.

2\. Devi – gopis previously goddesses or devis. These take birth in heaven when a partial incarnation of Krishna, an amsa, takes birth there. Vamana is such an avatar. The wives of the gods are vibhinamsa expansions of Krishna. The Padma Purana states that the goddess Gayatri Devi took birth in Vraja as a gopi and attained the association of Krishna, at which point she acquired the form of kama-gayatri.

3\. Nitya-priya – eternally dear gopis, such as Sri Radha and Sri Chandravali. They are usually yutheshvaris, or group leaders. Among them, eight have been designated as pradhana, most prominent and fortunate. The eight are headed by Radha. Their names are Lalita, Vishakha, Chitra, Champaklata, Tungavidya, Indulekha, Rangadevi and Sudevi. There are hundreds of yuthas, some obedient to Radha and some to Chandravali.

The names of the gopis are mentioned in Padma, Skanda and Bhavishya Puranas.

Srimati Radharani is the embodiment of the hladini-shakti and is served by sixteen excellences of beauty and twelve embellishments. She has twenty-five prominent attributes.

The gopis in Radharani’s group are embellished with all perfections which help them to create a romantic ambience to attract Krishna.

Radharani has five types of friends:

1\. Sakhi – friends, such as Vrinda and Dhanistha

2\. Nitya-sakhi – eternal friends, such as Kasturi and Manimanjari

3\. Prana-sakhi – friends as dear as life, such as Sasimukhi and Vasanti

4\. Priya-sakhi – dearmost friends, such as Sasikala, Malati and Madhavi.

5\. Parama-prestha-sakhi – the supremely dear friends: Lalita, Vishakha, Chitra, Champaklata, Tungavidya, Indulekha, Rangadevi and Sudevi.

There are three types of nayika or heroine: svakiya, parakiya and samanya or general, such as Kubja. Both svakiya and parakiya are of three types:

1\. Mugdha, captivated – youthful, innocent.

2\. Madhya, intermediate – variable temperament

3\. Pragalbha, imperious – proud, enthusiastic, may be harsh.

All the above can be either senior – jyestha – or kanistha – junior. There are eight specific psychological conditions displayed by a nayika. Many different symptoms are herein described.

The sakhis in their individual yuthas are divided into three, according to the status of their position and skill:

1\. Adhika – of the greatest fortune and dexterity.

2\. Sama – intermediate position.

3\. Laghu – light in dexterity and position.

In addition, they may be divided according to their temperament:

1\. Prakhara – of imperious temperament.

2\. Madhya – of intermediate temperament.

3\. Mridvi – of soft, sweet and compliant temperament.

Some sakhis act as dutis or messengers. They arrange the abhisara, or tryst, between the lovers who are at that time apart from one another.

There are sixteen primary activities of the sakhis, including to describe the glories of the hero and heroine, to make rendezvous, to make jokes, to carry messages and to be attentive and diligent about the smallest details of service.

When the qualifications of the nayikas are found in the eight kinds of circumstances the total is one hundred and twenty categories. In three categories of qualification, kanistha, (smallest) madhyama (middle) and uttama (highest) that brings a total of three hundred and sixty nayikas. The uddipanas, the stimulants, in madhurya-rasa are:

1\. Gunas – the personal qualities

2\. Nama – the names

3\. Charita – the activities

4\. Mandana – the decorations

5\. Sambandhi – the items directly related to Krishna

6\. Tatastha – the items indirectly related to Krishna

Gunas, or personal qualities are three: manasika – of the mind; vachika – of speech; kayika – physical attributes.

Manasika: gratitude, compassion, forgiveness, kindness.

Vachika: sweet and pleasing words that delight the ears.

Kayika: vayasa-yauvana – the age of fresh youthfulness; rupa – splendorous and lustrous beauty; lavanya – charming loveliness; saundarya – beautiful figure and posture; abhi-rupata – the ability to enhance the beauty of others; madhurya – sweetness; mardava – mildness, gentility and sensitivity.

Yauvana, or youthfulness, is of four kinds:

Vayah-sandhi – transition to adolescence

Navya-vayasa – new adolescence

Vyakta-vayasa – blooming adolescence

Purna-vayasa – the prime of youthfulness

Madhurya-rati is nourished by seven causes, listed in order of ascending merit:

1\. Abhiyoga – expression of one’s emotions to Krishna

2\. Visaya – perception of Krishna through the senses

3\. Sambandha – items in relationship to Krishna

4\. Abhimana – by asserted choice

5\. Tadiya-visesa – Krishna’s unique nature and qualities, which act as catalysts

6\. Upama – poetic comparison of Krishna to other things

7\. Svabhava – one’s innate nature of spontaneous love

When madhurya-rasa reaches the heights of extreme delectability, it is described as sringara- rasa and is of two kinds: vipralambha – love in separation; and sambhoga – love in union and enjoyment together.

Vipralambha has the special quality of enhancing sambhoga and is also known as viraha, separation, and viyoga, absence. Just as a coloured cloth looks brighter when a contrasting colour is added. Vipralambha is of four kinds: purvaraga, mana, prema-vaicittya and pravasa.

## Purva Raga

The rati is increasingly generated in the hearts of Radha and Krishna by darshana and shravana, seeing and hearing, before their actual meeting takes place.

Darshan – the various ways the lovers may glimpse each other: seeing Krishna in person, seeing his picture, and seeing him in a dream.

Shravana – the various ways the lovers may hear of each other: hearing poems about Krishna, descriptions from another sakhi, messages from a duti.

Purvaraga awakens first in the bhakta and Krishna reciprocates accordingly, thus following in the footsteps of the devotee.

Purvaraga is of three varieties:

praudha – fully matured, boundless loving attachment.

samanjasa – intermediate,experienced prior to meeting Krishna. sadharana – general, yearning.

## Mana

Mana is that bhava that prevents the nayaka and nayaki from expressing their mutual love, even though they may be directly in one another’s company. There are two types: sahetu, with cause; and nirhetu, without cause. When the hero exhibits a heightened affection to a consort of a rival camp, this is sahetu and evokes mana. Nirhetu-mana is generated when, without any apparent cause, the heroine construes an imaginary reason to enter mana, thus augmenting the deep affection.

Placating mana is achieved by different means according to the seriousness. A particular place and time, and the sweet sound of the flute can mitigate mana. More serious mana needs careful and concerted application. Most serious, almost implacable mana demands immense patience and resourcefulness. Krishna is called many bad names by the heroine in mana.

## Prema-vaichittya

Immersed in intense love, heart, and mind overwhelmed, the heroine experiences distressful feelings of separation from the beloved, even while he is present.

## Pravasa

After they have spent time together, suddenly they are separated because one of them has left for a far place, or if the mellow has changed. This obstruction is known as pravasa.

The intense yearning stemming from viraha, separation, is a part of the prakata-lila in this world. In the spiritual world, sambhoga-rasa is perpetual. Sambhoga-rasa is of two types: mukhya and gauna:

**Mukhya-sambhoga**, or principal enjoyment is of four kinds:

sanksipta-sambhoga – that which occurs soon after the initial meeting

sankirna-sambhoga – (restricted) that which occurs after feelings of mana sampanna-sambhoga – (enriched) that which is relished after a separation samriddhiman-sambhoga – (opulent, flourishing) after a long separation

**Gauna-sambhoga**

When Krishna appears in a dream and performs pastimes. These dreams are unfettered in their reality.

## Eight periods of the day

Krishna’s lila is divided into eight time periods of the day. These are known as the asta-kaliya-lila:

Nisanta – pre-dawn

Pratah – early morning

Purvahna – forenoon

Madhyahna – midday

Aparahna – afternoon

Sayam – late afternoon and dusk

Pradosa – evening

Ratri – night

Daily bhajan of the qualified sadhaka on the path of raganuga-sadhana consists of meditating on services appropriate for the time of day.

## Dynamic communicators

**After many pages of philosophy and yogic technique, the reader might conclude that the path of the Vaishnava is a quiet life of thoughtful contemplation, something more suited to elderly hermits. But spiritual life awakens the innate qualities of the soul and these include compassion, the inability to tolerate the suffering of others and the desire to be of practical help. So, the Vaishnavas have always been dynamic communicators.**

There are, of course, those Vaishnavas who find that their powers of concentrated meditation falter if they engage in practical missionary work. They are known as bhajan-anandis, or ‘those who gain bliss from meditation.’ There are always a minority of them, practising in secluded places, their compassion limited to a small number of immediate followers. The majority of Vaishnavas, however, are known as goshti-anandis, or ‘those who gain bliss from increasing the community.’ Their happiness is to see newcomers take to a life of devotion.The life of a devotee, whether bhajananandi or goshtianandi, is not the same as those who only seek their personal liberation. Saving oneself from the cycle of birth and death is a noble pursuit, but even greater is saving oneself and helping others. Whereas the yogi retires to the forest or the mountain top to meditate, the devotee places himself in the busy village or bustling town to help the maximum number of people. This tradition of helping others goes back many centuries. The great boy-saint, Prahlada, after being saved by an avatar of Vishnu and offered salvation told him:

**“My dear Lord Narasimha deva, I see that there are many saintly persons indeed, but they are interested only in their own deliverance. Not caring for the big cities and towns, they go to the Himalayas or the forest to meditate with vows of silence. They are not interested in delivering others. As for me, however, I do not wish to be liberated alone, leaving aside all these poor fools and rascals. I know that without taking shelter of your lotus feet, one cannot be happy. Therefore, I wish to bring them back to shelter at your feet.”**

**Shrimad Bhagavatam 7.9.44**

For thousands of years, devotees of Krishna have been messengers and guides. They have travelled from village to village to enlighten others about the messages of the Vedas, the importance of leading a pure life and following dharma. They have urged their listeners to spend time each day in recitation of the divine names, japa, in sacred study, in congregational singing, kirtan, and worship. And the response has often given birth to mass movements that inspired art and architecture, social upliftment, and political emancipation.

The methods of the Vaishnavas have been innovative, artistic, and taken many attractive forms. For the saintly Alwars of southern India in the fifth to ninth centuries, it was storytelling, poetry, songs, gorgeous temple worship and colourful processions. For the Haridas movement, beginning in the early 1500s in Karnataka, south-west India, it was the criss-crossing of the countryside by wandering bards who sang beautiful Carnatic songs in praise of Krishna, drama festivals, dance troupes, and social reform. All of this was accompanied, of course, by philosophy, music, and widescale distribution of delicious sacred food.

This movement of compassionate outreach to the public reached a mediaeval zenith under the gaze of Sri Chaitanya and his followers. Chaitanya Mahaprabhu inspired a mass movement for congregational singing of Krishna’s names, took part in dramatic re-enactments, colourful festivals, public talks, and grand feasts. He wept with compassion to see people suffering, and his devotees inherited his feelings.

For this reason, the Six Goswamis – the first followers of Chaitanya - wrote books, had them painstakingly copied by many other writers, then formed communities to read and discuss them. They excavated holy places that had fallen under jungle growth, established pilgrimage centres, built temples, and arranged for festivals. Over in Bengal, eastern India, Nityananda Prabhu and his sixty-four main followers created a vast popular movement and left no part of Bengal untouched by the sound of kirtan. The devotees of Orrisa, on the eastern coast, kept the movement strong there. Years later, other intrepid preachers took the sankirtan movement out to the far east, beyond Bengal, to Assam and Manipur, where it met with royal patronage and grand success.

## Consolidation

The leaders developed preaching networks and educational systems. They developed these to better establish and consolidate the mission of Chaitanya. Every acharya in the line of Mahaprabhu was also a preacher. Not merely a teacher who taught a subject, but a preacher who exhorted others to wake up, abandon old habits, and taste the true nectar of life.

Jiva Goswami was the youngest of the Six Goswamis and became such a well-respected theologian that the Emperor of all India, Akbar the Great, personally came to see him in 1570. The Emperor was so impressed he built the Goswami a library and gave permission for three large temples to be erected in Vrindavan. He even arranged for large quantities of paper, a rare commodity, to be delivered so that Jiva Goswami could continue his writing. In 1580 the grand festival of Kheturi in eastern Bengal took place. Organised by Srinivasa Acharya, Narottama Das Thakur and Nityananda’s wife Jahnava Thakurani, it was to mark the first major celebration of Chaitanya’s birth in 1486. Functioning as a religious council, the meetings held around the festivities consolidated the three major communities of Vaishnavas: Jagannatha Puri in the southeast, Bengal in the east, and Vrindavan in the north, into philosophical agreement and a united force for mission. Together with the saintly Shyamananda, Narottama and Shrinivasa were responsible for transporting and teaching the Six Goswami’s books outside the Vrindavan area for the first time. They did this with great success.

In 1612, at the age of 90, a disciple of Raghunatha Das Goswami, Krishnadas Kaviraja, completed his monumental work Chaitanya Charitamrita. The book was to inform and strengthen the widespread community of devotees for hundreds of years to come. In 1740, the great scholar and debater Baladeva Vidyabhushana, wrote a commentary on the Vedanta Sutra which elevated the Chaitanya sampradaya amongst the intellectuals of the region, and set the seal on the royal approval and patronage of the king of Jaipur.

In the late 1800s, Bhaktivinode Thakur, the author of the Jaiva Dharma, whose ten maxims we have used as the framework for this book, single-handedly restarted the mission to spread the sankirtan movement in Victorian India. Confronted on all sides by social accommodation to the British Raj and their indoctrination of his countrymen with the supposed inferiority of ‘the Hindoo,’ the Thakur knew that he must create a spiritual and intellectual resistance movement amongst his countrymen. The lineages of Chaitanya Mahaprabhu were riddled with hypocrites and scoundrels, snake-oil salesmen and exploiters of all kinds. Even the good name of the Vaishnavas had been lost. Those pure-hearted souls, the genuine Vaishnava saints who could have saved the situation, were scanty in number, old and reclusive.

So, the Thakur used his social position to influence the Calcutta elites and restore some respectability for the Vaishnava community. He wrote as if he was on fire, producing many solid books of theology in accessible language. As a deputy magistrate he toured the local towns for his work, then held kirtans in the evening. In this way he established more than five hundred kirtan groups. He wrote devotional songs in the common Bengali language and had them mass-printed and distributed wherever he stopped for the evening. Bhaktivinode unearthed the original site of the birthplace of Chaitanya Mahaprabhu in Mayapur, its location lost for many years because of the changing course of the Ganges river. He personally begged a rupee from every respectable home in Calcutta and built a beautiful temple there. He opened a three-story building as headquarters in Calcutta in 1911 and personally trained his son, Bimala Prasada, in the matter of writing and printing.

## Expansion

In turn, his son went on to start a fellowship among his student friends, the ‘August Assembly,’ and then became a sannyasi and inaugurated the Gaudiya Math in 1918. Now known as Bhaktisiddhanta Saraswati Thakur, he created 64 branches of his organisation throughout India, Burma, and England; he wrote as copiously as his father, whether it was books, booklets, and regular magazines, tracts, and newspapers. He was the first ever person to record Vaishnava songs to vinyl, the first to broadcast on the BBC, the first to stage a travelling waxworks exhibit, and the first to organise agricultural shows for the local people – which included wrestling matches! At its height, the organisation had two steamships full of devotees preaching up and down the Ganges, hundreds of saffron-robed brahmachari monks throughout India, and eighteen sannyasis to guide around ten thousand disciples.

Inspired by all of this and having been given the order by his guru to preach in the English language, A. C. Bhaktivedanta Swami Prabhupada took the message of Krishna all over the world. His compassion for others began with just a small band of young people underneath a tree, in 1960s America, yet grew to a world-embracing fellowship of more than a million.

He wrote eighty books, gave two or three public talks every day for twelve years, circled the world fourteen times, initiated 5,000 disciples, established 108 temples, and inspired hundreds of thousands. Through his books he has reached 500 million people to date. The movement he started now has more than 900 branches, including farms, schools, and restaurants, two hospitals and programmes to feed more than a million children and vulnerable adults every day.

It would not be unreasonable to say that Srila Prabhupada became one of the more well-known religious leaders of the 20th century who has now exceeded the popularity expected from such a figure in the 21st. As other luminaries have faded, his fame has increased due to a burgeoning number of followers attracted to his message. One of the reasons for the success would surely be that he preserved intact the most essential elements of a successful belief and cultural system which was already thousands of years old. Vaishnavism had already withstood the test of time. Professor A.L. Basham (1914-1986), a world-renowned Indian religions expert and author of The Wonder that was India, explained:

“Since the Second World War there has been a great increase in the number of practitioners and teachers of Indian mystical gnosis in the Western world. These people have been essentially teachers of yoga, but they have not been, in the same sense as in your Hare Krishna movement, religious leaders.

The numerous organizations which have brought Indian ideas to the Western world have essentially been societies of people interested in mysticism and so on, mostly middle-aged people who met together once a week and listened to the local swami lecturing to them, and then went back and carried on with their conventional secular lives. But the Hare Krishna movement demands a significant change in one's way of life if one is to become a full member of it. As such, I don't think anything like it has occurred in the European context since the days of the Roman Empire when Christianity, Judaism, and other religions made numerous converts in the West. It is, therefore, something which hasn't happened for a very long time indeed, and so I feel that it is very important historically.”

“This attitude of loving devotion to a personal God, as developed in Indian bhakti tradition, is undoubtedly one of India's greatest gifts to the world, and a very precious and a very valuable gift if the world will accept it.”

But although he changed history by single-handedly spreading the mission of Chaitanya Mahaprabhu in the modern world, and left behind him some very determined and deeply committed followers, Srila Prabhupada did not expect that the matter of continuing the mission would be left only to a few. He described the purpose of his organisation, ISKCON, as ‘the respiritualisation of the world,’ and requested all his disciples and other followers, whether monk or married, to ‘work co-operatively to spread this movement.’

Every Vaishnava who understands the importance of Krishna consciousness as given to us by such remarkable teachers, and who strives daily for spiritual advancement, must also accept the personal responsibility to help others. It will not be easy, because the world sinks deeper into the mire of Kali Yuga every day, but if we dedicate ourselves to the task, learn from one another how it can be done, and pray to all the saints for guidance, humility, and compassion, the progress will be made. The challenges may be great, but the divine rewards are even greater. May the readers of this book live long, grow in devotion, and help others to awaken their love for Krishna.

## Appendix 1. Bhagavad-gita verses in support of the ten lessons

**Lesson One - Evidence: The Vedas received through guru-parampara are known as infallible knowledge.**

15.15 - sarvasya caham hrdi sannivisto

I am seated in everyone’s heart, and from me come remembrance, knowledge, and forgetfulness. By all the Vedas I am to be known; I am the compiler of the Vedas, and the knower of the Vedas.

4.1-3 – imam vivasvate yogam

I instructed science of yoga to the sun god and it was passed on through a lineage of disciples. Because you are a devotee you can understand the transcendental mystery of this science.

4.34 – tad viddhi pranipatena

Approach a guru, inquire submissively, and render service.

2.7 – karpanya-dosopahata-svabhavah

(Arjuna said) Now I am your disciple. Please instruct me.

**Lesson Two - Ultimate Reality: Sri Hari is the Absolute Truth.**

7.7 - mattah parataram nanyat

There is no Truth superior to Me. Everything rests upon Me, as pearls are strung on a thread.

10.8 - aham sarvasya prabhavo

I am the source of all spiritual and material worlds. Everything emanates from Me. The wise who know this perfectly engage in My devotional service and worship Me with all their hearts.

**Lesson Three - Source of all energies: Sri Hari is the possessor of all potency.**

7.4-6 - bhumir apo 'nalo vayuh

4 - Earth, water, fire, air, ether, mind, intelligence, and ego—altogether these eight comprise My separated material energies.

5 - Besides this inferior nature, O mighty-armed Arjuna, there is a superior energy of Mine, which are all living entities who are struggling with material nature and are sustaining the universe.

6 - Of all that is material and all that is spiritual in this world, know for certain that I am both its origin and dissolution.

**Lesson Four - Ocean of Rasa: Sri Krishna is the ocean of nectarean mellows.**

10.9 - The thoughts of my pure devotees dwell in me, their lives are fully devoted to my service, and they derive great satisfaction and bliss from always enlightening one another and conversing about me.

10.10 - To those who are constantly devoted to serving me with love, I give the understanding by which they can come to me.

**Lesson Five - Souls: Both the liberated and the conditioned jivas are his eternally separated parts and parcels.**

Never was there a time when the soul did not exist; nor in the future will it ever cease to be. As the soul continuously passes through all changes of body, the soul similarly passes into another body at death. The soul does not die when the body dies. Happiness and distress arise from sense perception. – 2.12-14

For the soul there is neither birth nor death at any time. He has not come into being, does not come into being, and will not come into being. He is unborn, eternal, ever existing and primeval. The soul accepts new bodies as a person puts on new garments. – 2.20-22

15.7 - mamaivamso jiva-loke

The living entities in this conditioned world are My eternal, fragmental parts. Due to conditioned life, they are struggling very hard with the six senses, which include the mind.

**Lesson Six - Conditioned souls: Some souls are subject to the control and covering of maya.**

14.4-10 - All species are made possible by birth in this material nature, and I am the seed-giving father. Nature consists of three modes – goodness, passion, and ignorance. When the living entity encounters nature, he becomes conditioned by these modes. Goodness, being purer than the others, is illuminating, and it frees one from sinful reactions. Those situated in that mode become conditioned by a sense of happiness and knowledge. Passion is born of unlimited desires and longings, and because of this the living entity is bound to material fruitive actions. Darkness, born of ignorance, is the delusion of all living entities. The results are madness, indolence, and sleep, which bind the soul. The mode of goodness conditions one to happiness; passion conditions one to fruitive action; and ignorance, covering one’s knowledge, binds one to madness. Sometimes the mode of goodness becomes prominent, defeating the modes of passion and ignorance. Sometimes passion defeats goodness and ignorance, and at other times ignorance defeats goodness and passion. In this way there is always competition for supremacy.

15.8 - sariram yad avapnoti

The living entity in the material world carries his different conceptions of life from one body to another as the air carries aromas.

**Lesson Seven - Freed souls: Other souls are free from maya.**

14.18 - Those situated in goodness gradually go upward to the higher planets; those in the mode of passion live on the earthly planets; and those in the abominable mode of ignorance go down to the hellish worlds.

7.14 - daivi hy esa guna-mayi

This divine energy of Mine, consisting of the three modes of material nature, is difficult to overcome. But those who have surrendered unto Me can easily cross beyond it.

14.26 - One who engages in full devotional service, unfailing in all circumstances, at once transcends the modes of nature and thus comes to the level of Brahman.

8.15 - mam upetya punar janma

After attaining Me, the great souls, who are yogis in devotion, never return to this temporary world, which is full of miseries, because they have attained the highest perfection.

**Lesson Eight - Oneness and difference: The entire universe is inconceivably and simultaneously different and non-different from Sri Hari.**

9.4 - maya tatam idam sarvam

By Me, in My unmanifested form, this entire universe is pervaded. All beings are in Me, but I am not in them.

9.5 - na ca mat-sthani bhutani

And yet everything that is created does not rest in Me. Behold My mystic opulence! Although I am the maintainer of all living entities, and although I am everywhere, still My Self is the very source of creation.

**Lesson Nine - Pure Bhakti: Pure devotional service is the only means to attain perfection.**

9.13 - mahatmanas tu mam partha

O son of Prtha, those who are not deluded, the great souls, are under the protection of the divine nature. They are fully engaged in devotional service because they know Me as the Supreme Personality of Godhead, original and inexhaustible.

9.14 - satatam kirtayanto mam

Always chanting My glories, endeavoring with great determination, bowing down before Me, these great souls perpetually worship Me with devotion.

**Lesson Ten - The Final Goal: Transcendental love and affection for Krishna is the one and only final object of attainment.**

9.22 - ananyas cintayanto mam

But those who worship Me with devotion, meditating on My transcendental form-to them I carry what they lack and preserve what they have.

9.29 - samo 'ham sarva-bhutesu

I envy no one, nor am I partial to anyone. I am equal to all. But whoever renders service unto Me in devotion is a friend, is in Me, and I am also a friend to him.

18.65 - man-mana bhava mad-bhakto

Always think of Me and become My devotee. Worship Me and offer your homage unto Me. Thus, you will come to Me without fail. I promise you this because you are My very dear friend.

18.66 - sarva-dharman parityajya

Abandon all varieties of religion and just surrender unto Me. I shall deliver you from all sinful reaction. Do not fear.

## Appendix 3. Lord Chaitanya’s instructions

Lord Chaitanya gave individual instructions and responsibilities to his followers:

1\. Srila Svarupa Damodara Goswami – to disseminate rasa-upasana, the process of bhajan inculcated with rasa. He composed his diaries on this subject in two sections: antah-pantha – the esoteric, internal means of attainment, and bahih-pantha – the exoteric, external means of attainment.

2\. Srila Raghunatha Das Goswami – was entrusted with the esoteric process.

3\. Srila Vakresvara Pandit – was entrusted with the exoteric process.

4\. Srila Gopala Guru Goswami – was given the teachings by Vakresvara Pandit.

5\. Sri Dhyanachandra – was given the teachings by Gopala Guru Goswami and wrote the Paddhati.

6\. Sri Nityananda – was requested by Lord Chaitanya to spread the glories of Harinama.

7\. Srila Advaita Acarya - was requested by Lord Chaitanya to spread the glories of Harinama.

8\. Srila Rupa Goswami – was asked to explain the principles of rasa

9\. Srila Sanatana Goswami – was asked to delineate the principles of vaidhi-bhakti, the relationship between vaidhi-bhakti and raga-bhakti, and the subtle truths of prakrita and aprakrita pastimes in Vraja.

10\. Srila Jiva Goswami – was asked, through Sri Nityananda and Srila Sanatana Goswami, to properly analyse and annotate the principles of sambandha, abhidheya and prayojana.

11\. Srila Ramananda Raya – was asked to broadcast the glories of rasa tattva.

12\. Srila Sarvabhauma Bhattacarya – was asked to teach the tattva of devotional service and surrender.

13\. Srila Raghunatha Bhatta Goswami – was told to propagate the pre-eminence and glories of Srimad Bhagavatam.

14\. Srila Gopala Bhatta Goswami – was told to write treatises by which one can easily detect and expose aberrant philosophies and practises adulterating the principles of pure sringara-rasa. Simultaneously he was to ensure the importance of vaidhi-bhakti was understood and followed.

15\. Srila Prabodhananda Saraswati (uncle and guru of Gopala Bhatta Goswami) – to establish within the world that the path of loving devotional surrender steeped in the sentiments of Vraja is the highest point of spiritual realisation.

## About the author

Kripamoya Das

Kripamoya Das teaches courses and classes on the Bhagavad-gita and Practical Bhakti around the UK and Europe. He is a regular speaker at the numerous kirtan groups that have sprung up in many cities. He provides encouragement and support as a mentor; conducts celebrations such as weddings, and occasionally guides groups of pilgrims to holy places in India. He lives close to the Bhaktivedanta Manor in Hertfordshire with his wife Guru Charana Padma, and has three grown children, Jahnavi, Tulasi and Mali. He was born in 1956 and has been a disciple of His Divine Grace A.C. Bhaktivedanta Swami Prabhupada since 1975.
